Chapter 1

Two Months on a Doorstep

The first thing I ever learned was the cold. The second was that someone, somewhere, had already disagreed with it. —the journals, vol. I (begun much later, remembering)

Interlude: Privet Drive, 1 November 1981, shortly before midnight

The cat had been watching the corner of Privet Drive since dawn, and the cat was not a cat.

When the man appeared—elderly, silver-bearded, dressed in a purple travelling cloak that no one on Privet Drive would have approved of, had anyone on Privet Drive been awake to disapprove—the street lamps went out one by one, politely, as if they had been asked. The man put a small silver device back into his pocket and sat down on the garden wall beside the cat.

“Fancy seeing you here, Professor McGonagall.”

The cat gave him a flat look and became a severe-looking woman in square spectacles. They talked in low voices about the thing the whole hidden world was celebrating that night, and the two deaths at the centre of it that rather fewer people seemed to be mourning, and about the child.

“You can’t mean the people who live here,” said Professor McGonagall, rising to look at Number Four as though it had said something rude. “I’ve watched them all day. They are the worst sort of Muggles. Albus, this boy will be famous. Every child in our world will know his name. And these people will never understand him—”

“Which is rather the point,” said Dumbledore gently. “Famous before he can walk and talk. Famous for something he won’t even remember. Can you not see how much better off he will be, growing up away from all of that, until he is ready to take it?”

McGonagall opened her mouth, and found it full of objections too large to fit through, and closed it again.

The motorcycle fell out of the sky at a quarter past, carrying a man twice too large for it and a bundle of blankets much too small. There were tears, most of them Hagrid’s. There was a letter, tucked inside the blankets with care. There was a minute, perhaps longer, when the greatest wizard of the age stood over the sleeping child on the doorstep of Number Four and looked very old, and said something quiet that neither of the others heard.

Then the street was empty. The lamps came back on. A cloud crossed the moon, and on the doorstep the bundle of blankets shifted, a small hand working free into the November air.

Albus Dumbledore was, that year, the most accomplished wizard alive, and he had thought the matter through with very great care. He had considered the wards that would grow from a mother’s sacrifice and an aunt’s grudging roof. He had considered fame and its particular poison. He had considered Voldemort’s followers, the Ministry, the press, and his own heart, which he trusted least of all.

He had considered everything, in short, except the one thing that no one alive could have considered, because it had never happened before, to anyone—and it was lying on the doorstep in a basket of blankets, and it was about to wake up.

The cold came first.

It came in slowly, the way everything came in now—and that was the thing, that was the enormous thing he had no words for, because he had almost no words at all: everything had gone slow. Or he had gone somewhere the slowness lived. The cold crept up through the blankets like a tide that took hours to cross an inch, and he had time to feel it arrive, time to dislike it thoroughly, time to grow bored of disliking it, all before it finished arriving.

He was fifteen months old. What he had, in the way of a mind, was what any small child has: a warm bright patchwork of faces and sensations, a handful of almost-words, a limitless appetite, and no idea whatsoever that he was anyone in particular. On an ordinary night he would have cried, and slept, and remembered nothing.

This was not an ordinary night. Something had happened to him the night before—a green that was not like other greens, a voice, an ending—and whatever else that something had done, wherever else its marks had landed, it had reached into the small soft clockwork of him and spun the one wheel that mattered up to a speed no human wheel had ever turned.

He woke on a stone doorstep in Surrey, in the dark, in the cold, inside a single second that went on and on and on.

He cried, of course. He was a baby; it was the tool he had. And this was the first lesson the new world taught him, before language, before names, before anything: the cry left him and nothing happened. The sound itself came out wrong—long, bending, a foghorn heard through deep water—and then it was gone into the dark, and no one came. No one came for what felt like days. He lay in the vast silence between his own sobs, each one a slow tide going out, and somewhere in that silence the crying stopped being something happening to him and became something he was doing, a thing with a shape, a lever that was not attached to anything. He let go of the lever.

That was the first night of his life, and in one sense it lasted about seven hours, and in the sense that counts it lasted two months, and he spent almost all of it learning the three objects available for study.

The first was the cold, which has been mentioned, and which repaid study poorly.

The second was the light. There was a lamp somewhere above and to the left—orange, steady, humming a note so low and stretched that it was less a sound than a place. The light did not change, but the sky behind it did, so slowly that watching it move was like watching the minute hand of a clock, if a minute hand were the lid of the whole world. He watched a star cross a gap between rooftops. It took an age. He had an age. It was the first thing he ever watched all the way to the end on purpose, and when the star slid at last behind the chimney he felt something small and fierce that, years later, with a vocabulary, he would identify as satisfaction.

The third object was the warmth, and the warmth was the strange one.

The blankets were ordinary blankets. He knew nothing about blankets, but even to a baby, cloth is cloth: it keeps the warmth you bring to it and gives nothing of its own. These blankets gave. When the cold tide crept too far up, something in the blankets rose to meet it—a soft pushing-back, a warmth arriving from nowhere, exactly where the cold bit worst and nowhere else. He did the experiment before he knew what an experiment was: pushed one hand out into the night air until it ached, pulled it back in. The warmth found the hand. He did it again. The warmth found it again, patient, unbothered, as many times as he asked.

Somebody had disagreed with the cold. Somebody had left the disagreement here, folded into cloth, working all night with no one to see it work. He had no words for any of this and would not for another year, but the shape of it went in deep and stayed: the world had rules, and something in the world could tell the rules no, and the no was kind.

He fell asleep testing it, and in his sleep the green came back—the green, and the voice, a woman’s voice, two slow syllables he had used to know—and even the dream ran long now, long enough to live in, and this, too, he would keep.

Much later—years later, when he had clocks, and numbers, and a cupboard to be systematic in—he would measure it properly, with a pendulum made of washers and string and a counting trick for the gaps. Call it two hundred to one. Two hundred seconds of him to every second of the world. Two hundred days inside each day; a fat subjective fortnight in every hour; between any tick and its tock, time enough to think a thought the whole way through, turn it over, and file it. But on the doorstep he had none of that, no numbers at all. He only knew that the night was an ocean, and he was in it, and it could not be cried at, and the star had crossed the gap, and the warmth kept coming.

By morning he had stopped being afraid of the dark. There was too much of it, too steadily, for fear to keep its footing; fear is a sprinter, and the night asked for something with longer legs. What settled in fear’s place had no name yet either. Patience is close, but patience implies effort. It was more like the beginning of a long professional acquaintance with time itself.

The sky went grey, then pearl. A bird started up somewhere, its song unrolled into a slow bright procession of separate notes, each one arriving like a scheduled train, and he was listening to it with his whole mind—there was so much room in a sound, nobody could have guessed how much room—when the sky wheeled, enormous shapes crossed it, and a face came down at him like a falling moon.

The face had curlers in its hair and an expression he would come to know professionally. The mouth opened. The scream took a very long time to arrive, and when it arrived it went on, a siren winding slowly up through its registers, and he lay in the warm disagreeing blankets and watched the sound happen the way he had watched the star.

That was his aunt. That was the first morning. The letter lay under his hand, and seventy miles away a hidden world was toasting his name, and he was two days old, or two months, depending on how you counted.

Depending on how you counted would turn out to be the story of his life.

Of the time that followed—the cot years, the kitchen-floor years—he remembered, afterwards, all of it. Whatever had spun the small wheel of him up to its impossible speed had built the shelves to match. The days did not run together the way anyone else’s second year runs together. They stayed where he put them. Years later he could walk back into any afternoon he chose and find it waiting, down to the weather on the window.

The house at Number Four resolved slowly, the way a photograph develops. He assembled it by sound long before he was allowed to walk its rooms: the hall clock with its fat dry tick (an event; a landmark; the metronome of the universe), the boiler’s whoosh at seven, the letterbox’s double clack, the particular grief of the stairs under his uncle’s tread. He knew the house the way a sailor knows a coastline from offshore.

The people took longer, because the people were enormous and arbitrary and mostly cross. There were three. He sorted them long before he could name them.

The Sharp One moved fastest and was afraid of almost everything, though it took him a long time to work out that the thing she was most afraid of was him. She looked at him the way one looks at a stain that might spread. But she fed him, on a schedule as reliable as the boiler, and reliability was currency in his economy; he could do a great deal with anything that repeated.

The Purple One was a weather system: slow to build, loud at landfall, gone by supper. The trick with weather is not to argue with it. He had worked that out by the second winter, which for him was the second half-century, and after that his uncle's rages passed over him like fronts over a well-built shed.

The Small Loud One was, he eventually accepted with real surprise, the same kind of thing as himself—another child, two months older by the calendar and getting on for a thousand years younger by any measure that counted. Dudley wanted things, screamed, got them, and wanted new things, a loop that ran all day with the dull dependability of the hall clock. He was also, and this was the useful part, a torrent of experimental data: everything Harry was not permitted, Dudley was given, so everything—toys, telly, tantrums, trips—could be observed in trial without the cost of running it himself. Other people, he understood very early, were experiments the world had already paid for. You only had to watch.

And he watched. Great heavens, how he watched. There was nothing else to do.

This is the part that is hard to say lightly, and it should be said anyway, once, so that it never needs saying again: a mind is an engine, and an engine without fuel does not idle politely. It eats itself. Boredom—real boredom, the deep kind, boredom at two hundred to one in a playpen in a spotless silent lounge—was the first enemy of his life, the first thing he ever met that could actually kill him, and he knew it the way a child knows a hot stove, wordlessly and absolutely. The afternoons were a hundred days long. The nights were worse. No one was coming with fuel. If the engine was to run, it would have to run on what it could make.

So he made things. It began as flailing—counting games, at first with nothing to count but heartbeats and the hall clock, and the games grew rules, and the rules grew consequences, until by the time he was three the counting had become a kind of kingdom, with provinces (the sevens, the dozens, the lovely treacherous elevens) and laws and border disputes that could be prosecuted for a profitable week inside a rainy morning. He mapped the sounds of the house and set them to work as a calendar, a clock, an early-warning system. He took the day’s thin events—a milk float, a dropped fork, one of Dudley’s cartoons overheard from the hall—and rebuilt them at leisure, better: the fork falling in twelve ways, the cartoon’s story repaired where it was stupid, which was mostly everywhere. He discovered that a remembered thing could be walked through if you fixed it to places—he hung his numbers on the cot bars and his words, when the words came, in the rooms of the house like coats on pegs—and so, in the second year of his life, in a terraced house in Surrey, a small boy in a hand-me-down babygro independently reinvented the memory palace of Simonides, and furnished it, and moved in.

It was not a sad childhood, whatever it looks like from out here at one-to-one. That is the other thing that must be said once and then left alone. From inside, it was a crowded childhood: crowded with projects, with campaigns in the counting kingdom, with the slow delicious assembly of the great jigsaw of English. He was never alone in there. He was the king and parliament and census of a country of one, and the country was always busy, and when he thought about the stain-look his aunt gave him it was mostly with the distant interest of a meteorologist logging one more kind of weather.

But the engine needed fuel, and the richest fuel in the house was language, and language is where he spent his treasure.

Speech, at two hundred to one, is geology. A sentence is an era. Between the first syllable of a word and its second, there was time to take the sound apart, hold the pieces to the light, compare them with every neighbouring sound in the collection, and shelve them, and wait, refreshed, for the second syllable to lumber in. He heard perhaps an hour of honest talk a day—the Dursleys were not a talkative family, except at the television—and that hour was two hundred hours long, and he mined it to bedrock. Grammar surrendered slowly, a border province at a time. Words he could not crack he stored whole, labelled with their contexts, three hundred of them waiting in a lobby of the palace like strangers at a visa office, each one eventually processed, stamped, admitted.

By his second birthday, by the calendar, he understood effectively everything said in that house, including most of what was said over his head in the deliberate spelled-out code the Sharp One used for secrets—P, A, R, K was an early, valuable break—and the television had become what the lamp and the star had been on the doorstep: the brightest object in the sky. The television! A firehose of worlds. He was not often allowed in its room, but walls are nothing to sound, and he lay in the lounge playpen or sat small and forgotten in the hall and drank it: adverts, snooker, the shipping forecast, Dudley’s cartoons, the nine o’clock news with its grave slow music. The news was the best thing in his universe. The news said, every night, in the voice of final authority: the world is enormous, and full of events, and it is out there.

Speaking was another matter. His mouth was not fast; nothing about his body was. The tongue is a slow student even for ordinary children, and he had to drill it in real time like everyone else—silently, at night, under the blanket, shaping soundless syllables over and over while the house ticked around him. He was in no hurry. He had learned the shape of his situation by then, learned it from a thousand small returns of the same lesson: his advantages lived inside; outside, he was a very small person in a house of large suspicious weather, and anything the weather noticed, it rained on.

He did not yet have the rule in words. The words arrived the day he made his first real mistake.

He was two and a half. It was a Tuesday in spring, the back door open to the garden for the first warm day, and the Sharp One had spent twenty minutes—a two-month expedition, from where he sat—turning the kitchen upside down for her shopping list, with rising notes in her voice that promised weather by lunchtime. He knew where the list was. He had watched Dudley, that morning, in the way he watched everything, peel it off the pad and post it with ceremony into the gap behind the cooker, for no reason whatever, because Dudley was two and a half.

The calculation that followed was quick, which is to say it took him most of an hour and she had barely crossed the kitchen in it. Distress, cause known, remedy cheap. Helping would cost nothing and might purchase goodwill, and goodwill converted, at a miserable exchange rate but reliably, into the outdoors. He had been drilling the sentence silently for weeks, as he drilled all his sentences, a long patient queue of them waiting for their moments. He stood up in the playpen and deployed it.

“Behind the cooker, Aunt Petunia,” he said. “Dudley posted it there this morning.”

Clear as a newsreader. Consonants finished, the way no child of two finishes consonants. Posted. He was proud of posted.

His aunt did not move for one and a half seconds, which gave him a long, long time to study what was happening to her face, and what was happening to her face was not gratitude, and it was not even surprise. It was the stain-look, arrived suddenly at flood stage. It was fear, white and total, and her hand came up to her collarbone, and she said—quietly, which was the worst part, she was never quiet—

“Just like—”

—and stopped the sentence with her own mouth, as if a syllable more might let something out of a drawer. Then she took the list out from behind the cooker without looking at it, and took Dudley upstairs on her hip, and the kitchen door clicked, and he stood alone in the playpen in the first warm day of the year, listening to the house tick, with an era of uninterrupted time in which to review his results.

The data, he found, were quite clean.

Item: she had not been pleased. Item: she had been afraid, and of nothing in the room but him. Item: the fear had an old shape, worn smooth, a groove her face already knew the way the stairs knew his uncle’s tread—this was not the first time she had been afraid of a child who spoke too well. Item, from the archive: just like. Just like whom? There was a whom. Somewhere upstream of him there was a whom, filed in the same drawer his aunt had just slammed, and the drawer had a label, and he had heard the label through the wall at night in his uncle’s weather-voice, and the label was freak.

He turned the word over for a while, there in the slow Tuesday light. It did not hurt. He examined it carefully to see whether it would hurt and found only taxonomy: the house contained a category for people whose insides did not match their outsides, the category was feared, and he was in it. Noted. Everything useful he had, he had because his insides did not match his outsides; the mismatch was the whole estate. The error had not been having it. The error had been showing it, spending a two-month drill on a sentence that bought him nothing and cost him the one thing he could not afford, which was the attention of the weather.

So he made the rule, the first law ever passed in the country of one, and he made it in words, because by then he had plenty:

Be small.

Not forever, he amended at once—even then, even at two, the amendment went in before the ink was dry. The world was enormous and full of events and it was out there; the news said so every night; smallness was a tactic, not a destiny. Be small here. Be small for now. Fold the whole estate down until it fits behind a toddler’s face, pay out the difference in patience, and wait.

He was, as has been mentioned, very good at waiting.

The waiting had a shape, and the shape was a campaign, and the campaign took a year.

He became, by degrees too fine for the Dursleys to see as degrees, a convenient child. Not a charming one—charm drew the stain-look; he had the data on that now. Convenient. Quiet in the right ways, at the right hours. A finder of lost things, provided the finds were spaced out and plausibly accidental: the car keys in the fruit bowl, the chequebook under the Radio Times, each discovery costing him nothing but surveillance he was doing anyway, each one lowering, by a fraction of a degree, the temperature of suspicion that followed him around the house. He learned to be where work disappeared from: shoes paired by the door, toys herded to Dudley’s end of the lounge. The Sharp One never thanked him and never quite caught herself ceasing to mind him, which was the intended product. You cannot make such people love you, he had concluded, watching her the way he watched everything; her drawers were shut and nailed. But fear needs fuel, and a sufficiently boring child starves it.

He gave her eleven months of boring, and then, on a bright cold October morning, standing in the hall in his duffel coat at precisely the moment the logistics of taking Dudley to the shops collided with the horror of leaving Harry in the house unsupervised, he looked up and deployed—in the flat Surrey vowels of the house, consonants carefully unfinished, a sentence rehearsed so many hundreds of times that it had worn its own groove in him—

“I c’n hold the basket.”

Four words. A year of engineering. His aunt looked down at him, and he watched the cost-benefit run across her face like cloud shadow crossing a lawn, slow, slow, and he kept his own face toddler-blank through all of it, and at the end of it she said, “You’ll walk and not dawdle,” and took down his reins from the peg.

And the door opened, and the world came in.

He had seen the front garden. He had seen the car’s windows, on trips to Mrs Figg’s at the end of the road, who smelled of cabbage and kept a great many cats and of whom much more would need thinking later. He had not seen this: the whole street at walking pace and his own eyes free to spend. It was two hundred yards to the parade of shops and he passed through them the way other men pass through a decade. Every gate a different latch, every car a different badge with different words on it, a postman whose bag's settling told him roughly what the post weighed, pavement cracks running their own slow civil engineering seminar, and the people, the people—every face doing a dozen things at once and all of it legible at his speed, boredom and hurry and small kindnesses, a man lying cheerfully to a neighbour about a hedge. The visit to the greengrocer's alone was a university. The basket was heavy. He held it the whole way, both arms, because he had said he would, and because the saying and the doing being the same thing was going to be, he had already decided, part of the estate.

And then, outside the newsagent’s, the world—which had already given him more that morning than the whole house had given him in a year—leaned down, unasked, and handed him the key to everything else.

A man was standing at the rack with a newspaper open. Harry knew newspapers; one came through the Dursleys’ letterbox every day with its double clack and was read by the Purple One at the kitchen table like a wall being held up. He knew the marks on them, rank upon rank of grey marks, and he had long filed the marks where he filed most mysteries, in the lobby, pending. Now he stood at knee height in the cold bright morning and watched the man’s eyes, and the eyes were moving—jump, rest, jump, rest, little precise saccadic hops along the ranks of marks, line after line, the exact motion of his aunt’s eyes crossing a letter, his uncle’s crossing the sports page, the newsreader’s crossing the thing below the camera. The eyes walked along the marks, and at the end of some lines the man’s face changed, a flicker of the same traffic that talk produced.

The marks were speech. Fixed speech. Speech that stood still and waited for you, that could be read as slowly as you liked, as often as you liked, speech from people who had never set foot in Little Whinging, from people—the thought arrived with the force of the doorstep cold—who might even be dead, and it would keep. Every rank of marks was a voice that could not be used up. The newsagent’s rack alone held more fixed speech than the Dursleys produced in a month. And somewhere—the news had shown him pictures, shelves and shelves in buildings built for nothing else—somewhere there were rooms of this.

His aunt took his hand, and he walked home at the end of the reins, three years old or six hundred depending on how you counted, past the gates and the latches and the slow seminar of the pavement, carrying the first page of the rest of his life.

The marks could be learned. Dudley owned books he never opened; the house took a newspaper every day; the television wrote words at the bottom of its pictures and spoke them aloud at the same time, which made it, he saw now, a patient tutor that would drill him for hours and never once notice him learning, never once look down and be afraid.

Be small, said the first law, and he would be, he would be the smallest thing on Privet Drive, a convenient, boring, duffel-coated nothing, for exactly as long as it took.

A second was a long time, after all. And he had so very many of them.

Chapter 2

The Operating Manual

Every household keeps two sets of rules: the ones it announces and the ones it runs on. Nobody writes the second set down. Somebody should. It took me years to accept that I had been given the only copy. —the journals, vol. I

The cupboard under the stairs became his in the spring after his fourth birthday, by a process that no one in the house would have been able to reconstruct afterwards, because no one in the house had noticed it was a process.

The official sequence of events ran as follows. Dudley’s toys, breeding steadily in the second bedroom, reached the density at which Aunt Petunia could no longer open the door without preparing herself first, and began to annex the box room where Harry slept. Uncle Vernon observed, over several Sundays, that the boy hardly had anything in there anyway. Aunt Petunia observed that the camp bed would fit perfectly well under the stairs, where she had been meaning to clear out the hoover attachments for years. It was all extremely sensible, and the Dursleys congratulated themselves on their sense, and if either of them felt a flicker of something less comfortable about billeting a four-year-old in a cupboard, the flicker was smothered under the warm agreement that it was only practical, and that he was a strange, quiet child who probably preferred it.

The unofficial sequence of events had taken Harry five months.

It had begun the day he understood—watching Dudley receive, inhabit, and lay waste to the second bedroom—that in this house, rooms were status, and status would never flow his way, but that space and status were different currencies, and he only needed one of them. What he needed was a door that shut. A place where no eyes went. Somewhere the estate—the whole folded-down country of him—could be unfolded, inventoried, and worked, without first checking the hall for weather.

The second bedroom could never be that; it had traffic. But the cupboard under the stairs had a door, a latch, a light with its own switch, and a social meaning so low that no Dursley would ever open it except to shove something in. It was the one space in the house that suspicion had no reason to visit. He had conducted the campaign with the patience of a man moving a garden wall six inches by leaning on it: toys he was alleged to share migrating to Dudley’s room a piece a week; the hoover attachments developing a tendency to be in Petunia’s way; the virtues of the arrangement arriving in Vernon’s mind, by way of small staged inconveniences, as his own shrewd idea. When the camp bed finally scraped in under the sloping ceiling and the door clicked shut on him for the first time with his things inside it—one bed, one blanket, one shoebox—he lay in the dark of the smallest office in England and felt the nearest thing to proprietorship he had ever been permitted.

The spiders held a general meeting and decided to tolerate him. He decided to learn their names.

It would be put about, much later, by people writing about his childhood with more indignation than information, that he had been kept in a cupboard, and the word did an honest day’s work for the indignant, and he never bothered to correct it. But the truth, set the same night on the growing shelf he kept for the house, where no one could read it because it was in his head and nowhere else, was this: he had surveyed Number Four, Privet Drive, identified the one defensible position it contained, and taken it.

He had been in possession of the marks—the whole lovely standing army of them—for a year by then, and this is how the campaign had gone.

The problem of reading, approached from zero, with no teacher, by someone who must not be seen learning, decomposes into three problems. First: which marks exist? (Manageable. The ranks of marks that mattered came in two parades, big and small, of twenty-six each, plus a rabble of numbers and dots; he had the full set collected within a fortnight of the newsagent’s, harvested from cereal packets at breakfast while appearing to mash his cereal.) Second: what sounds do the marks make? (Hard. The marks of English, he would eventually conclude, had the loosest professional discipline of any system he ever studied; the letter combinations drank, moonlighted, and lied about their hours.) Third: how do you get a corpus—marks and speech, certified to mean the same thing, delivered side by side where a small boy can study them without anyone once saying, what’s he looking at?

It was the third problem that should have been impossible, and the television solved it for him, twice over, because the television was the one member of the household that answered questions tirelessly and never looked down.

The first gift was the adverts. Adverts repeated—the same twenty seconds, dozens of times a week—and adverts loved to print their slogans on the screen while a warm voice spoke them aloud. Marks, sound, meaning: certified, synchronised, and repeated until even an ordinary child half-learned them, which was the camouflage. Nobody marvels at a four-year-old chanting adverts.

The second gift he found by accident on a wet Saturday, when Dudley—mashing the remote control in pursuit of something that had stopped pleasing him—struck the sequence that summoned page 888, and words appeared at the bottom of the film, white on black bars, and changed when the people spoke. Subtitles. For the deaf, said the continuity announcer. Harry sat very still on the lounge carpet, in the blind spot behind the armchair where he was tolerated when quiet, and inside him the whole country of one came to a halt and stared.

A machine that wrote down speech as it happened. In the house. Available, on quiet afternoons, to a boy who had learned the remote control’s logic through the back of an armchair by sound alone.

After that it was not a question of whether but of how long, and the answer was five months—five months of real time, which was, in the only hours he truly lived in, the length of an honours degree. He worked the way water works, every allowed afternoon, matching the moving speech to the standing marks, prising the system open combination by combination: the hard C and the soft one, the silent K (filed under ambush), the behaviour of GH, which he came to regard the way Petunia regarded next door’s dog. The spelled-out secrets of the kitchen—P, A, R, K; S, W, E, E, T, S—converted overnight from a code he had cracked by frequency into plain text, which was a strange small loss; he found he missed the puzzle.

And then one evening in late spring—he was not quite four—the local news put up a caption under a man standing in front of a half-built road, and Harry read it. Not assembled it, not solved it: read it, the way the man at the newsagent’s had read, the marks going silently in and arriving as speech, no border, no customs, the whole sentence simply present in his head in the half-second his eyes crossed it. BYPASS DELAYED AGAIN, COUNCIL BLAMES COSTS. Fixed speech, flowing free.

He had expected satisfaction; he had logged, in advance, the slot where the satisfaction would go. What arrived instead frightened him for a second with its size. It was the doorstep feeling—the warmth rising through the blankets, somebody somewhere disagreeing with the cold—except that this time he had built the disagreement himself, plank by plank, in five months, under the weather’s eye, and nobody knew, and nobody could ever take it out of him. They could lock every door in the house. The marks were everywhere, and all of the marks were his now, and each one would stay read for the rest of his life.

That night in the box room (this was before the cupboard) he lay planning in long luxurious detail the reading of everything—everything, the word had a taste—and ran, almost at once, into the fact that would govern the next year of his life.

Number Four, Privet Drive, contained almost nothing to read.

He conducted the census over the following week, mostly while dusting; Petunia had begun delegating him small chores, which suited them both for different reasons. The haul, in full: one daily newspaper (excellent, but confiscated to Vernon’s chair by evening and binned by morning); the Radio Times; Petunia’s magazines (a rich seam of the language of insinuation, if thin on facts); a phone book; the instruction manuals for every appliance Vernon had ever bought, pristine in a kitchen drawer; a Bible with a wedding inscription and an unbroken spine; one shelf of hardbacks in the lounge, bought by the yard for their bindings; and, in Dudley’s room, forty-one picture books and annuals in various states of structural failure, none opened voluntarily in living memory.

He read all of it. Of course he read all of it. He read the phone book—it taught him the shape of names, the geography of Surrey, and the pleasing fact that the world contained an A. Aardvark, Aquatics, of Byfleet. He read the manuals and became, silently, the only person in the house who knew what the washing machine’s third dial did. He read the hardbacks in burgled instalments, a chapter per dusting. The famine was not of marks but of new marks; at his speeds, the house was a water glass and he needed a sea, and the sea—he knew exactly where the sea was, he had known since before he could read it. The word was painted on a building he had passed on the shopping run, gold on green, and it was the most beautiful word in Little Whinging:

LIBRARY.

The problem was that the library was a quarter-mile beyond the end of the reins, in territory he was never taken to, and that between him and it stood the whole architecture of being four: no key, no rights of movement, no account of his own, and a guardian whose policy on Harry-related requests was refusal as reflex. A frontal ask would not just fail; it would flag the library as something he wanted, and everything he wanted was, by the operating logic of the house, suspect.

So he did not ask. He opened a file, and gave the file a year, and the file’s first entry was an observation that would have surprised everyone who ever met the Dursleys: the household, examined coldly, was not a fortress. It was a machine with exactly three moving parts, and all three parts ran on schedules, and schedules were the native terrain of a boy who had once watched a star cross a gap between rooftops just to see it done.

The machine’s parts, as he had them that spring:

The Purple One ran on esteem. Vernon Dursley needed the world to confirm, hourly, that he was a man of substance and sense, and would trade almost anything for the confirmation without ever noticing a trade had occurred. He was dangerous the way a sash window is dangerous: not malicious, but heavy, and inclined to come down hard on whatever was under it when the cord slipped. Management protocol: stay out from under. Supply, in homeopathic doses, the pleasure of being right. Never be interesting.

The Sharp One ran on fear, and the fear ran on two fuels. The surface fuel was the neighbours—what might be seen, what might be said at a fence. But under it, in the nailed-shut drawer, was the other thing: the sister-shaped hole, the whom, the old fear that the freakishness would bloom again in her own house and swallow a second family. He understood perhaps a tenth of this at four—the tenth that could be read in a face at flood stage—and he handled her accordingly, as one handles a cracked vessel: no pressure, no sudden temperatures. Boring, boring, boring, eleven months and counting of boring. It was working. She had begun leaving him unsupervised with the chores.

The Small Loud One ran on appetite, and was therefore—Harry recorded this with the dispassion of a naturalist—the easiest instrument in the house. Dudley wanted; whatever stood between Dudley and the want was demolished; the want, obtained, died instantly and was replaced. You could not reason with the loop, but you could steer it, the way you steer a shopping trolley with a mad wheel: small inputs, early. A word about what was on the other channel. A toy left visible at the right moment. Dudley’s rampages, which had once cost Harry his few possessions on a monthly basis, had not touched the shoebox in half a year, because the shoebox was boring and the alternatives never were. The books Harry was quietly accumulating—rescued annuals, a one-eyed Ladybird Book of London—lived their public lives as “Dudley’s old things Harry tidies,” a category Dudley defended as property when challenged and forgot otherwise, which made Dudley, functionally, their armed guard.

It was not kindness, any of it; he did not pretend to himself that it was. He kept a place on the shelf where he was honest about the whole arrangement. These were the people he had, and none of them could be trusted with a single true thing about him, and he was going to run their house from a cupboard because the alternative was to be run. But he noted—because the place required honesty and the honesty went both ways—that the machine, well-steered, was calmer. Vernon shouted less, finding less to shout at. Petunia’s headaches thinned. Dudley, nudged away from his worst boredoms, broke fewer things, including, lately, fewer parts of other children. A better-run household was its own camouflage, and it was also—the thought arrived unbidden one evening, watching Petunia almost smile at a tidy hall—simply better, in some ledger larger than his, which he had not yet drawn up but could feel, sometimes, like weather beyond a hill.

He was four. He filed it under later.

Twice that year, the world showed him its second set of rules.

The first time was at Mrs Figg’s.

Mrs Figg lived two streets over in a house that smelled of cabbage and was upholstered in cats, and she was what the Dursleys did with Harry when even the cupboard would not do—weddings, hospital visits, Dudley’s birthday outings. The Dursleys considered her a batty old woman convenient precisely because she was the sort of person who would take ten pence an hour to mind a boy nobody wanted. Harry, by four, had a different entry for her, flagged and growing.

Item: Mrs Figg was the only adult in his life who asked him questions and then waited, actually waited, for the answers. Item: the questions had a pattern. Sprinkled through the chatter about Tibbles and Snowy and Mr Paws came, regular as posts in a fence, the other kind—was he sleeping, was he eating, had anything odd happened lately, anything funny, any accidents?—delivered in the same batty singsong, but landing, every visit, in the same places. Grown-ups repeated themselves; that was baseline. These repeated like a form being filled in.

Item—and this was the day it stopped being a curiosity and became a file—the photograph.

It was a dim afternoon in March, rain on the windows, Dudley at the zoo without him. Mrs Figg had gone to make tea, and Harry was conducting his standing survey of the mantelpiece, which held nine framed photographs of cats, because at two hundred to one even a mantelpiece of cat photographs is worth a periodic audit. Eight of the frames held what they always held. In the ninth, behind glass, a grey cat with peculiar square markings around its eyes sat on a garden wall—and as his eyes crossed it, the cat turned its head.

Fast, for the world. Lazy, for him. A long feline swivel, the head coming around through forty degrees of arc to look—he would have sworn it, did swear it, to the only court he answered to—directly at him, with an expression no cat on any tin of cat food had ever worn: appraising. Interested. Professional. Then the kettle shrieked its slow shriek in the kitchen, and the cat was a photograph again, and Mrs Figg came in with the tray and followed his eyes, and he watched her face do a thing that Petunia’s face could not have done to save its life: go perfectly, instantly, boringly blank. She set down the tray, picked up the ninth frame as if straightening it, and carried it out of the room in the same motion that offered him a biscuit.

He took the biscuit. He ate it slowly, with the full honours of toddler absorption, eyes on the middle distance, while inside, the country of one declared a state holiday and every office of the government turned to the same page.

The warmth in the blankets, that first night, that answered need and could not be accounted for. Filed three years, pending, flagged memory of an infant; unreliable; do not build on. And now: a photograph that moved, in the house of the one adult who asked, every visit, on a schedule, whether anything funny had happened to him—a photograph she removed, with tradecraft, the moment she saw it seen.

Two anomalies did not make a theory. He set the theory on the shelf anyway, that night, and put a box round it, which was his own mark for expensive if true, and the shelf was the only place it went, because he had no need of paper and a page would have cost him an hour of his hand for a thing he could not have forgotten if he tried:

There is more than one set of rules. Somebody runs on the second set. Somebody is watching to see if I do.

The second time, the world used a gentler instrument: a nursery school teacher named Miss Henmore, twenty-four years old, kind as bread, who very nearly saw him whole.

St Gregory’s Pre-School had entered his life that September, three mornings a week, and he had walked in through its gate with a complete, rehearsed, written policy. Dossier name: the Bright Little Boy. Specification: top quartile, never top. Errors: two per ten, varied, plausible—reversed letters, miscounts of one. Speech: full sentences permitted (school had different physics from home; here, articulate was safe, even rewarded) but vocabulary capped at a ceiling he revised weekly by observing the cleverest ordinary children. Questions: asked often, because silence was conspicuous, but calibrated to things a bright four-year-old wouldn't know. It was, he believed, a conservative design, and he shipped it with confidence.

It survived eleven days.

The failure analysis, conducted in the cupboard the same evening, was instructive, and he kept its conclusions for the rest of his life. The policy had failed not through a breach but through accumulation. He had made no single error. That was the error. Miss Henmore, dispensing nothing more sophisticated than attention, had noticed that Harry’s mistakes never repeated. Ordinary children's errors had signatures—the same letters flipped, the same sums overshot, week after week. His were perfect strangers to one another, a different plausible slip every time, because he had been generating them from a model of “plausible slip” instead of from a hand that genuinely slipped. Randomness, it turned out, was a tell. True noise had structure, and his noise was too clean, and nobody consciously sees that, but bread-kind young women who watch children all day see it without knowing what they’ve seen.

She had kept him back at tidy-up time, friendly as anything, and shown him a word no four-year-old in the catchment should have been able to read—yesterday, on a staffroom notice about the boiler—and asked him, smiling, no trap in her anywhere, to tell her what it said. And he had looked at it, and in the two seconds he took to decide (a comfortable six minutes, in the only hours he truly lived in, and he needed all of them) the courtroom of the country of one heard the following:

That a lie direct—I can’t read it, Miss—would hold today and corrode tomorrow; she would watch now, and the policy could not survive watching, as the last eleven days had proved. That the bell, once rung, could not be unrung; her belief had already updated, and the only live question was what shape it settled into. That there existed, between the Bright Little Boy and the truth, a defensible middle fortification: the odd, clever child—reads early, self-taught, solemn, a bit alone; a known type, a filed type; remarkable enough to explain everything she had seen, mundane enough to explain nothing that mattered. The world had a drawer for that child. Drawers were safety. The whole art, he decided that night, was never to be drawerless.

“Yesterday,” said Harry, and then, laying the first brick of the fortification, with the solemnity the type specified: “The boiler’s going to be mended on Thursday. What’s a flue?”

Miss Henmore’s delight was total, warm, and—he noted it with something that was not entirely the ledger talking—rather nice to stand in, nicer than he had budgeted for. He let the policy die gracefully. In its place rose the odd clever child of St Gregory’s, who read to the hamster at break, who was allowed—the single negotiated treasure of the whole retreat—to take books home from the classroom shelf, two at a time, officially because Miss Henmore believed in meeting children where they were.

There was a price, and the price arrived by telephone, as all the worst things did. A gifted child, Mrs Dursley. You must be so proud. He listened from the cupboard to Petunia’s half of it, the strangled civilities, the receiver going down like a lid going on, and he sat in the dark among his named spiders and did the sums on the fear that would now be loose in the house. The sums came out survivable. School-clever was not the freakishness; even Petunia’s taxonomy kept those shelved apart. Lily Evans, he would learn in time, had been school-clever too, and the school-cleverness had not been the thing that took her through the drawer in the nailed-shut dresser and out of the ordinary world; but Petunia could not nail shut a parents’ evening, and Vernon found, upon reflection and two helpings of esteem, that a nephew being called gifted reflected, when you thought about it, rather well on the household that was bringing him up. Right-thinking people’s children did well at school. It was the sort of thing one might mention, lightly, at the Rotary.

Harry, listening through the cupboard door to his uncle rehearse the sentence, closed the file on the incident and opened, with great care, the one it had been blocking for a year.

If the school could praise him and the house absorb it—if gifted could be laundered into Vernon’s esteem and come out safe—then the library was no longer a quarter-mile away. It was one well-built sentence away, and the sentence would not even have to be his. Miss Henmore, who believed in meeting children where they were, would write it for him by October, in a voice no Dursley could refuse without looking, at a fence, at the Rotary, like the wrong sort of family.

He lay back on the camp bed in the smallest office in England, hands behind his head, and listened to the house tick, and let himself, for one whole glorious unbudgeted minute—three hours and twenty subjective minutes, the longest holiday of his life to date—simply gloat.

The sea. Shelves and shelves of it, gold on green. All the voices that could not be used up.

Four years old, or eight hundred, depending on how you counted, and the second set of rules was out there waiting for him, and now, at last, so was the first.

Contents

Chapter 3

The Lending Library

People speak of hunger for knowledge as a figure of speech. I should like them to spend one year at two hundred to one in a house with a phone book, and then we can discuss figures of speech. —the journals, vol. I

The sentence, when it finally came, was even better than the one he had designed, because he had not written it. Miss Henmore had.

It arrived in July, on the last parents’ afternoon of the nursery year, in front of two other mothers and the vicar’s wife, which Harry could not have arranged better with a year’s notice and suspected Miss Henmore of not arranging at all; kind people, he was learning, wandered into perfect positions the way rivers found the sea. She had taken Aunt Petunia’s hand in both of hers and said, warmly, audibly:

“You must be taking him to the library every week, Mrs Dursley—a reader like that. It’s the single best thing a family can do.”

And there it was. Not a request from Harry, which would have been refused on principle; not advice in private, which could be quietly binned; but a public assumption of virtue, witnessed by the vicar’s wife, with only two available replies: yes, of course we do, which became a commitment the moment it was spoken, or a correction, which was unthinkable—what sort of family would one have to be? Harry, standing at knee height with his face arranged into the solemn middle distance of the odd clever child, watched his aunt say, “Yes—well—of course,” and felt the latch of the last door between him and the sea lift with a small, silent, well-oiled click.

The following Saturday, in her second-best gloves, wearing the expression of a woman discharging a tax obligation, Petunia Dursley walked her nephew the long quarter-mile to the Little Whinging branch library and signed, at the counter, the form that changed the economics of his existence.

He behaved very badly in the first minute, by his own standards, and forgave himself later. He stopped dead on the threshold. He simply stood, holding up foot traffic, a small boy in Dudley’s cut-down shorts, and looked.

It was one medium-sized room of a suburban branch library: parquet, radiators, a smell of polish and old paper, perhaps nine thousand books. It was the largest concentration of recorded human thought he had ever been in a room with, by a factor of roughly two hundred, and the symmetry of the number did not escape him. Shelf after shelf after shelf of fixed speech, voices that could not be used up, arranged—he could see the system from the door, numbers on the spines, order breathing through the whole room like the ticking of a vast calm clock—for retrieval. Somebody had not merely collected the sea. Somebody had indexed it.

“Well,” said Petunia, already at the door, gloves twitching, “you’ve seen it. Choose your books. Quickly.”

Junior members were entitled to three tickets. Three books a week. He had done the arithmetic before they arrived and done it again, bleakly, standing in the children's corner with its wire carousel of picture books: at his reading speed—eye-speed, the eyes were always the bottleneck—three books was an afternoon, and the week had six more days in it. The sea, and a teaspoon to drink it with.

He solved the teaspoon inside a month. Strictly, two other people solved it for him, which was the shape, he was coming to understand, of all his best solutions: the trick was never to pull on the world directly, but to arrange matters so that the world, pursuing its own comfort, pushed.

Petunia solved the supply side. The Saturday walk, it emerged, was insupportable—twenty-five minutes of being seen in public with the nephew—and by August she had discovered that the boy could be trusted to go alone, a discovery Harry had administered in careful doses: the first trip with her waiting at the corner, the second with her waiting at home with the kitchen clock running, the third unsupervised, each return punctual to the minute, each stack of books displayed on request, blameless as bread. Of course he may go alone, it’s only the library, Vernon; it keeps him from underfoot and it’s the sort of thing one might mention, lightly, at the Rotary. By September the library run was policy, and the policy was a standing grant of the one resource the house had never before issued him: unsupervised time, in bulk, every week, with a destination so respectable that suspicion itself signed the chit.

Mrs Prentice solved the demand side.

Mrs Prentice ran the branch the way a good stationmaster runs a junction: nothing escaped her, and almost nothing moved her. She was fifty-some, grey-bunned, built like the radiators, and she had watched, across her counter, forty years of children pretending to read above their age to impress mothers who were pretending to care. She had Harry filed within three visits. He knew the moment it happened—he was at the returns desk, and she looked at his stack (the three books: one picture book for the record, The Observer’s Book of Birds for cover, and the first volume of a children’s encyclopedia for actual caloric content), and then she looked at him, one beat too long, and said nothing at all.

On the fifth visit she conducted the test. She did it properly, without warning, which earned her his permanent respect: as she stamped the encyclopedia’s return she said, idly, eyes on the stamp, “Anything worth knowing in there about cuckoos?”

The courtroom convened; the courtroom, by now, could convene, hear argument, and rise inside a second and a half. Underplay, urged the standing policy, the odd-clever-child fortification, two years in the building. But he had watched this woman reunite a lost toddler with its mother using nothing but a biscuit and two sentences, watched her repel a loud man demanding the removal of a book she disliked more than he did, on principle, with the Dewey decimal calm of a woman who had seen off worse; and the shelf had a growing section, started the day of Miss Henmore’s telephone call, titled allies are leverage you don’t have to hold, and the section said that the fortification had a gate for a reason.

“They don’t build nests,” said Harry. “They lay in other birds’ nests, and the chick pushes the real eggs out, and the parents feed it anyway, even when it’s four times their size. The book says it’s remarkable. I think the interesting part is the parents—why can’t they tell? It’s four times their size. I think the parents must have a rule they can’t stop running even when it’s wrong. Lots of things seem to work like that.”

Mrs Prentice stamped the last book. She looked at him for a long moment—a genuinely long one, nearly four seconds; he had time to inventory the whole shape of her attention and find, nowhere in it, the flood-stage fear, nothing of Petunia at the cooker, only something drier and infinitely more useful: a professional, recognising stock.

“Hm,” she said. And then: “The junior tickets are a nonsense for you. You’ll take adult tickets on your aunt’s member­ship—she won’t be using them—and you’ll have the run of that side of the room, and if anyone asks, the books are for your uncle.” A beat. “Your uncle has catholic tastes.”

“Thank you,” said Harry, with great care, because the sentence was carrying more weight than any sentence he had been issued in years.

“Mondays and Thursdays we’re quiet after four,” said Mrs Prentice, already turning to the next borrower, as if the single most consequential administrative act of his life to date were a stationmaster waving a train through a junction, which, to her, it was. “Mind the stepladder. It sticks.”

Six tickets, both sides of the room, a standing timetable, and a cover story, in eleven seconds, unasked. Allies, he said to himself that night, under the heading that was coming to organise everything: the first set of rules says three tickets. The second set of rules is called Mrs Prentice.

What does a mind that lives two hundred days inside each day do with a public library?

It eats it, is what it does—but even eating has an order, and the order he imposed on himself that autumn would still be legible, decades later, in the architecture of everything he built. The temptation was to gorge: stories, dinosaurs, volcanoes, the lurid and lovely children’s shelf of Amazing Facts. He permitted the gorge for exactly three weeks—even the king of the country of one, he noted, must sometimes throw a festival—and then, the first Monday of October, after four, in the quiet, he climbed the sticking stepladder in the adult section, took down the first volume of the shelf-worn Children’s Britannica the branch kept beside the proper one, and began, with the first page of the letter A, the project he privately titled the Survey.

Not to learn everything; he knew better by now. To map everything. To walk, once, at speed, the whole coastline of what humanity knew, noting the harbours, noting above all the places where the map said here be nothing—because a hole in a map was the most informative mark on it, and he had personal reasons, filed on a shelf in his head and boxed as expensive if true, to care about one particular species of hole.

The Survey ran eight months. He took it in strict rotation with the gorging shelf (the festival clause; also, three fat reference books a week would have drawn even Mrs Prentice’s eye, and the first law was still the first law), and what it did to him was not what the books themselves did. Any single volume was a water glass. The Survey was the first time he saw the shape of the sea: that knowledge clumped; that the clumps had unguarded borders where nobody seemed to hold title (the chemistry books and the biology books described the same world and barely spoke); that everything had turned out to be made of smaller things that obeyed stranger, simpler rules, every single time anyone had looked hard enough, atoms under matter, cells under creatures, grammar under speech; and that the books written for children stated as settled what the books across the room confessed, in their final chapters, to be open. He developed a fixed habit that never left him: read the last chapter first. The last chapter was where the authors kept their honesty.

And in the whole eight-month Survey, in nine thousand books, there was no entry—not one line, he checked the indexes of everything plausible, supernatural, see folklore; magic, see conjuring—for warmth that answered need in a blanket on a doorstep. Nothing for photographs that turned their heads. The map of everything had a hole in it, and the hole was shaped exactly like his life, and nobody else’s map seemed to show it at all.

He was five years old, and he had conducted a literature review and found the field unclaimed.

School proper began that September—St Gregory’s Primary, reception class, full days—and school proper brought Dudley, and Dudley brought the gang, and the gang brought the game that was played at every school everywhere by boys shaped like Dudley against boys shaped like Harry. It had no name yet. Dudley would eventually coin one—Harry Hunting, he had a gift for nomenclature if nothing else—but by the time he coined it the game was already dead, strangled in its cot over one patient autumn term, and nobody, least of all the hunters, ever understood that it had been strangled, which was the entire art of the thing.

The courtroom had considered and rejected grander options. Turning the gang on some other child was effective—he modelled it in an afternoon—and went into the small but growing locked drawer in him, the options one does not use, with a note that lengthened each year he lived: the machine is easiest to steer toward the worst things; this is a fact about machines, not a licence. Adult intervention was worse than useless; he had watched it tried on other victims—it fed the game. What killed a game, every game, was the same thing that had nearly killed him in a playpen: boredom. A game survives on reliable fun. Make the fun unreliable, and make the unreliability look like the weather, and the game dies of natural causes with no fingerprints on it anywhere.

So the hunt simply never prospered. The quarry had an unsporting knack of being elsewhere—through doors that had been about to close, in the one corridor with a teacher in it, absorbed blamelessly into the skipping-rope crowd whose churn no pursuit could penetrate. Cornered—he permitted a cornering roughly monthly; total success would itself have been a pattern—the quarry was bafflingly dull: no chase, no tears, no satisfying squeak, just a small boy looking politely past them at something which, when the gang turned to check it (they always turned; he had tested each boy’s turning latency separately and could play the five of them like a bell-rig), was never quite anything. Twice that term, pursuits ended in front of witnesses so inconvenient—the head outside her office; the vicar, improbably, both times—that Piers Polkiss developed a private theory that Potter was unlucky to chase, which Harry, who had assembled the vicar coincidence with a fortnight’s planning apiece, considered the finest review his work had ever received.

By December the gang hunted other things. He spent a further month, in the locked-drawer accounting he forced himself to do honestly, on the other children it might hunt instead, and found the game, deprived of its founding quarry, had lost altitude generally—Dudley’s boredom now discharging mainly into football, at which, Harry noted with the pleasure of a well-fed meteorologist, his cousin was actually, legitimately, not bad.

The school itself he ran on the St Gregory’s protocol, third revision: the odd clever child, reading age politely off the charts, maths carefully two years ahead instead of the truth, questions calibrated, friendships—

Friendships were the file with nothing in it. He was aware of the file. At two hundred to one, a playground conversation was a correspondence chess game in which only he could see the board between moves, and the other children, bless them, were children: he liked them, rather, the way one likes ducklings, and a duckling is not company. The file stayed open. The shelf, which required honesty in both directions, took the following, the night of his sixth birthday, between an anomaly and a run of Dewey numbers: Mrs Prentice asked what I'd been reading, and I told her about map holes for four minutes before the desk bell went, and she said, Hm, bring the cuckoo energy to the holes, and I have been in a good mood for eleven days about it. Query: what is the thing of which this is the small dose?

Interlude: the staff room, St Gregory’s Primary, a wet Tuesday lunchtime in November

“I’m only saying,” said Miss Weald, who had the reception class, “that I’ve been doing this nine years, and the reading cards don’t go up that far, and when I gave him the Year Six comprehension for a lark he finished it and asked—nicely, mind—whether the passage about the lighthouse keeper was meant to contradict itself about the tides, because if it was, it was quite good.”

“Gifted children exist,” said Mr Ollerton, from behind the sports pages. “There’s a register for it.”

“I put him on the register in September.”

“Well then.”

“The mother—the aunt,” Miss Weald corrected herself, and frowned at her tea, because the frown had been waiting all term for somewhere to go, “came in for ten minutes, said they didn’t want any fuss, no assessments, no county people, nothing in writing, thank you, and sat the whole time with her handbag on her knees like I was going to bite her. And the boy.” She stopped.

“The boy what?”

“He manages her,” said Miss Weald. “Four years old—five, now. She started twisting her gloves before she’d even sat down, and he put his hand on the chair next to her—didn’t say anything, just—staged it, like a little equerry, and she sat, and she was better. He does it to me. He does it to all of us. Watch him at home time tomorrow, by the gate, when the mums come: he stands where the traffic sorts itself. I counted. He adjusts four collisions a week out of existence, and nobody’s ever seen him do anything.”

Mr Ollerton lowered the sports pages a careful inch. “Nine years,” he said, “and your considered professional alarm about this child is that he’s polite in a load-bearing way.”

“My considered professional alarm,” said Miss Weald, “is that I’ve taught four hundred children, and every single one of them, by November, I knew. What they wanted, what they feared, which drawer they went in. And this one—I know exactly which drawer he goes in. It’s labelled odd clever little boy, and it’s the right drawer, everything fits, and some mornings I catch him deciding to fit.” She drank her tea. “You’ll say that’s not a thing.”

“It’s not a thing,” said Mr Ollerton, going back to the football. But he did, as it happened, stand by the gate the next afternoon; and he watched; and that evening he told his wife, over the washing-up, that the funny thing was you couldn’t see anything at all, and his wife said then there was nothing to see, and Mr Ollerton dried the plates and did not quite agree.

The haircut happened in February, and the haircut changed everything, and he slept through it.

Not the haircut itself; that he attended in full, with the resignation of a man watching cost-cutting arrive at his own department. Petunia’s war on his hair was a standing seasonal campaign—it grew wrong, it stuck up at the crown exactly the way, though he did not yet know it, a dead man’s had—and that Saturday she lost patience in a single escalation and took the kitchen scissors to the whole estate, cropping him to the skull but for a ragged fringe left, she said, “to hide that horrible scar.” The result, inspected in the dark window of the oven, was genuinely alarming; he filed Monday, school, and the word convict under problems with a forty-eight-hour fuse, shelved the day’s observations, and went to sleep in the smallest office in England.

And woke, at six, in the grey, with the weight on his scalp wrong, and lay perfectly still for one full real minute—a subjective working afternoon—while his hands reported, hair. All of it. To the half-inch, by later measurement against the door frame where he’d marked the cropping: the whole black unruly estate restored, overnight, including—he checked with both hands, twice—the stubborn crown that had never once obeyed a comb in its life.

What he felt first, and he kept it because the shelf required honesty and he never afterwards pretended otherwise, was not wonder. It was outrage. Three years. Three years the anomalies file had sat on its shelf, boxed as expensive if true, starved: one warmth he’d been fifteen months old for, one photograph with a kettle shrieking over it, a handful of maybes—a fall off the garden wall that had hurt less than physics suggested, a jam jar that he was almost sure had been out of reach. Three years of pending, unreliable, do not build on. And now the largest magical event of his life—measurable! repeatable-sounding! his own body the apparatus!—had happened six inches above the finest observation instrument in Little Whinging, while the instrument was switched off. He had slept through his own experiment. The universe had, at last, submitted data, and the recording equipment had been unconscious in its camp bed, dreaming—he had checked the dream log too, it was the first thing he checked—of absolutely nothing relevant.

Downstairs, Petunia dropped the milk bottle. The weather that followed was severe—how did you, what did you, you wicked— cut off, as always, at the drawer’s edge—and he stood in it with his face arranged into frightened bafflement, which required no arranging whatsoever for once, and took his week in the cupboard without protest, and was grateful for the dark and the quiet, because he had work to do and the office door was shut and the universe had finally, finally, blinked first.

The anomalies file came down off the shelf that night and did not go back up. By Sunday it had become the Programme, with its own shelf, its own order, and its first honest casefile, because the hair was different in kind from everything before it: bounded (overnight), targeted (the hair, the whole hair, nothing but the hair), corrective (the universe had not given him something new; it had put something back the way it was supposed to be), and correlated, like the warmth, like the soft landing off the wall, with a need. He had gone to sleep dreading Monday in his skin. Something had audited the dread, identified the cause, and quietly reversed it—while he slept, which was the pattern’s cruellest feature and, the Programme noted in its first formal hypothesis, perhaps its most informative one:

H1. It does not run while I am watching for it. Corollary: it uses the same machinery I watch with—and when that is busy elsewhere (asleep, or flooded), it answers need directly.

Which would mean the power was not out in the world, in blankets and photographs, visiting him.

Which would mean the power was in here. Standing behind the watcher. Waiting, with two hundred times a lifetime’s patience, for the watcher to leave the room.

Harry Potter sat in the dark of the smallest office in England, five years old, or a thousand, depending on how you counted, with the Programme laid out where he kept it and his outrage cooling into something with much longer legs, and began—under a staircase in Surrey, in his head, where everything lived and nothing was ever lost—to draft the experiments.

The watcher, he thought, can be sent out of the room on schedule. I do it every night.

We are going to need a better dream log.

Contents

Chapter 4

The Week

The week in the cupboard was the best thing Aunt Petunia ever did for him, and she never knew it, and he never told her.

Seven days with the door shut. Meals passed through. No school; she had rung Miss Weald and said a chill in the voice she used for the neighbours. Nothing to do but lie on the camp bed under the slant of the stairs and listen to the house tick. Seven days by the hall clock, and by his own the better part of four years, and he meant to spend all of them on his hair.

He kept no notes. He never had. A page took him an hour to fill at the speed of his hand, and he had never forgotten anything since a doorstep in November, so a notebook would have cost him hours to tell him what he already knew. Everything he had — every strange event since the warm blanket, every rule, every face — sat in his head in order, where he could look along it like a shelf. He looked along it now.

The hair was good work and he kept it. Cropped to the skull on Saturday, whole by Sunday morning, every hair back to the half-inch. Nobody had touched it. He had slept through the only interesting thing that had ever happened to his own body, and he was still annoyed, and the annoyance was useful; it made him careful. By the time the boiler clicked off that first evening he had a year of experiments laid out, a better way of recording his dreams, and a table of every odd event in his life with the hair at the bottom of it, and he lay in the dark feeling the way a department feels when its filing is straight.

Then, with nothing left to do, he asked himself a question he had never once asked in five years, and it was so simple he was ashamed of it before he had finished thinking it.

Why am I hiding?

Not from Petunia. He knew why he hid from her. He had her face at the cooker when he was two, and just like—, and the drawer she kept nailed shut. That was a real fear with a real address, and the rule he had made at two to live with it — be small — had been right. He had never doubted the rule.

He doubted its reach.

Because he had been small everywhere. At nursery, where he had built a persona with a ceiling two years above his age and two errors in ten, and when it failed had built a lower one. At the branch library, where he had taken three books a week for two years before Mrs Prentice gave him six without being asked, because he had never once walked up to the desk and said I have read all of these. At St Gregory’s, where Miss Weald had put him on a register and his aunt had sat with her handbag on her knees and said no fuss, and he had let her. None of those rooms had his mother in a drawer. Miss Henmore had wanted to give him more. Mrs Prentice had been holding a door since he was four. He had made a rule for one frightened woman in one kitchen and had been paying for it in every room in Surrey.

He did the sum. Three years since he could read; six hundred of his own. Three books a week when the whole building was his for the asking. A ceiling two years above his age when the county had people whose whole job was children it could see, and he had made himself invisible to them on purpose. He had rebuilt arithmetic from Dudley’s old textbook under these stairs at four and gone on from there into places he had no names for, and had never shown a page of it to anyone alive.

He had been running the cuckoo’s rule. The parents who go on feeding the thing in the nest because the rule says feed. He had said that himself, at five, at a returns desk, about birds, and been pleased with it.

“Oh,” said Harry Potter, out loud, in the dark, five and a half years old. “Oh, I’ve been stupid.”

It was the first time he had said that about himself and meant it. It went through him clean. It did not hurt at all. It felt like a window going up.

The costs took him about as long as the boiler took to run its evening cycle, which was an afternoon by his own clock, and he thought afterwards that it was the most expensive afternoon of his life — not for what it found but for how long he had waited to spend it.

He went through every room. Nursery: a year of calibrated errors and a kind young woman who would have given him the moon and had been given a boy who read to the hamster. The branch: two years of a teaspoon when there was a sea. School: Miss Weald, who could see him deciding to fit, and had been given a reading age politely off the charts and maths two years ahead instead of the truth. The county, hidden from, by policy, for a year.

He put the costs in a column and added them. He did not like the total and he kept it anyway, where nothing could soften it.

Then he did the other column, because an audit with only costs in it was a confession, not an audit. The rule had got him the cupboard. It had kept him out from under his uncle’s temper. It had kept Petunia’s fear at a temperature he could live in, and that was real, and he would keep that part of the rule for exactly as long as he lived in her house.

Outside the house it had bought him nothing, for three years, at the price of everything, and he had never noticed because he had never once reviewed it. A two-year-old had made it in an emergency, and two-year-olds do not schedule reviews.

He scheduled one. Every rule I have, I made in a hurry. Every year — my year — I will take them all down and ask whether the boy who made them is still here. Mostly he will not be.

The plan took longer, because a plan has other people in it, and other people have to be run forward through every thing you might say to see what they do. He had five years of six people to run, and he ran them, and by the hall clock it took perhaps twenty minutes.

A child of five who walked into a library and announced he had read it would be patted on the head. A child of five who could prove it would frighten people, and he knew from a shopping list what frightened people did. So the question was not whether to stop hiding. It was who could bear the truth, in what order, and how much of it.

Mrs Prentice could bear all of it. She had tested him with a cuckoo and given him the adult shelves without blinking. She had a face that filed things rather than fled them. He would go to her first and he would not go small.

Mrs Bell could bear most of it, if it came through Mrs Prentice. The county could bear a great deal, if someone with letters after their name wrote it down. The newspapers could bear anything and should be given nothing yet. Uncle Vernon could bear exactly as much as made him look good at the Rotary — a lever, not a limit, and he had been pulling it for a year without admitting what it was for.

And Petunia.

He had meant to keep the rule for her whole. Small at home, large everywhere else. But the rule had a hole in it the size of her kitchen: she was going to find out anyway. The school would write. The county would telephone. Vernon would boast. She would hear in her own hall that the boy was being tested by a lady from Kingston, and she would hear it without his having told her, and the not-telling would frighten her far more than the fact.

He had managed her like weather for five years and had never once tried the front door. The sentence he would say to her arrived whole, in the same instant he saw the hole — one sentence, true and short — and he turned it over once to be sure and put it away until Friday.

Then the hair again, because the hair was why he was in here, and the audit had just told him he had been treating it like a hobby.

He had. A table of odd events in his head, worked at between library books, as if it were a crossword. Something in him could regrow hair overnight and warm a blanket on a doorstep and set a jam jar down soft. People in cloaks knew what it was. One of them had bowed to him in the street. One of them lived two streets away and asked him every month whether anything out of the ordinary had happened, and wrote the answer down after. He had been filing all of that under pending for years, and going to school.

“Stupid,” he said again, more gently. He was getting used to it.

So it went first. Not before the library — he could not live without the library — but beside it, every day, with the same hours, not only when a cat was poorly. He did not know how to make it come. His one hypothesis, that it ran when he was not watching, had been made by a boy who had just slept through his own experiment and was annoyed, and he did not trust it. He set out the questions instead. What is it for? When does it come? Who else has it? How do they make it come? And under the last one, after a long time, the plainest answer, the one he had been avoiding for two years:

Ask Mrs Figg.

She knew. She had known since the photograph of a cat turned its head on her mantelpiece. She had a report to make every month and somebody to make it to. At three he had decided not to poke her, and had called that wisdom. It had been the rule in a coat.

The rest of the week he waited.
