That was what the week actually taught him, and it was not what he had gone in to learn. Everything — the hair, the question, the costs, the plan, the sentence for the kitchen, the question for the garden — was finished by the second morning. The door would not open until Monday. He lay under the stairs with five days ahead of him and a plan he could not start, and he was not bored.

He had never in his life waited for anything. He had waited; he had done ten centuries of it, in a playpen, at a bus stop, in this cupboard; but it had been the way a stone waits, with nothing on the far side. This had a Monday on the far side of it. There was a woman with a stamp, and a sentence for a kitchen, and a garden with cats in it. He spent the five days on the mathematics, which asked nothing of anyone; on the hair table, which needed rebuilding now that the hypothesis was suspect; and on the seven things he was going to say in seven rooms, composed to the word, because his mouth could manage perhaps a hundred and twenty words in a minute and would need every one of them ready.

On the Thursday, in the dark, he very nearly laughed.

On the seventh day he went through the whole plan once more and it held, the way a good proof holds, with no soft place anywhere he could lean on and feel it give.

Then he said the new rule. Out loud, in a whisper, in the dark. The old one had been passed in silence by a boy of two. He wanted this one to have been said once in that house, in his own voice, so that the house had heard it even if nobody in it had.

“Be small in one room. Everywhere else, be the size you are, and let them deal with it. That’s their job. Mine is to stop making it hard.”

He listened to it with the air round it, to see if it still sounded true. It did.

He slept the whole night through and did not dream. It was, he would say later, the best week of his life, and he had said that about several, and this time he meant it.

“Mrs Prentice,” he said, at four on the Monday, with the stepladder between them, “I need to tell you some things. All at once, I think. I’ve been not telling you them for two years and it was a mistake and I’d like to stop.”

She was shelving. She did not stop, but she slowed, and he had learned in two years that the slowing was the thing to watch.

“Go on, then.”

He had not rehearsed it. Rehearsing was for being small well, and he was done with that. He told her. That he had read every book in the building, both sides of the room, including the ones on the top shelf behind the stepladder, and could tell her what was in any of them. That the Survey she knew about had been a map, and he had walked the map for a year looking for its edges and had found some. That he had rebuilt arithmetic from his cousin’s old textbook under the stairs at four and gone on, and some of where he had gone was in the fat books on the adult side with German names, and some of it he had not found in any book and did not know whether that meant it was new or that the branch did not have the book. That he had been pretending at school for two years by a rule he had made at two, and had spent last week in a cupboard working out the rule was wrong, and was not going to pretend any more anywhere except at home, where there was a reason.

That he needed a bigger library. Not the county’s reserve stock. A university.

Nine minutes at the speed of his mouth. The longest he had ever spoken to anyone. Partway through she had stopped shelving and sat down on the second step of the ladder with her hands in her cardigan pockets, listening the way she listened to nothing else in that building.

When he finished she said nothing for a while.

“Well,” said Mrs Prentice. “What took you so long?”

He had expected a dozen things and not that. He found he was smiling.

“I was two,” he said. “When I made the rule.”

“Yes. I did gather it was something of that order.” She got up off the step and dusted her skirt, which did not need it. “I knew about the top shelf, dear. I knew in October. I moved the Hilbert to the bottom one so you’d stop risking your neck for it, and you noticed, and you never said, and I never said, and I have been waiting since October for you to walk up to this desk and say.” She went to the desk. “I shall ring Mrs Bell. I know her from the churchyard committee. She has a drawer with your name on it that she has never liked and I am going to tell her she may open it.” She wrote on a date-due slip. “And I shall ring somebody else.”

“Who?”

“My husband’s brother lectures at the university in Egham. Chemistry. He is a tiresome man and I have not spoken to him since the funeral, and I am going to telephone him tonight and tell him there is a child of five in the Little Whinging branch who has read Hilbert off the top shelf, and ask him what the university does about that, and he will say nothing, Edith, universities do not take children, and I shall say then find me the person who decides that, and he will, because he has always been afraid of me.” She squared the slip. “It may take a fortnight.”

Harry stood by the stepladder with his hands at his sides and found, for the second time in his life, that he had nothing ready.

“Thank you,” he said. “For the door. For October.”

“Hm,” said Mrs Prentice. “Go and do the crossword. There’s one in the bottom corner I can’t get and I shall be very annoyed if you can.”

The lady from the county came on the second Thursday in March with a big folder, and he did it straight, and she lasted forty minutes before she had to go and stand in the corridor.

Her name was Dr Prosser. She had glasses on a chain and a cardigan the colour of oatmeal and a stopwatch she worked with her thumb under the table where children were not supposed to notice it. He noticed it and did not pretend not to, and that was the first thing she wrote down.

He was gentle with her. He answered at the speed of his mouth, not his mind. He did not finish anything before she had finished asking it. When the strings of numbers went past the end of her sheet he said them back carefully, as if they were a shopping list. But he did not stop. Not at fourteen, where the folder stopped, nor at twenty, nor at thirty, where Dr Prosser stopped reading them out and looked at him across the little table for a long moment and said, “Right,” and squared the cards, and said “Right” again, and said, “Would you excuse me one moment,” and went and stood in the corridor, and he sat with the orange squash and listened to her breathe out there, slow and even, like a woman doing an exercise from a leaflet.

She came back and did the rest with great steadiness. She was very good at her job. She had simply not known there was a far side to it. At the end she closed the folder and took the chain glasses off and looked at him the way Mrs Prentice had looked at him over a cuckoo: a professional, given cause.

“When I was training,” she said, “a long time ago, there was a girl in one of the studies. Up in the valleys. Read the back of the medicine packets in the surgery for something to do, and came out ordinary on every test we gave her, test after test, until one of us noticed she was ordinary the way a swan is clumsy. On land, you follow me. Because it suited her to be, where she lived.” She clipped the rubber band back round her second pile. “I have wondered on and off for thirty years what became of her. I rather think she’s still in the valleys.”

Harry looked at the box of blocks.

“I was being a swan,” he said. “Until last month. On purpose. It was a rule I had, and it was wrong, and I’ve stopped.” He looked up. “Dr Prosser, I don’t know what you can write. I know the forms stop. But I’d like you to write the true thing and not the kind one, because the kind one is what I’ve been getting for two years and it’s cost me.”

Dr Prosser was quiet a moment. Then she opened the folder to the last page, where there was a box for Recommendations, and he watched her, upside down across the table, cross out something she had already written — very able; enrichment — and write underneath it, in a hand that did not hurry:

Ceiling not reached. In my professional judgement not reachable with any instrument available to this authority. Recommend immediate referral to university-level provision, with whatever precedent has to be invented to do it. I will put my name to that.

She signed it. She turned it round so he could see.

“That,” said Dr Prosser, “is the true thing. Heaven help us all.” And at the door, holding it open for him, in the voice people use for remarks about the weather: “It gets easier to find them, you know. The swans. Once they decide to be found.”

He said the sentence to Petunia on the Friday, at the sink, after Vernon had read the letter from the school aloud and taken it into the hall to ring Marge.

He had watched the side of her face while Vernon read — exceptional, the county’s most senior educational psychologist, the university at Egham has expressed an interest — and the white had come up as he had known it would, and her hand had gone towards her collarbone, and he had put down the tea towel and said quietly, under Vernon’s voice in the hall:

“It’s only school, Aunt Petunia. Being good at school isn’t the other thing. I promise you it isn’t. I’ll never let it be, in this house.”

She did not answer. Her hand came down. She stood with it flat on the letter and read it again, and what was in her face when she finished was still fear, but under the fear was something that had been there for five years and that he had never once given her a reason to show.

She was tired. She had been frightened for five years of a thing she could not say, in a house where nobody would have let her say it, and here was the boy at the sink telling her in one sentence that he knew what it was and would keep it out of her kitchen.

“Well,” said Petunia, to the letter. And then, to the hall, in the voice for the neighbours: “Vernon. Tell Marge the university.”

Vernon told Marge the university. Harry picked up the tea towel and finished the plate. Forty seconds, which for him was a working day, and he spent the rest of the washing-up quietly amazed at how little it had cost. Five years of being a convenient nothing to spare one woman’s fear, and when he finally told the fear the truth, it smoothed a letter flat and asked Vernon to say university down the telephone.

The front door. Even with her. The whole time.

He asked Mrs Figg on the last Saturday in March, in the garden, with the cats, because he had saved her for last on purpose, the way you save the thing you most want.

“Mrs Figg. What are you?”

She was deadheading the geraniums. She did not stop, but she slowed, and her shoulders settled the way Petunia’s had at the sink — a woman putting down something she had carried a long way.

“Well,” she said. “There it is.” She snipped a stem and dropped it in the trug. “I wondered when. I had money on next year. Tibbles said this spring.”

“I know there’s a second set of rules,” said Harry. “I’ve known since I was three. I know the man who bowed to me runs on it, and the photograph on your mantelpiece that moved. I know my parents were part of it and didn’t die in a car. I know you watch me for someone, and ask me every month whether anything out of the ordinary has happened, and write it down after. I know my hair grew back in a night, and something warmed my blanket on the doorstep when I was a baby, and whatever does that isn’t in any of nine thousand books at the branch, because I looked.” He stopped. “I don’t know the word. I’ve never had the word. I’ve spent two years not asking you for it because I thought asking would make you choose between me and whoever you write to, and I’ve just spent a week in a cupboard working out that not asking has cost me more than anything in my life. So I’m asking.”

Mrs Figg put the secateurs down on the wall, slowly. She sat on the garden bench and patted the slats beside her, and he sat, and Mr Tibbles came down off the wall and sat at their feet with his lecture face on, as if the examiner had finally turned up.

“I can’t tell you,” she said. “Not today. Not because I don’t want to — Harry, I’ve wanted to since the cat with the markings, and you were three. Because it isn’t mine to tell. There’s a man I write to, and he’s a good man, and he asked me five years ago to watch and report and not interfere, and telling a child what he is counts as interfering, and I’ve kept to it. I’ve told him he was wrong to my face once already and he listened and did it anyway.” She looked at her hands. “But I’ll write to him tonight. I’ll tell him you asked. I’ll tell him how you asked — the two sets of rules and the nine thousand books, all of it — and I’ll tell him that if he says no I shall think a great deal less of him, in those words.”

“How long?”

“A fortnight. He’s a busy man. Sometimes less.”

He was not disappointed. That surprised him, and he looked at the not-being-disappointed to see what it was made of, and it was made of something he had never had.

She had said yes to being asked. She had not given him the word, but she had sat down beside him and told him plainly that there was a word, and a man, and a letter going tonight, and that she was on his side and would say so in writing. For five years he had been the only person in the world who knew there was a question. Now there were two of them, and one had a stamp.

“Thank you,” he said.

“Oh, get on,” said Arabella Figg, and picked up the secateurs, and her voice had gone odd.

The reply came in eleven days. She told him at the gate on the Wednesday, in passing, with Tibbles under her arm: “Saturday, dear. Come at two. Bring an appetite; I’ve opened the good tin.”

He came at two. The good tin was on the table — chocolate biscuits, the Edinburgh one, the one he had seen on the top shelf for three years and never been offered from — and she poured out with her left hand as she always did, and sat, and looked at him across the little table.

“He said yes. He said—” she took a folded paper from her apron pocket and read from it in a different voice “—if he asks you what you are, tell him the truth. I suspect he already knows. I suspect that is the least of what he knows.” She folded it away. “He had the right of that, I think.”

“What’s the word?”

“The word is magic, dear. Just that. You’ll think it’s a silly word. Everybody does, the first time. It isn’t.”

He had expected to feel something and he did, but not what he had planned for. He had known the thing for years; a word was only a label on a drawer. What he felt, at a table in Wisteria Walk with a biscuit he had not touched, was smaller and older than that. It was company. Somebody had said it to him out loud, as a plain fact, the way you tell a child the name of a bird.

“Tell me,” he said. “Please. Everything you’re allowed.”

She told him. Not everything; he could feel the gates she kept shut and he left them shut; they were not hers. But a great deal. That there were people who could do what had been done to his hair — thousands of them, a whole hidden world with its own streets and shops and newspapers, living in the same country behind a kind of curtain, which was why nobody at Number Four had ever met one except by accident. That they were called witches and wizards and the words were not insults where they came from. That she was what they called a squib, born to a family of them — two brothers who could, and she who couldn’t — and so she lived on this side and kept cats and did what she could, and what she could, for five years, had been to watch over one small boy for a man who had asked her to.

That there was a school, in Scotland, where children like him went at eleven to learn to do properly what his hair had done by itself. That a letter would come, on his eleventh birthday, because it always came on the birthday. That his mother and father had both gone to that school and had been very good — “your mum especially, dear; everyone said” — and that what had happened to them had happened in that world and not this one, and was not hers to tell, and he would learn it, she promised, soon enough.

“Eleven,” said Harry.

“The thirty-first of July, 1991. I’ve had it in my diary five years.”

Five years and four months. A world with schools and streets and newspapers was going to send for him, a world that had sent a man in a violet hat to bow to him and a kind woman to watch him, and had taken his parents in some way he was not yet allowed to know. Five years and four months by the hall clock. By his own, a thousand years and more. And nobody on the far side of the curtain, by the sound of it, had the least idea that the boy on this side had just spent a week in a cupboard deciding to stop hiding, and had rebuilt arithmetic at four, and was about to be given the run of a university.

He had wasted six hundred years being small. He was not going to waste one more.

“Mrs Figg,” he said. “Thank you. For all of it. For writing to him.”

“Eat your biscuit,” said Arabella Figg, and turned to the window so he would not see her face, which he had already seen, and would keep.

That night the plan got its last line, and the line was a date: 31 July 1991.

Under it, what he had and what he meant to do with it. Five years and four months. A university, soon, if Mrs Prentice’s brother-in-law was as frightened of her as she said; everything in it, as fast as his eyes would go, every subject to the edge and past it where he could. The hair, and whatever had grown it, every day, until he knew what it was for and when it came and how the cloaked people made it come. Mrs Figg, asked everything she was allowed to say, and the rest worked out from the shape of what she wasn’t.

Be the size he was. Let them deal with it.

Then he did a thing he had never done, and it was not for remembering.

He took a square of paper from Dudley’s old exercise book and a pencil and wrote the date on it, alone, large, in the round careful hand that was the only hand he had. It took most of a minute, which was three hours of his own. He pinned it to the inside of the cupboard door with a drawing pin he had been saving, at the height of his eyes when he sat up in bed, which was also the height of Petunia’s eyes when she opened the door in the morning with the plate.

She would see it. She would know what it was; she had known that date since before he could read. He had said one sentence to her this week, across a kitchen table, and this was the second, and it did not need her to answer.

He lay down in the smallest office in England, five years and eight months old by the hall clock and well over a thousand by his own, and for the first time since a doorstep in November he was not waiting for nothing.

He had a date. He had a thousand years. He was going to go and get ready.

Contents

Chapter 5

The Shelves

Mrs Prentice’s brother-in-law was called Dr Gerald Prentice, and he was exactly as tiresome as advertised, and he was so frightened of her that he had the meeting arranged inside nine days.

It was held in a room at the university in Egham that had a long table and a view of trees, and there were seven people at it, and Harry was the only one who had not been told what it was for, because nobody had thought a child of five needed telling. Dr Gerald Prentice sat at one end with the expression of a man who had been made to do something and wanted it known. A woman from the county called Mrs Achebe sat with a folder and a pen she clicked when she was thinking. Dr Prosser sat beside her with her chain glasses and said nothing for the first twenty minutes and watched Harry the whole time. Mrs Bell had come, in her good coat. Uncle Vernon had worn the funeral suit and sat in it with his hands on his knees and the look of a man at his own investiture. Petunia sat beside him in her gloves and did not take them off.

And at the far end, with her feet on a stack of paper under the table where she thought nobody could see, sat a grey-haired woman in a jumper with a hole at the elbow, who had been introduced as Professor Carrow, who ran the mathematics, and who had the face of a good sharp knife kept for best and had not yet used it on anyone.

They talked about him for twenty minutes. He let them. He had set this table; the food on it was his; he could wait for the plates. Dr Gerald Prentice said that universities did not take children. Mrs Achebe said that the county had a referral from its most senior psychologist and clicked her pen. Dr Prosser said, when asked, that she stood by every word of it and would say so to anyone, and clicked nothing. Vernon said the family had always encouraged him. Petunia said nothing. Mrs Bell said that she had a drawer.

Professor Carrow said nothing at all until the twenty-first minute, and then she took her feet off the paper and said, to Harry, directly, across the whole length of the table, in a Yorkshire voice that gave nothing away:

“You. What have you got.”

It was not a question. He had been waiting for it since Mrs Prentice had said mathematics on a date-due slip.

“Forty pages,” he said. “In English. I wrote them in the last fortnight; before that it was only in my head. It’s the beginning of what I worked out under the stairs from my cousin’s old textbook, done properly so that somebody slow could follow it. I’m sorry it’s only forty. My hand is slow and I didn’t know how long I’d have.”

He took them out of his bag and walked the length of the table and put them in front of her, and walked back, and sat down, and Vernon made a small noise that was the beginning of now then, boy and stopped, because Professor Carrow had opened the first page and begun to read, and something about the way she read made even Vernon Dursley fall silent.

She read for eleven minutes.

Nobody at the table spoke. Mrs Achebe clicked her pen twice and then, catching Dr Prosser’s eye, put it down. Dr Gerald Prentice looked at his watch and then at Carrow and then at the trees. And Harry sat with his hands in his lap and watched a grown woman read his handwriting with a pencil, turning pages at the speed of someone who was checking, and it was, he thought, the longest he had ever gone without being bored in a room where nothing was being said.

At the ninth page she stopped, and went back, and forward again, and wrote something in the margin.

At the end of the eleventh minute she closed the pages and took her glasses off and looked down the table at him, and the knife-face had not softened at all, and he was glad of it.

“Page nine is wrong,” she said.

The room went quite still. Harry felt it go through him like the morning of the ice — not the fall; the gear, the world going vast and slow and bright.

“Where?”

“Fourth line from the bottom. You’ve got the idea — it’s the right idea, it’s a famous idea, a German had it a hundred years ago and it caused a great deal of trouble — but you’ve let the thing you build have two names in your notation and you haven’t noticed, and so you’ve proved slightly more than you meant to, which here means you’ve proved nothing. It is a famous mistake. One man made it, in print, in 1893, and it took thirty years to dig the subject out from under him.” She put the glasses back on. “The other thirty-nine pages are correct as far as I can see at speed, and page thirty-one is not in any textbook I know, and I should like to think about it. But page nine is wrong, and you walked up this table and gave it me yourself, so I am telling you to your face. How does that feel?”

He had been waiting for someone to do that to him on purpose since before he could say purpose, and he sat at the end of a long table in Egham with seven adults looking at him and found he had to take a moment before he could answer, because the honest answer was absurd.

“It feels wonderful,” he said.

Professor Carrow looked at him for a long time. Then one corner of the knife-face did something it had plainly not done in a while.

“Yes,” she said. “It does, doesn’t it. Nobody ever believes me about that.” She turned to the rest of the table, and the knife came out at last, and it was for them. “Gerald. Mrs Achebe. Whoever else is deciding things. I don’t care what this university does or does not take. A child of five has handed me forty pages that I would be pleased to get from a second-year, with one mistake in them that I would be pleased to find in a third-year, and he thanked me for finding it. I want him in my library. I want him on Thursdays in my office. I want to know what is on page thirty-one. Write down whatever you have to write down to make that happen and I will sign it, and if there is no form, Gerald, make one.”

Dr Gerald Prentice opened his mouth.

“Edith said you would say universities do not take children,” said Carrow. “Edith was right. They don’t. They are about to.”

The card was ready in a week, and Harry walked across the park in the rain with it in his pocket to a building with a copper roof gone green, and a woman at a desk read the note that came with it and looked at him and read it again and said, “Well, I never,” and let him in.

He stopped on the threshold. He always did. This was the one all the others had been practice for.

Six floors. A hundred and twenty thousand volumes. And the journals — rows of them, bound by the year, going back to before his grandparents were born, the whole slow conversation of a century shelved in order and waiting. He stood on the ground floor with the card in his hand and the rain on the copper roof and felt a mistake he had been making his whole life come loose and fall away.

He had thought the hunger was him. Two hundred to one, and no engine runs that fast on a water glass; he had taken the hunger for a fact about his own machinery, like the slow hands, like the glasses, a thing he would carry to the end.

It wasn’t. It had only ever been the shelves. He stood in a building with enough on its shelves to feed even him, and the hunger that had sounded like the hall clock for four years went quiet, all at once, as if someone had reached into the hall and stopped the pendulum with a finger, and in the silence where it had been he could hear the rain, and the lift, and his own breath, and nothing else at all.

Then he went and found the mathematics, because he had promised Carrow page thirty-one by Thursday, and he was five, and he had the afternoon.

Getting there was the problem.

Egham was eleven miles up the line. A child of five could not take a train alone, whatever the child could read; Petunia had said so at the long table in a voice that had surprised the whole room by existing, and had been right. Vernon had the car and worked. Mrs Prentice had the branch. The county had a form for everything except this. And so for the first fortnight it was Petunia herself, on the train, twenty-two minutes each way, sitting opposite him in her good coat not speaking, walking him up through the park to the building with the copper roof and standing at the bottom of the steps and saying, “Well. Go on, then,” and not coming in, and going back down to the station to wait, with a magazine, for four hours, every day — and on the ninth day he came out at five and found her on the bench by the lake with the magazine shut on her knee, looking at the water, and understood that she had not read a page of it in nine days, and that this could not go on.

He said so to Mrs Figg, in the garden, on the Saturday.

“She sits by the lake. Four hours. She doesn’t read. I think she’s—” he had the word and did not like it “—I think she’s waiting, Mrs Figg. The way you wait outside a place you can’t go into.”

Mrs Figg snipped a stem.

“Well,” she said. “That won’t do.” And then, after another stem: “I’ve a bus pass, dear. And a great many cats who can mind themselves till teatime. And I was asked, a long time ago, by a man I write to, to keep an eye on you, and I don’t see that it matters which town I do it in.” She dropped the stem in the trug. “Monday. Half past eight. I shall bring a book. I shan’t come in; I can’t see the half of what’s in there, I don’t suppose, and I shouldn’t want to. But I can sit in a café with a pot of tea as well as anyone in Surrey, and I’ve been meaning to catch up on my reading.”

So it was Mrs Figg. Monday to Friday, from the second week of May, on the eight thirty-one, in a mackintosh with a library romance in the pocket, sitting in the university café from nine until five with a pot of tea and a scone and a succession of novels with castles on the covers, while two floors up a boy of five read a hundred and twenty thousand books at the speed of his eyes. She never asked what he had read. He told her anyway, on the train home, some days, and she listened to all of it, and said “Well I never” at the end.

Petunia did not say thank you to Mrs Figg. It was not a thing the house permitted. But on the first Monday that Harry went with Mrs Figg instead, there was a tin of biscuits on Mrs Figg’s doorstep when she got home at six — the good kind, from the shop in town, not the Edinburgh ones — with no note, and Mrs Figg looked at it for a long moment and took it in and said, to Tibbles, “Well. There’s hope for us all.”

He read the university that summer the way he had read the branch, in order, at eye-speed, with a map.

Not all of it. Even he could not read a hundred and twenty thousand books in a summer; the eyes were the eyes, and a thick book was still an hour, and a summer was still a summer. But he read the shape of it in a week — every floor, every bay, every run of spines — and then he went at it the way he went at everything, which was by refusing to gorge. Mathematics first, because he owed Carrow and because it was the one subject where he could carry his own water: three thousand years of it, shelved, and he had rebuilt perhaps the first two from one textbook and found, walking the shelves, where he had been right, and where the Germans had got there first with better names, and where — this happened four times that summer, and each time he stood between the stacks and simply breathed — he had got somewhere nobody on the shelves had been, and went back to the cupboard that night and put it into English for Carrow, slowly, at the speed of his hand, because a result nobody else could read was not yet a result.

Then the physics, because it was next door and because he had been wondering since the pendulum. Then chemistry, which he had dismissed at five as cookery and discovered was a language. Then — because Carrow had said, on the second Thursday, you cannot be a mathematician and nothing else; it is a disease; I have it — history, and the beginning of the philosophy, and a shelf of linguistics that explained in two afternoons a thing about English he had been working at since he was two.

He kept the hours of the building, nine till five, and the hours were, by the only clock that counted, about two years a week. He did not fall behind. He did not know how to fall behind. He sat in a carrel on the fourth floor with a stack beside him and the rain or the sun on the copper roof and read, and understood, and kept — every page, every word, for good; the shelves inside him had never once failed to hold — and at five he went down to the café and found a woman in a mackintosh with a castle on her lap, and they caught the five fourteen, and he told her about the Persians, or the Krebs cycle, or a man called Gödel who had proved that you could not prove everything, and she said well I never, and they were home for tea.

He was five years old and ten months. By midsummer he had read, by his own count, the whole of the mathematics on the shelves that he could follow without a teacher, which was most of it, and the whole of the undergraduate physics, and had started on the journals, and had written two hundred and eleven pages in English for a reader who was slow, and had been wrong, by Carrow’s count, nine times, and had loved every one.

“You are not,” said Carrow, on a Thursday in July, with page thirty-one in front of her for the sixth time and the window open on the lake, “going to tell me how you do this.”

“No, Professor.”

“Not the mathematics. Any fool with a mind like yours could do the mathematics. The reading. I had the library check. You have taken out four hundred and six books since May. Six a day. You cannot read six books a day. Nobody can read six books a day.”

“I read quickly,” said Harry. “My eyes are the slow part.”

Carrow looked at him over the glasses for a long moment, and he watched her decide — the way he had watched Dr Prosser decide over the blocks, the way he would watch a great many adults decide over the years — to take the answer she had been given and stand a little to one side of the door she had found behind it.

“Your eyes are the slow part,” she repeated. “Hm.” She pulled a pad towards her. “Then let’s see what the fast part makes of this. I’ve been stuck on it since 1983 and I’m sick of the sight of it. It’s about colouring. Don’t let that fool you.”

The boxes came from the spare room in June, and the margins in them were a dead man’s, and he read the margins before the text, and kept doing it for years.

Mrs Prentice brought them to the branch on a Thursday — four cardboard boxes gone soft at the corners, and she let him do the string, which he always suspected her of knowing was the best part. Inside were the Open University course units: slim pale books, dozens of them, with course codes on the spines and the dates of the broadcasts pencilled inside each cover in a hand that was not hers. Mathematics. Science. The arts foundation, which turned out to have the Renaissance in it and a unit on Beethoven he read on the train. And in the margins, in the same pencil hand, Mr Prentice.

Surely not — on page forty of a unit about infinite sets. He had been right; the book had been careless. Lovely — beside a proof Harry had found himself at four and never had anyone to show. Ask R. — he never found out who R was. Enough for one night, at the end of a hard chapter, with the date, and the next date three days later, and Harry sat in the returns cupboard with the pale book open and understood that he was reading a man learning, slowly, at one-to-one, in the evenings, after work, for the pleasure of it, nine years dead, and that the man had been good at it, and had stopped at the hard bits and come back to them, and had never once written of course.

“He watched them on the box,” said Mrs Prentice, from the desk, not looking round. “Nights. BBC2, after one. He said the maths ones were the only thing on the television worth losing sleep for.” A pause, and the stamp came down on a book. “The listings are in the Radio Times, dear. If you were ever awake at that hour. I shouldn’t think a child would be.”

He was awake at that hour. He had been awake at that hour, most nights, since he was three, in the smallest office in England, with nothing to do but the country of one; and from the second week of June he was awake at that hour on the lounge carpet at Number Four, eighteen inches from the screen, with the sound at two and the Radio Times open on his knee, watching men in brown jackets write on boards with squeaking pens in studios that looked like the waiting rooms of provincial dentists.

He loved them past all reason. For four years every voice he had learned from had been on a page — fixed, finished, certain. Here were people teaching. Thinking aloud, going back, saying in fact let’s do that again more carefully, hesitating before a hard step in a way that told you where the hard steps were. The first time a man in a brown jacket got to a place Harry had got to alone under the stairs and went a different way — a shorter way, through a wall Harry had spent a subjective month going round — he sat on the carpet and laughed out loud in the dark, and clapped his hand over his mouth, and listened to the house for a full minute, and laughed again more quietly, because it had been such a good wall.

He was wrong twice that summer, against the men in brown jackets. Twice they taught a thing he had derived alone and taught it differently, and he sat there certain in the dark that they had slipped, and worked it through, and found they hadn’t, and that he had, years ago, in a place he had never gone back to check because nobody had ever been there to make him. He went back and fixed everything downstream of both, and kept the two mistakes where he could see them, with something close to affection, and thought, lying on the carpet after the second one: Three times in one summer. Carrow once, and now these two. For four years there was nobody to catch me, and I begin to think that was a kind of poverty.

Mrs Prentice never asked whether he watched them. But in July she brought in a new Radio Times, unasked, every Thursday, with the Open University slots ringed in pencil in the hand he knew from the margins, which she had plainly been practising, and left it on the returns desk where he would find it, and said nothing, and that was Mrs Prentice.

Mr Tibbles stopped doing the stairs in June, and Harry noticed before Mrs Figg said anything, because he had been watching that cat with professional attention for two and a half years.

Tibbles had a spot on the landing sill — first sun in the morning — and all through the first week of the month the spot stayed empty, and the old cat lay instead on the folded blanket by the kitchen radiator and watched the doorway as if the stairs had personally let him down.

“He’s nine, is Tibbles,” said Mrs Figg, on the Saturday, setting down the tea. “Ten in March. It’s his hips, the vet says. There’s a tablet, but he won’t have it in sardines, he won’t have it in butter, he knows it’s there, don’t you, you old fool.” She said it to the cat, fondly, and the cat ignored her with the whole of his back. “Old cats get stiff. There’s nothing for it really. You just keep them warm and don’t move the furniture.”

She went out to see to the kettle. The rain ticked on the window — it was a wet June, the kind the roses hated — and Harry sat down on the floor by the radiator, which he was allowed to do, and Mr Tibbles looked at him with his lecture-hall face, and Harry looked back, and what he felt, watching the old cat decide whether getting comfortable again was worth the trouble of moving, was not curiosity.

He had been trying, since the week in the cupboard, to make the warming come on purpose. Three months, every day, with the hypothesis — it runs when I am not looking — and a dozen experiments built round it. He had tried not looking. He had gone lucid in his own sleep, night after night, and waited there for whatever had grown his hair to show itself, and it had shown him nothing but dreams, which were liars. He had wanted, hard, with his whole mind, at cold tea and wilting cress and the latch of the cupboard door, and nothing had come, not once, not a degree of warmth. Three months, which was fifty years of trying, and the hypothesis was wrong in some way he could not see.

He was not trying now. That was the whole of it. He had three years of this cat — three years of being watched from the armchair like a colleague, of Tibbles sitting on his feet for a whole visit the winter he was five on a day that had been bad — and the cat was old in his hips and would not take the tablet, and there was nothing for it really, and Harry sat on the kitchen floor in the rain and was sorry, properly, the way you are sorry about a thing you cannot fix.

He put his hand on the cat’s side, in the deep fur over the ribs, which was permitted on quiet days.

And it came.

Quietly. No fear, no falling, none of the bell-struck hugeness of the doorstep. It rose up through him the way the warmth had risen through the blankets that first night, patient and friendly and completely unhurried, down his arm and out through his palm into the old cat’s side as if it had been waiting three months — three years — for somebody to ask it properly. He felt it go looking. That was the only way he could ever say it afterwards: it went looking, the way Mrs Prentice went looking along a shelf, hand out, sure of the general area. It found the hips. Something there was the wrong shape of warm, and it made it the right shape, gently, taking its time, and then it sat back, so to speak, and was done, and the rain went on ticking.

Mr Tibbles raised his head. He looked at his own back legs for a moment with an expression of deep suspicion. Then he stood up, stretched himself the whole length of the blanket, back feet trembling with the luxury of it, stepped off, crossed the kitchen, and went up the stairs like a kitten, straight to the landing sill, where there was no sun at all because it was raining, and sat in the grey light looking extremely pleased with himself about nothing.

Mrs Figg came in with the pot and stopped.

The kitchen was very quiet. Harry stayed where he was, on the floor by the radiator, hand still warm, heart going, and for once he did not arrange his face at all, because there was no longer anyone in that house he needed to arrange it for.

“He just got up,” said Harry.

Mrs Figg looked at the cat on the landing for a long time. Then she looked at Harry, over the top of her glasses, and there passed over her face a thing he would come to know well in the years after and had no file for yet: surprise, and arithmetic, and underneath the two of them, unmistakably, like the radiator under the blanket, gladness.

“So he did,” she said. “Well. There’s hope for us all.” She poured the tea. Her hand had a small shake in it that was gone by the second cup. “You’ll want to remember that, dear. I expect it’s important.”

“I don’t forget things, Mrs Figg,” said Harry. “But yes. I think it’s the most important thing I’ve got.”

On the train home, with Mrs Figg beside him reading a castle, he put it into words. Not on paper; there was nobody yet to hand paper to. In English, in his head, in the shape he would use when there was somebody, because it was the most important thing he had and he wanted it in a form that could be given away.

It does not come for wanting. I wanted for fifty years and it never came once. It does not come for watching or not watching; the hypothesis was wrong, or it was the wrong hypothesis. It came today for a thing that was genuinely wrong, that I genuinely minded about, with my hand already out and nothing in my head but sorry. It answers caring. Not the word. The thing.

The hair was my need. The doorstep was my need. The cat was his. It does not seem to mind whose. It only seems to mind that it’s real.

So the question of my life is not “how do I make it come.” That is a two-year-old’s question, like the rule. The question is: what, in the world, is genuinely wrong, that I can genuinely mind about, with my hand out — and how many of those things are there, and how fast can I find them.

I think there are a great many. I think I have a thousand years. I think I had better start.

Mrs Figg turned a page of her castle. Outside the window, Surrey went by at the speed it always went by, which was slowly, and he sat with his hands in his lap and the shelves full and the hunger quiet and a cat on a landing sill eleven miles behind him, and was five years and eleven months old, and well over a thousand, and happy.

“Well I never,” said Mrs Figg, to her book.

“Yes,” said Harry. “That’s about right.”

Contents

Chapter 6

Real Work

He did the cats first, one a Saturday, through the whole of July.

Not because they needed it most. Because they were there, and because the magic — he had the word now, and used it, carefully, the way you use a new tool — answered what was wrong, and old cats are nothing if not a catalogue of small wrongs. Snowy’s ear, torn in a fight the spring before and healed crooked. Mr Paws’s eye, which wept. Tufty’s limp, which Mrs Figg said was from a fall off the shed in 1983 and which Harry, with his hand in the fur over the haunch and his mind on nothing but sorry, felt go looking and find a place where a bone had set a quarter-inch wrong and been aching about it for three years.

He did them on the kitchen floor with Mrs Figg in the room every time. She never said anything. He never said anything. The biscuits improved each Saturday until by August he was eating things from Fortnum’s off a plate with a gold rim, and the two of them had still not discussed it once.

Then the frost came early, in September, and took the roses at Number Six, and he stood at the fence on the way to the station with Mrs Figg waiting at the corner and minded about one in particular until it was true, and it came back black and then green over a fortnight, from the bottom up, in a way roses don’t. Then Mrs Prentice’s teapot, with the crack down one side that had been there since the Silver Jubilee, which he held one Thursday to help with the drying and let himself truly mind that it leaked on her, and when he handed it back the crack was a line of nothing. She turned it to the light. She said “Hm,” and went on drying, and the next Thursday the teapot was in use and no word was spoken.

The door opened easier every time. That was what the summer taught him. Not wider — the things stayed small, an ear, a rose, a crack — but easier, like a hinge working loose, and once or twice towards September he caught himself able to find the caring on purpose: to look at a thing and choose to mind about it so truly that the choosing and the minding were the same, and the warmth came up to meet it.

Still nothing for wanting. He had tested that honestly, four times, and nothing had come.

And nothing for himself. He tried that too, once, properly, on his own cold feet one night in the cupboard with the purpose built as true as he knew how — and the magic declined, politely, the way Mrs Prentice declined to lend the reference section, as if to say that it would be delighted to help with the roses, and the cats, and the teapot, but that this was simply not what it was for.

He kept that under the heading he used for the facts he liked least.

The door faces outward.

The first thing he ever did on purpose that wasn’t mending was Mrs Figg’s tea, in the café at Egham, on the fourth of October, and it took him a month to build.

It had been bothering him since the roses. Everything he could do, he could do because something was wrong — a bone, a crack, a frost — and the wrongness gave him the handle. But the thing that had warmed his blanket on the doorstep had not been mending anything. He had not been broken. He had been cold, and somebody had minded, and the minding had come down to him through cloth. If it could do that — if it could answer a need that was not a wound — then wrongness was not the handle. It was only the easiest one.

So he changed the question. He stopped asking what was wrong and started asking what was for.

And it turned out — this was the month — that purpose was not something a thing had or didn’t have. It was something you could build. He built the warming of Mrs Figg’s tea the way he had built the plan in the cupboard: honestly, in pieces, over weeks, on the four-floor stairs of the library with his hands in his pockets. She sits in that café from nine till five for me. She has a pot at nine and it is cold by half past and she drinks it cold until eleven because she will not ask for another; she told me once that asking for a second pot was what people with no manners did. If it were warm, the sitting would be a little better. She has given me four hundred and six books’ worth of sitting since May. He did not know whether that would be enough. He only needed to mind about it so truly that the minding and the wanting were the same — and on the fourth of October, coming down to the café at eleven for the biscuit she always had waiting, he found that he did.

He put his hand on the side of the pot, as if steadying himself against the table.

It came. Slowly, like the roses, but it came: the warmth rising up through him and down his arm and into the china, and he felt it find the tea, and find the cold in the tea, and go about undoing the cold without any hurry at all, and then he took his hand away and sat down and took his biscuit.

Mrs Figg poured. She looked at the steam. She looked at Harry.

“That pot,” she said, “was stone cold at a quarter to.”

“Yes.”

“You did that.”

“Yes. I’ve been trying to for a month.” He ate the biscuit. “It doesn’t only do broken things, Mrs Figg. It does needed things. I just have to find out what the need is and mind about it properly. The broken ones are only easier because they tell you.”

Mrs Figg sat with the pot in her hand for a long moment, in the university café, with the students going past with their trays, and then she poured herself a cup, and drank, and put it down.

“I have known a great many witches and wizards, dear. My brothers. Their friends. The man I write to. I have watched them do magic since I was in a pram.” She looked at the steam. “I have never once seen any of them do it like that.”

“Like what?”

“Like you’re asking it a favour.” She picked the cup up again. “They tell it. With a stick and a word. You — sit on the floor and feel sorry for a cat.” She drank. “I shall have to write that down. I don’t know how I shall put it.”

“Put it like that,” said Harry. “I think that’s exactly right. I’ve been trying to find the words for a year.”

The watching came after, and it could only have come after, because he had never before been calm while it happened.

The cats, the roses, the teapot — he had been in those, inside the minding, with nothing left over to look with. But the tea had been built with his head as well as his hand, and there had been a moment, with the warmth going down his arm into china, when he had found himself standing far enough back inside himself to see.

So he practised seeing. That was the whole of October.

It needed a quiet effect and a long one, and he used the roses at Number Six, which went black with every frost and could be minded about honestly, and he stood at the fence on the way to the station with his hand on the cold wood and Mrs Figg at the corner not looking, and when the warmth began to rise he did not go with it. He stayed behind. He let it pass through him the way you’d let a train pass a platform, and he watched it go by.

One rose a morning. That was the pace, and there was nothing he could do about the pace, because he could only watch the warming when the warming happened and it only happened when something was wrong and he minded, and a frost came when it came. For once the slow part was not his hand. It was the weather.

What he saw he could not have told anyone. He tried, in English, in his head, and the words went round it like water round a stone. But he saw it, every frost for a month, and it did not change, and it had a shape.

Not a feeling. He had expected a feeling — warmth, kindness, the bath filling, the words he had used for it all his life — and those were there, but they were what it felt like, not what it was, the way the heat of a flame is not the flame. Underneath the warmth there was structure. The thing that passed through him went past in an order, and the order was the same every time, and parts of it were about the rose — not a picture; a grammar, the way a sentence about a rose has a rose-shaped hole in it where the word goes — and parts were about green and about back and about now, and the parts were arranged, and the arrangement was what did the work. It was a sentence. A sentence in a language he had never heard spoken, going past at a speed nobody else on earth could possibly have stood still enough to read.

He read it for a month, a frost at a time. Then he started to take it apart.

Not in English. English had no words for the parts, so he gave them names of his own, the way he had given names to the Dursleys at two. The pointing part, which said this one. The pushing part, which said so. And the bracket in the middle, which was the hardest, because the rose-shaped hole was not a rose; it was what a rose was for, and it took him four frosts to see the bracket whole and be sure of it. He laid the tea beside the rose and found they shared a spine and differed in the middle, as he had hoped. He laid Tibbles’ hips beside both — he could; he had the whole of that afternoon on the shelf — and found that one was longer, and had a part the others didn’t, a part that went looking before it pushed, and he lay on the camp bed in the dark and understood that he was holding the difference between warming a thing and mending it, taken apart, in a grammar nobody had ever taken apart before, because nobody else had ever been slow enough inside themselves to see it go by.

The boiler whooshed. He said, very quietly, to nobody, what he had been circling since the cat:

“It has parts.”

If it had parts it could be taken apart. If it could be taken apart it could be put together — differently. On purpose. He did not yet know how to make the sentences; he could only read the ones that came. But he had learned to read before, under this same staircase, off cereal packets, with nobody to teach him, and it had taken five months.

He gave himself till Christmas.

The pencil took till the second week of December.

The difficulty was that a pencil did not need anything. A rose needed to be green and a pot needed to be warm and a cat’s hips needed to be the right shape, and he could find the purpose in those and build on it honestly. A pencil lying on a camp bed under a staircase needed to lie there. That was the whole of its wants. And the magic would not come for a lie — he had proved that forty times now; I want the pencil to move got nothing, every time, not a flicker — so if he was ever going to do anything but mend and warm, he was going to have to find out what real purpose a pencil could possibly have for being an inch to the left.

He found it in the end by being honest about what the pencil was for. The pencil was for this. For the language. For finding out whether a sentence he had read off the roses could be written — made from scratch, with the pointing part and the pushing part and the bracket, and sent down his arm on purpose, and whether the world would read it back. That was not a lie. That was the truest purpose in the house; he had built his whole life on it since the doorstep. If the pencil moved, he would know. If he knew, he could learn. If he could learn, then every rose and every teapot and every pair of old hips in the world got a little nearer to being mended by someone who understood what he was doing, instead of by a boy minding on a kitchen floor.

He built that, over three weeks, until it was true in him all the way down. Then he sat cross-legged on the camp bed with the sentence he had composed held ready — his first, three parts and a bracket, this pencil, an inch, so — and put his hand flat beside the pencil, and held the sentence the way you hold a note in singing, and asked.

Nothing happened for two seconds, which was a long bad afternoon.

Then the pencil moved an inch to the left.

Not fast. Not far. It slid, the way a pencil slides when a table is nudged, except that nothing had been nudged, and it stopped exactly where the bracket had said, and lay there, and he sat and looked at it, and the house ticked, and he found that he was shaking.

The warmth had been different. That was the first thing he noticed, when he could notice anything. Thinner. Pushed, not risen; he had made the sentence and sent it, and the sending had cost something the mending never had. He was tired — actually tired, the way he had been once after a bad week, as if he had carried the pencil up a hill. The magic had not come to meet him this time. He had gone to it, with a sentence he had composed himself, and it had read the sentence, and done what it said, and let him feel exactly how much it had cost.

An inch. One pencil. He did it four more times to be sure, and the fifth time fell asleep sitting up with his hand flat on the blanket beside a pencil that was now five inches from where it had started, and dreamed, lucidly, standing in the green with his mother’s voice saying his name, and said — for the first time in five years of visiting that dream — “I moved a pencil,” and the voice did not answer, because it never did.

He had the next thing before he was properly awake, and it was so obvious that he lay on the camp bed in the dark feeling stupid for a quarter of an hour, which by his own clock was most of a season.

The slowest thing in his life was his hand.

It had always been his hand. He read at the speed of his eyes, which was five times a grown man’s and not one page faster, and he thought at a speed that had no name, and between those two and the world there was a hand that made letters the way a five-year-old’s hand makes letters, one careful loop at a time, and in May he had told a professor across a long table that he was sorry it was only forty pages because his hand was slow and he had not known how long he would have. Forty pages. He had the rest of it — hundreds of pages, the whole of what he had rebuilt from Dudley’s textbook under the stairs — in his head, in order, every line, and it was no use to anyone there, because the only way out was a pencil in a fist, and the fist belonged to a child.

A pencil had just moved without the fist.

He did not sleep again that night. He sat up with the pencil and a sheet from the back of Dudley’s old exercise book and built the purpose, which was the same purpose as before, only larger — this is for the language; this is for Carrow; this is so that what is in here can get out — and composed a sentence that was not an inch but where I look, and sent it, and the pencil stood up on its point like a dancer and fell over, and he sent it again, and it drew a line, and the line cost like a hill, and he was done for the night.

That was the problem for a week. Every stroke was a sentence, and every sentence was sent, and every sending cost. He could make the pencil draw one letter and then he had to sleep. At that rate the fist was faster.

He found the answer on the fourth night, and he found it because of the tea.

He had never let go of the warming in Mrs Figg’s pot. He had put his hand on the china and the warmth had risen and he had stayed with it while it worked — he had thought of that as watching, and it was, but it was something else as well. He had been holding the sentence open while the world read it. Not sending it, once, like a stone thrown; holding it, like a note. And the holding had not cost. The sending cost. The holding was only attention, and attention was the one thing in the world he had more of than anyone alive.

So he did not send the pencil sentence. He composed it — this pencil, where I look, so — and he held it, open, the way he had held the warmth in the pot, and he looked at the paper, and he looked at the place where the first letter should go, and the pencil went there, and he looked at where the second letter should go, and it went there, and the third, and it was writing, in his own round careful hand, because the hand it had was the one he knew, and it was writing e and v and e and r and y and he looked and it followed and he looked and it followed and the cost did not come. He was holding a note, and the note was this is for the language, this is for Carrow, and holding it was no harder than meaning it, and he meant it.

It wrote a line. He looked at the line. It was his handwriting and it had taken four seconds.

He did a page. It took two minutes, and most of the two minutes was the pencil, which was a pencil, and could only go so fast over paper before the point skidded or the paper tore. He did a second page to be sure. Then he put the pencil down and sat with his hands in his lap and looked at two pages of number theory that had been in his head since he was four and had never been anywhere else, and he understood that the thing which had been slow about him since the doorstep was over.

He gave himself to Christmas to learn the pencil properly, and it took till Christmas, because the pencil had a hundred small lessons in it. The point blunted and he learned to see it blunt and lift and turn. The paper was not flat on a camp bed and he learned to hold the sheet as well as the pencil, a second sentence beside the first, and found that he could hold two, and then that he could hold three — the sheet, the pencil, and a small warmth in his own cold fingers, which the magic allowed, it turned out, when the fingers were the thing that held the paper for someone else. He learned to look ahead of the pencil instead of at it, so that the point was always arriving at a place he had already left, and once he had that the pencil was as fast as a pencil could be, and no faster, and that was a page a minute, and he found that he did not mind the limit at all, because for once the limit was not him.

By New Year he had written three hundred and twelve pages.

He wrote them for Carrow, in English, for a reader who was slow, the way she had taught him on Thursdays a result should be written — from what you knew, towards what you wanted, with every step shown — and it was the whole of the number theory, from the first page he had worked out under the stairs with Dudley’s textbook propped against the wall to the place where he had stopped, that autumn, because he had run out of anyone to tell. He kept the pages in the biscuit tin from Edinburgh, which had been empty since the last of the good biscuits, and the tin filled, and he started a second tin.

He thought, while the pencil went, about what else was in his head that had never been anywhere else, and the list was long, and he found he was not frightened by the length of it. He had been living with a locked door between what he knew and what he could give, and the door was open, and on the far side of it was everyone.

Carrow got the tin on the first Thursday in January, in her office, with her feet on the desk.

He put it on the desk beside her feet. She looked at the tin, and at him, and took the lid off, and took out the first inch of pages, and read the first page, and the second, and put the pages down and took her feet off the desk.

“How many.”

“Three hundred and twelve. It’s the rest of what I gave you in May. All of it, from the start, so you can see where it came from. The last forty pages are new since the summer.”

She turned a page. She turned another. She looked at the handwriting, which was the handwriting from May, and at the thickness of the stack, and at him.

“In a month.”

“Since the second week of December.”

“Your hand was slow,” said Carrow. “You stood at the end of my table and apologised for it. I have had forty pages a fortnight out of you since, and I thought that was the child working all night, and I was going to say something about it.” She put her hand flat on the stack. “I write about four pages a day, Potter, on a day when I have nothing else to do, and I have been at it thirty years.”

“I write faster now,” said Harry. “I found a way.”

She looked at him for a long moment, and he watched her decide not to ask, and the deciding had the shape of a woman who had been handed a thing too large for her office and had chosen to be glad of it rather than frightened.

“Right,” she said. “I can’t read this in a term. Not properly. Not checking. I shall need people.” She put the lid back on the tin. “I am going to give some of this to Pole, who is sound, and some to a woman at Cambridge who owes me a favour and will hate every minute of it, and I am going to keep the number theory for myself because it is mine, and by Easter the three of us between us may have got through half.” She pushed the tin an inch towards him and then pulled it back. “You understand what you have done. You have made yourself faster than the people who are meant to be checking you. That is going to be the whole of your life from now on, and I should like you to be ready for it, because most of them will not be me.”

“I know,” he said. “I’ve known since I was four. I used to think it was the price. It isn’t. It’s only the shape.” He looked at the tin. “Professor. There’s more. Not mathematics. Other things, that I’ve worked out and never written, because the hand was the hand. I should like to write them. I don’t know yet who they’re for.”

“Write them,” said Carrow. “Find out who they’re for afterwards. That is the usual order.” She put her feet back on the desk. “Page nine is still wrong, by the way. You have it right in here on page two hundred and six and you have not gone back and crossed out the May version. Do it in front of me. I want to watch.”

The candle took a further month and was the hardest thing he had done, because a candle needed to be lit even less than a pencil needed to be moved, and because fire was a different bracket altogether — a thing that happened rather than a thing that was. He got there. On the eleventh of January, in the cupboard, with the door shut and a saucer under it and a stub from Dudley’s Christmas cake, a small blue flame stood up out of nothing on the wick, and burned, and he blew it out at once because of the smoke and the smell and the household, and sat in the dark that smelled faintly of Christmas and grinned.

It was not the candle he wanted. He had wanted it for a different reason, and the reason was hands.

He had found, with the pencil, that he could hold three sentences at once, and that the cost of holding was attention, and he had thought about that for the whole of one night with the pencil going beside him on a page about primes, and had seen where it went. It went to lenses. It went to the small screws in Mrs Figg’s clock that she could not see to turn. It went to threading a needle, which Petunia did with her tongue between her teeth, and to the pages of a book, which could be turned without a hand if the sentence was this page, over, so, and he tried that on the twelfth of January with the enchantment of a candle still in his fingers and read a chapter of the library’s book on optics without touching it, lying on his back on the camp bed with the book open in the air above his face and the pages going over one by one as his eyes reached the bottom of each, and understood that his reading, too, had been waiting on a hand.

It went further than that, and he made himself stop at the edge of it, because the edge was where you fell. He was six. He could move a pencil and a page and warm a pair of fingers and light a candle and mend a cat, and every one of those was a small thing, and the magic had declined, politely, every time he had tried to make it a large one. A small thing done at the speed of his attention was not a small thing. He had a shelf in his head now that had not been there in November and he called it, in English, things a steady hand could make, and he put on it, that month, a telescope, and a microscope, and a clock, and the instruments in Lindqvist’s physics lab on the second floor that he had looked at through a window and not been allowed to touch, and he did not know yet how any of it was done, and he did not need to know yet. He needed to know that it was reachable. It was. Everything was. He had thought, for five years, that what he was for was finding out. It was that. It was also, now, making, and he had not been ready for how much larger the second word was than the first.

He put it into English, in his head, in the words he would use for whoever he one day told:

I can read it. I can make four sentences of it; three cost like a hill and one costs nothing, because it is held and not sent, and the held one writes. Nobody else can read it at all, I think; Mrs F says they tell it with a stick and a word and never look. I am the only one who looks.

And I have been slow my whole life in exactly one place, and the place was my hand, and that is finished.

He did Mrs Figg’s hands on the last Saturday in January, and it was the first time he did a person, and the first time the magic came like a tide.

He had seen her hands before. He had seen everything before; that was the whole trouble with him. But he had never looked, not the way he looked now, with the hinge loose and four sentences of his own and the new rule running — every day; find what is wrong; look. Her knuckles were swollen. The joints of two fingers on the right hand went off at an angle, and she poured with the left, and he went back along four years of Saturdays in a quarter of a second and found she had always poured with the left, and he had never once asked himself why.

“Your hands look sore, Mrs Figg,” he said.

The teapot paused. “They’re old, dear. Old hands get sore. It’s the damp.”

“May I hold them? Just for a minute.”

He had never asked her for anything. He had never, he realised, asked anyone in that town for anything, except Mrs Prentice for books and Mrs Figg herself for a word, and the asking stood in the warm cabbage-smelling room like a fourth cat.

Mrs Figg looked at him over her glasses for a long moment. Then she put the pot down, and sat, and held out both her hands across the little table, palms down, the way you’d show a child a trick, and he took them.

They were cold, and dry, and the knuckles under his thumbs were hot in the wrong way, the way the old cat’s hips had been. He did not try anything. He had learned better than trying. He only sat there in the January light holding an old woman’s hands and let himself mind — not about a thing, this time, a rose or a crack, but about her, the whole of her, four years of ten pence an hour and biscuits off her own plate and nothing out of the ordinary, dear every month in the same place, and a mackintosh and a castle in a café from nine till five since May, for someone who had never once thanked her properly because he had been too busy being clever to notice there was anything to thank her for.

It came up through him like a tide.

Not the slow trickle of the roses. A tide — warm and sure and enormous, as if whatever it was had been waiting for him to mind about a person and had rather a lot to say on the subject. It went down his arms and into her hands and he felt it go looking, the way it did, hand out along a shelf, and find the knuckles, and find the two bent fingers, and consider them with great patience, and undo something, slowly, like a knot being worked loose by someone who had all day.

Mrs Figg made a small sound, the kind a person makes when something stops hurting.

He let go. Her hands lay on the table between them. The knuckles were knuckles. The two fingers on the right hand lay straight beside the others, and she looked at them, and turned the hand over, and made a fist, and opened it, and made a fist again.

Mr Tibbles, on the sugar bowl, had stopped looking like a man at a lecture and started looking like a man who had seen the examiner handed the answers.

“Well,” said Mrs Figg, very quietly, to her own right hand.

“I’m sorry I didn’t notice before,” said Harry. “I should have. I notice everything. I just wasn’t looking.”

She looked up at him then, and it was the face from the day of the cat — surprise, and arithmetic, and gladness — but with something added, something careful, like a woman closing a gate behind her to keep a thing in.

“Harry,” she said. It was the first time in four years she had used his name as the start of a sentence rather than the end of one. “You be careful who you show that to. You hear me? There are people who’d — well. You be careful. Not everybody who’d want to see it would be kind about it.”

“I know,” he said. “That’s why I showed you.”

Mrs Figg looked at him a moment longer. Then she picked up the teapot, with her right hand, and poured, and her hand did not shake at all.

“Chocolate biscuit,” she said. “The good tin.”

That night he took stock, under the date on the door, in the words he would have used if there had been anyone to use them to.

Can warm. Can mend, if it is truly wrong and I truly mind, and today a person. Can move a pencil and light a candle by building the purpose honestly and composing the sentence; sent, each one costs; I sleep more after. Held, a sentence costs nothing but attention, and I have more attention than anyone alive. Cannot do anything for myself, except warm the fingers that hold a page for someone else, which is the same rule from the other side. The door faces out.

Have the beginnings of the grammar. Nine parts and a bracket. Can read what comes. Can make four sentences.

The pencil writes. Three hundred and twelve pages in a month, a page a minute, and the minute is the paper’s. Carrow has them and cannot read them alone and is finding people who can. The hand was the whole of what was slow about me, and the hand is done.

What is in here can get out now. All of it. That is the fact of the year and I have not got to the bottom of it yet. Mathematics first, because it is the thing I have most of and the thing they can check. Then the rest, in the order it is useful to somebody.

Four and a half years. Nine hundred of mine, give or take.

And under it, the word he had added to every plan he had ever made and would go on adding to the end of his life:

Faster.

Then he turned the light off, and lay in the smallest office in England with a candle stub in a saucer and a date pinned to the inside of the door at the height of his eyes, six years and six months old by the hall clock and the better part of a thousand by his own, and did not sleep, and did not mind, because there was a man in a brown jacket on BBC2 at ten past one who was going to explain the structure of the cell to nobody, and Harry Potter had nine hundred hours to get ready and intended to use every one.

Contents

Chapter 7

Interlude: Arabella and Albus

Wisteria Walk, Little Whinging, a Thursday evening in February 1987

Arabella Figg had written sixty-three letters from the kitchen table at Number Two, Wisteria Walk, over the past five years, and had never before got stuck on the first line.

The pad was the same pad. The pen was her mother’s, a fountain pen with a lever, and she filled it, this evening, holding the barrel steady in her right hand while the left worked the lever, and caught herself doing it, and stopped to look at the hand in the lamplight. Straight fingers. She made the fist and opened it. Forty years of pouring with the left and the thing was simply gone, as if she had dreamed the mornings when she couldn’t do the buttons on her own coat.

Mr Tibbles sat on the table by the sugar bowl, which he was not allowed to do, and watched her not write.

“Don’t you look at me like that,” said Arabella. “You were there. You tell me how to put it.”

She had been posted here to watch for them. For a cloak in the wrong season, a stranger asking after the house, the wards shivering in the night the way Albus had said she might feel them even without a wand, in her teeth, like weather. Five years she had watched for that and seen none of it — a few tourists, the Diggle man bowing in the street like a fool — and every month she had written it down, and at the end, in the space Albus had never asked her to fill, she had put the boy. Quiet. Clever. A reader. Too thin but they feed him. He smiles at the cats.

And then last March he had sat on her garden bench and asked her what she was, and she had written to Albus that night with her heart going, and Albus had said tell him, and she had told him, and since then every letter had been a different kind of letter, and this one was the most different yet.

She uncapped the pen.

Dear Albus, I write with the right hand. You will not know what that means, so I shall tell you first, and then the rest in order. Since the autumn of 1946 I have not been able to hold a teacup in my right hand. You have seen my hands; you have been kind enough never to mention them. Last Saturday the boy looked at them across my kitchen table and said they looked sore, and asked — asked, Albus, very politely, he has never asked me for a thing in five years — if he might hold them for a minute. And he held them, and said nothing, and I felt it. I cannot do magic, but I grew up in a house full of it and I know the feel of it in a room the way you know when the kettle is about to go. I felt it come up through him and into my hands like a bath filling, and it went to the knuckles as if it knew the way, and when he let go my fingers were straight. They are straight now. I am writing this with them. He is six years old. He has had no wand, no teaching, no one to show him. He has worked it out for himself on my kitchen floor, and I have watched him do it since June, one cat at a time, and I have said nothing, and nor has he, and we have both known exactly what the other was not saying.

She stopped there, and looked at what she had written, and found that her eyes had gone hot, which was absurd at her age over a sentence.

And here is the part I do not know how to put. He does not do it the way they do. I have watched witches and wizards do magic since I was in a pram — my brothers, their friends, you — and they TELL it. A stick and a word and the thing is done. He does not tell it anything. He sits on the floor and is sorry for a cat until the cat gets up. He told me, in a café, that it answers "caring", and that the broken things are only easier because they tell you what is wrong, and then he warmed my teapot so that I should see. I have watched magic done for sixty years and I have not seen it done like that, and I do not think you have. He is also, since last spring, at a university. I do not think I have told you that properly. I have been taking him on the train every weekday since May. The professor there found a mistake on page nine of forty pages he had written and he thanked her for it, and she has told the county, in writing, that the university will take him or she will know the reason why. He is to sit Muggle examinations this summer that children sit at sixteen and eighteen, all of them at once, because the woman from the county cannot think what else to do with him and nor can anyone. He reads six books a day. I have counted. I sit in the café and they bring me the slips. And he writes, Albus. I do not know how much; the professor told me on the platform that she has had three hundred pages from him since Christmas and has had to find two other people to read them, and that his hand was slow in May and is not slow now, and she looked at me when she said it as if I might know why. I do not. I did not ask him. I find I would rather he told me. He knows I write to you. He has known for years. He asked me what I was so that I would not have to choose between him and you, and he waited two years to ask it, and when I told him he said thank you, for writing. You asked me once to tell you if he was happy. He is. He is happier than I have ever seen him, and he is six, and he can light a candle with nothing in a cupboard, and he has pinned the date of his letter to the inside of the cupboard door, and I think he has decided to spend the next four and a half years getting ready for you in a way that nobody in the history of that school has ever been ready, and I do not think any of you have the faintest idea what is coming up the drive. That is what frightens me, Albus. Not him. He is the gentlest creature I have ever met. The rest of you. I remain, as ever, at my post, which is now a café in Egham with a pot of tea. Arabella
