She read it through twice, blotted it, and folded it. Mr Tibbles watched her address the envelope with the right hand.

“There,” said Arabella. “Now he knows.”

The cat looked at the envelope, and at her, with the expression that Harry Potter would have recognised at once and that she had never managed to name in nine years: the look of a creature who has sat through the whole lecture and is waiting, not optimistically, to see whether anybody was listening.

The Headmaster’s office, Hogwarts, the following Monday

Minerva McGonagall had the letter in her hand. She had read it standing, twice, and was now reading it a third time sitting down, which Dumbledore had observed over sixty years to be the sequence in which she received news she did not like. Behind her, Fawkes shifted on his perch and made a small enquiring sound, and the hundred small silver instruments on the side tables went on turning and puffing in their mild way, and the February light came in thin and white across a desk that had five years of Arabella Figg’s letters on it, in a stack, in order, with the newest on top.

“Her hands,” said McGonagall.

“So she writes.”

“I have known Arabella Figg since 1961. She has not been able to hold a teacup in her right hand since the Grindelwald years. A child of six has mended them on her kitchen floor by being sorry for her, and you are about to tell me about animals.”

“I was about to tell you,” said Dumbledore mildly, “what I believe. Which is that the boy is extraordinarily gifted, as we had every reason to expect, and that accidental magic in a strong child can take the shape of wishes, and that a child who loves an old woman—”

“She says it is not accidental. She says he does it on purpose, every Saturday, one cat at a time, and that he warmed her teapot in a café to make a point. Albus, that is not a wish. I have taught a thousand first-years. I know what a wish looks like.” She put the letter down flat on the desk and her hand flat on the letter. “And she says he does not do it the way we do. No wand. No word. He asks it. Have you ever — in a hundred years — have you ever once heard of anyone—”

“No,” said Dumbledore.

The office went quiet. He had turned a little in his chair, towards the window, and the thin light caught the half-moon spectacles and hid what was behind them, which she had also observed over sixty years to be a thing he did at the exact moments he least wished to be read.

“No,” he said again, more slowly. “I have not. I have read of it. There are accounts — very old, mostly unreliable — of what the writers called petitionary magic. Asking rather than commanding. It was thought to be a thing of the very first wizards, before wands, before incantations, and to have been lost when the tools came, the way a people loses a dialect. I had always assumed it to be a story.” He turned back. “I begin to think I was reading the wrong shelf.”

McGonagall looked at him sharply. “Then do something.”

“I am doing something, Minerva. I am waiting. It is the hardest thing I do and I am very good at it.” He took his spectacles off and polished them on his sleeve, which she had seen him do perhaps four times in sixty years. “Four and a half years. He has pinned the date to a door. Arabella says he is getting ready for us. I find I am inclined to let him. If I walk up that garden path now, he stops getting ready and starts performing — for the man who left him on the step — and whatever it is he is building in that cupboard, I should rather see it finished than see it bent round me. I did enough to him on that step. I will not do that as well.”

“And the university? The examinations? Albus, he is six, and the Muggles are about to—”

“The Muggles,” said Dumbledore, “are about to do what Muggles do when they find a child they can see, which is a great deal, clumsily, with forms. Arabella says the professor found a mistake on page nine and the boy thanked her. Minerva, I have had letters from Ministers. I do not think I have ever read a sentence that reassured me more.” He put the spectacles back on. “He is being seen. He has decided to be. By a librarian, and a county psychologist, and a professor of mathematics with a knife for a face, if Arabella’s description is to be trusted, and every one of them has looked at him and stood a little to one side and held a door. That is not nothing. That is, I rather think, the thing I hoped for and could not arrange. I will not spoil it by arriving.”

McGonagall sat with that. Outside, a crow went over the lake.

“A café,” she said at last. “She sits in a café. From nine until five. Every day.”

“With a pot of tea, which he warms. Yes.” The corner of his mouth moved. “She has turned the post into a commute. I shall have to raise her stipend. I had not budgeted for scones.”

“Albus.”

“Yes.”

“The last paragraph.”

He read it again. She watched him arrive at it — not him; the rest of you — and stop there, as she had, three times.

“She is not afraid of him,” he said slowly.

“She is afraid for us. Which is a very different thing, and in my experience the more sensible.” McGonagall stood, and smoothed her robes. “Four and a half years, you say. I shall hold you to it. On his eleventh birthday I am delivering that letter myself, in person, by hand, and I am going to look at that child with my own eyes.”

“Yes,” said Dumbledore. “I think you had better.”

She put the letter down on his desk and went to teach the third years.

He sat a while after she had gone. The light moved. Fawkes sang two notes and stopped. On the desk, in a hand that had changed since the autumn from a careful left-handed slant to something freer, a squib in a housecoat had written he sits on the floor and is sorry for a cat until the cat gets up, and Albus Dumbledore, who had read every book on magic in the castle and most of the ones outside it, sat with the sentence for a long time and found that he had no shelf to put it on.

Then he took a fresh sheet and wrote to Arabella. He thanked her. He told her to go on exactly as she was, and to be kind to him, and to let him see nothing in her face but what was there. He raised her stipend by four galleons a month and said it was for the scones. And at the end he added one line that he had not planned, and did not cross out.

If he asks you what the first wizards did, before wands — tell him I do not know, and that I should very much like to.

Wisteria Walk, the Thursday after

Arabella Figg read the reply at the kitchen table with Mr Tibbles on the sugar bowl, and when she got to the last line she read it twice, and put the letter down, and looked at the cat.

“Well,” she said. “He heard that much. And he wants to know.” She made a fist of her right hand, and opened it. “That’s new. I don’t think I’ve ever had him ask me a question.”

Then she got up and put the kettle on, and took the good tin down from the top shelf, where it had been for five years waiting for somebody worth opening it for, and found it was nearly empty, and wrote biscuits on the pad with the right hand, and underneath it, after a moment, another tin.

Contents

Chapter 8

Fifteen Papers

The examination boards said no, and then they said no again in writing, and then Mrs Achebe clicked her pen in a particular way and they said that they would need to consult, and in April a man came down from London to consult, and Harry did the consulting in a room at Royal Holloway with a view of the lake while Professor Carrow sat in the corner with her feet on a stack of paper and did not help at all.

The man from London was called Mr Bellamy and he represented four boards at once, which Harry gathered was unusual, and he had a face that had been built for saying no to parents and had never before been asked to say it to a child of six and three-quarters who was reading his own regulations upside down across the table.

“The thing is,” said Mr Bellamy, for the third time, “there is no lower age limit, as such, because it has never been necessary to have one. The youngest candidate any of the boards has on record was eleven. We have no procedure for—”

“Then you don’t need one,” said Harry. “You need a candidate number and a centre, and the centre needs to agree to invigilate, and Mrs Bell has already said Stonewall will. The regulations say any person. I’ve read them. I’m a person.” He said it without any edge at all, because he had none; he liked Mr Bellamy, who was plainly trying. “Mr Bellamy, I’m not asking you to decide whether I can do them. The papers will decide that. I’m only asking you to let the papers try.”

Mr Bellamy looked at Mrs Achebe. Mrs Achebe clicked her pen twice.

“Fifteen,” said Mr Bellamy. “Ten at one level and five at the other. In one summer. At six.”

“Seven, by the time of the second week,” said Harry. “My birthday’s the thirty-first of July. And I’ve read the syllabuses. All of them. They’re not very long.”

From the corner, Professor Carrow made a small sound into her coffee that Harry had learned, over a year of Thursdays, was the nearest she came in company to a laugh.

Mr Bellamy went back to London. In May, a letter came to Mrs Bell on four different letterheads, and the letters all said the same thing in slightly different ways, and the thing they said was that the boards had no objection in principle, that this was without precedent, and that they would be obliged if the centre could confirm that the candidate was able to hold a pen for the duration.

Mrs Bell confirmed it. She did so, Harry was later told, in a single sentence, and the sentence was He has been holding one longer than most of your examiners.

The exams were a fortnight. The spring was the tins.

There were four of them by Easter, Edinburgh and then three from the Co-op, and they went up to Egham on the train one at a time in his bag, and Carrow had kept her word and found people. Ashworth, from the office along the corridor, who was sound and said little and read with a pencil behind his ear that he never took down. A woman at Cambridge called Dr Tennant, who owed Carrow a favour from 1971 and discharged it by reading two hundred pages of a six-year-old’s algebra over a weekend and telephoning on the Monday to say, in a voice Carrow reported with relish, Helen, tell me he is not six. And Carrow herself, who kept the number theory, because it was hers, and who by the end of April had read every page of it twice and found four things wrong, and three of them Harry had already found and fixed in the next tin, and the fourth was real and he sat with it on the train home for most of a week of his own and thanked her for it on the Thursday.

“Don’t thank me,” said Carrow. “Write the next one. I have Tennant asking me whether there’s a paper in the fourth tin and I have told her there are three and she is not to have any of them until Ashworth has finished arguing with the second.”

There were three. That was the thing that spring had done, and he had not seen it coming, because he had been looking at the pages and not at what the pages were for. He had written his mathematics down so that one woman could check it. Four people had checked it. And four people, it turned out, did not simply say correct and hand it back; they said this bit is new, and nobody has this, and who else knows this?, and the answer to the last was nobody, because he had worked it out at four under a staircase and it had been in his head and nowhere else. Ashworth had found the first paper in the second tin — twelve pages on a thing Harry had thought was obvious, which Ashworth said was obvious to exactly nobody and had been an open question since the war. Carrow had found the second in the number theory and said it was going out under his name alone, because it was his alone and she did not put her name on things she had not done. The third was the colouring problem, and it was not written yet, because it was not solved yet, and it was the one that mattered to both of them.

He wrote at night. He had never needed much sleep and now he needed less, because the pencil did the work and he only had to look, and by May the writing was no longer the number theory, which was done, but the next shelf down: the physics he had rebuilt from the Open University over two years of nights, written for Lindqvist on the second floor, whom he had not yet met and who was going to be, Carrow said, impossible about it. He wrote that in a fortnight. He started on the thing he had been circling since he was two — the shape of English, the thing about why the sentences went the way they went that no book on the fourth floor had got right — and found he did not know yet who it was for, and wrote it anyway, because Carrow had said find out afterwards.

The Dursleys saw none of it. He wrote behind a shut door with the light on and a boiler for company, and the tins went up the stairs in a bag, and what Vernon saw was a boy who went to a university and came back, and that was already more than Vernon knew what to do with.

The sports hall at Stonewall High smelled of floor polish and fear, and on the first Monday in June he walked into it with his two pens and his ruler and a hundred and forty sixteen-year-olds turned round in their chairs to look at him.

He had thought about this moment more than it deserved and had concluded there was nothing to be done about it but to walk in. So he walked in. The invigilator — a tall stooped man in a cardigan the colour of a wet pavement, with chalk on both elbows, who had plainly been told and had plainly not believed it until now — checked his candidate number three times, and then went and fetched the deputy head to check it a fourth, and Harry sat at the front desk, desk one, and laid his pens parallel, and waited to be allowed to sit a mathematics examination.

“It’s right,” said the deputy head, who had a list. “Potter, H. Front row, desk one. Boards’ special arrangement.” She looked down at him over the list. “Good luck, Harry.”

“Thank you, Mrs Okafor.”

The man in the cardigan looked at him a moment longer than the number needed.

“Haines,” he said. “Physics. I’ll be doing most of yours. If you need anything—” he paused, visibly discarding the toilet as inadequate to the occasion “—put your hand up.”

“Yes, sir.”

“You may begin,” said Mr Haines, to the room, and a hundred and forty papers turned over with a sound like a flock lifting off a field.

Harry turned his, and read it, and that took nine seconds, and then he had the problem he had been thinking about for a month and had never quite solved, and the problem was this: an hour and a half.

Ninety minutes, at two hundred to one, was three hundred hours. Twelve and a half days at a desk in a room where he was not permitted to read, or move, or do anything whatever but the paper in front of him — a paper he could finish, at the speed of his hand, in perhaps twenty-five minutes. His hand, in here. The pencil that moved by itself was for behind a shut door, and he had decided that before he sat down, and would not have changed it for ten minutes saved. He had sat tests before. He had never sat one with rules, where the end of the paper was not the end of the sitting, and he had spent some of the spring genuinely worried about what twelve days in a sports hall would do to a mind that had very nearly been killed by a playpen.

He had decided, in the end, to treat it as a gift. Nobody else in his life had ever given him twelve days with nothing to do but think, and an obligation to look as if he were doing nothing.

So he did the paper. Carefully, at the speed of his hand, showing all working, because the mark scheme would want it and because a dead man’s margins had taught him that writing for someone slower was a courtesy, not a chore. It took twenty-six minutes. He checked it twice. Then he laid the pen down parallel to the ruler, and folded his hands, and looked at a point on the far wall just above the badminton net, and went inside.

He spent the first day on the colouring problem, because he owed Carrow and because there was a thing he had seen on the Thursday that he had not had a chance to turn over — a place where two pieces of the construction could slide past each other that they had both been assuming could not. He turned it over. He turned it a long way. By the time Mr Haines walked past his desk for the third time, glanced down at the folded hands and the finished paper and the small boy gazing peacefully at the badminton net, and walked on, Harry had the rough shape of what he would say to Carrow on Thursday, and it was going to make her swear.

The second day he spent on the people. A hundred and forty of them, breathing. He had never been in a room with so many minds working so hard at once and he could hear them — not the thoughts; the weather: the pens that stopped and started, the sighs, the boy at the back who was going to fail and knew it and kept turning to the last page as if a different question might have appeared there. He minded about that boy, properly, for most of an hour of his own time, and nothing came, because there was nothing to mend; the boy simply hadn’t done the work, and the magic had no sentence for that, and he filed it under limits, and then, because a limit was only a thing nobody had got round yet, he filed beside it a question: what would have had to happen, a year ago, for that boy not to be at the back? He did not have the answer. He had a shape of one, and the shape had a library in it, and a Saturday morning, and someone who could explain a thing nine different ways until one of them went in.

The third day he thought about his mother. He let himself. He had very little of her — a word in a dream, a sentence from Marge, your mum especially; everyone said from a woman on a garden bench — and he had learned to be careful how often he took it out, because it did not wear well. But he was sitting in an examination she had never sat, in a world she had left, being the clever one, and he found he wanted to tell her. I’m doing the papers, Mum. Fifteen of them. The lady from the county says it’s never been done. I’m letting them see. The badminton net did not reply. He hadn’t expected it to.

“Pens down,” said Mr Haines. “Stop writing now, please.”

He had been sitting with his pen down for sixty-four minutes, which was most of a fortnight, and had not been bored for one second of it, and he thought, standing up into the flock-noise of a hundred and forty people exhaling, that he had learned something that morning no board had a grade for. He could sit in a room where the rules said nothing and fill the nothing himself, for twelve days, out of his own head, and come out having done good work and thought about a boy at the back and said a thing to his mother. The sports hall had been the playpen. He had been afraid of the playpen since he was two.

He wasn’t any more.

Mr Haines caught him in the corridor after the fourth paper, which was physics, and which Harry had finished in nineteen minutes and then spent eleven days thinking about a thing in the sixth question that the paper had got, very slightly, wrong.

“Potter.”

“Sir.”

“Question six.” Mr Haines had the paper in his hand — the question paper, not Harry’s; invigilators were not supposed to read the scripts and Mr Haines, Harry had already decided, was a man who did not do things he was not supposed to. “You wrote something in the margin of your script. I didn’t read it. I saw that there was something, and I saw you underline it, and I have been teaching this syllabus for twenty-nine years and I know what is on page four of that paper, and I should like to know, as one professional to another, whether you found what I think you found.”

“The moments one, sir,” said Harry. “The diagram and the words don’t agree about which way the force acts. The words are right. If you use the diagram you get the wrong sign.”

Mr Haines looked at the ceiling of the sixth-form corridor for a moment.

“I have written to that board about that diagram,” he said. “Four times.” He looked at Harry. “You put it in the margin.”

“I didn’t know who else to tell.”

“Hm.” Mr Haines put his hands in his cardigan pockets, and seemed to come to a decision. “I shall be invigilating the chemistry on Thursday. And the further mathematics, which I don’t understand, and the biology, which I understand less. If you find anything else wrong with the papers, Potter, don’t put it in the margin. Put your hand up. I’ll come over. I should like to see it for myself.”

“Yes, sir.”

“And Potter.” He had turned away and turned back. “The moments one. Apart from the diagram. It was straightforward?”

“Yes, sir. Once you know which way it goes.”

“Good,” said Mr Haines, and went off to find a cup of tea, and Harry stood a moment longer in the corridor with the thing in his throat that was not for question six, and then went to catch the train, where Mrs Figg was waiting with a castle.

He put his hand up twice more that fortnight. Both times Mr Haines came over and bent down and looked, and said “Hm,” and once he said “Yes,” quietly, with real pleasure, and wrote something on his own pad, and Harry gathered that the board would be receiving a letter.

“Say it,” said Carrow.

It was the Thursday after the mathematics paper, in her office, at twenty past four, with the lake going grey outside and the board so covered in chalk that she had started writing on the back of the door. The colouring problem — stuck since 1983; a corner of it found by Harry in his second week at the library a year ago; and since then, every Thursday, the two of them going at the general case from every side either of them could think of and a great many sides that neither of them would have thought of alone.

That was the thing he had not expected about working with someone. He had thought it would be faster. It wasn’t. He could think a year’s worth in a week; she couldn’t; the pace of the thing was set by the slowest mind in the room, and the slowest mind in the room was a professor of mathematics, and it was still two hundred times slower than him, and he had spent the first month quietly frantic about it and the second month understanding why it didn’t matter.

It didn’t matter because she could see things he couldn’t. Not faster. Differently. She had thirty years of knowing where the bodies were buried in six fields he had read but never lived in, and when he came at the problem down a road that looked clear to him she could say, without looking up, “That’s the Hungarian trap, Potter, three people have died in it, go round,” and he would go round, and the road he hadn’t seen would be the one. He had a thousand years of thought. She had a map. And a map, he had decided that spring, was worth most of a thousand years, if the years had all been spent in one cupboard.

“It doesn’t hold,” he said.

“Where.”

“Here. The step from the third case to the general one. We’ve been assuming the pieces don’t overlap. They can. I saw it on Monday, in the sports hall. I had twelve days.” He drew it on the back of the door. “They slide past each other. Like that.”

Carrow came to the door and looked at it for a long moment, and said something under her breath that he had not heard her say before and that he filed, with interest, for later.

“Yes,” she said. “Yes, damn it. They do.” She took the chalk out of his hand. “All right. Then we don’t go through the third case. We go—” She stopped. The chalk stopped. He watched her face do a thing he had been waiting a year to see it do, and the thing was the ice — the world going vast and slow and bright — except that it was happening to someone else, at one-to-one, and he could see every frame of it. “We go under. If they can slide, they can be made to. All of them. The whole way down. Potter — if we force the overlap—”

“—then the overlap is the thing we count,” said Harry, “not the pieces,” and he had it, and she had it, at the same moment, two people standing in front of a door in the rain with their mouths slightly open, and neither of them said anything for three full seconds, which for him was most of a morning, and he spent it looking at Helen Carrow’s face and thinking that he had never in his life seen anything so entirely worth the price of admission.

It took them till seven to write it down so that it held. She rang the Dursleys herself, and spoke to Vernon in the voice she used for the head of faculty, and Vernon said of course, Professor, and she put the phone down and said, “That man would sign anything,” and went back to the door. When it held she stood back and looked at it — nine feet of chalk and most of a door — and took her glasses off, and said quite quietly, “Four years,” and then, in the ordinary voice: “Right. We write it up. Properly. Both names.”

“Carrow and Potter.”

“Alphabetical. It’s the convention and I don’t hold with fiddling it.” She looked at him over the glasses. “You understand what this is. It’s a paper. In a journal. With your name on it. Not a certificate. The conversation — the one I told you about, the one that goes back a hundred years. You’ll be in it. At six. Three times over, by October, if Ashworth ever stops arguing.” A pause. “They won’t believe it, you know. The referees. They’ll think I’m having a joke.”

“Then we’d better make the proof very good,” said Harry.

Carrow laughed — the first time he had heard it properly, out loud; he started a tally — and threw him his coat.

He wrote the paper that night. Twenty-two pages, both names, every step shown, in the hand she knew, with the pencil going and the boiler for company, and he put it in her tray on the Friday morning before she was in, and she found it at nine and read it standing up and did not sit down until page eleven, and when he came up at eleven for the biscuit she said, without looking up, “I was going to spend August on this.”

“I know,” he said. “I thought you might like August.”

He spent August with Mr Haines, and it was the first time he had made a thing with his hands that was worth making, and he found out something about his hands that nobody had told him.

Haines had asked. That was the shape of it — in the corridor after the last paper, with the chalk on his elbows and his hands in his pockets, in the voice of a man who did not ask for things: that he had the physics lab at Stonewall for the summer, as he had had it every summer for twenty-nine years, and a six-inch mirror he had been grinding on and off since 1979 and had never finished because there was never anyone to finish it with, and that if Potter had nothing better to do on Tuesday and Thursday mornings, and if Mrs Okafor and the aunt agreed, which Haines would see to, he might come and learn what a telescope was from the inside, and they might finish the mirror, and see Saturn in September.

Harry had nothing better to do. He had a thousand years and a cupboard and three tins, and he had read every book on optics on the fourth floor and never once held a lens.

So Tuesdays and Thursdays he went to Stonewall on the bus with Mrs Figg, and she sat in the staff room with the castle and a cup of tea Mr Haines made her himself, and Harry stood at a bench with a tool and a disc of glass and ground.

It was slow. It was meant to be slow; that was the whole of grinding, Haines said, you took off less than you could see and you took it off evenly and you did it ten thousand times, and the mirror would be a mirror when it was ready and not before. And Harry stood at the bench with the tool under his hands, going round, and found that the thing which had made him slow at everything with a hand in it was not, in fact, the hand.

It was that nobody had ever told the hand anything.

His hand had been a child’s hand because it had been taught by a child’s attention — one glance a second at what it was doing, the way every hand on earth was taught. Here, going round on a disc of glass with Haines beside him saying evenly, Potter, evenly, he had two hundred glances a second, and every one of them was a correction, and the hand took the corrections. Not at once. But within an hour the strokes were even in a way Haines looked at twice, and by the second Tuesday Haines had stopped saying evenly, and by the third he had stopped watching, and was telling Harry about his wife, who had died in 1981, and the lathe in his garage that he had built from a kit in 1968 and was going to leave to the school.

“You’ve good hands,” said Haines, on the fourth Tuesday, with his back to the bench. “I don’t say that. I’ve had sixth-formers with worse.”

Harry looked at his hands on the tool and did not say that they had been bad hands in June. He understood what he had found, and it was not a magic thing at all, and that was why it mattered. He had thought his body was simply slow, a thing to be waited on, like the weather. It was not slow. It was untaught, and the teaching it took was attention, and attention was the thing he had. Every hand he had ever been clumsy with — the pen, the shoelace, Petunia’s needle — had been clumsy because he had never once stood over it and watched. He had a shelf for that now, beside things a steady hand could make, and the shelf was called the body can be taught, and he put on it, that August, the pen, which he would make fast the honest way as well as the other; and the needle; and a piano, if he ever found one; and whatever it was that Dudley did to a football that he had never been able to do.

They finished the mirror on the last Thursday in August, and Haines tested it with a knife-edge and a candle in the dark of the storeroom, and the shadow came across the face of the glass straight and even and all at once, which Haines said he had seen perhaps twice in twenty-nine years, and he said nothing for a while, and then he said, “Well,” and then he said, “We’ll have Saturn in September,” and they did, on the school field, at ten at night, with Mrs Figg in a deckchair and a flask, and the rings were there, tilted, exactly where the book had said and nothing at all like the book, and Harry looked through a mirror he had ground with his own hands at a thing nine hundred million miles away and thought that this, too, was a kind of reading, and that he had been doing it off pages for too long.

He asked Haines, walking back across the field with the tube on his shoulder, about the lathe.

“It’s a Myford,” said Haines. “Nineteen sixty-eight. It’s in my garage and it wants a man who can stand at it, and I can’t, any more, not for long. Why?”

“Because I should like to make a clock,” said Harry. “A pendulum one. I’ve read how. I’ve never held a piece of brass.”

Haines walked a while.

“Saturdays,” he said. “Not before ten. I don’t get up before ten on a Saturday and I’m not starting for a boy of seven, however many papers he’s sat.”

Results came on a Thursday in the third week of August, both sets on the same morning, because the boards had decided, in a spasm of administrative mercy, that if they were going to do this they might as well do it once.

Stonewall gave them out in the hall, in envelopes, from a table with a tablecloth on it, and the corridor was full of sixteen-year-olds screaming or going very quiet, and eighteen-year-olds doing the same more loudly, and Harry stood in the middle of it, up to most people’s waists, with his hands in his pockets, and waited his turn at the P’s.

Mr Haines was behind the table. He had taken the P’s on purpose; Harry could see it in the way he was sitting. He found the two envelopes without looking, and held them, and did not hand them over.

“I’ve known since Monday,” he said. “They telephone the centre first. I have been teaching for twenty-nine years, Potter, and I have never once wanted to open someone else’s results, and I have had these in my desk since Monday, and I have not opened them, and I should like that noted.”

“Noted, sir.”

Mr Haines gave him the envelopes. Harry opened the first standing up, with the hall roaring round him, and read it, which took a second, and then the second, which took another, and then stood holding both, and the two seconds went on for a very long time.

Ten A’s. Five A’s. Fifteen letters down two sheets of paper, all the same letter, and his name at the top of each, and it was the first piece of paper in his life from the outside world that said, in a form the outside world could not argue with, what he was.

He had expected to feel something large. He felt something small and precise, like a key turning. They can see. That was all. Seven years of being the only one who knew, and now there was a certificate, and the certificate was in the handwriting of four examination boards who had said no twice.

Mr Haines was watching his face.

“Well?”

Harry turned the sheets round and held them up.

Mr Haines looked at them. He took his glasses off and looked again. Then he put them back on and sat back in his chair behind the tablecloth and said, quite quietly, so that only Harry heard it under the roar, “Twenty-nine years,” and then, a little louder: “Yes. Well. I did say the moments one was straightforward.”

“You did, sir.”

“Go and telephone your uncle, Potter. Before the newspapers do.”

The newspapers did it anyway.

The Surrey Advertiser was Thursday evening, because Mrs Achebe clicked her pen. The nationals were Saturday, because the Advertiser had a stringer, and because a boy of seven with fifteen top grades was the sort of thing that filled the whole of page three in the dead week of August when nothing else was happening. BOY, 7, SITS FIFTEEN EXAMS — AND GETS TOP GRADE IN EVERY ONE. Four of them ran it. Two of them ran the photograph the Advertiser had taken on the Thursday — a small boy in a school jumper holding two sheets of paper, carefully, with both hands, as if he were carrying them for somebody else — and all of them used the word prodigy, and the word sat on the kitchen table at Number Four for a whole weekend being read aloud by Vernon in a voice that got slightly deeper each time.

Vernon, that weekend, was a man transfigured. Harry had seen him pleased before — the university letter, the Rotary, the funeral suit at the long table — but this was a different order of thing, and he watched it with the attention of a naturalist who has finally seen the rare bird at the top of the tree. His uncle moved differently. He answered the telephone before the second ring. He said my nephew to the man from the garage, to Mrs Next Door, to Marge — twice to Marge, who had rung back to make sure she had heard right, and had been told, in a voice Harry would remember for the rest of his life with something close to affection, that she had heard perfectly well the first time, and that the Dursleys had always encouraged him, and that if she had anything further to say about bad blood she could say it to the Daily Mail.

Petunia said nothing, which was what Petunia said. But on the Saturday morning, at the till at the greengrocer’s, she opened her purse to pay, and Harry, standing with the basket at knee height as he had stood at that till since he was three, saw inside it, folded small behind the stamps, a square of newsprint with a photograph on it, and looked away before she could see him seeing.

He never mentioned it. He never would. He put it on the shelf with the straight fingers and the garden bench and the margins, in the place where he kept the things that had been done for him quietly by people who would have denied it, and the shelf was getting, he noticed, rather full.

He gave one interview, by design, and it was the most boring interview of 1987, and he was prouder of it than of the fifteen A’s.

The nationals wanted television. He said no, through Mrs Bell, who was delighted to say it for him. He had no intention of being a thing that people watched in their lounges. But a newspaper interview could be managed, if you were patient and dull and had rehearsed every answer for a fortnight, and it bought something he wanted, which was to be a known thing. A filed thing. A boy in a drawer marked prodigy, Surrey, polite that the world could stop wondering about and leave alone.

So he sat in Mrs Bell’s office with the orange squash, with Mrs Bell behind her desk and Vernon in the funeral suit in the good chair, and a young man from the Telegraph with a notebook and a tape recorder asked him what it was like, and he told him, in sentences a seven-year-old might plausibly produce, that he liked reading, and that the school and the university had been very kind, and that his uncle and aunt had always encouraged him. He said he wanted to go on learning things. He said his favourite subject was mathematics because you could always check whether you were right. The young man asked whether it was true that he had a paper coming out in a journal, and Harry said yes, three, with Professor Carrow and Dr Ashworth, and that they were about colouring and numbers and he did not think the Telegraph’s readers would want the details, and the young man agreed with visible relief, and wrote three papers and underlined it, and Harry watched him underline it and was content.

He had planted exactly one line, and he planted it at the end, where the young man asked — because they always asked — who he would like to thank.

“Mrs Prentice at the library,” said Harry, “who held the door. And Mrs Figg, who takes me on the train, and has kept an eye on me for a very long time. I don’t think I’d have done any of it without them.”

It went in. He checked, the following Tuesday, on page nine of the Telegraph, under a headline about a modest prodigy. It was a perfectly ordinary thing for a child to say. It would mean nothing to anyone who read it on this side of the curtain. And if anyone on the far side read Muggle newspapers — he did not know whether they did; he suspected the man Mrs Figg wrote to did; he suspected the man read everything — then somewhere in Scotland, or wherever such men sat, a person who had asked a squib to watch over a small boy had just been thanked for it, in the Telegraph, by name, by the boy, who had known about the watching for years and had decided to say so in print where everyone could see and no one could understand.

It was the nearest thing to a letter he could send. He hoped it had arrived.

The money came last, and came strangely, and taught him what the ordinary world did when it decided to be kind to a child.

A man called Pargeter telephoned Mrs Bell in September. He was a solicitor, in Guildford, and he represented a client who had read page three and wished to do something about it, and the something, when it had been explained to Vernon across Mrs Bell’s desk at a meeting Harry was permitted to attend in silence, turned out to be a trust. An educational trust, in Harry’s name, funded to a sum that made Vernon sit very still in the funeral suit, administered by Mr Pargeter’s firm, to pay for books, tuition, examination fees, travel to and from any place of learning, and such other expenses as the trustees shall in their discretion deem conducive to the beneficiary’s education, until the beneficiary attained the age of twenty-one.

“The trustees,” said Vernon, after a moment, “being—”

“Myself,” said Mr Pargeter, “and my partner, Mrs Hale. Our client was specific on that point. He felt — forgive me, Mr Dursley — that the funds should be kept quite separate from the household, so that the family should never be put to the awkwardness of seeming to benefit. It’s a standard provision.” He said this in a voice as smooth as a bowling green, and Harry, watching his uncle discover that a large sum of money had arrived in the family and could not be touched, understood that it was not a standard provision at all, that Mrs Achebe had clicked her pen somewhere behind this, that Mr Pargeter had met Vernon Dursley before he had met Vernon Dursley, and that kind people, when they chose to be, could be quite as careful as he was.

Vernon signed. He had to; the alternative was explaining to the Rotary why he had refused a scholarship. He signed.

Harry asked two things at the door, because the door was where Mr Pargeter was out of Vernon’s hearing.

“Who is he? The client.”

“I’m afraid I can’t say.”

“Can you tell him thank you?”

Mr Pargeter looked down at him from a great height with a face that gave away nothing at all, and then it gave away a very small amount at one corner.

“I can tell him,” he said, “that you asked. I rather think that will do.”

“And the trust,” said Harry. “Can things go into it? Not from him. From me. If I wrote a book, say, and somebody paid for it. Could it go in there, where my uncle can’t reach it, and could the trustees spend it on what I asked, if what I asked was conducive?”

Mr Pargeter looked at him for rather longer than the first question had taken.

“Yes,” he said. “It could. I should want to see the book.” He put on his hat. “I find I should rather like to see the book.”

That night he took stock, the way he did at the end of a thing, in the words he would have used if there had been anyone to use them to, and it was short, because the long version was going to be the rest of his life.

Fifteen papers. All of them. Nobody has done it and the boards said so twice. Three papers in journals going off in October with my name on them, one alone. Eleven hundred pages written since December and read by four people, and the four are the slow part now, and there is nothing to be done about that but find more of them.

A mirror I ground, and Saturn in it. The hands can be taught; it only wants attention, and I have that. A lathe on Saturdays and a clock to make. A man who waited eight years for someone to finish a mirror with.

A trust that pays for travel to any place of learning, and will take what I earn, and a man who wants to see the book. I have not written the book. I know what it is: the one for a reader who is slow, the one that would have had the boy at the back of the sports hall not at the back. I am going to write it this autumn, at night, and give it to Mrs Prentice first.

Four years. Eight hundred of mine, roughly.

I have read everything on the fourth floor that I can follow and most of the fifth. I have the grammar to eleven parts and a bracket and I can make six sentences of it, and hold two of them. I have not seen one of their books. Mrs F will not bring me one; she says it is not hers to do. I have not asked where the shop is. I think she would tell me. I think I am going to find it myself first, because I want to have found it, and then ask.

Then he turned to the inside of the cupboard door, where the date was pinned at the height of his eyes, and under the date, on the same square of paper, in pencil, small, he added the one word he had been adding to every plan he had ever made — not to remember it; so that it was said, on a door, where the house could read it.

Faster.

He was seven years and one month old, and the most examined child in the history of four examination boards, and he lay down in the smallest office in England and did not sleep, because it was ten past one and a woman in a committee blouse on BBC2 was about to explain the French Revolution to nobody, and he had eight hundred years, and a bookshop to find.

Contents

Chapter 9

The Edge of the Map

“Mrs Figg,” he said, in the café at Egham, on a Tuesday in October, with the pot warm and a biscuit in his hand, “I’m going to find the street. The one with the shops. I thought you should know before I did it rather than after.”

Mrs Figg put down her castle.

“I can’t take you,” she said. “You know that. It isn’t mine to—”

“I know. I’m not asking you to take me. I’m asking you to come to London on Saturday and sit in a café while I look, because you come everywhere else I go and it would be strange if you didn’t, and because I’d rather you were there.” He ate the biscuit. “You don’t have to point. I don’t want you to point. I want to have found it. But I’d like you to be across the road when I do.”

She looked at him for a long moment, over the glasses, with the face that had been changing all year — the surprise gone out of it, mostly, and the arithmetic, and only the gladness left, and under the gladness something he had begun to recognise as pride, which was not a thing anyone had ever pointed at him before and which he was still learning to stand in.

“How will you know where to look?” she said. “London’s a big place.”

“I’ve worked it out. I think. If I’m wrong I’ll have had a day in London, and you’ll have had a scone somewhere new.” He finished the biscuit. “Will you come?”

“I’ll come,” said Arabella Figg. “I shan’t point. And Harry — if you find it, I shall have to write and say so.”

“I know,” said Harry. “That’s why I told you first.”

He had worked out where to look the way he worked out everything, which was by refusing to guess.

He knew almost nothing. A street, Mrs Figg had said, with shops, and a bank, and a newspaper. London. People in cloaks went to it to do their shopping and nobody on this side had ever noticed; that was the whole of his information, and it was, he had decided in the sports hall, enough.

Because a street like that had to have a door. People in cloaks had to come from somewhere and go to somewhere, and the somewhere had to open onto an ordinary pavement, and the pavement had to be one where a cloak did not draw a crowd. Where in London could you walk in a floor-length cloak and a hat and not be looked at twice? He had thought about it on the four-floor stairs of the library and arrived, by the second landing, at an answer that pleased him with its plainness: the theatres. Costume shops. The streets round Covent Garden and Soho and the Charing Cross Road, where half the people on the pavement were dressed as something and the other half had seen everything.

So that was where he went, on the Saturday, with Mrs Figg beside him on the train in her mackintosh with a castle in the pocket, and then on the top deck of buses, because the top deck of a bus was the best observation platform ever built for a boy who could read a crowd at a face every two seconds and had an afternoon.

He rode the 24, and the 29, and the 176, with his forehead against the glass and Mrs Figg a seat behind pretending to read, and watched the pavements of the West End go by in a slow river of faces, and looked for the thing he had seen three times in his life — the cloak in the wrong season, the hat with the wrong brim, the particular wrongness of someone dressed by a different century’s rules and not in on the joke — and for an hour he saw nothing, and then on the Charing Cross Road, going north, he saw a woman.

Emerald green. Ankle-length. A pointed hat she was carrying rather than wearing, as if she had been told to and resented it. She walked fast, like someone with an errand, and the Saturday crowd parted round her and did not look at her, and that — the not looking — was what caught him, more than the green. The crowd should have looked. Londoners looked at everything for a quarter-second and moved on; he had watched them do it all morning. They did not look at her at all. Their eyes went over her the way water goes over a stone.

Behind him, Mrs Figg turned a page of her castle with great deliberation, and said nothing, and he loved her for it.

He got off at the next stop.

He stood on the east side of the Charing Cross Road for two hours, in a shop doorway and then at a bus stop and then in a shop doorway again, and he watched the far pavement, and in two hours he saw eleven of them.

Mrs Figg sat in the window of a sandwich bar directly opposite with a pot of tea and the castle, not reading, not pointing, with her face turned very slightly away from the stretch of frontage between a large bookshop and a record shop in the manner of a woman who had been asked not to help and was finding it the hardest thing she had done all year.

He kept a tally. A tall man in a cloak like a monk’s. Two women arguing cheerfully, one with a basket that moved on its own — he saw it move; nobody else did. A boy his own age in ordinary clothes holding the hand of a woman who was not in ordinary clothes at all, looking round at the street with the wide-eyed air of a child being taken to town for something exciting. An old man with a beard who stopped on the pavement, looked both ways like a child crossing, and stepped in — and that was the thing.

They all stepped in. At the same place.

Not through a door he could see. He had expected that to be the problem and had sat on the train composing how he would handle it. Between the bookshop and the record shop there was a stretch of frontage perhaps thirty feet long, and eleven people in two hours had walked along the pavement towards it and had — he could not put it better — gone. Not vanished. Nothing so vulgar. They had simply, at a point about two-thirds along, stopped being on the pavement, and the crowd had flowed on over the place as if it had always been empty, and every time it happened Harry found that he had been looking at the record shop.

Every time. Eleven times. He was the best watcher in Surrey and probably in England and he had been looking at the record shop.

That was the moment he understood the curtain, and it was not at all what he had imagined. He had pictured it like a wall, or a lock — a thing on the far side, keeping people out. It was nothing of the kind. It was on his side. It was in his head. Something in that thirty feet of frontage reached into the place where he decided what to look at, and moved his looking, gently, an inch to the left, and let him think he had done it himself. He had felt his attention go. He had every one of those eleven moments on the shelf and he went back through them now at leisure and found the same thing in each: a small sideways pressure, like a hand on the back of the head, so soft and so timed to the ordinary jump of the eye that no one on earth could possibly have noticed it happening.

No one who looked at the world at one-to-one.

He stood in his doorway with his heart going and understood what he had. It was a sentence. It was the roses — a shape, a grammar, working on him, written by somebody long ago and left running, like the warmth in the blankets. And it was not asking what he was. The boy in ordinary clothes had gone through with his hand in a witch’s, and it had let him, because the hand knew the way. It was a sentence for keeping a secret from people who did not have it yet, and he did not have it yet. And it was written for people whose attention jumped once and landed. His jumped two hundred times in the space where theirs jumped once. There was room in his looking that the sentence had never been built to fill.

So he went and stood in it.

He crossed the road and walked, slowly, along the thirty feet, and felt the hand come — soft, kind, nothing to see here, dear — and let it move him, and watched it move him, the way he had watched the roses. Then he walked back, and let it do it again, and watched again. Then a third time, and this time, where the hand came, he did a thing he had been practising on the roses for a year without knowing what it was for: he stood still inside himself and didn’t go with it. He let the push arrive and simply declined to look at the record shop, the way you decline a biscuit, and held his looking where it was, on the stretch of grimy brick between the two shopfronts, and for a second nothing happened, and the hand pushed harder, almost puzzled —

and there was a pub.

Small. Grubby. A low door and a window of thick greenish glass and a sign hanging crooked, and it had been there the whole time, and it was there now, and a man in a cloak came along the pavement and nodded to no one and went into it, and the door shut, and the Saturday crowd went by on either side of Harry Potter where he stood on the kerb with his mouth slightly open, looking straight at the edge of the map.

He turned round.

Across the road, in the window of the sandwich bar, Mrs Figg had put down her castle and was looking at him, and her face was doing the thing it had done on the day of the cat, and she raised her cup to him, very slightly, an inch, the way you would to someone across a room who had just done a thing you had bet on.

He crossed back.

“Well,” said Arabella Figg, when he had sat down opposite her. “Now you’ve found it.” She poured him a cup from the pot, which was still warm, because he had seen to that at half past two from forty yards. “Two hours and eleven minutes. Tibbles had you at three hours. I shall enjoy telling him.”

“You could see it the whole time.”

“I could see it the whole time. I’ve been able to see that pub since I was four years old; my brothers used to take me. I have sat here two hours with it in the corner of my eye and not said a word, and I should like you to know, Harry, that it is the hardest thing I have done since 1946.” She drank her tea. “What did it feel like? Finding it. I’ve always wondered. I never had to.”

He thought about how to say it. “Like a hand on the back of my head,” he said. “Very gentle. Pushing me to look somewhere else. And I just — didn’t.”

Mrs Figg looked at him over her cup for a long moment.

“Nobody can do that,” she said. “It turns away anyone who doesn’t know it’s there. Wizards know. A Muggle with a witch holding her hand gets through, because the hand knows. Nobody finds it. That is the whole of what it’s for.” She put the cup down. “I shall have to find a way to put that in a letter too. I don’t know how I shall. He declined the Leaky Cauldron.” And then — he saw her decide, the way he had seen so many of them decide that year, to stand to one side of a door — “Well. You’ve found it. What now?”

“Now I ask you to take me in,” said Harry. “To buy books. I’ve found it myself, which is what I wanted. The rest I can’t do alone; I don’t know how the door in the wall works, or what the money is, or what’s a sensible thing to say to a goblin. You do.” He looked at her. “I know you’ll have to write and say so. I know he might say you shouldn’t have. But Mrs Figg — I have read every book on the fourth and fifth floors of a university, and I have never seen a page of theirs, and there is a bookshop on the other side of that wall, and I have four years.”

“He’ll say no,” said Mrs Figg. “If I ask him.”

“Then don’t ask him,” said Harry. “Tell him after.”

Mrs Figg sat very still in the window of the sandwich bar, with the pub in the corner of her eye and the boy in front of her and forty years of doing what she was told behind her, and he watched her — he had all the time in the world to watch her — go back through those forty years and find the two occasions on which she had told Albus Dumbledore he was wrong, and weigh them, and find them good.

“I’ve got the scone money,” she said slowly. “He raised it. Four galleons a month since March; I’ve not spent a knut of it; it’s in a jar. It would be about—” she did the sum “—thirty galleons. That buys a good many books.” She picked up the castle and put it in her pocket. “And he can have the bill. In the letter. Itemised.”

She stood up.

“Finish your tea, dear. And Harry — when we go through, you hold my hand. Not because you’re seven. Because I am sixty-one and I have not been in there since my brother died and I should like something to hold.”

The wall in the yard behind the pub needed a wand, and Mrs Figg had none, and so they waited, as he had planned to wait anyway, and in four minutes a family came through from the street and the father took a stick from his sleeve and counted bricks and tapped one, and Harry stood eight feet behind with an old woman’s hand in his and saw it.

The brick wriggled. To anyone at one-to-one it simply moved; to him it shivered, like a dog shaking off rain, and then the brick beside it, and then the wall was doing something — not opening, not folding, but rearranging, each brick turning on an axis it should not have had and sliding past its neighbour in an order, a long patient order that he could read, that had the same spine as the roses and the tea, the pointing and the pushing and a bracket in the middle so large and so old that it took him the whole of the half-second to get to the end of it. It was a sentence. Somebody had written it into a wall a very long time ago, so well that it had run every day since without anyone needing to think about it, and it said, as near as he could put it, for those who know the knock, a way, and he stood in the yard and watched it say so, brick by brick, and thought: I could write that. Not today. Not this year. But he could see every mark of it, and nothing he could see had ever stayed closed to him for long.

“You’re reading it,” said Mrs Figg, very low, beside him. “Aren’t you. The wall.”

“Yes.”

“Nobody reads it, dear. They just knock.”

Then the wall was an archway, and the family went through, and Mrs Figg’s hand tightened on his, and they went through behind them, and the wall closed with a sound like a sigh, and he stopped.

He had stopped on every threshold of his life that mattered, and this was the one all the others had been practice for.

A street. Cobbled, crooked, climbing a little as it went, and full — of shops with bowed windows and hanging signs, of people in cloaks and robes and pointed hats and ordinary cardigans, of noise, of smells, of owls. There were owls in cages outside a shop, dozens, blinking at the daylight. There was a stack of cauldrons on the pavement with a sign that said ALL SIZES — COPPER, BRASS, PEWTER, SILVER — SELF-STIRRING. A shop whose window was entirely broomsticks, with a crowd of boys in front of it. A woman going past with a basket in which something was unmistakably moving, and nobody minding. The sky was the same sky. And under it ten thousand people were going about a Saturday’s shopping in a street that nobody on the far side of a wall had ever seen, and the air was thick with the thing from Mrs Figg’s kitchen floor, so thick he could almost see it, the whole street humming at a pitch just under hearing like a struck bell that had never been allowed to stop.

“Oh,” said Harry.

“Yes,” said Mrs Figg, beside him, in a voice he had not heard her use, looking at a street she had not seen in nine years. “Yes. It does rather.”

The bank was at the top, and it was white, and the goblins at the door looked at the old woman in the mackintosh and the small boy with his hand in hers with such complete and professional contempt that Harry, who had spent seven years studying faces, felt an instant and slightly alarming kinship.

The goblin at the counter had a long nose and a quill that moved very fast, and he did not look up when Mrs Figg put thirty galleons on the marble in a jam jar, and he did not look up when Harry said, “And I’d like to change Muggle money as well, please. Into yours,” and put a hundred and twelve pounds beside it, which was the whole of the toffee tin and had been counted out by Mrs Prentice that morning with one raised eyebrow and no questions.

The quill stopped. The goblin looked up — a long look, down the long nose, taking in the jumper, the glasses, the fact that the boy was seven — and held out one hand, palm up, without a word.

He counted the money twice, fast, with the air of someone who could have done it once and was being insulting on purpose. Then he did something with brass scales and a small book of tables, and wrote a figure, and began counting coins onto the marble in three stacks with a sound like the beginning of rain.

“Twenty-two galleons,” he said. “Six sickles. Eleven knuts. Seventeen to the galleon, twenty-nine to the sickle, if you don’t know, and you don’t.” He pushed the stacks across. “Next.”

“Thank you,” said Harry.

The quill paused — a quarter-second, which was most of a minute — and the long nose came up a fraction, and the goblin regarded Harry Potter with something that was not warmth, nothing so vulgar, but was perhaps the faintest flicker of a professional noticing that it had been thanked, correctly, by a child who had not been told to.

“Keep it inside your shirt,” said the goblin. “Next.”

Outside, on the white steps, in the sun, Mrs Figg said, “That was Bogrod. He counted my brother’s money twice in 1951. I believe he considers it a courtesy.”

Flourish and Blotts had a window full of books stacked to the ceiling and a bell on the door, and inside it was every library he had ever walked into and none of them, because the books moved.

Not all of them. Most sat on their shelves like books, floor to ceiling, in leather and cloth and something that might have been dragon, with ladders on rails and a smell of paper and dust and binding glue that went into him like the first day at the branch. But some shifted. A row of fat volumes on a high shelf were, very slowly, rearranging themselves into a better order. A thin book near the door kept trying to turn its own pages and being prevented by a strap. And in a cage by the till — he stopped, and looked, and thought of a woman in a garden saying some of them bite — a dozen heavy books with fur on their covers lay in a heap, breathing, and one of them opened one eye at him and closed it again.

He had fifty-two galleons and some silver, and he had planned this hour the way he had planned the sports hall, and he went to work.

He read the spines. All of them. He had read ten thousand spines in the Survey at five and knew what a shelf of spines could tell you — what a field thought was basic, what it thought was advanced, what it had too much of and what it was embarrassed about — and he walked the shop slowly with his hands behind his back like a boy told not to touch, and read, at eye-speed, every title in Flourish and Blotts in about forty minutes, which in the hours he actually lived in was a solid week of cataloguing, and came out the far end with a map.

The field had a canon. There were books that appeared in stacks — set texts, for the school — and books that appeared singly and dusty, and the stacked ones were cheap and the single ones were not. He took the stacked ones that mattered: The Standard Book of Spells, all seven grades, because the grammar of the thing was what he was after and seven years of its sentences for the price of a few good dinners was not a thing you walked past. Magical Theory, by a man called Waffling, which he opened at random and read a page of standing up and found to be exactly what the title said and badly in need of a Mr Prentice in its margins. A History of Magic. Hogwarts: A History, thick. A copy of the day’s Daily Prophet from a pile by the door, because a newspaper was the fastest way into a place he had ever found.

And then, on a high shelf in the back room where the single dusty books lived, a thin grey volume with no title on the spine at all, which he only took down because it was the one book in the shop his eyes had slid past, twice — and he had learned that very afternoon what it meant when his eyes slid past a thing — and which turned out, when he got it open, to be called On the Composition of Charms: Notes Towards a Grammar, by nobody he had heard of, printed in 1911, and which began, on the first page, with the sentence: It has long been held that a spell is a thing one learns. The present writer wishes to suggest that it is a thing one reads.

He stood in the back room with the thin grey book in both hands and the breathing books in their cage and the street humming outside, and read the first chapter standing up, and when he had finished he had to sit down on the bottom rung of the ladder, because somebody — seventy-six years ago, in a hand that disliked writing — had stood where he was standing.

Not as far. Nowhere near; the man had worked at one-to-one and said so, apologetically, in a footnote: the present writer regrets that observation of the casting moment is beyond any human faculty and must be inferred. He had inferred. He had got the spine, and the pointing part, and a bad guess at the bracket, and then he had run out of road, and the book was thin because the road was short. But he had seen there was a road. And nobody, by the dust, had read him since the war.

Harry bought it. It cost more than the seven grades together, and he paid without a word, and the young woman at the till with ink on her fingers — who had watched a child of seven read every spine in the shop without touching one and had decided, visibly, not to have an opinion about it — wrapped the thin grey book separately, in brown paper, and said, as she handed it over, “Nobody’s bought that in the whole time I’ve worked here. What did you want it for?”

“The margins,” said Harry, which was true, and she laughed, not understanding, and he put it in the canvas bag with the others.

He had two galleons left. He stood outside the shop in the sun with a bag that cut his shoulder and Mrs Figg beside him with the jam jar empty in her pocket, and the whole street going past, and he took out A History of Magic, because it was the thickest, and turned, as he had turned every book since he was five, to the index first.

He was looking for his mother’s name. That was the whole of it. He had a word in a dream and a sentence from Marge and your mum especially, and he had come to a street with a bookshop in the hope that a street with a bookshop would have a page.

He found Potter in the index. It had seven entries. The last one said Potter, Harry — see also Boy Who Lived, The.

Mrs Figg found a bench. She did not say anything. She sat him down on it, outside a shop that sold quills, and sat beside him, and took his free hand in her right one, the mended one, and held it, and he read.

Four minutes. The entry, and the pages it pointed to, and the three further pages in Hogwarts: A History that those pointed to, and the lower half of the Prophet’s front page, which carried, by the kind of timing he had once called luck, a small piece on the sixth anniversary that autumn of the fall of You-Know-Who. Four minutes, with the street going past, and then he closed the books and sat with his hand in Mrs Figg’s and let the four minutes catch up with him, which took, by the only clock that counted, most of a year.

He had known. He kept coming back to that, the way you press a bruise. He had known since a violet hat that strangers were glad to see him; since a garden bench that his parents had been of this world and died in it; since a letter in eleven days that there was a man who had reasons. He had known all the pieces. He had never had them in print, in order, in the cold plain prose of a reference book that did not know he was reading it, under a heading with his own name.

Lily Potter (née Evans) and James Potter were murdered at their home in Godric’s Hollow on the night of 31 October 1981 by the Dark wizard Lord Voldemort, whose name most of the wizarding community still declines to speak. Their son Harry, then fifteen months old, survived an attempt on his life by the same wizard — the only person ever known to have survived the Killing Curse — and Voldemort’s power was broken in the attempt, by means which remain unexplained. The scar on the child’s forehead is believed to be a consequence of the curse. He is widely referred to as the Boy Who Lived.

The green light. Six years of a word in a dream, and it had been this. A man in his parents’ house, and his mother’s voice saying his name, and then a thing that had killed her and been meant to kill him and had — for reasons the reference books did not know — not. And had, instead, perhaps, done something else. Spun a wheel. He had wondered since he was four where the two hundred had come from. He had never once let himself wonder whether it came from the same place as the scar.

He wondered now, on a bench in Diagon Alley with an old woman holding his hand, and found he could not stop.

“Mrs Figg,” he said, after a long time. “You knew all this.”

“Every word, dear. Since before you could talk.”

“Why didn’t you—”

“Because it wasn’t mine to tell. And because—” her hand tightened “—because I have watched you read ten thousand books, Harry, and I thought: he should have this one off a page. Like the rest. Not off my face at a kitchen table. You’d have read my face. You read everybody’s. I wanted you to have it plain, the way you have everything, and then I wanted to be sitting right here when you did.” She was not looking at him. She was looking at the street. “Was I wrong?”

He thought about it honestly, which was the only way he knew how.

“No,” he said. “No. You were right. I’d rather have had the page.” And then, because it was her, and because he had read the paragraph four times and it had not changed: “They don’t say he’s dead.”

“No.”

“They say vanished. Power broken. Many believe. The careful words. I’ve been reading careful books for three years; I know what the careful words are for.” He looked at the Prophet on his knee. “That’s why you watch for cloaks in the wrong season. That’s why there’s a — a protection on my aunt’s roof; she’s never said, but there is, I can feel the edge of it on the step some nights. That’s why the man who writes to you won’t come up the path. It isn’t only for me. It’s against something.”

Mrs Figg said nothing for a moment.

“Yes,” she said. “That’s about the size of it.”

He sat with that for a long time, carefully, the way he sat with anything that had edges. Then he picked it up and put it on a shelf — not the shelf of things that had made him who he was; a new one, which he named things I will deal with when I am ready and not one day before — and found that the shelf held.

Because the other thing was true too. Every person in this street had woken up, six years ago this month, into a world that was suddenly and inexplicably safe, and had been told that a baby had done it, and had been glad. They had bowed to him in Little Whinging because he was the morning they had woken up to. He had done nothing. He had been fifteen months old. He had, apparently, not died. But he could not, sitting there, resent them for it, any more than he could resent Mrs Figg for drinking the tea.

“All right,” he said. “All right. I’ve got the page now. Thank you for sitting here.”

“Oh, get on,” said Arabella Figg, and did not let go of his hand, and they sat a while longer on the bench in the sun with the owls and the cauldrons and the boy with the toad going past, and then she said, “Tea at six. Your aunt will have my hide,” and they went back down the crooked street, and at the wall they waited for a family and went through behind them, and the pub was dark, and the Charing Cross Road was loud and ordinary and full of Saturday, and somewhere between the pub and the station, for the first time in six years, properly, with facts, in the middle of a London afternoon with an old woman beside him not looking, he cried.

Not for long. He let it come, and let it go, and they caught the train, and they were home for tea.

Wisteria Walk, that night

Dear Albus, I took him to Diagon Alley this afternoon. You would have said no, so I did not ask. I should tell you first that he found the Leaky Cauldron himself, from the Muggle side, in two hours and eleven minutes, by standing on the Charing Cross Road until he could feel the charm turning his eyes and then — his word — declining it. Nobody had told him it was there; it had every right to turn him, and it could not. I watched him do it from across the road. I have never heard of anyone finding it from the outside and nor, I think, have you. He says it felt like a hand on the back of his head and he simply didn't go with it. Then he asked me to take him in for books, and I did, because he is seven and has read a university and had never seen one page of ours, and because I have told you twice in my life that you were wrong and I find I had it in me a third time. I spent the scone money. Thirty galleons. The bill is enclosed, itemised, and I shall thank you to pay it without comment. He bought all seven Standard Books, Waffling, Bagshot, Hogwarts: A History, today's Prophet, and a thing from 1911 that nobody has bought in living memory and that he read standing up and then had to sit down. He read the wall in the yard — READ it, Albus, the bricks, he says it is a sentence and he could write it given a year — and Bogrod at Gringotts changed his Muggle money and did not sneer, which I did not know Bogrod could do. And then he looked himself up in the index of A History of Magic, on a bench, and I held his hand while he read it, and I will tell you what he said when he had finished, because you should hear it in his words and not mine. He said: "They don't say he's dead. They say vanished. I know what the careful words are for. That's why you watch for cloaks. It isn't only for me. It's against something." He is seven. He cried on the way to the station. Not for long. He has a shelf, he says, for things he will deal with when he is ready and not one day before, and he put it there, and I believe him, because I have watched him put things on that shelf for a year and I have never once seen one fall off. I am not sorry. I shall not be. He asked me whether I was wrong to have waited, and I said I wanted him to have it off a page, like everything else, and he said I was right. I think I was. I think it is the first thing in six years I have been entirely sure I did right by him, and I did it against your instructions, and I should like you to sit with that. Arabella P.S. He has four years. He has read the Standard Books already — all seven — on the train home and before bed; he told me so at the gate. He says he can see the sentences under the words. He says it is like being handed a dictionary for a language he has been reading off roses. I do not know what any of that means. I think you had better.

Chapter 10

Petunia’s Drawer

The first spell in The Standard Book of Spells, Grade 1 was the one for making light, and he had read it on the train with Mrs Figg beside him, and he read it again that night under the stairs with the torch on and the house asleep, and halfway down the page he put the torch down, because he did not need it.

Not because he had done the spell. He had no wand, and the book was very clear that one needed a wand, in a tone that suggested the question had never seriously been raised. He put the torch down because he had read the description — the Wand-Lighting Charm produces a steady light at the tip of the caster’s wand, sufficient to read by; the incantation is accompanied by a short upward flick — and under the description, in the place where he had learned at four to look for the real meaning of a thing, he had found a shape he knew.

It was the candle.

Not exactly. The candle had been fire, a happening, a thing he had spent a month learning to bracket. This was light, a happening of a different family — but the spine was the same, the pointing and the pushing, and the bracket in the middle was a cousin of his own, a cleaner one, a bracket that someone had plainly spent a great deal of time sanding down. He could see it. He sat in the dark with the book on his knees and the words Lumos and upward flick on the page, and behind the words, the way the pub had been behind the record shop, he could see the sentence they stood for — and it was his sentence. A version of it. Written by somebody else, long ago, so well that it had been given a name and a wrist movement and put in a book for eleven-year-olds, who would say the name and do the movement and never once, in seven years of schooling, see the sentence underneath.

He had been reading their language off roses for a year, with no dictionary. Here was the dictionary. Seven volumes of it, bought with scone money, in a canvas bag at the foot of the camp bed.

He read Grade 1 that night, all of it, every spell, lying on his back with the book open in the air above his face and the pages going over as his eyes reached the bottom of each, and for each one he did the same thing: read the description, found the sentence under it, took it apart into the parts he had names for, and then — this was the part that kept him up till the boiler — compared. His bracket for warm against their bracket for warm. His mend against their mend, which they called Reparo and did in a way he found both clumsier and more general than his own, like a spanner against a key. Theirs were better, mostly. Not always. Theirs were polished, worn smooth by centuries of hands, and his were rough-cut from a kitchen floor, and where theirs were better it was the way a road is better than a path, and where his were better it was the way a path sometimes goes where the road can’t.

And in the margin of the thin grey book from 1911, where its author had written, in despair, the present writer regrets that observation of the casting moment is beyond any human faculty, Harry wrote, in pencil, in the round hand, small, the first margin of his own life:

Not quite. — H.P., October 1987

He slept two hours. It was, he thought in the morning, the best night since the pencil, and he had said that about several.

He wrote, too, that month, because the pencil did not need him to stop reading in order to go. The book for a reader who was slow — the one that would have had the boy at the back of the sports hall not at the back — took the October nights: eighty pages, every piece of school mathematics up to the papers he had sat, each one explained the way the men in brown jackets explained things, going back, saying in fact let’s do that again more carefully, and where one way did not go in, a second, and a third, and for seven eights a ninth. He gave it to Mrs Prentice on the last Thursday in October, in a folder, and said it was for the library, if she thought it was any good, and she read it at the returns desk over a fortnight with a pencil and found two places where he had gone too fast, and he fixed them, and she said, “It’s right,” and then, after a moment, “It wants printing,” and gave it to Carrow, who gave it to a woman at a publisher’s in London who had published Carrow’s own book in 1974 and owed her nothing and read it anyway.

He read the seven grades in the week, which by his own clock was most of four years, and he spent the four years the way a man who has just been given a dictionary for a language he has been speaking alone spends them: finding out how much he had got right.

A great deal, it turned out. More than he had expected and less than he had hoped. The spine of every spell in seven volumes was the spine he had read off the roses — the pointing, the pushing — and he had that cold. The brackets were where the real work lived, and there were a hundred and sixty of them in the Standard Books, and he had nine, and he spent most of a subjective year simply collecting, the way he had collected spines in the Survey, until he had all hundred and sixty taken apart and laid out in his head where he could see, at once, what a civilisation had thought it worth sanding smooth over a thousand years.

They had warm and cold and light and dark. They had move in forty flavours. They had mend and break and clean and lock and open. They had a whole chapter, in Grade 5, on making things not be noticed, and he read it with the hand on the back of his head from the Charing Cross Road fresh in his mind and understood, with a pleasure that had him grinning alone in a cupboard at two in the morning, exactly how it had been built and exactly why it had not been built for him.

And they had almost nothing on why. That was the strange thing, and he turned it over for a long time.
