Seven volumes, a hundred and sixty spells, each with its name and its movement and its three paragraphs of what to expect, and not one line in any of them on what the sentence underneath was made of. Waffling tried. Waffling had two hundred pages of trying and Harry read them in an evening and found a decent man groping along a wall in the dark with a very good vocabulary, and he put a Mr Prentice margin on every third page, and the margins were mostly surely not and twice yes and once, on a page about why incantations in Latin should work at all, he has nearly got it and doesn’t know.

They told it with a stick and a word, Mrs Figg had said. The book was the word. The stick, he began to think, reading Waffling’s chapter on wands three times, was something more interesting than a symbol. Waffling called it a focus and spent nine pages failing to say what he meant, but Harry had spent a year shouting sentences across a field with nothing but his hand, and he knew what it cost, and he read between Waffling’s lines a thing that Waffling could not have known he was saying: that the stick was a tool. A specific one. A way of sending the sentence so that it arrived with most of its strength instead of a tenth. He had been shouting. They had a telephone.

He could not buy one. He had asked Mrs Figg, in the café, and she had gone still and said that a wand was the one thing she would not, could not, and that children were not sold them before their letters for very good reasons that were on the far side of a line she had already crossed once that month. He had not pushed. He had four years. He kept telephone, underlined, where he kept things, and went back to the sentences.

He asked his aunt about his mother on a Wednesday in November, with the kitchen window shut against the dark and the smell of the last of the bonfires coming in under it, and Vernon at a conference in Birmingham, and Dudley at Piers Polkiss’s for the night, and Petunia at the sink with her sleeves pushed up, washing a single cup.

He had made the tea. That was new — he had never made her tea in seven years; it was not a thing the house permitted — and she had looked at the cup when he put it down beside her, and then at him, and said nothing, and drunk it.

He had the facts. He had had them a month, off a page, on a bench, with a hand to hold; he had the date and the place and the name nobody said and the careful words. What he did not have was her. The woman on the swing — he had not seen the swing yet; he did not know there was a swing — the sister, the girl who had made a flower open and close in her hand. The reference books did not have her. The Prophet did not have her. There was one person in the world who did, and he had been managing her like weather for seven years and had never once tried the handle.

“Aunt Petunia,” said Harry. “Will you tell me about my mother? I know what happened to her. I read it. In a book, in the street Mrs Figg took me to. I know who, and when, and where. I don’t know her. You’re the only one who does.”

The cup went still in the water.

He watched the side of her face in the window’s reflection and saw it do what it had done at the cooker when he was two, the white coming up — and then, for the first time in his life, saw it stop. Hold. And slowly, like a tide going out that had been coming in for twenty years, go back down.

“I wondered,” said Petunia, “when you’d ask.”

“I didn’t want to frighten you.”

“No.” She took her hands out of the water and dried them on the tea towel, one finger at a time, the way she did when she was deciding something. “No. You never have. You’ve been very—” She stopped. “You’ve been very careful of me, haven’t you. Since you were two. I did notice.”

He said nothing. It was the most she had ever said to him that was not an instruction.

“Sit down,” said Petunia.

She told him at the kitchen table with the bonfire smell coming under the window and the garden gone black outside, and she did not tell it well, because she had never told it to anyone, and some of it came out in the wrong order and some of it came out as weather, and he sat very still and let it all come, in the long quiet, and arranged it afterwards.

There had been two girls in a town up north that she did not name, and the younger had been able to make a flower open and close in her hand, and the older had been able to make nothing at all. That was where it started, and in a sense that was all of it, and she said so, flatly, looking at her own hands on the table: “That’s all it ever was. She could and I couldn’t. People think there’s more. There isn’t.”

There had been a boy — “an awful boy, from the bad end of town, with hair that wanted washing” — who had known what the younger girl was before she did, and had told her, and the older girl had hated him for it with a completeness she could still feel, thirty years on, in her teeth.

There had been a letter, when the younger girl was eleven, on thick yellowish paper, in green ink. Petunia said the name of the school as if it were a stone she had carried in her mouth for twenty-five years: “Hogwarts.” And then, lower: “She went. Of course she went. And I wrote to them. Did you know that? I never told anyone that, not your uncle, nobody. I wrote to the headmaster, eleven years old, and asked if I could come too.”

“What did he say?”

“He said no.” She was looking out at the black garden. “He was kind about it. That was the worst part. He wrote back to a child of eleven in his own hand and he was kind. I’ve still got it. Somewhere.”

She gave the headmaster’s name. He knew it — it was in Hogwarts: A History, and in every account of the war, always with the same line beside it: the only one he ever feared — but he had never heard it said aloud, and he sat very still as she said it, because it was the name of the man Mrs Figg wrote to, and the man had just become a person who had once written kindly to a girl of eleven and told her no.

She told him about the summers, when the younger girl came home with her trunk and her wand and her new words, and the older girl had sat in her room with the door shut and heard them laughing downstairs. About how the younger girl had tried. “She tried. I’ll say that for her. She kept trying till the end and I wouldn’t have it.” About a wedding she had not gone to, and a christening she had not been asked to, and a card with a photograph of a baby with its father’s hair, which she had put in a drawer and had looked at, she admitted to the table, perhaps twice a year since.

And then there had been a night at the end of October, six years ago, and a man in a cloak on the doorstep at midnight, and a basket.

“There was a letter,” said Petunia. “In with you. From him.” Her hands had gone flat on the table, pressing down. “It said she was dead. Both of them. It said how — I didn’t understand half of it and I didn’t want to, there was a man, a wizard, a bad one, and he’d — and you’d — ” She stopped. “It said you’d lived. It said nobody knew why. And it said—”

She stopped again, for longer, and he waited, and understood that he had come to the drawer itself, the one that had been nailed shut his whole life, and that she was standing in front of it deciding, and that nothing he could say would help, so he said nothing.

“It said that as long as you lived under my roof,” said Petunia, very low, “there was a thing he’d done — a protection — that would hold. That you’d be safe here in a way you couldn’t be anywhere else. Because of her. Because she was my sister and I’d taken you in. It didn’t say from what. It didn’t need to. It said as long as he calls this place home.” She looked at Harry for the first time since she had sat down. “That’s why I sat by that lake. Did you think I didn’t know what I was doing? Nine days on a bench with a magazine I couldn’t read. I know what the university is. I know what the trains are. You’ve been walking out of this house an inch at a time since you were five and I’ve let you, every inch, because God knows there’s little enough here to hold anyone. But you sleep under this roof. You sleep under it until the letter comes. I said I would. To a piece of paper, in the middle of the night, with you asleep in a basket on my step.”

The kitchen was very quiet.

Harry sat in it and found that he could not, for a moment, say anything at all — and that the moment, which was long for anyone and for him was a working afternoon, was not the long quiet he lived in. It was something else. A stillness he had no name for and had never been inside, and he sat in it and looked at the woman across the table who had made his life a low grey misery for seven years and who had been keeping a promise the entire time, badly, grudgingly, in a house that hated it, to a sister she had not spoken to in a decade, with nobody to tell.

He had thought he had read her. He had read the fear. He had read the drawer. He had never once read this, because it had never occurred to him that there was anything in his aunt he could respect, and he sat with that and let it be true and let it be his fault, and then he put it down, because there was a thing to do, and the thing was in front of him.

“Thank you,” he said. “For keeping it.”

Petunia made a small sound that was not a laugh and not the other thing.

“Don’t,” she said. “Don’t you thank me. I have not been—” She shut her mouth on it. Then, after a while, to the table: “I have not been what I should. I know that. I’ve known it the whole time, and I’ve done it anyway, and I couldn’t have told you why, except that every time I looked at you I saw her at eleven with that letter in her hand, going somewhere I couldn’t follow. And then you turned out clever.” It came out almost as an accusation, and then wasn’t. “Clever like her. Cleverer. Fifteen papers. In the newspaper. A university. And I thought, there it is, there it goes again, and I couldn’t — I didn’t know how to—” She stopped. “You made me tea.”

“Yes.”

“Nobody in this house has made me a cup of tea in seventeen years.”

“I know,” said Harry. “I should have done it sooner. I was being careful of you. I think I was being careful of the wrong thing.”

She looked at him across the kitchen table in the dark, with the bonfire smell and the black window, and he watched a thing happen on her face that he had waited seven years to see without ever once letting himself expect it, and it was not love — he was not a fool — but it was the thing that lives next door to love and lends it sugar.

“You look like him,” she said. “Your father. The hair. But you’ve got her eyes, and you look at people the way she did. As if you were working out how to help and hadn’t decided whether they deserved it.” She stood up. “Wait there.”

She came back with a shoebox.

It was a different shoebox from his — hers was for a pair of court shoes, 1972, from a shop that no longer existed — and she put it on the table and took the lid off and did not look inside, and said, “I kept some things. I don’t know why. Don’t ask me why.”

He didn’t. He looked.

A letter, on thick yellowish paper, in a hand that slanted and was kind, beginning Dear Miss Evans and dated the summer of 1971. Another letter, heavier, newer, folded small, which she took out and put face-down under her hand before he could read more than the first word, and the first word was Petunia, and the hand was the same, and he let it go. A christening card with a photograph of a baby with black hair standing straight up, and a woman holding him, laughing, with red hair down her back, and the baby’s eyes were green and so were hers.

And under that, at the bottom, gone soft at the corners from a quarter-century of being not looked at twice a year, a photograph of two girls on a swing in a park. The older one pushing. The younger with her head back and her hair flying, in the middle of a shout of laughter you could hear through the paper, and the older one — eight, perhaps, or nine; a year older than he was now — looking at her with an expression that Harry recognised at once, because he had seen it on his aunt’s face that evening across the table and had only now understood what it was.

She had loved her. Before the flower, before the boy, before the letter in green ink. There it was, in black and white, in a shoebox, on a swing.

“You can have that one,” said Petunia, not looking. “The swing. I’ve got the rest.”

He took it. He held it the way he had held two sheets of paper in a school hall in August, with both hands, as if carrying it for someone else.

“Aunt Petunia. The letter. The one from the step. May I—”

“No.” It was quick, and then softer. “No. Not yet. There are things in it about that night you don’t need, and things about the protection I don’t understand, and I think — I think he’d want to tell you himself. The headmaster. When they send for you.” She put the lid back on the box. “Ask him. Ask him why he left you with me. He had his reasons. I’ve had six years to think about whether they were good ones and I still don’t know, and I’d like to hear him say.”

“I will,” said Harry.

“And Harry.” She had never used his name as the start of a sentence either. It was a night for it. “When you go. When the letter comes and you go off up north with your trunk and your — all of it. Come back in the summers.” She said it to the shoebox. “Not for me. I shan’t pretend. For the roof. The paper said as long as he calls this place home, and I don’t know how long that’s meant to hold, and I’m not having it fall down for want of you sleeping here six weeks a year.” A pause. “And your uncle will want to tell the Rotary.”

It was so exactly her, and so exactly not, that he laughed — a real one, out loud, in that kitchen, for the first time in his life — and after a second, to the shoebox, so did she.

He lay under the stairs that night with the photograph of the swing propped against the wall beside the date, and he did not open the Standard Books, and he did not turn the television on, and he went back through the evening once, slowly, and then put it on the shelf where he kept the things that had made him who he was, in the place of honour, next to the straight fingers.

As long as he calls this place home. A protection, left on a doorstep with a baby and a warming charm, that depended on the roof of the one person in the world least willing to hold it up, and who had held it up anyway, for six years, with her jaw set, for a sister on a swing. He had felt the edge of it on the step some nights, going out for the milk, a faint hum in the stone like the street in the Alley turned down to nothing; he had thought it was the house. It was her. It had been her the whole time.

He had been so sure the Dursleys were weather. He had built his whole childhood on it. And weather had turned out, at a kitchen table in the dark, to have kept a promise.

And he had a new rule, and he said it, in a whisper, in the dark, under the swing, because the evening had earned a thing said:

“I don’t lie to Aunt Petunia. Not any more. She has kept a thing for six years that she did not have to keep. The least I owe her is the truth when she asks for it, and the truth when she doesn’t.”

He looked at the swing for a long time.

Three years and eight months. He was seven, and he had the facts off a page and now, at last, the person off a face, and seven volumes of a dictionary in a bag at the foot of the bed, and a photograph of his mother laughing, and an aunt who had made him laugh, and a hundred and sixty brackets taken apart in his head, and a protection on the roof above him that he now understood well enough to respect.

He was going to need every one of them.

“You made her tea, I suppose,” said Mrs Figg, in the café, on the Thursday, when he had told her — not all of it; some of it was Petunia’s and would stay so — but the shape.

“How did you—”

“Because that’s what you do, dear. You find the door they already use.” She poured, right-handed. “Your mother was the same. Everyone said.” She drank. “And I shall tell you something now that I shouldn’t, because you’ve had a week of being told things and one more won’t hurt. I’ve lived two streets from that woman for six years, Harry, and I’d not have put a knut on her. Not one. I owe her an apology I shan’t ever be able to make, because she’d take it as an insult, and she’d be right.” She set the cup down. “Keep the swing somewhere safe.”

“It’s on the wall,” said Harry. “Next to the date.”

“Good,” said Arabella Figg. “That’s about where it belongs.”

Contents

Chapter 11

On Holding

The second time Mrs Figg took him to Diagon Alley, in the week before Christmas, she had a list, and the list was in his handwriting, and it was two pages long, and she looked at it on the train and said, “Well, he can afford it,” in a voice that made the man opposite look up from his paper.

“Who can?”

“The man I write to. He paid the last bill without a word. Not a word, Harry — I had the galleons back by owl in four days and a note that said thank you for the itemisation and nothing else, and I have known that man forty years and I have never once known him pay for anything without a word.” She folded the list. “I think he wants to know what you’ll buy next. I think he is finding out what you are from my receipts.”

“Then I’ll make them interesting,” said Harry.

He made them interesting. He had read the seven grades and Waffling and Bagshot and the two histories in six weeks, which was most of twenty years, and he had come out the far end knowing exactly where the shelf stopped — the way he had known at five where the branch stopped and at six where the fourth floor stopped — and this time he had a map of the shop in his head from forty minutes of spines and knew which dusty singles were the ones that went past the edge. A Guide to Advanced Transfiguration. Ancient Runes Made Easy, which was not easy, which was the point. Magical Drafts and Potions, and the two fat books that followed it on the shelf. A thing called The Theory of Enchantment that the young woman with the inky fingers said nobody had asked for since she had worked there, and which Harry bought on that recommendation alone. A history of the Ministry. A volume of wizarding law — the actual law, the statutes, dry as the phone book and twice as useful — and a bound year of the Daily Prophet, 1981, which cost more than he had expected and which he bought anyway, with his hand flat on the cover, because it was the year, and because he had learned at five that a newspaper was the fastest way into a place.

Fifty-eight galleons. Mrs Figg wrote it down, itemised, on the train home, in the right hand, and said, “He’ll have a fit,” with every appearance of pleasure, and the fit, when it came, arrived by owl four days later in the form of fifty-eight galleons and a note that said thank you for the itemisation and, underneath, in a smaller hand, as if added after the ink had dried: The enchantment one is very good. Tell him to read chapter nine twice.

Harry read chapter nine twice. It was very good. He put a margin on it that said he knew I would need this, and underlined nothing, and lay awake till the boiler thinking about a man in Scotland who paid for books he was not supposed to know about and told the boy which chapter to read.

That winter he read the far side, and for the first time in his life he wrote it down.

Not all of it; even with two trips and a list he had perhaps forty books, and forty books was two afternoons at eye-speed and he knew it. But forty books chosen with a map, by a mind that had just spent twenty years on the hundred and sixty brackets of the Standard Spells, was not two afternoons’ worth of thinking, and he gave them the winter, under the stairs, after the university closed, with the swing on the wall and the house asleep and the pencil going.

The transfiguration book was the one that frightened him, in the good way, the way the fourth floor had. The Standard Spells had been sentences — this, so. Transfiguration was grammar: a hundred pages on how the bracket for what a thing is could be rewritten, from the inside, if you held the whole of the old thing and the whole of the new thing in your head at once and did not let either slip for the length of the casting. The book said this was very hard and that most students never managed more than a matchstick. Harry read it and thought: they cannot hold both for a quarter of a second. I have been holding three sentences at once since December. He did not try it. He had no telephone, and the book was very clear about what went wrong when the holding slipped, and he had promised Mrs Figg on a bench that he would be careful. But he composed the sentence — the whole of it, the old bracket and the new and the long patient becoming between them — and held it, and knew he could hold it, and kept it with the others he was saving for the day he had a wand.

The runes book he read in a night and understood in a week, and what he understood changed the shape of everything after it. Runes were sentences in matter. You did not hold them; you carved them, and the carving held them for you, for years, for centuries, the way the wall in the yard had held its sentence since whoever had built it died. The warmth on the doorstep had been a sentence. The hand on the back of his head had been a sentence. And here was a book that said, in effect, sentences can be written down somewhere other than a mind, and he sat under the stairs at two in the morning and saw, very clearly, a thing that nobody in the hundred and sixty brackets or Waffling’s two hundred pages had seemed to see: that if a sentence could be carved, a sentence could be made. Manufactured. Put in a thing and handed to someone who could not read it. The way a lecture on BBC2 put a thought in a box and handed it to a boy on a carpet at one in the morning.

He gave it a title, because Carrow had taught him that results should be named, and the title was Sentences in Things, and under the title: This is where the lever is. Not in the hand. In the carving. Find out everything about carving.

And it was the runes book, in the end, that made him write the grammar down, because the runes book was proof that a sentence could outlive the mind that held it, and he had a grammar in his head that nobody else alive could read, and if he fell under a bus it would go with him, and the next person to stand still inside a quarter-second and see a sentence go by would start, as the man in 1911 had, from nothing.

He had never written a word of it. The parts had names, and names were enough for a mind that did not forget. But a reader was not that mind. A reader needed marks, and English had no words for the parts, and he was not going to spend a thousand pages saying the pointing part and the pushing part. So on the ninth of January, 1988, in the cupboard, with the pencil going and the swing on the wall, he made marks: one for every part he had a name for, forty-one of them that first night, with the nearest English he could find beside each, and it was the first time the grammar had existed anywhere but behind his eyes. He called it On the Composition of Charms: A Second Edition, because the thin grey book from 1911 was a first edition, and its author had been a decent man who could not see down the road and had said so, and deserved the credit, and under the title he wrote: Begun from where E.B. stopped, by someone who can see the road, for whoever comes after and can’t. By the end of January it was two hundred pages, in the marks, with the English beside them, in the Edinburgh tin under the camp bed, and he had no idea who would ever read it, and he wrote it anyway, the way Carrow had said.

The law he read because he had read the phone book at three and knew what dry books were for. It told him the Ministry was a government with departments, and that there was a Decree about children and magic — the Trace, it was called, in a footnote; a thing that watched for magic near the underage and sent a letter, and that it had never once sent a letter to Little Whinging in six years, which he filed and thought about and concluded, provisionally, that a sentence asked for on a kitchen floor without a stick did not look, to whatever did the watching, like what it was watching for. He filed that under provisional and decided not to lean on it.

And the Prophet of 1981 he read last, in one night, from January to December, and he did it the way he had read the General’s waiting room at five — for the shape of the fear in the careful prose, the way a newspaper goes quiet around a thing — and found the fear, and found it going out, like a tide, through November, and found, in the issue of the third of November, himself. A paragraph. No photograph. The infant son of Lily and James Potter. And in the issue of the ninth, a letter to the editor from a witch in Kent asking where the boy was, and whether he was safe, and whether someone was looking after him, and an editorial reply, brief and careful, saying that the matter was in good hands.

He sat with that for a while. A woman in Kent had written to a newspaper to ask if he was all right. He had been in a cupboard, being small. He put the bound year on the shelf by the camp bed with the swing above it and the date beside it and thought that he would like, one day, to find the witch in Kent and tell her yes.

The feather held on the second of February, 1988, at a quarter to midnight, in the cupboard, and it was the first time his magic had ever looked like theirs.

He had been working towards it since the second trip. Grade 1, chapter four: the Levitation Charm, swish and flick, the single most-practised charm in the first-year curriculum, according to Waffling, who disapproved of it. He had had the sentence under it since October, cleaner in his own parts than the book described it — but he had always known it was a thing he could not do, because a thing sent without a telephone arrived at a tenth of its strength and a tenth of lift was not lift. The pencil had taken three weeks and an inch. A feather was lighter than a pencil. But the book did not want the feather lifted. It wanted it held.

And holding was the thing he had. He had found it with a pencil in December and he had been living on it since — three sentences at once, a page a minute, a book turning its own pages above his face — and it had never once occurred to him, until the runes, to ask whether he could hold one of theirs.

Because the book was full of the opposite. Waffling had a whole sour paragraph on first-years whose feathers shot into the ceiling or wandered off sideways; the grey book from 1911 had said flatly that imprecision in the casting moment is the universal experience and may be regarded as a law. The sentence went out of them ragged. It had to. They sent it in a quarter of a second they could not watch, with their eyes shut, and so it arrived with the bracket slightly wrong and the feather went where the wrongness pointed. They had a telephone and they shouted down it.

He had no telephone. But he could hold a sentence steady for the whole of a casting moment that nobody else alive could even see. If the sentence was perfect — not his version; theirs, every mark of it, the way they had sanded it — and if he held it, then there would be nothing in it to wander. The feather would go exactly as far as a tenth of a telephone could send it, and stop, and stay.

He spent January learning their sentence. Not to send it; to hold it, in the back of his mind, on the train, in the café, in the fourth-floor carrel, the way he held the pencil, until he could carry it through a conversation with Mrs Figg about Snowy’s ear without dropping a mark. Then he built the purpose, which for a feather took the same honesty it had for a pencil — so that I know whether I can — and on the second of February, with a feather from the pillow on the camp bed and his hand flat beside it and the house asleep, he held Wingardium Leviosa in his head the way he would have held a note, and sent it, and the feather rose.

An inch. Then two. And then — this was the thing, this was what he had been working six months towards without quite knowing it — it stopped. Not fell. Stopped, two inches above the blanket, and hung there, perfectly still, turning very slightly in the air from the cupboard door, and held, while he held the sentence, for four seconds, which for him was the better part of a quarter of an hour of looking at a feather hanging in the dark doing exactly, precisely, to the hair, what he had told it to.

He let it down. He was shaking, and not from the cost, though the cost was real and he slept till the boiler.

Because he understood, lying there, what he had just seen. Not that he could do it — he had known since the pencil that he could do something. That it had held. The book said the feather would rise and the student would struggle to control it. Waffling said they went into the ceiling. The grey book said it was a law. And his feather had gone two inches and stopped and turned in the draught and stayed, because he had held the sentence steady through a moment nobody else could see, and so there was nothing in it to wander.

They could not do that. He was sure of it now. Not the children in the book; not, he suspected, most of the adults; not the man in Scotland who read receipts. They had a telephone and could send a hundred times what he could — but they sent with their eyes shut, and the sentence went out ragged, and the feather shot into the ceiling. When he had a telephone, he would be sending with his eyes open.

He named it, because results were named, and the name was On Holding, and it went into the Edition that night as its third chapter, in the marks, with this in English under it for the reader:

This is what the two hundred is for. Not the maths. Not the fifteen papers. I have been reading sentences nobody else can see since I was six, and in three and a half years I will be handed a telephone, and I do not think anyone on the far side has any idea what happens when you give one of those to someone who can hold.

I intend to find out carefully.

The pot stand was Easter, and it was the first thing he ever made that had a sentence in it, and he made it for Mrs Figg because she was the one who had never once asked.

It had been coming since the runes. If a sentence could be carved and the carving would hold it, then the thing to find out was whether he could carve one — not with a wand, which set runes the way the book described, with a word and a tap; with holding. He had thought about it for most of a week of his own on the train and decided that it ought to work, because a rune was only a sentence that somebody had held steady long enough for matter to take the shape of it, and holding steady was the one thing he could do that nobody else could.

He needed something to cut with, and something to cut. The cutting tool he made on Haines’s lathe on a Saturday in March, from a length of silver steel Haines had in a drawer, while Haines told him about the clock — the pendulum clock was finished by then; it had been finished in February; it stood on the bench in the Stonewall physics lab in a case Haines had made and lost four seconds a week, which Haines said was better than the one in the staff room and Harry said was not yet good enough. Haines watched him grind the point and said, “That’s a graver. What do you want with a graver?”

“To cut letters in wood,” said Harry, which was true.

“What letters?”

“Ones I made up.”

Haines looked at him for a moment and went back to his tea.

The wood was an oak offcut from the same drawer, four inches across and an inch thick, and he took it home in his bag and sat with it on the camp bed for three nights composing the sentence before he cut a mark. It was his warming — the one from the doorstep, the one from the café pot, the one he knew better than any sentence in the world — with a bracket he had never built before: not this pot, now but whatever stands on me, as long as this wood is whole. A sentence with no now in it. A sentence that was meant to go on without him.

He cut it on the fourth night, with the door shut and the graver in his right hand and the sentence held open in his head the way he held the pencil, and he found that the graver was the pencil — that the hand knew already, from the mirror and the lathe and three hundred pages a month, how to be told something two hundred times a second and do it — and the marks went into the oak clean and small, forty of them, round the rim, and as the last one closed he felt the sentence leave him. Not sent. Not held. Set: taken out of his mind and put into a disc of wood, where it sat, running, very quietly, the way the roof ran, and did not need him any more.

He put the cold teapot on it. He waited. The teapot went warm.

He sat and looked at it for a long time by his own clock. A thing in the world was doing magic and he was not doing anything at all. He had made it, and it worked, and it would go on working for anyone who put a pot on it, whether they could read or not, and that — he had known it since the runes, and knowing it was not the same as holding a warm teapot in a cupboard at one in the morning — that was the lever.

He gave it to Mrs Figg on the Saturday, wrapped in brown paper, at the kitchen table, with Tibbles on the sugar bowl.

She unwrapped it. She looked at the marks. She looked at him.

“Put the pot on it,” said Harry.

She put the pot on it. She waited, with her hand on the lid, and the lid went warm under her hand, and she took the hand away and looked at it, and put it back.

“Harry,” she said. “What is this.”

“It’s the warming. The one from the café. In the wood, instead of in me. It’ll go on as long as the wood’s whole. I made the letters up; they’re not anybody’s runes. I thought — you sit in that café with a pot that goes cold by half past, and I warm it when I come down, and I’m not always going to be there to come down.”

Mrs Figg sat with her hand on a warm teapot in a kitchen that smelled of cabbage and looked at a boy of seven across the table and did not say anything for a while.

“My brother Edgar,” she said at last, “was a very good wizard. He could do things with a wand I never saw anyone else do. He could not have done this. I don’t know anyone who could have done this. People buy things with runes in them, dear, from shops, from men who spent twenty years learning the runes and have a wand to set them with, and they cost a great deal, and they do about what this does, and not one of them was made by a child at a kitchen table with a — what is that?”

“A graver. I made it. On a lathe. Mr Haines has a lathe.”

“A graver,” said Mrs Figg. She took her hand off the pot and put it over her mouth and he saw, above it, that her eyes had gone wet, and that she was not going to let them do anything about it. “I shall have to put that in a letter,” she said through her fingers. “I have been putting things in letters for seven years and this is the first time I have not known where to start.”

“Start with the teapot,” said Harry. “That’s what it’s for.”

The pot stand stayed on her kitchen table for the rest of the book. He saw it there every Saturday, with a pot on it, and she never once mentioned it again, and the biscuits were from the good tin every time.

The paper appeared in April, and it was eleven pages long, and it had been in print since 1865.

Not the paper. The journal. Carrow handed him the offprint in her office on a Thursday with the rain on the windows and the lake gone pewter, and he held it and read his own name in the font of a conversation that had been going on for a hundred and twenty-three years — H. Carrow and H. Potter, Royal Holloway and Bedford New College, University of London — and thought about Mr Prentice’s margins, and the reader in fifty years, and found that for once he was not thinking about the far side at all.

“The referees,” said Carrow, “did not believe it.”

“You said they wouldn’t.”

“They said so in writing. Politely. The first report said that while the proof appeared sound they could not help noticing that the second author was reported in the national press to be seven years old, and would the first author care to comment.” She took her glasses off. “I commented. I said the second author had found the overlap on the back of my door while I was looking at the front of it, and that if the referee wished to dispute the proof he was welcome to find the hole, and that if he wished to dispute the author he was welcome to Egham on a Thursday and could bring his own chalk.” She put the glasses back on. “He found no hole. He did not come to Egham. The second report was shorter. Ashworth’s is out in June and yours — the one alone — in September, and the referee on yours asked whether the author had a supervisor and I said yes, in the sense that I supervise the weather.”

Harry turned the offprint over. The back was blank. He had not expected it to be anything else and he looked at it anyway.

“It gets easier,” said Carrow, watching him. “The first one. You think it’s the thing. It isn’t the thing. It’s the start of the thing.” She took the offprint back and put it on the stack. “Now. Three things. The woman in London says yes to the book, and she says it in the form of a contract, which Pargeter has, and which says money, which Pargeter says goes where you said. It comes out in September. She wants a title that isn’t For a Reader Who Is Slow and I told her that was your business.” She held up a second finger. “There is a problem I have been stuck on since 1979 that I think you ought to see. And there is a man in the physics department called Lindqvist who has read our paper and the two hundred pages you wrote him in May and has been, I am told, impossible to live with since, and wants to know whether the second author would care to come and tell his third-years why their last problem sheet was wrong, because he had marked it and it was, and he could not for the life of him see where.”

“Where was it wrong?”

“I haven’t the faintest idea. It’s physics.” The knife-face did its small thing at one corner. “Tuesday. Two o’clock. He has a board.”

He went on the Tuesday. The problem sheet was wrong in the fourth question, in a way that was not wrong so much as careless — a limit taken in the wrong order, the kind of thing a man in a brown jacket at one in the morning would have stopped and said in fact let’s do that again more carefully — and he stood at the board in a room of nineteen-year-olds who had been told nothing and turned round in their chairs to look at the child in the school jumper, and he showed them, at the speed of his hand, which was fast now and he let it be only as fast as they could follow, and Dr Lindqvist sat in the back row with his arms folded and his eyebrows up a quarter of an inch, and at the end said, “Yes. Yes, damn it. Thank you,” and then, as the third-years filed out, to Harry, in a lower voice:

“The two hundred pages. I have read them four times. There is a thing on page ninety that I think is wrong and I cannot show it, and there is a thing on page one hundred and forty that I think is right and nobody has it, and I have been a physicist for twenty-two years and I have not had a month like the one I have just had.” He unfolded his arms. “The half of physics that is not in books needs a room. Equipment. Someone standing next to you saying no, not like that. I have a room. It has equipment in it that cost more than this building and a technician called Mrs Osei who has been saying not like that to undergraduates for twenty years and would, I suspect, enjoy saying it to someone who listened. You are seven. I am not supposed to let you anywhere near it.” He looked at the board. “Thursdays, after Carrow, if Carrow can spare you. Mrs Osei will stand next to you. You will touch nothing she has not said you may. Yes?”

“Yes,” said Harry. “And page ninety is wrong. I found it in March. I’ll bring you the page.”

He went on Thursdays. Mrs Osei said not like that to him eleven times in the first month and he kept all eleven and never needed any of them twice, and by June she had stopped saying it and started saying go on, then, and the other half of physics — the half that needed a room — began, at last, to be a thing he could read. He built a spectroscope in May from a prism and a slit and a length of tube, because Mrs Osei said the department’s was out for repair and would be for a year, and she looked at it and at the lines it threw on the wall and said, “Hm,” which from Mrs Osei was a paper in a journal, and it stayed on the bench, and the third-years used it.

Saturdays at the branch were Mrs Prentice’s idea, and he had been waiting for someone to have it since the sports hall.

She said it at the returns desk with the folder of the book in front of her, the week the contract came: that there were children in Little Whinging who were going to be the boy at the back, and that she knew which ones, because she knew which mothers came in on a Saturday morning to get out of the house, and that the children’s corner was empty from ten till twelve, and that if a person were to sit in it with a pencil and explain seven eights nine different ways until one of them went in, nobody at the council would need to be told.

“I can’t teach,” said Harry. “Not properly. I learned it all by myself and I don’t know how it looks from the outside.”

“You taught your cousin.”

He had not told her about the tennis ball. He looked at her, and she looked at the returns desk, and he understood that Mrs Figg and Mrs Prentice talked, on Thursdays, about more than castles.

“Ten o’clock,” said Mrs Prentice. “I shall put a notice up. I shan’t put your name on it.”

Two came, the first Saturday. A girl of nine called Rachel, who was Mrs Okafor’s daughter and had not been told that the boy from the sports hall would be there and went red to the hairline when she saw him, and sat down anyway; and a boy of ten called Lee who could do any sum you gave him in his head and could not read the question. He sat with them in the children’s corner on the small chairs with the pencil going on a pad between them, and he did not teach. He had learned that from Dudley. He found what Rachel already loved, which was horses, and put the fractions in a stable; and he found what Lee could already do, which was the sums, and made the reading a thing you did to get at them; and by twelve Rachel had done a page of fractions without once looking at him and Lee had read a whole question aloud, slowly, with his finger, and got it right, and looked at the page as if it had been hiding from him.

They came the next Saturday, and the one after. By June there were five. He never once said anything clever in that corner, in two hours, in a year, and it was the hardest discipline he had ever kept, and he found he looked forward to it more than Thursdays.

Dudley’s tables he taught in March, sideways, through a tennis ball, and the teaching taught him more than the sum did.

Year Three did tables tests on Friday mornings, out loud, down the rows, and Dudley — who could do things with a football that Harry privately rated above anything in the fifth-floor journals, but for whom seven eights sat in the mind like a wasp in a jar — had come home three Fridays running the colour of putty. There was to be a grand one at the end of term, up to twelves, in front of the class, and Dudley had got it into his head that he would be made to stand up and fail it out loud, and the dread had taken the stuffing out of him so thoroughly that he had gone off his food, which at Number Four was a national emergency.

Harry watched it build for a fortnight and found, to his mild surprise, that he minded.

Not strategy. There was nothing in it for him; a frightened Dudley was a quieter Dudley. He just did not like watching it. Dudley was not clever and never would be, and had spent years making Harry’s life a low grey misery over exactly that gap, and here he was all the same, eight years old, sick with fear over seven eights, and the fear was real, and Harry had two hundred times as long as anyone else to sit and feel how real it was. Knowing a thing is small doesn’t make it light to the one carrying it. He kept that.

So he fixed it, and the fixing was the lesson.

He could not teach Dudley. Teaching Dudley was like the tablet in the sardines — the moment the lesson showed its face, Dudley clamped shut and bolted. But Harry had spent his whole life getting into rooms by other doors, and he knew by now that Dudley did not learn through the front of his head at all. Dudley learned the way he learned goal nets and the batting order and the names of all forty-odd men who had ever played for his team — through the side, through chant and rhythm and not-noticing-you-were-doing-it.

Three weeks of small deniable sideways work. A football rhyme, invented and left lying about, that happened to carry the sixes in its bounce. The eights banged out on the stairs with a tennis ball, two steps to a line, which Harry started doing himself, pointedly, until Dudley took it up to prove he could do it louder. A long car journey to Marge’s turned, by Harry asking Vernon innocent questions about mileage, into forty minutes of Vernon doing sums aloud and getting them wrong and Dudley, delighted, correcting him. None of it looked like teaching. By the end Dudley thought he had simply stopped being frightened of his own accord, the way the weather clears, and that was exactly the shape Harry had built it to have, because a confidence you think you found yourself is worth ten that somebody handed you.

Dudley stood up at the end of term and got to twelve twelves with one wobble, and came home two feet taller than he’d left, and told the whole table that tables were easy, really, you just had to not be thick about it, and Vernon beamed, and Petunia did a steak-and-kidney to mark the occasion, and looked, once, across the table at Harry, with the face with the wall out of it, and said nothing, and he understood that she had seen every tennis ball.

Underneath the table, in the country of one, he turned the whole operation over and found it good, and found also the thing it had taught him, which was bigger than Dudley and bigger than tables and which he would spend the rest of his life proving out:

You almost never move a person by the front door. You find the door they already use. Dudley through football. Petunia through a cup of tea. Vernon through the Rotary. Rachel through horses and Lee through sums. Mrs Prentice through a held-open door, and Mrs Figg through a mended cat, and Carrow through a mistake on page nine. Everyone he had ever managed, he had managed sideways, through the thing they already loved, and it had never once failed, and the one time in his life he had gone through the front door unasked — behind the cooker, Aunt Petunia — he had frightened a woman into five years of cold.

The magic answered caring, and people answered the same way. It was the same law twice, he thought, lying under the stairs that night with the swing on the wall and a good dinner in him for once. You could not make either come by force. You went to where it already lived, and you asked properly, and you were patient, and then — cat’s hips, boy’s tables, warmth in a blanket on a cold step — it came.

He put it in the Edition, in English, under On Holding, because it belonged there. The front-door law. Same as the other. Hold the sentence; ask properly; it comes.

He turned eight on the last day of July, and nobody at Number Four marked it, which was the custom, but when he came back from Egham that evening there was a parcel on his camp bed in brown paper with no name on it, and inside was a blue fountain pen — a good one, old, with a lever — and a card that said only for the pads. E.P., and he sat on the bed and held it for a long time by his own clock, and then he used it, that night, on the thirty-first pad of mathematics for Carrow, because that was what it was for, and it was a good pen, and it did not know that the hand holding it was not doing the work.

Then he took stock, because Carrow had taught him that you took stock before you went on, and he did it in English, in his head, in the words he would one day say to somebody.

Eight. Well over a thousand of mine.

Read: the branch, the fourth and fifth floors, the journals to 1960 in maths and physics, all seven grades and forty of their books. The runes are the lever. The law has a footnote about a Trace and it has never once written to this house.

Written: twenty-three hundred pages since the pencil. Three papers out by September, one alone. Two hundred pages for Lindqvist, who has read them four times. The book, in September, with a title I have not chosen, and money from it going where Vernon cannot reach. Four hundred pages of the Edition, in the marks, in a tin, for a reader I have not met.

Made: a mirror, and Saturn in it. A clock that loses four seconds a week. A graver. A spectroscope the third-years use. A pot stand with a sentence in it, on a kitchen table two streets away, warm.

Can: hold. That is the whole of it and it is enough. A feather that stays. A hundred and sixty of their brackets and forty of my own. Six sentences sent cold, each costing like a hill; three held, costing nothing; one set, in oak, costing nothing ever again. Nothing for myself; the door faces out; I have stopped minding.

Have: a professor with a knife for a face and another with a room. A woman at a desk who says go on, then. A man with a lathe. Five children on Saturdays who do not know what I am and do not need to. A squib who spends scone money and a man in Scotland who pays the bills and tells me which chapter. An aunt who made me laugh. A swing.

Have not: a telephone. Three years.

He looked at that last line for a while. Then he turned to the inside of the cupboard door, where the date was pinned at the height of his eyes and the swing beside it, and under the date, under Faster, in the new blue pen, small, he added to the door — where things were said — what the year had taught him and what he would carry up the drive in three years like a lamp:

They shout. I can hold. Find out what that is worth.

Then he went down to the lounge in his socks, because it was ten past one, and a man in a brown jacket on BBC2 was about to explain thermodynamics to nobody, and Harry Potter, eight years old and the author of three papers in a journal older than his grandparents, sat on the carpet eighteen inches from the screen with the sound at two, and listened, and compared.

Contents

Chapter 12

What Is Owed

The two newspapers lay side by side on the camp bed on a Tuesday night in October, and he had put them there on purpose, and he looked at them for a long time before he let himself understand why.

The one on the left was the Daily Prophet, Monday’s, bought by Mrs Figg at the pub on the way to the third trip and read by him on the train home in eleven minutes. Page four, lower half, a piece about St Mungo’s Hospital for Magical Maladies and Injuries — he had known the name since the law book; he had not known the place, the wards, the Healers in lime-green — and a woman who had been brought in on the Friday with her spine broken in three places from a fall off a broom, and had walked out on the Sunday. Walked. The piece was not about her. It was about a dispute over the hospital’s funding, and she was the example, mentioned in passing, the way the time of the last bus is mentioned, of course.

The one on the right was the Telegraph, Vernon’s, Saturday’s, rescued from the bin. Page seven. An appeal, with a photograph, for a hospital in London that mended children, and a girl of six in the photograph with no hair, and three paragraphs about what the money would be for, and the word spine in the second paragraph, and the word months.

He had put them side by side because he had been not putting them side by side for a year, and he had caught himself at it, and the catching had the taste of the cupboard week.

Two hospitals. Eleven miles apart, roughly — he had worked out where St Mungo’s must be from a hint in the law book and a shop that had always seemed to be closed. One of them mended a spine in three places in a weekend and mentioned it in passing. The other put a girl’s photograph in the paper and asked the public for money and said months.

He had known about the curtain since he was three. He had read the law that kept it up — the Statute, the whole dry hundred pages of it, with its reasons given in the preamble in the careful prose of people who had been frightened once and had written it down so as not to forget. He had understood the reasons. Some of them, reading them under the stairs at seven, he had even agreed with. He had not, until a Tuesday night in October with two newspapers on a blanket, let himself put a face on the far side of them.

The girl in the photograph had a face. She had no hair and a brave smile that somebody had asked her for, and she was six, and eleven miles away there were people in lime-green who did spines in a weekend.

He lay down with the two papers still on the bed and did not sleep, and that was not unusual; but he did not go down to the television either, and that was.

He found the essay on the Thursday, on the fourth floor, in the philosophy, which he had been working through in order since the summer because he had come to the end of the mathematics journals he could follow without Carrow, and had decided that a person who was going to spend his life deciding what to mend ought to know what the people who had thought about that for a living had concluded.

It was in a collection bound in green, a thin thing by an Australian, written before Harry was born, and it began with a famine, and then it put a child in a pond.

He read it on the train. He read everything on the train. But a third of the way down the second page he did a thing he had never done with any piece of writing in his life: he stopped. Not because he didn’t understand it — the argument was short and clean and he had it whole in the time it took to read — but because it was not the kind of page you understood. It was the kind you answered. And he sat with the green book open on his knee and Egham going by in the dark and Mrs Figg beside him with a castle, and found that he did not have an answer, and that he had been living for seven years as if he did.

The argument was this, and he would never afterwards be able to make it sound as plain as it had been on the page. If you walked past a shallow pond and saw a child drowning in it, you would wade in and pull the child out, and you would do it even if it ruined your shoes, and anyone who did not would be a monster, and nobody disagreed about that. And the man then said, very quietly, as if sorry to have to mention it: distance makes no difference to the drowning. Nor does the number of other people walking past. Nor does the fact that the child is in a pond in a country you have never been to and the ruining of your shoes takes the form of a cheque. If you can save a life at a cost that is nothing like a life, and you don’t, the pond does not care how far away it was.

That was all. Four pages. Harry had read something over ten thousand books and none of them had ever made him feel as if he had been caught doing something.

He got off at Little Whinging and walked home with Mrs Figg beside him not asking, and the stone in his shoe from the two newspapers, and the whole of his life — seven years of it, every rule, every honest cost — laid out in front of him in the long quiet, and he went back along it looking for the place where he had asked the Australian’s question of himself, and could not find one.

He had asked how do I get out. He had asked how do I get more. He had asked, every night for seven years, what can I learn, what can I build, how do I get to the shelves — and all of it had been true and good and he did not regret a line. But there was a thing he had never asked, and it had taken a girl in a photograph and a thin green book to put its hand up, and the thing was:

You have two hundred years for every one. What do you owe for that?

The argument did not take long. It only looked long on the page, because the Australian had written it for people who needed persuading, and Harry did not need persuading. He needed to stop pretending he had not already been persuaded, and that took him the walk from the station to Privet Drive, with Mrs Figg beside him saying nothing.

The obligation first, and he could not get out from under it, and stopped trying before the end of Wisteria Walk. He had the speed. Wherever it had come from — the green light, the scar, a wheel spun on a doorstep — he had it, and nobody else did, and he had not earned it any more than Dudley had earned his appetite. Two hundred to one. More than a thousand years of thought that nobody else on earth had had time for. If that was not a man standing beside a pond in the best pair of shoes in the world, he did not know what the phrase could mean. The Australian had asked people to give up a cinema ticket. Harry had been given time itself, more of it than anyone in history, and had spent the great bulk of it on himself, and he had been right to, because a child must grow before he can carry, and he had been wrong to, because he had never once looked up from the growing to ask who was in the pond.

The objection came with it, because an argument that did not stand up to its own best objection was a feeling in a coat, and the objection was good: you are eight. Eight, with a trust he could not touch and a season ticket and a feather that held. Wade into which pond? He could straighten an old woman’s fingers. He could not straighten a famine. He could give the chess money to the people who put children on posters, and he would — he decided it at the corner of Magnolia Crescent and never went back on it — but the chess money was two hundred pounds, and the Australian had been very clear that the drowning did not care how good you felt about the cheque.

So the question was not what can I do today, which was a first-year’s question. It was where is the floor. Where, in the whole of what he could see, was the one place a boy with two hundred years for every one could put his weight and have the world move?

He knew before he reached the gate. He had known since a kitchen floor at Mrs Figg’s. He had known since two newspapers on a blanket. He had only never put it in the same sentence as the pond.

An old cat had got up and gone upstairs. An old woman’s fingers had come straight. A rose had gone green from the bottom up. He had done that with no teacher, no wand, no word, by minding, at six, on his knees — and eleven miles from a girl with no hair there was a building full of people who did spines in a weekend. Trained men and women. In lime-green. With a telephone each.

He did not know why. That went first, in capitals, because Carrow had been training him for two years to put the thing he did not know first: I DO NOT KNOW WHY. He had read the Statute. He knew the reasons in the preamble, the burnings and the fear, and some of them were good reasons, and he had agreed with them under the stairs at seven. He did not know what St Mungo’s could and could not do; a spine was not a famine; perhaps there was a floor under their mending as there was under his, and it was lower than two newspapers made it look. He would find out before he judged anyone, because he had been judged from a doorstep by people who thought they knew what he was, and he had not enjoyed it.

But he knew what he owed, and it was not a cheque.

Behind a curtain there was a whole science of mending, and a school that taught it, and a hospital that practised it, and he had been born into it with a gift for reading its sentences that nobody else alive seemed to have — Mrs Figg had said so, in a café: I have never seen a wizard do magic that way — and the letter was coming in two years and seven months. He was going to walk into that school. He was going to learn everything in it. He was going to find out what magic could mend and what it could not, and why, and if the why turned out to be a bad reason — comfort, or habit, or a fear three hundred years stale, and a girl of six in a newspaper for want of someone in lime-green minding about her properly — then he was going to spend the rest of two hundred years for every one pulling the curtain down.

That was the floor. He had it by the gate. He put it into English, standing on the step with his hand on the door, in the words he would one day say to somebody:

I owe the pond everything I have. What I have is time, and a gift for reading the sentences, and two years and seven months. The biggest lever I can reach is not maths, or money, or chess. It is the magic, and the world that knows how to use it, and the question of why that world has St Mungo’s and mine has a photograph on page seven. I am going to go and learn the answer. Then I am going to act on it.

Everything else is shoes.

The university closed for Christmas on the sixteenth, and Mrs Figg went to her sister-in-law’s in Deal for the week, and Vernon took the family to Marge’s, and Harry — by an arrangement that had taken Petunia one sentence to make and Vernon four seconds to accept, he’ll be better at home with his books than underfoot at Marge’s, and Mrs Next Door will look in — had the house.

He did not need the week to decide anything. He had decided on a pavement. What he needed the week for, it turned out, was a thing he had never once in his life had to do, and was bad at: sitting with a decision he did not like the size of.

He laid the two newspapers side by side on the kitchen table where the light was best and looked at them for most of a morning. He read the Statute again, all hundred pages, and the preamble twice, looking for the place where it said and the children on the other side may be left cold, and did not find it, and did not find the place where it said they might not. He went down to the television at one in the morning and found he was not listening. Mrs Next Door looked in on the Wednesday and found him doing the crossword at the table and said he was a good quiet boy, and he said thank you.

He was eight and a half, and he had just told himself what the rest of his life was for, in a kitchen in Surrey, with nobody to tell, and the house ticked, and that was the whole of the week.

He asked Mrs Figg on the first Saturday in January, in the garden, with the cats under blankets on the bench, because she had been to St Mungo’s and he had not, and because he had resolved on a pavement not to judge before he knew.

“Mrs Figg. The hospital. Yours. What can they do?”

She did not ask which hospital. She had stopped asking which anything.

“A great deal, dear.” She snipped a dead stem off something that had given up in November. “Bones in an afternoon. Burns. Most poisons. Things that would kill a Muggle in a week, they send you home with a potion and a leaflet.” Snip. “My brother Edgar broke his back off a roof in 1963 and was walking by the Wednesday.”

“And the other brother?”

The secateurs paused.

“Amos,” said Mrs Figg. “He had a thing in his blood. They didn’t have a name for it; your side does, I found out after, it was in the paper that very year, some professor in America. They tried. They tried for a year, and they were kind, and he died in a bed in St Mungo’s in 1972 and the Healer said to me, we can mend what is broken, Arabella; this was not broken; this was wrong.” She looked at the dead stem in her hand. “Your side had a word for it. Nobody on mine had ever heard the word.”

Harry stood very still in the January garden.

“They can’t do everything,” he said.

“Nobody can do everything. They can do things yours can’t and yours can do things they can’t, and the two of them have never once sat down at a table, as far as I know, and I have wondered why for sixteen years and never found anyone to ask.” She dropped the stem in the trug and looked at him sideways, with the gladness in it and something else, something that had been getting steadier all year. “I expect you’ll ask.”

“Yes,” said Harry. “I expect I will.”

He walked home the long way, past the recreation ground, in the cold, and the floor he had found on a pavement had a shape now that it had not had then. It was not that one side had the mending and the other had the photograph. It was that each side had half. A thing in the blood with a name on one side and no name on the other; a spine in a weekend on one side and months on the other; and a curtain between them three hundred years thick, and nobody — Mrs Figg, who had buried a brother, had wondered for sixteen years and found nobody to ask — nobody had ever sat the two halves down at a table.

He had read both sides. He was, as far as he could tell, the only person alive who had. Fifteen papers and a university on one; seven grades and forty books and a feather that held on the other. He was eight years old and he was, by an accident of a doorstep and a cupboard and a scone jar, the table.

He kept that, underlined, which he almost never did.

Each side has half. Nobody has sat them down. I have read both. I am the table.

He gave the money on the Monday, with Mrs Prentice, in her good coat, who had insisted on coming.

Not all of it. He had thought about all of it, and decided against, and made himself say why, because the green book had taught him that the deciding was where people lied to themselves and he was not going to. There was two hundred and thirty pounds in the toffee tin — the crossword had kept paying, and the chess had started to, four tournaments that autumn with envelopes at the end, the last of them against adults in Guildford who had not enjoyed it — and he was going to need some of it. Not for himself. For the far side; there were books still, and things that were not books, and Mrs Figg’s scone money was Mrs Figg’s and he had decided in October that he would not spend it again. That was not shoes. That was the lever. He kept half.

The other half he took to the Oxfam shop on the high street in an envelope, and a woman with a badge opened it and counted a hundred and fifteen pounds in notes and coins and looked at the eight-year-old in the school jumper and said, “Is this — are you sure, love?”

“Yes,” said Harry. “It’s for the hospital. The one in the paper, for children. Could you put it towards that, please?”

The woman looked at Mrs Prentice.

“He’s sure,” said Mrs Prentice.

They walked back to the library in the thin January sun with nothing said for most of the way, which was how the two of them did their best talking, and at the door she stopped and looked down at him over the glasses.

“Half,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Not all.”

“No. I need the rest for something. I know what.” He hesitated, and then, because it was her: “I read a book that said you should give until it costs you something that matters. I think it was right. I think it was also written for people who’ve already got their shoes. I haven’t got mine yet. I need them to get to the pond at all.”

Mrs Prentice considered him for a long moment in the doorway, with the smell of polish coming out past her.

“My husband,” she said, “used to say that the trouble with saints was they were no use to anyone till they’d finished being saints.” She pushed the door open. “Half is right, dear. For now. Come and do the crossword. There’s a nine in the bottom corner that wants to be obligation and isn’t.”

The book had come out in September, with a title the woman in London had chosen — Working It Out — and a line on the cover that said by H. Potter, aged seven, which he had argued against and lost, and it had sold, by Christmas, eleven thousand copies, mostly to the parents of children at the back of sports halls, and the advance and the first of the money had gone where he had said, into the trust, where Vernon could see it on a statement and not touch it. He wrote to Mr Pargeter in the first week of January, on one side of one sheet, and asked whether the trustees would deem two thousand pounds to a children’s hospital in London conducive to the beneficiary’s education, and Mr Pargeter wrote back on the firm’s paper that the trustees had considered the matter and concluded that it was conducive to somebody’s, and had sent it, and that the receipt was enclosed. It was the first money he had ever moved that was large enough to be a thing — a bed, a nurse for a month; he had asked — and he found that it did not feel like the hundred and fifteen pounds had felt, and he sat with that on the train and decided that the feeling was not the point and the bed was.

He asked Carrow on the Thursday, because he had been wanting to ask someone who wasn’t eight and wasn’t kind, and she was the only person he knew who qualified on both counts.

“Professor. What’s mathematics for?”

She did not look up from pad thirty-one, which she was reading with the pencil, and he had learned to wait.

“For itself, mostly,” she said at last. “Which is a perfectly respectable thing for a thing to be for; I shan’t have it apologised for. Occasionally it turns out to be for bridges, two hundred years later, by accident, and everyone acts very surprised.” She turned a page. “Why?”

“I read a book. About a child in a pond.”

“Ah.” She did look up then. “The Australian. Yes. I read it at your — well. At twenty. It gave me a bad month.” She took the glasses off. “And now you want to know whether a person is allowed to sit in a room doing group theory while the child drowns.”

“Yes.”

“Then I shall tell you what I decided, which you may take or leave. I decided that I was no earthly use to the child. I can’t swim, I faint at blood, and I was already far better at this than at anything else I was ever going to be good at. So I resolved to be very good at this, and to give what I could spare, and to make sure the people I taught understood what they were for, which is more than my own teachers managed.” She put the glasses back on. “And I resolved not to pretend that was the same as wading in. It isn’t. It’s what I had. You put your weight where you’ve got weight, Potter. The man in the pond doesn’t need a geometer. He needs the person who happens to be standing there and happens to be able to swim.” A pause, and the knife-face did its small thing at one corner. “If you ever find you’re standing there and you can swim, you won’t need a book to tell you what to do. You’ll already be wet.”

He thought about a kitchen floor, and a pair of hands, and a hospital eleven miles from a photograph, and a woman in a garden who had buried a brother and found nobody to ask.

“Yes,” he said. “I think I might already be.”

Carrow looked at him for a moment longer than the remark warranted, and then decided, as she always did, to stand a little to one side of the door.

“Pad thirty-one,” she said. “Page sixty. You’ve dropped a sign.”

He made the List on the last night of the Christmas week, before the family came back from Marge’s and the house filled up again, at the kitchen table, in English, in his head, where it would be kept exactly, and he gave it a plain title because the plain ones lasted, and the title was Things Worth a Life.

He did not make it fast. He said each line to himself, and sat with it, and some of them he took back and said again, because he was making it for whoever he would one day hand it to, and for himself at fifty, and he wanted both of them to be able to see what he had meant at eight.

1. Learn what the thing is. Properly, all the way down. Not what it does — what it IS. Nobody on the far side has done this, or Mrs F would not say what she says and the grey book would not be thin. I can read the sentences. Read all of them. Then carve them.

2. Find out what each side can mend and what it can’t, and why, and sit them down at a table. Amos Figg had a thing in his blood that my side had a name for and his side had never heard of. A girl on page seven has a spine that his side does in a weekend. Nobody has ever put the two in one room. Be the room.

3. Death. (He said the word, in the kitchen, out loud, and sat with it for a very long time, and did not take it back.) Not for me. For Amos. For the girl. If the thing can mend a hip it can mend more than a hip, and if there is a floor I want to see it with my own eyes before I believe in it.

4. Pain. Mrs F’s hands for forty years. Edgar’s back off a roof. How much of that is there in the world, on both sides, that nobody is minding about?

5. Waste. People in cupboards. Not me — I was lucky; I had the cupboard and the time. The ones who have the cupboard and don’t have the time. Somewhere there is a child at a kitchen table that nobody can see. Be the one who sees.

6. The curtain. Find out why it is there. Read the preamble again. Then decide.

7. 141. (The page in the blue book about even numbers and two primes, which he had carried since he was six; he said the number and left it.) Not a duty. Not everything on a list of what a life is worth has to be something you owe. Some of it is why you’d want the life.

He went over it twice. Then at the bottom, smaller, because it was not a thing worth a life but a thing he wanted himself at fifty to know he had understood at eight, the last line.

The door faces outward. I could not mend my own eyes. I used to think that was a limit. I think now it’s the design. Whatever this is, it was never meant for the one holding it. That’s the right way round. Keep it that way.

The house ticked. Somewhere past the curtain there were people in lime-green who did spines in a weekend, and a bed where a man had died of a thing with a name his Healer had never heard; and on this side there was a photograph on page seven, and a woman in Kent who had written to a newspaper to ask whether a baby was safe; and a letter coming in two years and seven months; and a telephone at the end of it.

He turned the light off. He did not go down to the television. He lay under the stairs, eight and a half, well over a thousand, with the List where he kept it and the swing on the wall and the date beside it, and for the first time since the doorstep he was not thinking about what he could learn.

He was thinking about who was in the pond, and how many, and how fast.

Contents

Chapter 13

Finals

Mrs Osei said not like that for the last time on a Thursday in February, and then she said nothing for a long moment, and then she said, “Go on, then,” and stepped back from the bench, and that was how he knew.

It was a small thing. The whole of physics, he had come to understand in a year of Thursdays in Dr Lindqvist’s room, was built out of small things done carefully by people who were not in a hurry, and this was one of them: a measurement, of a quantity that everyone knew to three decimal places and nobody had bothered to take to four, because the fourth did not matter to anything anyone was building. It mattered to Harry because he had been reading the journals back to 1960 and had found, in a paper from 1974, a footnote in which a man had wondered whether the fourth place might not be quite what the third predicted, and had not had the equipment, and had moved on. Nobody had gone back. The equipment was in Dr Lindqvist’s room. Mrs Osei knew how to work it. He had asked her, in November, whether a thing could be set up, and she had looked at him for a long time and said, “Show me the paper.”

It took four months, Thursdays. It took him learning to do with his hands what he could already do in his head, which turned out to be a different thing entirely and a humbling one, and it took Mrs Osei saying not like that in eleven different tones of voice and once, memorably, not like that, Harry, unless you want to be the first eight-year-old in this department to lose an eyebrow. It took the man from 1974 being, in the end, right: the fourth place was not quite what the third predicted, by an amount too small to matter to anything anyone was building and too large to be nothing.

“Well,” said Dr Lindqvist, when he had looked at the numbers three times with his arms folded. “Well, damn it. Somebody should have done that fifteen years ago.”

“Somebody did,” said Harry. “He didn’t have the room.”

The paper was two pages. It went to a journal that printed short things quickly, and it had three names on it — Lindqvist, Osei, Potter, alphabetical, which Carrow approved of — and when it came back accepted in May, Mrs Osei pinned the acceptance letter to the noticeboard in the lab with her name on it in print for the first time in twenty years of standing next to undergraduates, and looked at it for a while with her hands in the pockets of her coat, and said, not to Harry, “My mother would have liked that,” and went to make the tea.

He put that on the shelf. The shelf was very full.

The finals were Carrow’s idea, and Gerald Prentice’s objection, and the Dean’s problem, and Harry stayed out of all three until he was asked.

“He has read the course,” said Carrow, at a meeting in a room with a long table and a view of trees, in March. “Not done it. Read it. The whole of the undergraduate mathematics, the whole of the physics, and I am told by Lindqvist that he has now also read the half of physics that lives in a room. He has a paper in a journal that most of our staff could not get into and a second one coming. He is going to be nine in July.” She did not look at the Dean. “I want him to sit finals. Not for the degree. For the paper. Because in two years he is going somewhere he will not tell me about, and I should like him to leave here with a piece of paper that says what he is in a form that nobody can argue with, including whoever is waiting for him at the other end.”

“The regulations—” said Gerald Prentice.

“The regulations say any registered student. Edith tells me there is a precedent for any person, at the examination boards, and that a child of six established it by reading the regulations upside down across a table.” Carrow’s knife-face did not move. “Register him. Backdate it. Call him whatever the form requires. I will set the papers myself and mark them blind and the external will mark them again, and if he fails them I shall eat the form.”

The Dean looked at Harry, who had been sitting at the far end of the table for twenty minutes with his hands in his lap.

“Mr Potter,” said the Dean. “Do you want this?”

He had thought about it since Carrow had raised it, which was most of a month. “Yes,” he said. “Not for the degree, Professor Carrow’s right about that. For what she said. I’m going somewhere in two years where nobody has ever heard of a GCSE, and I’d like to arrive with one thing in my hand that they have heard of, even if they don’t know what it means.” He paused. “And because I’ve read the course, and I’d like to know whether I read it right. Professor Carrow has found eleven mistakes in my pads in two years. I’d like to know if there are more I haven’t caught.”

The Dean looked at him for a while.

“Register him,” he said to Gerald Prentice. “Backdate it. And Gerald — if anyone from the Senate asks, send them to me, and I shall send them to Professor Carrow, and I think we may rely on the Senate not asking twice.”
