Chapter 15

The Grammar

The book about Azkaban was forty pages long and had been printed in 1931 and smelled of a shelf nobody had dusted, and he read it on a Tuesday night in September under the stairs by the light of a disc of oak with a sentence in it, and when he had finished it he put his hand over the disc and sat in the dark for a long time without doing anything at all, which he almost never did.

It was not a good book. The man who had written it had been a Ministry clerk with a grievance and a prose style like a wet dog, and most of the forty pages were about the prison’s administration. But in the middle, for six pages, he described the guards, and he described them the way a frightened man describes a thing he has seen once and cannot stop seeing, and the description went into Harry like the doorstep cold.

They were not people. They were not animals. They were — the clerk did not have the word, and groped for it for a page and a half — shapes. Tall, hooded, drifting. And where they went, warmth went out of the world: not heat, the clerk was careful about that, the air was no colder, but warmth, the other thing, the thing that made you want to get up in the morning. They fed on it. That was the word the clerk used, and he used it nine times, as if he could not get it out of his mouth. They fed. They stood at the doors of the cells and fed on whatever it was in a person that was glad, until there was nothing left but the memory of having been, and the prisoners went quiet, and then went mad, and the Ministry called it security.

Harry sat in the dark with the book on his knees and did not think about the prisoners first. He would, later, and for a long time; the List would get a line that night and the line would stay. But what he thought about first, because he could not help it, because it was the way his mind went, was the shape.

A thing that fed on warmth. On the thing in a person that was glad. On — he turned it over, carefully, the way he turned anything with edges — on exactly the thing that had come up through him on a kitchen floor at six and gone looking for a cat’s hips. The clerk had no word for what the guards ate. Harry had a word. He had had it since a bus stop. It answered caring; it was made, somehow, of the stuff of minding; and here was a creature that stood at a door and ate that, and left a person with nothing to mind with.

If it ate sentences, it was made of them.

He kept that as a question, because he had nothing yet to make it an answer: a sentence that eats sentences. Self-holding — nobody has to cast it; it casts itself, over and over, and the casting is the eating. If that is what it is, then it has a grammar, and if it has a grammar it has a part that could be made to stop. And under it, in English, for whoever he would one day say it to:

There is a prison in the sea where people are kept with these at the door. The Ministry calls it security. I have read the law and the law permits it. I do not know yet whether the men in that prison deserve to be there and I do not need to know, because nobody deserves that. The List has a line for pain. This is a different kind. This is the cold, and somebody built a building round it.

He took his hand off the disc and read the six pages again, slowly, the way he had read the General’s waiting room at five, for the shape of what the clerk was not saying, and found it, and kept that too, and did not sleep, and for once did not go down to the television either.

The List got its line in the morning. 8. The cold. Then, after a while, smaller, because it was not a thing worth a life, it was a thing he had noticed and wanted to remember noticing: Nobody has tried to read one. Everybody has tried to keep them off. Different question.

He mapped the roof in October, on four nights, in his socks, with the milk as an excuse.

He had felt the edge of it since he was six — a hum in the step some nights, going out early, like the street in the Alley turned down to nothing — and he had thought it was the house, and then Petunia had told him it was her, and then he had bought two monographs on the structure of wards at eight and read them in an evening and understood what he had been standing on. A ward was a sentence in a place. Not carved, like runes; anchored, to something that would hold it, the way the wall in the yard was anchored to the bricks. And this one was anchored, the monographs said in their dry way, to blood and residence: to a living person who shared the blood of the sacrifice, and to a roof that the protected person called home. Both or neither. The sentence held as long as both held, and when either went the sentence went, and that was why Petunia had sat by a lake for nine days, and why she had said come back in the summers to a shoebox.

He wanted to see it. Not feel it; see, the way he had seen the wall say its sentence. So he went out for the milk at six in the morning four days running in October, and stood on the step with the bottles in his hands and the frost on the path and listened, and on the fourth morning, with the fear-gear not available because nothing was frightening and the calm he had learned at the roses in its place, he stood far enough back inside himself to watch, and it was there.

It was enormous. He had expected a sentence; this was a book. It ran round the whole of Number Four, through the walls and under the floor and up through the roof-tiles into the grey, and it was old in the way the wall in the yard was old, and it was written — he stood on the step with the milk going cold in his hands and read it, a mark at a time, for a quarter of a second that was most of an hour — it was written in the same hand as the doorstep sentence. The same listening. The same bracket that checked and gave exactly the difference. Except that what this one checked for was not cold. It checked for intent. It stood at the edge of the garden and looked at everything that approached and asked, of each thing, a question Harry could not quite read — the bracket was too deep; it went down into something about blood that he had no marks for yet — and if the answer was wrong, the thing did not come in. Could not. Would find, he suspected, that it had been looking at the record shop.

And at the heart of it, where the whole book was anchored, was a shape he knew, because he had seen it on a swing.

He stood on the step a long time, that fourth morning. Then he carried the milk in, and put it on the table, and found his aunt at the sink, and said:

“Aunt Petunia. The protection. The one in the letter. I’ve just read it. It’s on the whole house. It’s — it’s very good. It’s the best thing I’ve ever seen anyone write.” He hesitated. “And it’s anchored to you. Not to the house. To you. If you weren’t here it wouldn’t be either.”

Petunia did not turn round. But her hands went still in the water, and after a moment she said, to the window, in a voice he had not heard before:

“I know. The letter said. As long as she lives and he calls it home.” A pause. “I didn’t tell you that part.”

“No.”

“I didn’t want you to think you had to be — careful of me. Again. For that.” She took her hands out of the water. “I’m not going anywhere, Harry. I’m fifty-one. I’ve no intention of making a liar of a piece of paper.”

He did not know what to say to that, and said, “Thank you,” and she said, “Lay the table,” and he laid it, and that was all, and it was the whole of a morning’s reading made into a sentence of its own.

He took the ward apart that night, every part of it he could reach, and gave it a name — the roof — and filed it beside the doorstep sentence, with this under it: Same hand. Same listening. He can still do it, or he could in 1981. The bracket at the heart of it is about blood and I cannot read it yet. Learn to.

And then, because the monographs had told him how a ward was anchored and he had just read one, he made a small one of his own, that week, on the inside of the cupboard door, under the date: a sentence of thirty marks cut into the paint with the graver, anchored to the door and to him, that did nothing but notice. Anyone who came down the hall towards the cupboard, it told him — a touch at the back of the mind, like a hand on the shoulder, three seconds before the footsteps. It was the roof turned down to nothing and pointed at a hall. He had not needed it in seven years. He made it because he could, and because a ward was the one thing in the two monographs he had not yet done with his hands, and because the next time he read the roof from the step he wanted to have built one, so that he would know which parts of the book were hard.

He finished the Second Edition on the twentieth of December, 1989, and it was nine hundred and forty pages, and nobody alive could read it.

He had been writing it for two years, since the ninth of January 1988, when the runes had made him see that a sentence could outlive the mind that held it. It had gone into the Edinburgh tin a hundred pages at a time, in the marks, with the nearest English beside each mark, and then into a second tin, and then into a box file from Carrow’s cupboard, and it was finished in the way a grammar is finished, which is that it had everything in it he knew and a chapter at the end for the things he did not.

The marks, and what each one was for: two hundred and twenty by the end, where there had been forty-one on the first night. The spine. The brackets, all of them — a hundred and sixty of theirs from the Standard Books and the shelf behind the shop, and sixty of his own — each with its name in their language and his, and what it did, and what it cost, and how it had been sanded: where theirs was a road and his was a path, where the road went wrong, where the path went somewhere the road could not. The chapter on holding. The chapter on carving, with the pot stand in it, and the light, and the ward on the door. The doorstep sentence, taken apart, and the roof, as far down as he could read it, with the blood-bracket left blank and marked cannot read yet. And the last chapter, which was a list of questions, and the first question was what are they, before wands? and the last was the cold.

He did not show it to anyone. There was nobody who could read it. Mrs Figg had seen the tins and had not asked, and Carrow had seen the box file go out of her cupboard and had not asked, and he had stopped minding that some years ago, mostly, because he had decided at eight that a reader in fifty years was a reader, and the book was for them.

And in the back of it, in a section of its own, were the sentences.

Not theirs. His. Built from the grammar, the way Carrow had taught him a result was built — from what you knew, towards what you wanted, with every step shown. Two hundred of them by December, composed on the train and in the carrel and in the long quiet, and most of them bad, and forty of them not, and of the forty, thirty he could send — by hand, without a telephone, at a tenth, each one costing like a hill, which meant perhaps one a night and sleep till the boiler. He had rationed them the way he had rationed the toffee tin. One a night, for a year, was three hundred castings, and he had learned something from every one, and the chief thing he had learned was which of his sentences were worth a telephone and which were only clever.

The one he was proudest of was a mending sentence that did not need to be told what was wrong.

Every mending in the Standard Books was a this. Reparo, for a thing that was broken; a dozen others, for a dozen specific wrongs, each with its own bracket and its own name. The Healing text was four hundred pages of specific wrongs with specific sentences for each, and a table on page ninety-one with its columns transposed. And the thing that had come up through him on a kitchen floor at six had not been told what was wrong with a cat’s hips. It had gone looking. He had seen it go. He had had a name for the looking part since he was six and had never once seen it in any of theirs.

So he made a sentence that was only the looking part. No this. A bracket that went into a thing and asked, of every part of it, are you the shape you are for? — and came back, not with a mending, but with a map. Where the wrong was. What kind. How deep. The way Mrs Osei’s instruments came back with a number and left it to you what the number meant.

It took him three weeks to compose it true. And then, on a Saturday in November, on Mrs Figg’s kitchen floor, with his hand in the fur over Tibbles’ haunch and Mrs Figg at the sink not watching, he sent it — and it went. Not at a tenth. Whole. A looking did not need strength; it needed only to be let in, and the old cat let it in, and it came back into Harry’s mind as a map, and the map said: hips, both, worn, the left worse; a tooth at the back, gone bad, that nobody knew about; nothing else. He sat on the floor with the map in his head and the cat looking at him with the lecture-hall face and understood that he had just made the first sentence of his own that worked without a telephone, and that it worked because it asked instead of pushed, and that of all the things in nine hundred pages this was the one.

“Mrs Figg,” he said. “Tibbles has a bad tooth. At the back, on the right. He’s not said.”

She turned round from the sink.

“He wouldn’t,” she said, after a moment. “He never does.” And then: “How do you—” and stopped, because she had decided at a café table at six that she would not ask, and she looked at the cat, and at the boy on the floor, and said, “I’ll take him Monday,” and went back to the sink, and the vet found the tooth on the Monday and took it out and said it had been bad for a year.

He called the sentence the asking, because that was what it was, and under it in the Edition he wrote, in English: A Healer with this would not need page ninety-one. Nobody has it because nobody can see the looking part, because the looking part is the part that happens before the shouting starts. I can see it. I can send it, now, without a telephone, because a question costs nothing. I have used it on three cats and a librarian’s wrist and my aunt’s knee, each with permission, and it has not once been wrong. When I have a telephone I am going to find out whether it can be taught, and if it can, I am going to teach it to a hospital.

The light had been easier, and he had made it first, in September, because the torch batteries were a cost and a disc of oak with the Wand-Lighting Charm cut into its face was not. It sat on the shelf by the camp bed and gave a steady white light when a hand was on it and none when there was not, and it would do so, he thought, for as long as the oak was whole, for anyone, and he had made a second one and given it to Mrs Prentice for the returns cupboard, where the bulb had gone in 1986 and the council had never replaced it, and she had put it on the shelf and switched it on with her hand and said “Hm,” and used it every day after, and never asked where the switch was.

Three and Thirteen took three nights in October and came out in February, and it was the only thing he had ever written that was for people who were slow on purpose, and Mrs Prentice read it first, which was the point.

He had had it in his head since the sports hall. The first book had been for the boy at the back; this was for the child at the kitchen table that nobody could see — the one on the List, under Waste — and children of six did not want stepping stones. They wanted someone to walk beside them and point. It was eighty pages, with the pencil going and the swing on the wall. It was called Three and Thirteen, because that was what sixteen was, and because he had sat under a staircase at six with a blue book about even numbers and had nobody to tell. It began with conkers and ended with a mountain nobody had climbed, and in between it did the thing the men in brown jackets had done for him at one in the morning for four years: it went a different way, and said so, and said in fact let’s do that again more carefully, and hesitated before the hard steps so that you knew where they were.

Carrow read the draft and said, “This is not for me,” and then, after a page, “No. This is not for me at all,” and then read the whole of it without stopping and put it down and said, “Give it to Edith first. If Edith says it’s right, it’s right.”

Mrs Prentice read it on a Thursday, after four, at the returns desk, with the stamp beside her and the oak light on the shelf behind her, and she read it the way she read everything, which was all the way through without looking up, and when she had finished she sat for a while with her hands on the cover.

“My husband,” she said, “would have liked this.”

“I wrote it for him. Partly. For the margins.”

“I know you did, dear. It’s got his enough for one night in it, on page forty, you’ve put it in a boy’s mouth.” She took her glasses off and polished them on her cardigan and put them back on, which was how she signed things. “It’s right. Every word. It’s the first book I have read since 1979 that I should like to put in a child’s hand and not watch them read it, because I shouldn’t need to.” She pushed it back across the desk. “Tell Helen’s woman to print a great many. And Harry — the money.”

“The hospital. The children’s one. All of it. Mr Pargeter’s drawing it up so it goes straight there and never touches the trust.” He paused. “The first book paid for a bed. This one isn’t shoes either.”

“No,” said Mrs Prentice. “No, I shouldn’t think it is.” And then, in the filing voice, going back to the stamp: “There’s a nine in the bottom corner. It wants to be generosity and it isn’t.”

The press printed a great many. Three and Thirteen, by Harry Potter, aged nine, with a two-line foreword by Professor H. Carrow that said I did not help with this book and I should like that known, went into the shops in the second week of February and was in its third printing by Easter, and the first cheque to the children’s hospital was for a sum that made Mr Pargeter’s bowling-green voice go, for one syllable, slightly uneven. There was a photograph in the Telegraph of a girl of seven reading it on a bench, and she was frowning at page forty, and he cut that one out and kept it, because it was the second time a newspaper had got the point.

Rachel Okafor, who was eleven now and had done a page of fractions in a stable two years before, read it on a Saturday morning in the children’s corner with the other thirteen round her, and looked up at the end and said, “It’s us. The kitchen table. You put us in,” and he said yes, and she said, “Good,” and went back to the beginning.

The branch library was to be closed in April, and he found out from the Advertiser on a Thursday, and by the following Thursday it was not, and he never told Mrs Prentice how, and she never asked, and both of them knew.

It was the council. There was a list, in the paper, of eleven branches in the county that were under review, which Harry had read enough council prose to translate, and Little Whinging was the fourth, and the paragraph beside it said low footfall, and he sat in the café at Egham with the Advertiser flat on the table and Mrs Figg watching him over her castle, and read the paragraph four times, and felt the thing come up in him that had come up over the newspaper with the girl in the gown — slow and hot, and answering nobody.

“Dear,” said Mrs Figg.

“They’re going to close it.”

“I saw.”

“She’s held that door for me since I was five. She bought the pads. She rang Gerald. She moved the Hilbert to the bottom shelf. There are fourteen children in that corner on a Saturday.” He put the paper down. “Low footfall. I’ve been in that building two hundred days a year for four years and they’re counting footfall.”

Mrs Figg did not say anything. She had learned, over four years, when he was thinking and when he only looked as if he were, and this was the first kind, and she turned a page of her castle and waited.

He thought about the front door. The council had a front door — a meeting, a petition, a form — and he could see exactly how it would go, because he had read four years of council prose and knew the shape of a thing that had already been decided. He thought about Vernon, and the Rotary, and rejected it, because Vernon’s esteem would not stretch to a library. He thought about Carrow and rejected that too, because a professor writing to a council about a branch library in Surrey was a thing that would be filed and forgotten.

And then he thought about the newspaper, and what it had wanted from him for three years, and what he had never once let it have.

“Mrs Figg,” he said. “I’m going to have to be in the paper. On purpose. Just once. Not about me — about her. I’ve spent three years being the dullest prodigy in England so that nobody would want to write about me, and I think I’m going to have to spend some of that now.” He picked up the Advertiser again. “They’ll print anything with my name in it. I’ve made sure of that by never giving them anything. So I’m going to give them something, and the something is going to be a branch library with a woman in it who held a door.”

Mrs Figg looked at him over her glasses for a long moment.

“Your mother,” she said, “would have done exactly that, and then been furious with herself for enjoying it.”

He rang the Advertiser himself, from the telephone in the university library, which was allowed for research, and asked for the young man who had done the degree piece, and the young man came on the line with the voice of a journalist who has been handed a thing he had given up hoping for, and Harry said, in the flat Surrey vowels, at the speed of his mouth, the sentence he had built on the train:

“I read that the council is reviewing the Little Whinging branch. I learned to read in that library. I read every book in it before I was six. The librarian, Mrs Prentice, is the reason I have a degree. If you would like to write about that, I’ll give you an hour, on Saturday, at the library, with her there and the fourteen children who come on Saturdays, and you can photograph us all in front of the stepladder that sticks.”

The piece ran the following Thursday, on page one, below the fold, with a photograph of a small boy and a grey-haired woman in a cardigan and fourteen children of assorted sizes standing in front of a stepladder in a room of nine thousand books, none of them smiling, the boy and the woman looking at the camera with the exact same expression, which the young man from the Advertiser described in his copy as formidable. BOY GENIUS: DON’T CLOSE THE LIBRARY THAT MADE ME. Harry had given him eleven sentences and the young man had used all eleven and had not needed to add a word, and the sentence that went under the photograph was the one Harry had built last and placed first: “There are children in Little Whinging right now who are what I was at four. Fourteen of them come here on a Saturday. Somebody has to hold the door. Mrs Prentice is who does it. Count that instead of footfall.”

The council took Little Whinging off the list on the Tuesday. The letter said in the light of representations received. Mrs Prentice pinned it to the noticeboard by the returns desk, under the Open University listings with the slots ringed in pencil, and said nothing about it to anyone, and the following Thursday, after four, when he came in, there was a new stepladder in the adult section, aluminium, that did not stick, and she said, without looking up from the stamp:

“Council sent it. Apparently someone wrote to the paper about the old one.”

“Did they?”

“Hm.” The stamp came down. “I kept the old one. It’s in the returns cupboard. I find I’ve grown fond of it.” She looked up, over the glasses, and her face was doing a thing he had seen on it exactly once, in a doorway, in January, over an envelope for a children’s hospital. “Go and do the crossword, dear. There’s a seven in the corner that wants to be gratitude and is one letter short.”

He went and did the crossword. The seven was not gratitude. He knew what it was before he sat down, and he did not fill it in for a long time, because he wanted to sit with the clue.

The thing from 1979 gave up its first piece in October, on a Thursday, at the board, and Carrow did not swear; she sat down, which was worse.

“Say it,” said Harry, because it was his turn.

“It holds.” She was looking at the board, not at him. “The lemma holds. That is a third of the problem and I have been stuck on that third for ten years and you have done it in a term, and I do not know, Potter, I genuinely do not know whether to be delighted or to retire.”

“Delighted,” said Harry. “You found the Hungarian trap in it in August. I’d have been in there a month.”

“You’d have been in there a week and you know it.”

Lemma two took the winter, because lemma two was hard — properly hard, the kind of hard that did not care how fast you thought, that only cared whether you had thought the right thing — and he gave it the nights the Edition did not need, and the train, and the long quiet, and had it on a Tuesday in February. Lemma three took a fortnight, because lemma three turned out to be lemma two seen from the other side, and once he had said so — standing at the board with the chalk, to a woman who had been looking at it from the first side for eleven years — Carrow had sat down very suddenly in the chair with the paper on it and said, “Oh, for God’s sake,” in a voice that was not angry at all. And then there had been the thing itself, the theorem the lemmas had been for, which was not hard once the three of them were standing, the way a roof is not hard once the walls are up, and he had built it over two Tuesdays while she watched with her feet on the paper and her glasses off, and on the third Tuesday he had written the last line and put the chalk down and turned round.

“Say it,” said Carrow, very quietly.

“It holds.”

“Yes.” She did not move. “Yes. It does.” And then, after a minute, in the ordinary voice, as if nothing had happened: “Eleven years. I started that the year you were born, Potter. I was thirty-eight. I had hair.” She put her glasses on. “A thesis. Write it. In English, for the examiners, who are slow, and one of whom is going to be from Cambridge and will arrive having decided not to believe a word, and I want him to leave having read every one.”

“Yes, Professor. Friday.”

“Friday—” She stopped. She looked at the box file on the shelf behind him that had come out of her cupboard. “Yes. I suppose Friday.”

He wrote it in four nights. A hundred and sixty pages, in the round hand that had learned at six to go at a reader’s pace, with every step laid down like a stepping stone and no more than one stone at a time, and he gave it to Carrow on the Friday in a box file, and she took it home to Yorkshire for Easter, and when she came back in April she put it on the desk between them and said:

“There is nothing wrong with it. I looked for a fortnight. I had my brother look, who is a better mathematician than I am and has never admitted it. There is a comma on page ninety that I would have placed differently.” She pushed the box across. “Submit it. The viva will be in June. I shall ask Aldous from Cambridge to be external, because he is the rudest man in the field and if he passes it nobody will dare say a word.”

The viva was on the ninth of June, in a room at Royal Holloway with a long table and a view of trees, and it lasted four hours and eleven minutes, and the rudest man in the field came out of it with his tie loosened and said, to Carrow, in the corridor, in a voice meant to be overheard, “Helen, I came here to fail a child and I have been taught.”

Professor Aldous had arrived from Cambridge having decided not to believe a word. Harry had seen it in the first second — the way the man sat, the way he put the box file on the table without opening it, the way he looked at the small boy in the school jumper at the far end with an expression Harry had last seen on Mr Bellamy of four examination boards, before Mr Bellamy had been shown the regulations upside down. The internal examiner was Dr Lindqvist, who had read the thesis twice and understood two-thirds of it and said so, which was honest, and which Aldous had plainly taken as a sign that the whole thing was a Royal Holloway conspiracy to put a child in a gown.

So Aldous had gone for the throat, and Harry had let him.

It was, he thought afterwards, the best four hours of his life to date. Because Aldous was good. Not kind — not remotely; Carrow had chosen him for exactly that — but good, the way the men in brown jackets were good, the way Mr Haines was good about diagrams: a man who had spent forty years knowing where the bodies were buried in this one field and had come to dig them up. He went for the lemma first, the one from 1979, and he went for it from the Hungarian side, and Harry said, “Yes — that trap; Professor Carrow found it in August; it’s the second footnote on page forty-one, and here is why it doesn’t bite,” and showed him on the board, and Aldous was quiet for a moment and then went for the second lemma from a side Harry had not seen anyone come from, and Harry stood at the board for eleven minutes — most of a day and a half — and thought, with the room watching, and found the answer, and it was in the thesis, on page ninety, next to the comma Carrow would have placed differently, and he said so, and Aldous turned to page ninety and read it and said, “Hm.”

By the second hour Aldous had stopped going for the throat and started going for the mathematics, which was a different thing and a better one, and by the third hour the two of them were at the board together, Aldous with the chalk and Harry at his elbow, working out whether a thing Aldous had suggested — a thing that was not in the thesis at all, that Aldous had thought of in the car — would extend the result to a case Harry had marked as open, and it did, nearly, and the nearly was interesting, and Lindqvist had stopped taking notes and was simply watching, and Carrow, who as supervisor was supposed to say nothing, said nothing with such force that Harry could feel it from the board.

In the fourth hour Aldous sat down and loosened his tie and said, “All right. All right. Last question, and it’s not a mathematical one, and you may decline it.” He looked at Harry down the long table. “You are nine years old. You have written a thesis that I would have been proud to write at forty and that I could not have written at any age, because I did not see the overlap and I have looked at that problem for a decade. I want to know — not how. Helen has warned me that you will not say how and that I am not to ask. I want to know why. Why mathematics. A mind like yours could have gone anywhere. Why this.”

Harry thought about it honestly, which was the only way he knew how.

“Because you can always check whether you’re right,” he said. “I told a newspaper that when I was seven and it was the one true thing I told them. Everywhere else I’ve ever been, people decide whether a thing is right by who said it. Here it doesn’t matter who said it. It matters whether it holds. A child of nine can stand at a board in front of a man from Cambridge who came to fail him, and if the thing on the board holds, it holds, and the man from Cambridge has to say so.” He paused. “It’s the only room I’ve ever been in where that was true. I wanted to find out what it felt like to be judged by the thing instead of the face. It feels—” he searched “—it feels like being seen. Properly. For the first time. I’d like everyone to have that. I think it’s what the maths is for. It’s not the only thing I do. It’s the thing I do that nobody can argue with, and I wanted one of those before I went and did the others.”

The room was quiet for a moment.

“Hm,” said Professor Aldous. He looked at Carrow. “Helen. I shall want it noted in my report that the candidate answered a question that was not on the examination, at length, in a way that I found I agreed with, and that I am sixty-three and did not expect to agree with anything this afternoon.” He stood up. “Pass. No corrections. The comma on page ninety is in the wrong place and I shan’t make him move it.” He put on his coat. “And I should like him in Cambridge for a day in the autumn. To talk. To a room. I have a room full of people who think they know what this problem was, and I should like them to be taught.”

The ceremony was on the fourteenth of July, in the hall with the long windows, on a hot afternoon, and the gown fitted this time, because Carrow had had it made, and he did not care at all, because of who was in the third row.

They had all come. Petunia, in the hat from last year and gloves that were new, with Vernon in the funeral suit beside her and Dudley — ten, enormous, in a tie Petunia had tied — on the end, eating nothing for once, watching the stage with an expression Harry had never seen on him and filed, carefully, as interest. Mr Haines, in the tie, with his hands on his knees and no chalk on his elbows. Mrs Prentice in the good coat and Mrs Figg in the cherry hat, side by side again, handbags on knees, not speaking, and this time — he saw it from the stage — holding hands, which they would both have denied to the grave. Dr Lindqvist, who had come to sit in the audience rather than on the stage because, he said, he wanted to see it properly. Mrs Osei, in a coat Harry had never seen her without the lab under. Mr Pole from the chess club, who had lost to him every Wednesday for three years and had come to lose to him, he said, in a gown. Rachel Okafor and her mother, and Lee, in a shirt with a collar, at the back, because Mrs Prentice had asked and the Dean had said yes. And at the very end of the row, in a suit that did not fit him either, with his tie loosened in a hall where every other tie was tight, Professor Aldous of Cambridge, who had not been invited and had come anyway, and who caught Harry’s eye from the stage and did not smile and inclined his head one inch, which Harry understood was a thing he had probably not done for anyone since 1962.

Carrow read the citation herself. She had told Lindqvist he could not be trusted to keep it under a minute, and she kept it under forty seconds, and the forty seconds said what the thesis was and what it had cost and who had been stuck on it since 1979, and ended: The candidate is nine years old. I have been asked by a great many people what that means. I do not know what it means. I know that he found the overlap on the back of my door while I was looking at the front of it, and that I have never once in four years had to tell him a thing twice, and that he has never once in four years failed to tell me when I was wrong. I have supervised nineteen doctoral students. I should like it on record that he was the easiest, and that this is the hardest thing I have ever had to say in public.

And then the Vice-Chancellor, who had been told what to say and said it anyway as if he had thought of it: “Doctor Potter.”

He walked across the stage in a gown that fitted and took the scroll with both hands, carefully, the way he had taken everything in his life that had been handed to him on a stage, as if carrying it for someone else, and he looked at the third row, and Petunia was looking at her gloves, and then she looked up, and her face did the thing, and he thought: for the roof. And for the tea.

Outside, in the sun, there were photographers — a great many; this one had gone round the world, and there had been a man from an American network on the telephone to Mrs Bell every day for a fortnight, and Mrs Bell had taken to answering it with no before he had finished the question — and he gave them one picture, and said no to everything else, and the picture was a small boy in a gown holding a scroll, and the headline, in nine countries, was some version of DOCTOR, 9, and he did not read any of them, because he had a year and a day, and nothing in nine countries was going to be as interesting.

Vernon bought every copy the newsagent had. The lounge ran out of wall.

“Doctor,” said Petunia, that evening, at the sink, to the window, in the fence voice. And then, drying a cup, in the other one: “She’d have — your mother. She’d have—” She stopped. “Well. She’d have known what to say. I don’t.”

“You came,” said Harry. “That was what to say.”

He turned ten on the last day of July, 1990, and took stock, in English, in his head, with the swing on the wall and the date beside it and a box file under the bed with nine hundred and forty pages in it that nobody alive could read.

Ten. The honest count is somewhere short of eighteen hundred and I have stopped doing it every birthday because the number has stopped meaning what it meant. I was a thousand at six and I am not twice the person. I am the same person with more shelves, and more of what is on them is out of my head and in the world, which is the only count that matters now.

Read: everything the shop will sell and six things it would not have sold if the young woman with the ink had known what they were. The roof. The doorstep. The clerk’s six pages. The grammar, whole.

Written: the Edition, finished — nine hundred and forty pages, for a reader I have not met. Two hundred sentences of my own, forty good, thirty I can send by hand, one — the asking — that goes whole without a telephone and has not once been wrong. Fourteen papers. A thesis. Two books, both of them paying for a ward.

Made: a light, two; a ward on a door; a stand on a table two streets away; a clock that loses two seconds a week now; a graver; a spectroscope. Nothing yet that I could give to a Muggle hospital, because I have read the Statute, and I do not know, and I am going to find out before and not after.

Done: a doctorate, which is a piece of paper from this side that the other side has never seen. A library kept open by eleven sentences in a newspaper, which I did not enjoy and would do again tomorrow. Fourteen children on Saturdays, two of whom are going to a school in September that their mothers did not think they could get into.

Have not: a telephone. One year.

Found: that he can still write. The roof is his hand. He has asked me two questions through Mrs F and I have answered both and asked him for the names of two dead people and he sent them, and I have written them in the back of the Second Edition, because somebody should have them written down.

He looked at the last line for a while. Then he turned to the inside of the cupboard door, where the paper with the date was nearly full now — Faster, and They shout; I can hold, and He listened once; find out who else can — and under them, in the blue pen, small, he added to the things said there what the year had taught him and what he would carry up the drive in twelve months like a lamp:

The cold has a grammar. Nobody has read it. Read it.

Then he went down to the lounge in his socks, because it was ten past one, and sat on the carpet eighteen inches from the screen with the sound at two, and a man in a brown jacket began to explain the structure of a cell to nobody, for the fourth time in four years, and Harry Potter, ten years old, Doctor of Philosophy, with a year to go, listened anyway, because the man might go a different way, and because he had promised himself at six that he would never again stop being found.

Contents

Chapter 16

The Press

Cambridge was a Tuesday in October, and a room with forty people in it who had come to see a child, and he gave them an hour and a half on the thing from 1979 and sent them away having seen a theorem instead, which was what Aldous had asked for.

He had thought about how to do it on the train, with Mrs Figg beside him reading a castle and the fens going past flat and wet. He had never spoken to a room. He had spoken to a long table at five and a sports hall at seven and nineteen third-years at a board, and none of those had been a room — forty people who knew the field, who had each read the thesis and come with a question folded in a pocket. He could not go at the speed of his mind; they would not follow. He could not go at the speed of his hand; they would be bored. He had to go at the speed of theirs, which was a thing he had been learning for five years from a woman with a knife for a face, and he found, standing at the front of the room with the chalk, that he knew it the way he knew the hall clock.

So he taught. He did what the men in brown jackets did. He went a different way, and said so, and said in fact let’s do that again more carefully, and hesitated before the hard steps so that they knew where they were, and when a man in the second row put his hand up with the question from his pocket, Harry answered it from the side the man had not come from, and the man sat for a moment and then wrote something down. By the end there was no sound in the room but the chalk, and when he put the chalk down Aldous, at the back, with his tie loosened, did not clap, because nobody there did, and inclined his head one inch for the second time in his life.

Afterwards, in a corridor with a draught, Aldous said: “What next?”

“Physics,” said Harry. “There’s a thing Lindqvist and I have been circling for two years. And then a thing I can’t tell you about, in September, for seven years.”

“Helen said.” Aldous looked at him for a while. “You are going to be the best mathematician of the century, and you are going to go and do something else. I find I mind that less than I expected. I think it is because I have just watched you teach, and I have been in this building forty years and had not seen it done.” He put his hands in his pockets. “Write to me. About the physics. Not the other thing.”

“Every term,” said Harry, who was collecting teachers to write to, and had four.

The physics was the thing on page one hundred and forty, and it had been sitting in a tin since he was six, and in November Lindqvist and Mrs Osei and he put it on paper together and Lindqvist, when it was done, sat with his arms folded and his eyebrows up and said a thing Harry put on the shelf next to Carrow’s citation.

He had written it at six, in the two hundred pages for a man he had not met, in a month of nights with the pencil going: a reason why the thing Mrs Osei had measured to the fourth decimal place in 1989 should be what it was and not what the third predicted. He had not known it was a reason. He had thought it was obvious, the way the twelve pages Ashworth had found in the second tin had been obvious. Lindqvist had read it four times in 1988 and said nobody has it, and then had said nothing more for two years, because — he admitted it in November, in the lab, with the rain on the skylight — he had not believed it, and had spent two years trying to show it was wrong, and had failed, and had then spent a third year with Mrs Osei trying to measure it, which was the only argument that counted, and in October the measurement had come in, and it was what page one hundred and forty said.

“I have been a physicist for twenty-five years,” said Lindqvist, with his arms folded. “I have eleven papers that people cite. This is the one I shall be remembered for and it isn’t mine. I should like that in the acknowledgements, in those words, and I should like it understood that I am not being modest, which I have never been accused of. I am being accurate.”

“It’s yours,” said Harry. “I wrote a page at six. You spent three years finding out whether it was true. That’s the physics. The page is only a page.”

“Hm,” said Mrs Osei, from the bench, and it was a different hm from the usual, and he kept it.

The paper went off in December with three names on it, alphabetical, and a line in the acknowledgements that said exactly what Lindqvist had said, and when it came back in March the editor’s letter said that the referees had asked for the raw data twice and had been sent it twice and had, the second time, apologised.

The bridge was Carrow’s doing, and the money was the first money he had ever made that was not from a page, and it taught him that the world would pay a great deal for a thing he could do on a train.

There was a firm in Reading that built footbridges, and they had built one over a river in the north that moved when people walked on it — not dangerously; uncomfortably, the engineer said, on the telephone to Carrow, as if a bridge were a shoe — and they had spent a year and a great deal of money trying to find out why and had not, and somebody on their board had read the Telegraph, and Carrow had been asked whether the University of London would consult, and had said that the University of London would do nothing of the kind but that she knew a boy who read on trains.

They sent the drawings. He read them on the eight thirty-one, with Mrs Figg beside him, and had the shape of it by Staines, and the reason by Egham, and it was not a hard problem; it was a problem nobody at the firm had thought to look at from the side, because the side was a thing you learned from the fifth floor’s history shelf and not from an engineering course — a thing about how people walk when a surface under them moves, and how the walking and the moving make each other worse, in a loop, that had been written down in 1831 after a bridge in Manchester fell down for exactly that reason and had been forgotten by everyone who did not read history. He wrote it out that night, twelve pages, with a diagram, in the round hand, with the fix on the last page, and Carrow sent it to Reading, and Reading sent back a cheque to the trust, through Mr Pargeter, for five thousand pounds, and a letter that said their engineers had read it and were mortified.

He sat with the cheque for a while. Not the number; the fact of it. He had not known, until that week, what his time was worth to anyone who did not love him, and it turned out that it was worth, to a firm in Reading, five thousand pounds for a train journey, and the train journey had cost him nothing at all.

He wrote to Mr Pargeter in January, on one side of one sheet, and said that the trust now held more than he could spend on travel to any place of learning in seven years, and that he should like the trustees to deem some things conducive. A year’s travel kept back, always, because a boy with no money had no doors. Everything from Three and Thirteen, still, to the ward. Everything from the first book, from now on, to the famine that the thin green book had begun with, which was still going on, under a different name, in a different country, and would be when he was fifty. Half of the bridge money to Mrs Prentice, for the children’s corner, in a form the council could not take back — books, and a second chair, and a cupboard with a lock. And the other half kept, in the trust, because he had read the Statute and did not know what was going to be allowed, and he wanted, when he found out, to have the price of a thing in his pocket and not have to ask anyone.

I am keeping the shoes, he wrote to Mr Pargeter. I am giving the rest. I should like a statement each quarter so that I can see whether I am doing it, and not only saying it, because I have read a book that says the saying is where people go wrong.

Mr Pargeter wrote back that the trustees had deemed everything conducive, that a statement would come each quarter, and that in thirty-one years of practice he had never before been asked by a beneficiary to be audited, and found that he did not know what face to make about it, and had settled on his ordinary one.

He designed the press in March — in his head in a night, and on paper for the back of the Edition in a week — and he could not build it, and he knew exactly how, and that was the strangest feeling of his life.

It had been coming since the runes. Sentences in things. He had named it at seven and had made, since, a pot stand and two lights and a ward on a door, one at a time, by hand, with a graver and a held sentence, each one a night’s work. And the enchantment book had told him how a sentence was put into a thing the other way — not carved; pressed, by someone who held the whole of it in their head and pushed it into the matter the way you press a seal into wax, and it took a master a day for a simple one and a week for a good one, and that was why enchanted things were rare and expensive and most people had never owned one.

And chapter nine of the enchantment book — tell him to read chapter nine twice, and he had, and then eleven more times — had said a thing in passing, which was that the pressing and the carving were not two things. The carving was a pressing that had been made permanent. And the author of chapter nine had wondered, in a footnote, whether a thing could be made that did the pressing — not a wizard; a thing, enchanted itself, that held a sentence and pressed it into whatever was put in front of it, over and over, the way a seal does — and had concluded that it could not, because no wizard could hold a sentence precisely enough to press it into the pressing-thing without the sentence going ragged, and a ragged seal made ragged copies, and ragged copies of a sentence were worse than none.

Harry had read that footnote at seven and put a margin on it that said they shout and gone on.

He read it again in March, at ten, with nine hundred and forty pages of grammar in a box file and three carved things in the world and a feather that held, and he sat under the stairs with the book open and understood, very clearly, in the long quiet, that the footnote was describing the thing he had been for since a doorstep.

Nobody could hold a sentence precisely enough. He could. He had been holding sentences precisely for four years; he had set three into oak with nothing but holding; there was a chapter in the Edition about it. If he had a telephone — if he had a telephone and a year — he could press a sentence into a thing so cleanly that the thing could press it into other things, and the copies would not be ragged, because the original would not be, because he could see it go in.

A seal. A press. A thing that made enchanted things the way a printing press made books, and he knew what the printing press had done to the world because he had read the whole of the fifth floor’s history shelf at six, and he sat under the stairs with his heart going and designed it.

Not the whole thing; he did not know enough about matter yet, about what would hold a sentence and what would spit it out, and the enchantment book was vague about that in the way books were vague about things their authors had never had to do. But the sentence — the one the press itself would hold, the one that said take what is held in you and put it into what is placed before you, exactly, and again — that he could write, and he wrote it, over a week of nights, in the marks, two hundred lines, with the asking built into it so that the press would check each copy before it let it go, the way the doorstep sentence checked a child’s temperature before it gave warmth. He could not cast it. He could not build it. He could look at it on the page and see, the way Carrow saw a proof, that it would hold.

And then, because he had read the fifth floor, he sat with the design and did the thing the fifth floor had taught him to do with any machine, which was to ask what it would be for, and the answer came up through him like the tide at Mrs Figg’s hands.

The doorstep sentence. The one that checked for cold and gave exactly the difference. Pressed into a blanket. A thousand blankets. In a children’s hospital eleven miles from a photograph on page seven, where a girl of six had no hair and the ward was cold at night because the heating was a line in a budget.

He made two.

Not pressed; he had no press and no telephone. Carved, the only way he had: the doorstep sentence, every mark of it, cut with the graver into two slats of oak from Haines’s drawer, each the length of a hand, over two nights in April with the ward on the door telling him the hall was empty, and the sentence held so steady while he cut that he felt it set with the last mark the way the pot stand had. Then he slid each slat into the hem of a blanket from the airing cupboard — Petunia counted blankets, and he told her, at the sink, that he had two for an experiment and would bring them back, and she looked at him and said an experiment and said nothing else — and he lay under one that night in the cupboard with the light off and waited.

It came. Warmth, through cloth, exactly where the cold bit worst and nowhere else, patient, unbothered, the same as a stone step in November. The sentence had not cared that the blanket was from a Muggle airing cupboard. It had not cared that the oak was from a physics teacher’s drawer. It had checked how cold he was and given exactly the difference and gone on checking, and he lay under it in the dark, nine and a half years after the first time, and understood that the thing he had been for since a doorstep worked in a blanket.

He did not give them to anyone. He folded them and put them in the trunk, under the Edition, with the slats in the hems, because he had read the Statute and he did not know, and he put it in capitals under the design, where the thing Carrow had trained in him insisted it go: IT WORKS. TWO BLANKETS, IN A TRUNK, WARM. I DO NOT KNOW IF THIS IS ALLOWED. FIND OUT BEFORE, NOT AFTER.

He called the design the press and the sentence in it the seal, and under them, in English, for the reader who was slow, he wrote the plainest thing in nine hundred pages: This is the lever. Not a wand in a hand. A sentence in a thing, made by someone who can hold, copied by a thing that can check. One good sentence, pressed a thousand times, is a thousand people warm. Nobody has built this because nobody could hold the first copy. I can. I have made two by hand and they work. Give me a telephone and a year.

Then he closed the trunk and lay in the dark with the swing on the wall and the date beside it — four months now — and found, for the first time since the cupboard week at five, that he was not waiting for the far side to send for him.

He was waiting for them to get out of the way.

Dudley asked him in May, on the stairs, with the Smeltings entrance paper in his hand and the look of a boy who had been trying for a week to find a way to ask that did not sound like asking.

“You did tables,” said Dudley. “With the ball. I know it was you. I worked it out ages ago.” He held out the paper. “This is in June. Dad says I’ll walk it. I won’t walk it. I can’t do the — the ones with the letters.”

Harry took the paper. It was the ordinary kind, the kind the boy at the back of the sports hall had failed; Dudley could do perhaps half of it, and would do a quarter, because the half he could do he would not believe he could.

“Not the front door,” said Harry.

“What?”

“Nothing. Yes. Three weeks. Not at the table — Uncle Vernon would want to help, and then you’d fail it. In the garden. You kick, I talk. You won’t notice.”

Dudley looked at him for a moment with his small eyes, and then, because he was eleven, and had been given a thing by a cousin he had spent six years not liking and did not know what face to make about it, said, “All right,” and went downstairs.

Three weeks in the garden, in the evenings, with a football and the letters-ones put into the football by the method of the tennis ball on the stairs, which Dudley pretended not to recognise and recognised. He sat the paper on the fourteenth of June and came home two feet taller than he had left, and told the table that it had been easy, really, you just had to not be thick about it, and Vernon beamed, and Petunia did a steak-and-kidney, and looked across the table at Harry, once, and he understood that she had seen every kick.

In July the blazer came, maroon, with a boater, and Dudley hated it, and wore it round the house for a whole weekend anyway, and on the Sunday he stopped at the cupboard door, in the blazer, and said, not loudly, “Thanks,” and went on down the hall, and Harry put it on the shelf with the straight fingers and the purse at the greengrocer’s, because it belonged there.

Why Things Fall came out in June, for the children in the corner who had finished Three and Thirteen and asked what the mountain was made of, and it was eighty pages, and it had Saturn in it, and a mirror a man and a boy had ground, and a bridge in Manchester in 1831, and the money went to the famine, and Mrs Prentice read it first and said it was right and that there was a six in the corner that wanted to be gravity and wasn’t.

There were twenty in the corner by then, on a Saturday, and Mrs Okafor came and sat with the younger ones, and Rachel, who was going to the grammar school in September with Lee, took the fractions, and Harry took whoever had the look — the look he had had at four, at a kitchen table, of a child who could see the whole of a thing and had nobody to tell — and there were three of those, now, out of twenty, and he had their names, and he had told Mrs Prentice that when he went in September she was to keep the corner open and write to him about the three, every term, and she had said hm and written it in the diary.

He had the trunk by then. Mrs Figg had brought it back from the fourth trip with a face like a woman carrying a coffin she approved of, and it stood in the cupboard with the Edition in it and the two blankets and the carved things in their tin and the robes and the cauldron and the books, and on the Thursday before his birthday the fourth paper of the year came out, the one with Lindqvist, and he put the offprint in the trunk too, on top, because it was the one he was going to show to whoever asked what a doctor was.

He took stock on the twenty-ninth of July, two days before, at the kitchen table, where the light was best, in English, in his head, because the cupboard was full of a trunk and because he wanted to do it somewhere he could see the garden.

Ten. Eleven on Wednesday. I have stopped counting the other way. Call it the better part of two thousand, and call it not the point. The point is what is out of my head and in the world, and I did not understand that until I was six and a pencil moved, and I have been making up for the first five years since.

Out of my head, on this side: twenty-two papers. Three books, two of them paying for a ward and a famine. A doctorate, in a world that has never heard of one, and a room in Cambridge that was taught. A bridge in the north that does not move. A mirror, a clock, a spectroscope, a graver. Twenty children in a corner, and three of them are what I was. A trust with more in it than I can spend, and a man who sends me a statement each quarter so that I can see whether I am giving it away or only saying so.

Out of my head, on the other side: nine hundred and forty pages of grammar that nobody alive can read, for a reader in fifty years. Two hundred sentences, forty good, thirty I can send by hand, one that goes whole and has not once been wrong. Three things in oak, and a ward on a door. Two blankets in a trunk, warm, that I do not know if I am allowed to give to anyone, and will not until I do. The seal. The roof, read. The doorstep, read. Six pages by a clerk about the cold, read until I could see the shape of what he wasn’t saying. Two names in the back of the Edition. Four hundred questions, in order, for a man who has asked me three.

Can: hold. A feather that stays. A pot warmed from forty yards. A pencil that writes, a page that turns, a graver that sets. The asking. Nothing for myself; the door faces out; that is the design.

Have not: a telephone. Two days.

He sat with that line for a long time. Then the last thing, the thing he was going to carry up the drive like a lamp, in the words he would give the reader in fifty years, because he wanted them to know what he had thought he was for, at ten, two days before:

I am not going to that school to learn magic. I have learned the magic that is in the books; I learned it on a kitchen floor and in a cupboard and on a bench in a street with an old woman holding my hand, and I have made things with it that work. I am going to that school because it is where the telephone is, and the hospital is, and the man who can listen, and the cold, and because the two halves of one country have never once sat down at a table and somebody has to be the table. I have read both sides. I am the only one who has. I am the better part of two thousand years old and I have a doctorate and a trunk with two warm blankets in it and I am eleven on Wednesday.

Let them deal with it. That is their job. Mine is to stop making it hard.

Outside, Petunia was deadheading the roses by the fence, right-handed, with her back to the window, and he watched her for a moment at the speed he always watched things, which was slowly, and then he went out to do the gutters, because he had promised, and because a doctor who kept his promises to his aunt was, he had decided, the only kind worth being.

The date on the inside of the cupboard door had two days left on it. He had stopped looking at it some time ago. He did not need to. He had known since a garden bench at five exactly how long he had, and he had used every day, and on Wednesday morning at nine o’clock somebody from the far side was going to knock on the front door of Number Four, Privet Drive, and he was going to open it, and whoever it was had better, he thought, up a ladder in the July sun with his hands full of leaves, have brought a very strong cup of tea.

Chapter 17

Interlude: The Watchers’ File

The Deputy Headmistress’s office, Hogwarts, a Thursday evening in late June 1991

Minerva McGonagall had forty-one letters to prepare that summer, and forty of them took her an hour and a half.

It was the same every June. The Book gave up its names; the Quill wrote the addresses in its slanting green hand; she checked each one against the register, because the Quill was old and had once, in 1974, sent a letter to a boy who had been dead for eleven years, and she had never entirely trusted it since. Then the Muggle-born and the Muggle-raised went into a separate pile for the visits, and the rest went to the owlery, and she had a cup of tea, and it was done.

The forty-first letter was on her desk by itself, and beside it was not a file. It was a drawer. Albus had sent the whole drawer down, that afternoon, by house-elf, with a note that said you had better have all of it, and it stood on her side table like a piece of furniture that had got lost, and she had been not opening it for two hours.

She opened it.

Five years of Arabella Figg, in order, in a hand that had changed in the winter of 1987 from a careful left-handed slant to something freer and faster and rather less legible. Two trays of cuttings — no; three; the third had been started in the spring — with a small boy in them growing up at the rate of a photograph a year: a chess cup, two sheets of paper, a scroll in a gown that did not fit, a scroll in a gown that did. Four receipts, itemised, each with Albus’s thank you for the itemisation in the margin and, on the second, in a smaller hand, tell him to read chapter nine twice, which she had not seen before and looked at for some time. A slim blue book with a child’s face on the cover and the words Three and Thirteen. Four offprints from Muggle journals. A thesis, in a box file, a hundred and sixty pages, which she opened and read the first paragraph of and closed again, because it was not her field and would not have been anybody’s at Hogwarts, and because the first paragraph had been written by a ten-year-old in a hand that went at exactly the pace a reader needed and not one step faster, and she had taught for thirty-four years and had never once seen that in a student of any age.

And at the bottom of the drawer, in an envelope of its own, four letters from Albus to Arabella and four from Arabella to Albus that were not reports. They were questions. Albus had asked the boy — through Arabella, in Arabella’s hand, with Arabella’s evident disapproval of the method in every line — four things. A dosage table in a Healing text. A thing about wards that Minerva did not follow. The year of a particular dissenting pamphlet. And, in the fourth, the one from May, a single sentence: Ask him what he thinks the guards at Azkaban are made of.

The boy had answered all four. The fourth answer was two pages long, in Arabella’s copying hand, and McGonagall read it standing, twice, and then sat down, which was the sequence in which she received news she did not like, and the news was this: that a child of ten in a cupboard in Surrey had read a Ministry clerk’s six pages from 1931 and concluded, with reasons, in order, that a Dementor was a sentence that eats sentences, and that nobody had ever tried to read one, and that he intended to.

She sat for a long time with the two pages in her hand and the June light going long across the desk and the forty ordinary letters in their neat pile, and then she said, aloud, to nobody:

“Doctor.”

Then she picked up the drawer — it was heavy; she did not care — and went to find the Headmaster.

“Albus. This child has a doctorate.”

“So I see. So, I think, does most of the world; Arabella sent the cutting from the Telegraph and three others, one of them in French. I have been meaning to ask you what a Vice-Chancellor is.” Dumbledore did not look up from the thing he was doing, which involved a small silver instrument and a very long pair of tweezers. “A sort of senior Deputy Headmaster, I believe. In a gown.”

“He is ten. He was nine on the day. He is eleven in a month and Arabella says it as though the month were a mercy.” McGonagall put the drawer down on his desk, on top of the instrument, which he permitted. “I have read the drawer. All of it. I have read a thesis I do not understand and a children’s book I did, and four questions you asked a child through a squib because you had forbidden yourself every other method, and his four answers, and the fourth answer, Albus, the fourth answer is about Dementors, and he is right, or if he is not right he is wrong in a way that nobody in this castle has had the wit to be wrong in for three hundred years.”

“Yes,” said Dumbledore. “I thought that too. I read it four times. I have shown it to Filius, who went quite pale and asked whether the boy had ever seen one, and I said no, he had read six pages by a clerk, and Filius said then God help us all when he does.” He set the tweezers down. The instrument, released, gave a small puff of lilac smoke and settled. “Sit down, Minerva. You have the face.”

“I have several faces. I have been accumulating them since the Quill wrote his address this afternoon and I have not yet decided which to wear.” She did not sit. “That is the other thing. The letter.”

She took it from her robes and turned it and held it so he could see the green ink, and the second line.

Mr H. Potter

The Cupboard under the Stairs

4 Privet Drive

Dumbledore read it. He read it for a long time, for a man who could read a page in a glance, and when he had finished he did not look up, and the room was very quiet, and Fawkes, on his perch, did not sing.

“I did not know,” he said.

“No.”

“Arabella did not know.”

“No. She could not have. In five years she has never crossed the step; she waits at the gate; she has told me so. The aunt has never asked her in and she has never asked to be.” McGonagall’s voice was perfectly level, which was how he knew. “Five years of letters, Albus. Three trays. A thesis. He has told her about the roses and the cat and the pot and the wall in the yard and the sentence in the bricks, and he has answered four questions you had no right to ask, and he has never once, in five years, mentioned where he sleeps. Nobody asked him. It never occurred to anyone in this drawer to wonder what was behind the front door, because everything in front of it was so—” she searched for the word and found it in his own file “—so remarkable.”

He closed his eyes.

She let him have the moment, because thirty-five years had taught her when to, and because she had had her own in her office with the Quill and knew what it cost. Then she said, because it needed saying and he was the only one who could hear it:

“It is in the file now. The Quill has put it there. We will not pretend afterwards that it was not.”

“No,” said Dumbledore. He opened his eyes. The mildness had not come down; she watched him decline to let it. “No, we will not. I wrote a letter in July of 1989, Minerva, that I did not send, and in it I said that I did enough to him on that step. I did not know the half of what I meant.” He looked at the green line. “And yet.”

“And yet?”

“And yet he wrote a thesis in it. And nine hundred pages of something Arabella says nobody can read, in a box file, under the bed. And a children’s book whose money goes to a Muggle hospital. He read the roof from the step with the milk going cold, Arabella says, and told his aunt it was the best thing he had ever seen anyone write, and she told him it was anchored to her and she had no intention of making a liar of a piece of paper.” He put one finger on the second line of the address. “I do not know what this means, Minerva. I know what it looks like. I have spent five years learning, from shopping lists, not to trust what things look like when they are about this boy. When you go — and you are going; it is in the file, in your own green ink, three times — ask him. Do not assume. He may have reasons for a cupboard that are his own. He seems to have reasons for everything else.”

“I shall ask,” said McGonagall. “I had already decided to. I shall ask, and I shall listen, and then, Albus, whatever he says, we are going to sit in this room and you are going to tell me the whole of the roof, and the whole of the step, and the whole of why you asked a child about Dementors through a squib, because I have spent five years watching you decide I did not need to know, and I have decided that I do.”

“Yes,” said Dumbledore. “Yes. I have the letter. It is in the drawer — the other drawer; this desk has several. I wrote it for him. You may read it after he has.” He took his spectacles off. He did not polish them. He simply held them, and looked at the thesis in its box file, and she saw the window open and stay open, which she had not seen in sixty years. “Minerva. Three weeks ago I said to Filius that I should prepare to receive a colleague who was eleven.”

“You did.”

“I think I underestimated the matter. In both directions at once. He is going to walk into this castle with a doctorate from a world that has never heard of one, and nine hundred pages in a box file that nobody here can read, and a theory of Dementors, and four hundred questions for me, in order, and he is going to be eleven, and homesick, and a boy, and all of those things will be true at the same time, and I have no idea how to hold them, and I have been a headmaster for thirty-five years.” He put the spectacles back on. “I find the prospect — I shall be honest with you, since you have the drawer — I find it the most frightening and the most interesting thing that has happened to this school in my lifetime, and I am not certain which of those is the larger.”

McGonagall looked at him for a long moment. Then she picked up the letter, and the drawer, and held them.

“Thirty-first of July,” she said. “Nine in the morning. I shall wear the tartan. He will have seen a witch — he has seen Arabella in a mackintosh for five years and Arabella is a squib in a housecoat and would tell you so herself — but he has not seen one who looked like something, and I should like the first one he sees to.” She went to the door. At it she stopped, and looked at the letter once more — The Cupboard under the Stairs — and then at him, and her face did the thing it did for students who had done something clever and were about to be told it did not excuse them, except that under the severity there was something else, which he had not seen there in some years.

“You asked a child of ten what Dementors are made of,” she said. “Through a squib. Because you wanted to know.”

“Yes.”

“And he told you. In two pages. With reasons.”

“Yes.”

“Then, Albus, I think you had better have a very strong cup of tea ready on the first of September, because Arabella says he has four hundred of those, in order, and I have met the sort of boy who writes things down in order, and they do not stop at four hundred.” She opened the door. “I shall look like something,” said Minerva McGonagall, and went to teach nobody, because it was June, and sat instead in her own office for a long while with a children’s book called Three and Thirteen open on her knee at page forty, where a boy in a story said enough for one night, and read it, and did not entirely understand why her eyes had gone hot, and did not look for the reason.

Wisteria Walk, the twenty-second of July

Dear Albus, This is the last of these, I think, in the old way. I shall write from the summers, of course; but not as a watcher. He will be eleven in nine days and the watching is yours after that, and you will do it from a good deal closer than two streets, and I hope you do it well, and I shall know if you don't. He knows the date. He has known it five years; he pinned it to the inside of a door at five and three-quarters and it has been there since, with other things written under it that I have not been shown and have not asked to see. He asked me on Saturday what to pack, and I told him warm things and a good pair of boots, and he thanked me for it as if I were somebody. The trunk is in the cupboard. I bought it on the fourth trip; the bill is enclosed and I shall thank you to pay it without comment, as usual. He has a photograph of his mother on a swing that he carries in his inside pocket, from the aunt, and I have seen him take it out when he thinks nobody is looking and put it back. He has done the gutters. The aunt asked. A Vice-Chancellor called him Doctor in front of nine countries a year ago, and on Monday he went up the ladder and did the gutters, because she asked, and because — he said this to me, on the train, and I shall put it down exactly — "a doctor who keeps his promises to his aunt is the only kind worth being." I do not think you will have had many first-years who said that, Albus. I do not think you will have had any. You asked me once, years ago, to tell you if he was happy. I did, and it was true, and it is still true. I should like now to tell you the thing I should have said first, five years ago, in the first letter after the cat, and did not have the words for. He is kind. Not polite — he is that too; he was that at three — kind. He mended my hands because he was sorry for them, and then he was sorry he hadn't noticed sooner, and said so. He gave half his money to a famine at eight and the whole of a book to a ward at ten and kept the rest for a reason he could say in one sentence. He makes his aunt tea. He asked me what I was so that I would not have to choose between him and you, and he waited two years to ask it, and when I told him he said thank you — for writing. He kept a library open with eleven sentences in a newspaper because a woman in it had held a door for him, and he has never once said so to her, and she has never once said so to him, and they do the crossword on Thursdays. He asked you for the names of two dead people because somebody should have them written down. He asked me, on Saturday, after the boots, whether I thought you would mind if he asked his four hundred questions in order or whether he should pick the most important ones first, and I said I thought you would mind very much if he picked, and he said good, because he had been worried about being rude. I have not been afraid for him since the hands. I find I am still, a little, afraid for the rest of you. But less, Albus. Much less, lately. He has read everything you have, and some things you haven't, and he has decided — I have watched him decide; it took him five years and he did it in a cupboard — that the two halves of one country have never once sat down at a table and that somebody has to be the table. He has a box file with nine hundred pages in it that he says is the grammar. He has a thing he calls the asking and a thing he calls the seal and he will not explain either to me because he says it would take a year and I haven't got one, and he said it kindly. He is going to walk into your school with all of that and a doctorate and his mother's eyes, and I think when you meet him you will understand why I have spent five years in a café in Egham with a pot of tea and never once minded. The Forth Bridge tin is nearly empty. I shall buy another for the summers. I find I am looking forward to the summers. Arabella P.S. He said to tell you — these are his words — that he hopes you have a very strong cup of tea ready, and that he has one question he would like to ask before the four hundred, and that it is the one on the step, and that he has been told you will have an answer and that it will be good and true and not the whole thing, and that he would like, if it is not too much to ask, to have the whole thing. He said you would know what he meant. I do not. I expect you do.

Dumbledore read it in the evening, at his desk, with the castle quiet around him and the lake going gold, and when he reached kind he stopped, as he had known he would, and read the word again, and sat for a long while with the letter in his hand and the window open, looking at nothing, in the way of a man who has asked for one thing for five years and has just, at the last possible moment, been given it by the one person he trusted to say so.

Then he read the postscript, and read it again, and sat for a while longer.

Then he opened the other drawer, the one with the four letters from sixty years in it, and took out the one from July of 1989, and read it through, and did not change a word, and put it in his inside pocket, where it would be on the first of September, and closed the drawer.

“The whole thing,” said Albus Dumbledore, quietly, to the empty office. “Yes. I rather think he had better.”

Then he took Arabella’s letter, and put it in the file, at the very end, after the green line and the cupboard and the thesis and the two pages about the cold, and closed the file, and did not open it again until the boy did.

Contents

Chapter 18

The Thirty-First

He did not sleep, and he had known he would not, and he had decided what the year would be for.

He spent the first part of it going through the plan — the whole of it, from be small in a shoebox to the five parts on a kitchen table to Faster to the paper on the door with its date and its four lines underneath — and he did not change a word, and he found when he had finished that there was nothing in it he was ashamed of, which he had not been sure of going in. He spent the second part on the box file: the nine hundred and forty pages, the two hundred sentences, the asking, the seal, until he could have written any of them in the dark, which he could anyway. And the third part he spent on nothing. Lying under the stairs with the trunk at his feet and the swing on the wall and the house ticking round him, in the long quiet that had been the whole of his life, doing nothing at all, on purpose, for the last time in the smallest office in England.

At half past five he heard the kitchen light go on.

He got up.
