He went through in his socks, and Petunia was standing at the sink in her dressing gown with the kettle not yet on, looking out at the garden, where there was nothing yet but grey, and she did not turn round, and he put the kettle on, and got two cups down, and neither of them said anything until it had boiled.

“They’ll come today,” she said.

“Yes.”

“A letter. It was a letter, for her. An owl, in the morning, on the step, and she screamed the house down.” She took the cup he gave her. “Your uncle doesn’t know what day it is.”

“I’ll manage Uncle Vernon.”

“You always do.” It was not quite a smile. “He thinks it’s a boarding school. He’s been telling the Rotary it’s a boarding school since the degree. I let him.”

“It is a boarding school.”

“Harry.” She looked at him for the first time that morning, over the cup, with the face with the wall out of it, and under the face he saw the thing that must have been there at eleven, in a town up north, watching a sister read a letter in green ink: not envy. The other side of it. The thing envy is the shadow of. “They’re going to take you somewhere I can’t follow. I know that. I’ve known it ten years. I’m telling you now, before they come, so that I’ve said it once and you’ve heard it: I’d have kept you. If there’d been no letter. If you’d been — ordinary, if a boy with a doctorate at ten can be called that. I’d have kept you, badly, and you’d have gone to Egham every day with that woman and her cats, and I’d have put the cuttings in my purse, and it would have been a life.” She put the cup down. “I wanted you to know there was a version where I kept you. That’s all.”

He stood in the kitchen with his own cup going cold and found, for the third time in his life, that he had nothing ready.

“I know,” he said. “Aunt Petunia. I know. I’ll come back in the summers. For the roof.” And then, because it was the morning it was: “And for the tea.”

Petunia Dursley made the sound that was not a laugh, and turned back to the garden, and said, “Lay the table. He’ll be down at seven and he’ll want the paper,” and that was all, and it was a great deal.

The doorbell rang at nine o’clock exactly, and he had known it would, and he was already in the hall.

Behind him, in the kitchen, Vernon said “Who the devil calls at nine on a—” and stopped, because Petunia had said his name in a voice Harry had never heard her use, low and final, like a door being locked from the inside. Dudley, at the table, home from Smeltings for the summer and twelve and enormous, went on eating toast and watched the hall with the expression Harry had filed at the ceremony as interest, and which had, over the summer, been getting slowly more frequent and no less surprising.

He opened the door.

The woman on the step was tall, and severe, and dressed from collar to hem in a tartan he did not know the name of, with a pointed hat squarely on her head and square spectacles on her nose, and she was looking down at him with an expression he had seen before, on Dr Prosser and Mrs Prentice and Professor Carrow and Professor Aldous of Cambridge and no one else: a professional, declining to be impressed until given cause.

“Good morning,” said the woman. Her voice was Scottish, clipped, and gave nothing away. “My name is Professor McGonagall. I am the Deputy Headmistress of Hogwarts School of Witchcraft and Wizardry. I have come about Harry Potter.”

“Good morning, Professor,” said Harry. “That’s me. Please come in. I’ve been expecting you.”

The eyebrows went up a quarter of an inch. He had learned from Mr Haines what that meant and what it cost.

“Expecting me.”

“Since the last Saturday in March, 1986. The date, I mean, not you in particular — I didn’t know who they’d send. I hoped it would be someone who’d look like something.” He stepped back from the door. “The lounge is on the left. My uncle is going to shout for about ninety seconds and then stop. I’d be grateful if you’d let me handle him; I’ve had five years’ practice and it’ll be quicker.”

Professor McGonagall stood on the step of Number Four, Privet Drive, in her tartan, with a letter in her hand addressed in green ink, and looked at the small boy in the doorway for a long moment — three seconds; most of a morning — and he watched her decide, the way he had watched Carrow decide over forty pages at a long table, to take the thing she had been given and see where it went.

“Very well, Dr Potter,” she said. “Lead on.”

He had not expected the Doctor. He put it on the shelf, walking ahead of her into the lounge, and found it had gone in warm.

Vernon shouted for seventy-one seconds. Harry timed it.

It was not the shouting he had expected. He had modelled, over five years, a Vernon who roared we swore when we took him in we’d stamp it out — Petunia had told him, at a kitchen table, what had been said in 1981, and he had built his handling round it. But five years of Rotary dinners had done something to Vernon Dursley that no amount of stamping could undo. He had boasted. He had bought every copy the newsagent had. He had told Marge to take her bad blood to the Daily Mail. He had sat in a hall with long windows and heard a Vice-Chancellor say Doctor in front of nine countries and had gone home and told the man from the garage. Vernon Dursley had spent five years building, in public, in front of everyone he knew, the character of a man whose nephew was a genius, and he could no more tear it down in front of a stranger in a hat than he could have returned the frames to the shop.

So the shouting was confused. It wanted to be get out of my house and kept turning, halfway through, into and what sort of school did you say, and Harry stood on the lounge carpet between his uncle and the Deputy Headmistress of Hogwarts and let it run for seventy-one seconds, and then said, in the voice he had used on Dudley’s tables:

“Uncle Vernon. It’s a boarding school. It’s very old — older than Eton — and it takes about forty children a year in the whole country, and you can’t apply, you have to be chosen, and it chose me. Mr Pargeter’s trust will pay for anything the school doesn’t. You won’t be out of pocket.” He let that land. “And you can tell the Rotary it’s in Scotland. That part’s true.”

Vernon stood in the lounge, purple, with his mouth open, and Harry watched the two Vernons fight it out behind his face — the one from 1981 and the one from the Rotary — and it was no contest, and had not been for four years, and the Rotary won on points inside three seconds.

“Chosen,” said Vernon.

“Chosen, Mr Dursley,” said Professor McGonagall, who had been watching this with an expression Harry could not read and was beginning to suspect he would enjoy learning to. “Hogwarts does not advertise.”

“Hm,” said Vernon. He sat down in his chair. He looked at the wall — the chess cup, the fifteen letters, the two scrolls, the nine countries — and Harry watched him do the sum he had been doing for five years, and arrive, as he always did, at the same comfortable total. “Well. I suppose. If it’s exclusive.”

“I have never heard it called anything else,” said McGonagall drily, and Harry understood that he was going to like her very much, and that it was going to take her some time to decide whether she liked him, and that both of those things were correct.

Petunia had not sat down. She stood in the lounge doorway with her arms folded, in the dressing gown still, and looked at the tartan, and the hat, and the letter, and her face did not do the white thing, not once, and when McGonagall glanced at her — a brief look, professional, taking the measure — Petunia Dursley met it and held it and said, flatly:

“You’ll be wanting to talk to him. Not to us. Dudley, upstairs.”

“But I—”

“Upstairs, Dudley.”

Dudley went, with his toast, looking back at the hat, and at the top of the stairs he stopped and said, not loudly, down the well of the hall, “Good luck, Harry,” and went into his room and shut the door, and Harry stood very still for a quarter of a second, which was most of a minute, because in ten years it was the first time his cousin had ever wished him anything, and he put it on the shelf next to the Doctor, and found that it too had gone in warm.

Vernon, after a moment, with great dignity, took the paper and went into the kitchen and shut the door. Petunia stood a second longer in the doorway, looking at Harry, and said, “I’ll be in the garden,” and went, and the lounge was empty but for a witch and a boy and a wall full of frames.

McGonagall looked at the frames for a long moment before she sat down.

“Your uncle,” she said, “appears to have changed his mind about a number of things, Dr Potter.”

“I changed it for him. It took five years.” He sat in the other chair. “He had to be proud of me in public before you came, or he’d have thrown you out. So I made sure he was. I’m sorry about the seventy-one seconds. I’d hoped for sixty.”

“You timed it.”

“I time everything, Professor. It’s a habit.” He took a folded sheet from his pocket and smoothed it on his knee. “I have four hundred and three questions. Most of them are for the Headmaster, in order, and I’ll keep them for him. Eleven of them are for you, because they’re about the school and you run it. May I?”

She let him ask the eleven, and answered nine, and the two she did not answer she said so plainly — that is a matter for the Headmaster and I do not know, and I will not pretend to — and he put a mark beside each and moved on, and somewhere around the third question she stopped looking at him like a woman delivering a letter and started looking at him like a woman conducting an interview, which was what he had wanted.

Was the protection on this house what he thought it was — anchored to his aunt and to his calling the place home, both or neither, and would it hold through the school year if he came back in the summers? She went still at that. She asked how he knew. He said he had read it, from the step, four mornings in October of 1989, with the milk going cold, and that it was the best thing he had ever seen anyone write, and that he would like to know who had written it so that he could say so. She said, after a moment, that the matter of the protection was for the Headmaster, and that she had been promised the whole of it herself only last month, and that she would see to it that he was told. He marked it and went on.

What, precisely, did the Trace detect, and had it ever detected anything at Number Four? It detected magic in the vicinity of the underage, she said, and sent a letter; and no, it had not; she had checked, that week, because it had occurred to her to. He said that was what he had thought, and that he had a provisional theory about why, and that he would rather not lean on it until someone who knew more had looked at it, and would she? She said she would.

Did she know where his parents were buried? She did. Godric’s Hollow. A churchyard, in the West Country, with a white stone. She said it quietly, and then she said — not as a Deputy Headmistress; he could hear the difference — that she had known them both, taught them both, and that if he wished, one day, when he was ready, she would take him. He put a mark beside that one that was not like the others, and said thank you, and did not trust himself to say more, and she let it go.

Did the school have a library, and what was in it, and were there books in it that bit? It did; a great deal; and yes, some of them, and he was to leave those to the librarian, who was called Madam Pince and had opinions. Pince, he thought, and put it with the names.

Did the school teach anyone how a spell was made? There was a pause before that one. Not in the ordinary curriculum, she said. There were books. There was a Charms master who thought about it more than most. There had been, long ago, a man called Blishwick. He said he had Blishwick, and had written in the margin, and she said, “Yes. I had heard,” in a voice that told him she had heard a great deal, and he decided not to ask how, because she would tell him when she chose, and because he had learned from Carrow that a person who has decided to stand to one side of a door should not be pushed through it.

And then the tenth, which was the one he most wanted to ask and had kept for tenth on purpose, because he had learned from the viva that the real question came after the examiners had stopped going for the throat.

“Professor. I have a design. It’s on paper; I can’t build it; I don’t have a — I don’t have a wand. It’s a thing that would take a sentence — a charm — and put it into an object, and then put it into other objects, the same way, over and over, like a seal. A press. I think it would work. I think nobody has built one because nobody could hold the first copy steady enough, and I think I can, and I’ve written down why. I have made two by hand — carved, not pressed; a night each — and they are in my trunk, and they work, and I have not given them to anyone.” He kept his voice level, because it was the sentence he had built on the train from Egham a hundred times and he wanted it to carry. “The first thing I would put in it is a warming charm I know. One that checks how cold a person is and gives exactly the difference. And the first place I would want to put the blankets is a children’s hospital in London, where a girl of six had her photograph in a newspaper two years ago because the ward was cold at night and the heating was a line in a budget.” He looked at her. “I have read the Statute of Secrecy. The whole of it. The preamble and the 1692 pamphlets. I do not think it allows that. I want to know whether I have read it right, and I want to know before I do anything, not after, because I have a rule about that. Have I read it right?”

The lounge was very quiet.

Minerva McGonagall, Deputy Headmistress of Hogwarts, who had delivered letters to Muggle-raised children for thirty-four years and had believed, until nine o’clock that morning, that she had seen every possible version of the conversation that followed, sat in a chair in Little Whinging with her hands folded on her tartan knee and looked at a boy of eleven — eleven today — who had just asked her, formally, in order, with reasons, whether he was permitted to warm a Muggle hospital.

“Yes,” she said at last. “You have read it right. As it stands, it does not allow that.”

“I thought not.”

“As it stands.” She said it with great precision, the way one reports an instrument reading one has decided to believe. “Dr Potter, the Statute of Secrecy has been amended eleven times since 1692. Each time, it was amended because someone brought the Wizengamot a case it had not imagined when it wrote the clause. I have never, in thirty-four years, heard a case it had not imagined put more plainly than you have just put one to me, in a lounge, before breakfast.” She unfolded her hands. “I cannot tell you it is allowed. I will tell you that the Headmaster sits on the Wizengamot, and that you have four hundred and three questions for him, and that I think you had better move that one rather higher up the list than tenth.”

“It’s first,” said Harry. “After the step. The step is first. Then this.”

“Yes,” said McGonagall. “I rather thought it might be.” And then, because she had asked properly and listened properly, and because she had a question of her own and he had seen it coming since she sat down: “Dr Potter. I have one question that is not on anybody’s list, and I have been asked to ask it, and to listen to the answer, and not to arrive with my own in my pocket.”

She took the letter from her robes, and turned it, and held it so he could see the green ink of the address, and the second line.

“The Quill that writes these does not make mistakes about rooms. It writes where a child sleeps.”

He looked at the green line. The Cupboard under the Stairs. He had wondered, since Hogwarts: A History, whether the letter would know, and had decided it probably would, and had decided also what he would say, and he said it.

“It’s mine,” he said. “I took it when I was three. Not — I wasn’t put there, Professor, or not the way that line makes it sound. There was a box room, and there still is, and I could have it tomorrow if I asked; my uncle would give it me with a bow, now. I don’t want it. The cupboard has a door that shuts and a light with its own switch and nobody has opened it in eight years except me. I learned to read in there. I wrote a thesis in there. There’s a box file under the bed with nine hundred pages in it that I’d not have written anywhere with a window. It’s the only room in this house that was ever mine, and I chose it, and I’d be grateful if you didn’t make it into something it wasn’t.” He stopped. Then, because she had asked properly, and because he had made a rule at a kitchen table about the truth: “It was what it looks like, when I was two. Before I chose it. I won’t pretend otherwise. But I was two for a very short time, Professor, and I’ve been the person who chose it for a great deal longer, and I’d like to be judged as him.”

McGonagall did not say anything for a while.

She looked at the letter, and at him, and then — this surprised him; he had not modelled it — at the frames on the wall again, the chess cup and the fifteen letters and the two scrolls and the nine countries, and he saw her put the two things side by side, the green line and the frames, and hold them, and not resolve them, which he thought was exactly right and which almost nobody could do.

“I will not make it into anything,” she said at last. “I will report what you have said, in your words, to the one person who needs to hear it. He asked me to ask you, and to listen, and I have, and I will tell him that you answered like—” she paused, and he saw her choose “—like a person who had thought about the question before I arrived, which I am beginning to think is going to be the shape of my year.” She put the letter down on the arm of the chair between them. “It is yours. You have not opened it.”

“I wanted to ask the questions first. In case the letter changed them.”

“Open it, Dr Potter.”

He opened it. He read it — the parchment, the crest, Dear Mr Potter, We are pleased to inform you — in the time it took her to blink, and then he read it again at the speed of his hand turning the page, for the pleasure of it, and then he looked at the second sheet, the list, Uniform, Set Books, Other Equipment, and he read down the set books, and stopped, and something happened at the corner of his mouth that he did not try to prevent.

“What is it?”

“I have all of these,” said Harry. “The books. Mrs Figg took me. Four times. You’ll have — I expect you’ll have had the receipts.”

“I have had,” said Professor McGonagall, “the receipts.” And for the first time since she had stood on the step, the severe mouth did something that he would spend a good part of the next seven years learning to provoke on purpose. “I have also had a thesis, which I did not understand, and a children’s book, which I did, and two pages on a subject I will not name in a lounge, which I understood rather better than I should have liked. I know what you have, Dr Potter. I came to find out what you are, and I have been asked to find that out by looking, and I have looked, and I am going to need to sit down somewhere with a very strong cup of tea before I attempt to put any of it into a report.” She stood, and smoothed the tartan. “In the meantime I believe you have some shopping to finish. You have the books. You do not have a wand. You do not, I think, have robes, or a cauldron, or the smallest idea what is in your vault, and I have a key.”

“A vault.”

“Your parents did not leave you poor.” She looked at the frames one last time, and then at the door through which his aunt had gone to the garden, and her voice changed, very slightly, into something he had heard once before in that lounge, that morning, from the other woman in the house. “And Dr Potter. Many happy returns. I knew your mother. She would have opened the letter first and asked the questions after. You are — I think — rather more like your father in that one respect, and nothing whatever like either of them in all the others, and I find I do not know yet what to make of that, and I intend to find out.”

“Thank you, Professor,” said Harry, and went to fetch his coat, and in the hall, out of sight, he stood for one second — a working afternoon — with his hand on the hook and his eyes shut and the letter in his pocket and the swing in his inside pocket and let the morning catch up with him, and then he opened his eyes and went out to the garden to tell his aunt he was going to the shops.

She was deadheading the roses by the fence. She did not look up.

“Well?” she said.

“Scottish,” said Harry. “Tartan. She knew Mum.”

Petunia’s secateurs paused.

“Go on, then,” she said, to the roses. “Tea’s at six.” And then, as he turned to go, still to the roses, in the voice from half past five: “Harry. She called you Doctor. At the door. I heard her.”

“Yes.”

“Good,” said Petunia Dursley, and snipped a stem, and dropped it in the trug, and did not say anything else, and did not need to.

Contents

Chapter 19

The Telephone

“How did you travel, the four times?”

“The train, Professor. With Mrs Figg. She sat in the sandwich bar opposite while I found it the first time, and after that she held my hand through the wall, because she said she was sixty-one and hadn’t been in there since her brother died and would like something to hold.”

“Then we shall take the train,” said Professor McGonagall, and did, in the tartan, with the pointed hat in a carrier bag from a shop that had closed in 1979, and sat for twenty-two minutes in a second-class carriage on a Wednesday morning being looked at by every person who got on, and looked back at each of them until they stopped, and Harry sat opposite her with his hands in his lap and did not say a word, because he had decided somewhere around Clapham Junction that this was the most entertaining journey of his life and he was not going to spoil it.

“You are enjoying this,” she said, at Vauxhall, without turning from the window.

“Yes, Professor.”

“Hm.” A pause. “Your mother would have enjoyed it too. She had no respect whatever for a Floo.”

He put that on the shelf with the others. It was a long shelf.

The Leaky Cauldron was different without the cap and without Mrs Figg’s hand, and he had known it would be.

Four trips in a football cap pulled to the eyebrows, with an old woman in a mackintosh steering him through the room at a pace that discouraged conversation: nobody in the pub had ever looked at him for longer than it took to see child. He walked in now a half-step behind the tartan with his face bare, and the toothless barman looked up from his glass and his rag, and saw McGonagall, and nodded, and saw Harry, and the rag stopped.

“Bless my soul,” said the barman, quite softly. “Professor — is that—”

“It is,” said McGonagall, in a voice that had closed staffroom doors for thirty years. “And he has a wand to buy and a train to catch home, Tom, and I will take it as a personal kindness if the room lets him do both.”

The room did not entirely let him. Chairs scraped. An old woman with a long pipe stood up and simply looked at him, both hands pressed flat to her chest, and said nothing, and sat down again. A man in a purple top hat — the man; five years older and exactly as small — came across the floor at a half-run and seized Harry’s hand in both of his and said, “Mr Potter! Dedalus Diggle! You won’t remember — Little Whinging — the bus stop—”

“I remember,” said Harry. “You bowed. My aunt bought my cousin a second ice lolly, which was against the rules of Saturday.” He shook the hand that was shaking his. “Thank you. It was the first time anyone was glad to see me. I was six. I thought about it for a long time afterwards, and I worked out from it that there were people who were glad, and that changed what I did next. So — thank you.”

Dedalus Diggle stood with his mouth open and his hat in his hand and his eyes going wet, and McGonagall said, “Dedalus,” in a tone that put him back in his chair as if on a string, and steered Harry through the room with one hand not quite on his shoulder, and out into the little walled yard, and shut the door.

“You remembered his bow.”

“I remember everything, Professor. It’s—” he hesitated, because he was closer to the edge of the real thing with her than he had been with anyone on this side, and stepped back from it, because it was not yet time and she would ask how “—it’s a habit I have.”

McGonagall looked at him in the yard, with the bins and the weeds and the wall, for a moment longer than the exchange warranted.

“Three up, two across,” she said, and took out her wand, and he watched the wall say its sentence, every brick, for the fifth time, and this time he did not try to hide that he was reading it, and she saw him read, and said nothing, and filed it, and they went through.

The bank was first, and the goblin on the left looked at the tartan and the boy with his face bare, and stepped back.

Not a bow. A quarter-pace, the way one steps back for a thing that has a right of way. Harry noticed, and knew why, and found he had preferred the contempt.

The goblin at the counter this time was older, with spectacles on a chain, and McGonagall put a small gold key on the marble and said, “Dr Potter’s vault. He will want to make a withdrawal,” and the goblin took the key and looked at it, and at Harry, and said, “Griphook,” to the air, and a younger goblin appeared, and Harry — who had stood in this hall four times under a cap with scone money in a jam jar — said, before he could stop himself:

“Is Bogrod on the exchange counter today? He changed my Muggle money the first time. He counted it twice.”

The older goblin’s spectacles came up.

“Bogrod counts everyone’s money twice,” he said. “He considers it a courtesy.” And then — the flicker, the professional flicker, the thing Harry had seen once in this hall and had filed as the finest compliment Little Whinging had never paid him — “You changed Muggle money. Four times. With a squib.”

“Yes. A hundred and twelve pounds the first time, from a toffee tin. I have—” he put his hand in his pocket and brought out what was there, which was one galleon and four sickles, the whole of what remained from the fourth trip “—I have this. I’d like to deposit it, please, before I draw anything. It was the first money I ever had on this side, or what’s left of it, and I’d rather it was in the vault than in my shirt.”

There was a silence at the counter. Griphook looked at the older goblin. The older goblin looked at one galleon and four sickles on the marble, and then at the boy, over the spectacles, for a long moment — three seconds — and Harry, who had spent ten years reading the sides of faces, could not read this one at all, and understood that goblins had been doing to wizards for a thousand years what he had been doing to the Dursleys for ten, and that he was in the presence of professionals.

“Noted,” said the goblin, and swept the coins into a drawer, and wrote a line in a ledger, and the line was longer than one galleon and four sickles warranted. “Griphook. The vault.”

The cart went down into the dark at a speed that would have been alarming to anyone who could not see it coming, and Harry could see it coming, every turn, and spent the ride instead looking at the walls — rock, old, and under the rock, faint, the hum of the street outside turned up until it was almost a sound, the whole mountain under London full of sentences a thousand years deep — and when the cart stopped and Griphook opened a door with the key and the green smoke cleared, he stood in the doorway of a vault full of gold and did not go in for a moment.

Mounds of it. Columns of silver. Heaps of little bronze. He had read vault on McGonagall’s lips in the lounge and written it beside one galleon four sickles and had not, he found, believed it until now.

He thought about a toffee tin under a returns desk. Nineteen pounds and twelve pence, in pencil, on the back of a catalogue card. A hundred and fifteen pounds to a children’s hospital in an envelope, and the whole of a book after it. Mr Pargeter’s bowling-green voice saying such other expenses as the trustees shall in their discretion deem conducive. A jam jar of scone money, spent by a woman who had told a great wizard he was wrong for the third time in her life to spend it. Ten years of counting, of shoes, of the far-side half kept in a tin for a reason he had said aloud once at eight, and here it had been the whole time, under London, in the dark, waiting for a key.

“Dr Potter,” said McGonagall, behind him. “Is something the matter?”

“No, Professor.” He went in. He took what the list needed and what he had worked out on the train that a year would need, and no more, and counted it into the bag Griphook handed him, and did the sum in sickles aloud so that Griphook would hear him do it right, and Griphook heard, and said nothing, and that was the second compliment of the morning.

“The money problem of my life,” said Harry, on the way back up, mostly to himself, with the dark going past, “just ended in about a minute. I’d planned for it to take until I was twenty-one.” He looked at his hands. “I don’t quite know what to do with the plan.”

“Keep it,” said McGonagall, unexpectedly. “In my experience, Dr Potter, the people who have had to count are the only ones who can be trusted not to.”

Ollivander’s was narrow and dim and smelled of dust and something under the dust, like the kitchen floor at Mrs Figg’s turned up loud, and he had been looking forward to it for five years and found, on the step, that his heart was going as hard as it had at the pub.

A telephone. Five years of shouting across a field, and behind this door was a telephone.

The old man came out of the back of the shop like something surfacing, with pale eyes that did not blink enough and a voice like paper, and said, “Ah. Yes. I thought I’d be seeing you soon, Harry Potter,” and then went on about Harry’s mother’s wand and his father’s, willow and mahogany, lengths and tempers, and Harry stood very still and listened to a man describe his parents as a pair of instruments — swishy, pliable, good for charm work — and put every word of it on the shelf, because nobody had ever given him that before, the shape of their hands.

Then the pale eyes went to his forehead.

“And that is where—”

“Yes,” said Harry. “I know what did it. I read it in a shop a hundred yards from here when I was seven, on a bench, with a squib holding my hand. I’d rather we got on, if you don’t mind, Mr Ollivander. I’d like to find out what mine is before I think about his.”

Ollivander looked at him for a long moment with the unblinking eyes, and something moved behind them that was not quite surprise.

“Yes,” he said softly. “Yes, I think that is the correct order.” And took out a tape measure that began to measure Harry by itself, and went to the shelves.

The trying took forty minutes by the shop’s clock and he loved it beyond reason, and he could not have explained to anyone why, except that it was the first time in his life anyone had handed him instruments, one after another, and said try. Beechwood and dragon heartstring. Maple and phoenix feather. Ebony and unicorn. He took each one, and lifted it, and Ollivander snatched each one back almost before he had, saying no, no, and Harry did not mind, because every one — every single one — had done something that nothing in ten years had done.

They answered. Badly. Like a telephone with the line crackling, a wrong number, a voice on the far end that was not meant for him. But he felt it each time: the sentence he had been shouting across a field for five years going somewhere. Into the wood. Down the grain. Looking for a way out and not finding it, in the wrong wands, and finding a little of one in the nearly-right ones, and he held each nearly-right one a half-second longer than Ollivander wanted and listened, the way he had listened to the roses, and heard the shape of the line, and understood what Waffling had been fumbling towards for nine pages and what Blishwick had regretted he could not see and what he himself had written in a biscuit tin at ten under the heading telephone without ever once having held one.

The wand was a channel, exactly as he had written. And the channel had a grain, and the grain had to match the shape of the one sending, or the sentence went down it sideways and came out ragged or not at all, and that was why the feathers shot into the ceiling, and that was why it mattered which wand, and he stood in the middle of Ollivander’s shop with his thirty-first rejection in his hand and said, aloud, before he could stop himself:

“They have a grain.”

Ollivander, at the shelves, went still.

“I beg your pardon?”

“The wands. They — I’m sorry, Mr Ollivander, I don’t know your words for it; I’ve read Waffling and he hasn’t got any either. But each one has a shape inside it that the — the sending — has to fit. Like a key. The nearly-right ones, I can feel the sending go in and get stuck, partway, and come back. The wrong ones it doesn’t go in at all.” He looked at the ebony in his hand. “This one it goes in about a third and stops. It’s very close. It’s just the wrong — turn. I don’t know how to say it. The wrong turn.”

Ollivander did not say anything for a very long time, for a man who had been talking without pause for forty minutes.

“The wrong turn,” he said at last, in the paper voice, very quietly. “Yes. That is — that is not a bad word for it. I have been in this shop for seventy years, Dr Potter, and my father before me, and I have never once heard a customer describe the fit from the inside.” He turned back to the shelves, and his hand went, without looking, to a box high up and far back that Harry had the strong sense he had been leaving for last. “I wonder,” said Ollivander. “Yes. Why not. Holly and phoenix feather, eleven inches, nice and supple. An unusual combination. Try.”

Harry took it.

He had been waiting five years for a telephone and had thought he knew what one would feel like, and he did not. It was not a crackle. It was not a fit, even, the key-and-lock thing he had felt in the ebony. It was the thing on the kitchen floor the day of the cat: the warmth coming up through him and finding the way already open — a line that ran straight from six years old to the tip of a piece of holly, with no field between, no shouting, no loss — and he felt the sentence he had been holding in the back of his mind since the shop door, the simplest one he knew, the candle’s cousin, light, go down the grain of the wand like water down a pipe that had been built for it, and he did not let it out.

He held it.

He had learned to hold on a feather at a quarter to midnight in a cupboard three years ago, and he held now, with the whole shop watching, the sentence at the tip of the wand like a note in singing, and then he let it out slowly — not the shower of sparks that the books said, that the first-years did, that Ollivander was plainly waiting for — but a single steady point of white light, at the tip, that came up out of nothing like the candle had, and stood, and held, and did not waver, and lit the dust in the air of the shop so that every mote of it hung still and separate and bright, for four seconds, which was most of a quarter of an hour of looking at a thing he had made exactly as he had meant to make it.

Then he let it go. The light went out. The dust fell.

Nobody in the shop said anything.

McGonagall, by the door, had one hand flat against the frame. Ollivander stood at the counter with his pale eyes very wide and his mouth a little open, and for the first time since Harry had come in, he blinked.

“Curious,” said Ollivander, and his voice was not paper now; it was something with more in it. “Very — Dr Potter. That was not sparks.”

“No, sir.”

“A first touch always gives sparks. Always. The wand answers and the wizard cannot yet shape the answer, and so it comes out as — as noise. Joyful noise, but noise. I have seen it ten thousand times. I have never seen it—” he searched “—steady.”

“I’ve been practising holding,” said Harry. “Without one of these. For three years. On a feather. I thought if I could hold with nothing, I could hold with something.” He looked at the wand in his hand. It was warm. It had, he thought, been waiting rather a long time. “I was right. It’s — Mr Ollivander, it’s so much easier with the telephone.”

“The — telephone.”

“It’s what I call it. Sorry. The channel. You haven’t got a word either; I checked.” He turned the holly over. “Thank you. This is the one. I knew the moment it went in. There wasn’t a turn at all.”

Ollivander came round the counter. He did it slowly, with the eyes still wide, and he stopped in front of Harry and looked at him — not the forehead this time; at him — the way Carrow had looked at forty pages at a long table, the way Aldous had looked at page ninety, and Harry stood and let himself be looked at.

“Curious,” said Ollivander again, softly. “Curious. I shall tell you, Dr Potter, because I think it is the sort of thing you would rather know than not, and because I rather think you will find out anyway. The phoenix whose feather is in your wand gave one other. Just one. Thirteen and a half inches, yew. It is very curious indeed that you should be destined for this wand when its brother—” he stopped, and the pale eyes went, briefly, to the forehead and away “—when its brother gave you that scar.”

The shop was quiet.

Harry stood with the holly in his hand and let it land, which took a while by his own clock, and then he did the thing he had learned to do on a bench in the Alley at seven with a reference book open on his knee and a squib’s hand in his: he picked it up, and looked at it carefully, and put it on the shelf marked when I am ready and not one day before, and found that the shelf held, as it had held everything he had put on it for four years.

“Thank you for telling me,” he said. “You’re right. I’d rather know.” He looked at the wand, warm, eleven inches, no turn at all. “Its brother did what it did. This one just held a light for four seconds in a shop. I think I’ll judge it on that, for now, and see how it goes.”

Ollivander looked at him a moment longer. Then, to Harry’s considerable surprise, he bowed — a small one, from the neck, the bow of a craftsman to someone who has understood his craft — and said, “Seven galleons, Dr Potter. And I think we may expect great things. I have said that to a great many customers, over seventy years, and I find I have never before said it with so little idea what the things will be.”

Madam Malkin’s took ten minutes and he said nothing in it worth recording, except that there was a pale boy on the next stool talking about brooms and houses who looked at Harry’s face and went quiet in a way that Harry filed under later. The cauldron, the scales, the phials, the telescope took twenty. Flourish and Blotts he had no need of, and said so, and McGonagall said “I know,” and let him go in anyway, for five minutes, to stand in the back room by the high shelf where a thin grey book had been, and was not now, because it was in a biscuit tin in Surrey with his margin in it, and the young woman with the inky fingers looked up from the till and said, “The margins boy — oh. Oh,” and did the sum, and Harry said, “I was the margins boy first. I’d rather be that one in here,” and she laughed, and he bought nothing, and McGonagall, behind him, made a small sound that he would spend seven years learning was the nearest she came in public to a laugh.

The owl he chose himself. It was not on the list, and she said so, and he said he had made promises to a physics teacher and a professor of mathematics and a woman with a stamp that he would write, and that there was a post from everywhere and it was only a question of stamps, and she looked at him over the spectacles and said nothing further, and he went into Eeylops alone and came out with a snowy owl in a cage who regarded him with the exact expression of a cat on a wall in Wisteria Walk, and whom he named, that night, out of A History of Magic, Hedwig, because the Hedwig in the book had been a woman who could not be told anything, and he had a feeling.

“Dr Potter,” said McGonagall, outside the Leaky Cauldron at half past four, in the Charing Cross Road, with the pointed hat back in its carrier bag and the holly in his pocket and the owl in his hand and the Wednesday crowd going round them like water round a stone, “I shall see you on the first of September. The ticket is in your letter. Platform nine and three-quarters, which does not exist, and you will find it anyway, I have no doubt, by declining.” The mouth did its thing. “I have one more question. It is not on anybody’s list.”

“Yes, Professor?”

“The light. In the shop. Four seconds, steady, on a first touch.” She looked at him with the square spectacles catching the afternoon, and her voice was not the Deputy Headmistress’s. “Dr Potter, I have taught transfiguration for thirty-four years and examined some two thousand first-years, and what you did in that shop is not possible. I do not say unusual. I say that I have, until half past twelve today, believed it to be impossible, for anyone, at any age, with any wand. I should like to know — not how. I will not ask how. I should like to know whether you knew it was impossible.”

He thought about it honestly, which was the only way he knew how to think about anything by then.

“Yes,” he said. “I read it in a book from 1911. The man said it couldn’t be done and apologised for it. I wrote not quite in the margin when I was seven and then I spent three years finding out whether I was right.” He shifted the cage to his other hand. “I’m sorry, Professor. I know it’s going to make your year complicated. I’ve been trying to think of a way to make it less so and I haven’t found one yet. But I promise you I’ll be careful, and I promise you I’ll tell you before I do anything I think might be impossible again, so that at least you’re not finding out in a shop.”

Minerva McGonagall looked at the Boy Who Lived on the pavement of the Charing Cross Road for a long moment, with her hand on the strap of a carrier bag from a shop that had closed in 1979.

“Tea at six,” she said at last. “Your aunt told me. Go and catch your train.”

The Headmaster’s office, Hogwarts, that evening

“Albus.”

“Minerva. Sit down. You have the face.”

“I have several faces. I have been accumulating them since nine o’clock this morning and I have not yet decided which to wear.” She did not sit. She put a sheet of her own notes on his desk, in green ink, closely written on both sides, and she stood over it with her hands flat on the desk and said: “You asked me for two things. Whether he is happy and whether he is kind. I will give you those first, because they are the simple ones.”

“Please.”

“He is happy. Not content — happy. He enjoyed the train. He enjoyed my being stared at. He laughed at a bookseller. He went into a vault full of more gold than he will spend in his life and stood in the door of it for four seconds and the thing on his face was not greed, Albus, it was grief, for a toffee tin and a jam jar and ten years of counting, and then he took exactly what a year needs and counted it aloud in sickles so the goblin would hear him do it right.” She breathed. “And he is kind. He deposited one galleon and four sickles before he would draw a knut, because it was the first money he had on this side and he wanted it in the vault and not his shirt. He remembered Dedalus Diggle’s bow, from five years ago, and thanked him for it, and told him it had changed what he did next, and Dedalus wept. He bought an owl because he promised three Muggle teachers he would write. He asked Ollivander to tell him about his own wand before the one that killed his parents, and when Ollivander told him about the feather anyway, he said — I have it here — its brother did what it did; this one just held a light for four seconds in a shop; I think I’ll judge it on that.”

Dumbledore had gone very still at feather.

“He knows about the brother wand.”

“Ollivander told him. He put it—” she read from her notes “—on a shelf. For things he will deal with when he is ready and not one day before. He has had the shelf since he was seven. He said it had never once failed to hold.” She turned the page. “Now the complicated ones. He asked me, in a lounge, before breakfast, formally, with reasons, whether the Statute of Secrecy would permit him to warm a Muggle children’s hospital with blankets that carry a charm he has designed, pressed by a device he has designed, that nobody has built because nobody could hold the first copy steady, and he can.”

Dumbledore said nothing.

“I told him it does not, as it stands. He said he had thought not. He said it was second on his list of four hundred and three, after the step, and that he wanted to know before, not after, because he has a rule.” She straightened. “And Albus. The shop. Ollivander’s. I have left it to last because I do not have words for it and I have been looking since half past twelve.”

“Tell me.”

“Holly and phoenix. His first touch. And he did not give sparks.” She said it very precisely, the way one reports an instrument reading one does not believe. “He held the wand, and he held whatever it is one holds, for a second — I saw him do it, I saw him wait — and then a single point of white light came out at the tip, steady, and stayed, for four seconds, and lit the dust, and went out when he chose. Ollivander blinked. Ollivander has not blinked in my presence in thirty years. He said he had seen ten thousand first touches and never once a steady one, and the boy said he had been practising holding without a wand for three years so that he could hold with one, and then he called the wand a telephone, and apologised for the word, and told Ollivander that Ollivander hadn’t got a word either because he had checked.”

She stopped. The office was very quiet. Fawkes, on his perch, had his head up.

“And then, outside, on the pavement, I asked him whether he had known it was impossible, and he said yes, he had read it in Blishwick at seven and written not quite in the margin and spent three years finding out, and he was sorry, Albus, he said he was sorry it was going to make my year complicated and that he would try to warn me next time so that I did not find out in a shop.”

Albus Dumbledore sat behind his desk with his hands folded and the green notes in front of him and the letter from July of 1989 in his inside pocket, where it had been since the twenty-second, and for a long time he did not say anything at all, and McGonagall let him, because she had been letting him for thirty-five years and knew the difference between the silence that was a blind coming down and the silence that was a man standing in front of something with his own eyes open.

“Minerva,” he said finally. “Three weeks ago I said I had underestimated the matter in both directions at once.”

“You did.”

“I think I had not finished underestimating it.” He took his spectacles off. He did not polish them. He simply held them, and looked at the green notes, and she saw the window open and stay open. “He put the feather on a shelf. He deposited one galleon. He is sorry for your year. He asked, in order, with reasons, whether he may warm a hospital. And he walked into Ollivander’s and held a light.” He put the spectacles back on. “I asked you whether he was kind because I believed it was the only thing that would matter. I find I was right, and I find also that I had not the faintest idea what I was asking, and that I have had five years and a drawer to find out and did not.”

“No,” said McGonagall. “Nor had I. Nor, I think, had Arabella, and she has been in love with him for five years and bought his wand-shop books with scone money.” She picked up her notes. “He is going to ask you why you left him on the step. On the first of September, or as soon after as he can get into this office, which I suspect will be the first of September. He has it first on a list of four hundred and three, and he told me he wanted it on record that he had asked before he saw the school, and I told him you would have an answer.”

“I have an answer. It is in my pocket.”

“Then, Albus, I should spend the next month making very sure it is the whole thing.” She went to the door. “Because I have met him now, and I will tell you what Arabella could not, having never crossed the step: he will know the difference. He has spent ten years learning to read the sides of faces for the part that is not being said. He told me so. I think he was being kind.”

She opened the door.

“Albus,” she said, from the threshold — and it was not a sentence; it was the whole of the day in a name — and went to find a very strong cup of tea.

Contents

Chapter 20

Sixteen Years of August

He did not use the wand. That was the first decision of the month and the hardest, and he made it on the train home from the Charing Cross Road with the holly in his pocket going warm against his leg, and he said it that night, aloud, to the inside of the cupboard door, so that it would be a thing said and not a thing he could pretend afterwards he had drifted into.

The Decree says no magic outside school before seventeen. McGonagall says the Trace has never once registered at this house. I have a theory about why. The theory says that what I do on kitchen floors does not look, to whatever watches, like what it watches for, because it has no shout in it. The theory does not say anything about a telephone. The moment I send a sentence down holly and phoenix, I am doing exactly what the Trace was built to see, in a Muggle house, under a protection my aunt is holding up with her life, and I promised a woman in tartan on a pavement that I would not do anything impossible without telling her first.

So: no. Not for a month. I have waited five years for this and I can wait thirty-one days. The telephone stays on the hook.

He said on the hook twice, and then sat looking at the wand on the blanket, eleven inches of holly with no turn in it at all, and felt the month ahead of him the way he had felt the twelve days of a sports hall at six: as a thing with rules, and nothing in it, that he would have to fill himself.

A month was sixteen years. He had never had sixteen years with a telephone he could not use, and he found, lying there with the house ticking, that he was looking forward to it.

He found out what a wand was by not using it.

That was the second thing the month taught him, and it was worth, he thought afterwards, the whole of the thirty-one days. He had read Waffling’s nine pages on wands and Blishwick’s two and the enchantment book’s chapter nine eleven times, and he had held thirty-one wrong wands and one right one in a shop, and none of that had told him what the thing was. It had told him what it did. He had learned at four that what a thing did and what it was were different questions, and that the second was the one worth asking, and now he had the thing itself on the blanket in front of him for sixteen years and no permission to make it do anything, and so he did the only thing left, which was to look.

He held it. That was all. Hours a night, under the stairs, after the broadcasts — he had kept the broadcasts; he would not have known how to stop — with the holly in his right hand and his eyes shut and nothing sent down it, and he listened to the line.

It had a grain. He had known that in the shop. What he had not known, and what sixteen years of holding told him, was what the grain was for. It was not a pipe. A pipe carried what you put in it and did not care what. This — he lay in the dark with the wand warm in his hand and read it the way he had read the roof from the step — this shaped. The sentence you sent down it came out the far end more itself: the pointing sharper, the pushing cleaner, the bracket held in a way that he, with his two hundred to one, could do by hand, and that nobody else alive could, and that the wand did for them, a little, without their knowing. That was why they could cast at all. Not because the wand carried the shout; because the wand tidied it, on the way through, enough that a ragged sentence sent with the eyes shut arrived at the far end as something the world could read.

And it did it according to its grain. Holly and phoenix tidied one way. Ebony and unicorn would have tidied another, and the other was the wrong turn, and a sentence tidied the wrong way for the mind that sent it came out less itself, not more, and the feather went into the ceiling.

It went into the Edition, in the marks, over four nights, and then in English, for the reader: The wand is not a telephone. I have been calling it that for five years and I was wrong, or not wrong enough. A telephone carries. This edits. It takes a sentence and sands it on the way through, the way a thousand years of hands sanded the Standard Books, and the sanding is the grain, and the grain has to match the hand. They have never known this because they have never once sent a sentence and watched it come out the far end; they shout and the wand tidies and the feather lifts and they think the word did it.

I can hold a sentence so steady it does not need sanding. So what does the wand do for me?

He did not know. He could not find out without sending something, and he had made a rule. But he turned the question over, night after night, in the dark, with the holly in his hand, and arrived at a guess that frightened him a little, in the good way, and gave it to the reader with provisional in capitals above it:

It will do what it does for everyone — carry, and tidy. But there will be nothing to tidy. So the whole of the wand’s attention — if a wand has attention; I think the grain IS its attention — will go into the carrying, and a sentence that arrives at the far end without a mark out of place, carried by a channel that has nothing else to do, will be the first sentence of its kind that anyone has sent since the first wizards, before wands, when people asked.

I think the light in the shop was that. I think four seconds steady was what it looks like when the sanding has nothing to do. I think I am going to find out on the first of September what else it looks like, and I think Flitwick — the Charms master; McGonagall says he thinks about this more than most — is going to need a chair.

The Trace theory he sent to McGonagall on the ninth of August, by Hedwig, and it was the first letter he had ever posted by owl, and he stood at the open window of the box room — Dudley’s room, now, by a negotiation Harry had not been party to and had watched with professional pleasure — and watched the white shape go up over the roofs of Little Whinging in the morning and turn north, and thought: a post from everywhere.

The letter was two pages, in the round hand, for a reader who was slow and had a department. It said what he thought the Trace watched for and why, and why it had never once registered a cat getting up off a kitchen floor or a pot warmed from forty yards or a feather that held, and it ended: I have not tested this and shall not. I am telling you because you asked me on a pavement to tell you before I did anything impossible, and I think this may be a thing that is impossible for a reason, and I should like someone who knows the reason to look at it before I am within a mile of a place where it might matter.

The reply came in two days, in green ink, on the back of his own second page, which he understood was a kind of compliment.

Dr Potter. Noted. Your theory is, as far as I can judge, correct, and nobody at the Ministry has ever thought of it because nobody at the Ministry has ever met a wizard who did not shout. Do not test it. I have shown it to the Charms master, who has asked me to tell you that he has read your two pages four times and has a chair ready. — M.M.

He read that eleven times, which was most of an hour, and put it in the box file with the Second Edition, because it was the first thing anyone on the far side had ever written to him about his work in their own hand, and it was two sentences long and had a chair ready in it, and he found he could not stop smiling at the ceiling of the cupboard for some time.

The goodbyes took the second half of the month, and he did them in order, because he had learned at five that the things you put off were the things you minded most, and he did not intend to arrive at King’s Cross with anything unsaid to anyone who had held a door.

Mr Pole first, on a Wednesday, in the geography room at Stonewall, over a board, because Mr Pole had lost to him every Wednesday for four years with undiminished pleasure and had earned a last game. Harry lost it. He did it carefully, over fifty-one moves, in a way that Mr Pole would not see and nobody else could have — a knight placed a square too far on the thirty-fourth move, a thing that looked like ambition and was a gift — and Mr Pole sat back at the end with his pipe-smelling jacket open and his face doing a thing it had not done in four years of Wednesdays and said, slowly, “Well, I’ll be damned,” and then, after a long moment, looking at the board, “You did that.”

“Yes, sir.”

“Why?”

“Because you’ve lost to me two hundred times and never once minded, and I wanted you to have one you could tell the club about.” He began putting the pieces back in the box. “And because I wanted to find out whether I could lose on purpose to someone who was really looking, and I could, and that’s worth knowing where I’m going.”

Mr Pole looked at the thirty-fourth move for a while longer.

“Son,” he said at last, “I have been playing this game for forty years and I have never seen a man throw a knight with that much love.” He shut the box. “Write to the club. Set us a problem a term. The juniors will want to know you’re alive.”

“There’s a post from everywhere, sir. I’ve got an owl.”

“Have you,” said Mr Pole, and did not ask, and shook his hand, and that was that.

Dr Lindqvist and Mrs Osei on the Thursday, in the room with the equipment that had cost more than the building. Mrs Osei said not like that once more, for the look of the thing, when he put a probe down the wrong way on purpose so that she could, and then she gave him a thing wrapped in a lab cloth, and it was a small brass instrument from a drawer that had not been opened since the 1950s, a thing for measuring a thing that nobody measured any more, and she said, “It still works. I checked. You never know what you’ll want to measure,” and Dr Lindqvist, from the doorway with his arms folded, said, “Potter. The fourth decimal place. Somebody will build on it one day and act very surprised. I should like you to know I shan’t,” and shook his hand, and Harry walked across the park to the copper roof for the last time with a brass instrument in his pocket and the strong sense that he had been given, by a technician, the single most useful object he would carry up the drive.

Carrow last of the university, on the Friday, in the office with the paper on every surface and the lake going grey outside, and she did not get up, because she did not do that, and she had the offprint of the 1979 paper on the desk between them — it had come that morning; eleven pages again, by a coincidence neither of them remarked on — and she pushed it an inch towards him, which was Carrow for take it.

“Scotland,” she said.

“Yes, Professor.”

“A sort of school.”

“Yes.”

“That you will not tell me about, and have never lied to me about, and are about to go off to for seven years with a doctorate and an owl.” She took her glasses off. “Potter. I have supervised nineteen students and I told nine countries you were the easiest. I did not tell them the other thing, because it was not their business, and I am going to tell you now, once, and you will not refer to it again.” She put the glasses back on. “You were the only one who ever made me better. Not quicker. Better. I have been a better mathematician since the day you walked up a long table with forty pages than I was in the thirty years before it, because I have had to be, to keep up, and I am fifty-two and I had not expected to get better again at anything.” She pushed a second thing across the desk. It was a stick of chalk, half used, from the board, in a paper sleeve. “That is from the back of the door. The overlap. I kept it. I find I should like you to have it, and I should like you to use it, wherever it is you are going, on whatever board they have, the first time you see a thing nobody else in the room can see. Which I imagine will be the first morning.”

He took the chalk. He could not, for a moment, say anything, and she saw that, and stood to one side of it.

“Write,” said Carrow. “Every term. You promised a physics teacher; you can promise me. And Potter — the comma on page ninety. It was right. Aldous was wrong. I checked.” The knife-face did its thing, one last time, at one corner. “Shut the door behind you. There’s a draught.”

Mrs Prentice on the Monday, after four, with the new aluminium ladder standing in the adult section like a stranger and the old one in the returns cupboard where she kept it.

He did not know how to begin, and so he did the crossword, and she let him, and at the end of it there was a seven in the bottom corner that was farewell and he did not fill it in.

“Well,” said Mrs Prentice, from the desk, not looking up from the stamp. “That’s that, then.”

“Yes.”

“Six years. You were five. You stood on the step and held up the whole of the Saturday morning and looked.” The stamp came down. “I knew then. I’ve had forty years at this desk and I knew in a second, and I thought: ah. There you are. Somebody’s got to hold the door.” She stamped another book. “And you held it back. The paper. The ladder. I never said. I’m saying now, once, and you’ll not refer to it.”

“No, Mrs Prentice.”

“Good.” She reached under the desk, into the place where the toffee tin had lived, and brought out a thing wrapped in a date-due slip, and it was a pencil. A stub of one. Worn to three inches, with teeth marks at the end, and the lead sharpened with a knife rather than a sharpener, the way a man does who has been sharpening pencils for forty years and does not hold with gadgets.

“His,” said Mrs Prentice. “The margins. Surely not and lovely and enough for one night. It was in the last box. I’ve had it in the tin since 1986 waiting for somebody worth giving it to, and I had decided it would be you by the cuckoo, and I have never once been wrong about a reader.” She pushed it across. “There’s a post from everywhere, I’m told. Only a question of stamps. The stamps are on me. They always were.”

He held the pencil. It weighed nothing. It was the heaviest thing he had ever been handed.

“Mrs Prentice,” he said. “Thank you. For October. For the Hilbert. For—”

“Oh, get on,” said Edith Prentice, who had never once in six years said get on to anyone and had plainly been saving it, and turned back to the stamp, and he saw that her hand was not quite steady, and did not refer to it, and went and sat with the crossword a while longer so that neither of them would have to say anything else, and then he went home.

The seven was farewell. He filled it in on the train, in the dead man’s pencil, and then sat with the paper on his knee for the rest of the line, not reading it, because he had read it, and because he wanted to sit with the clue.

Mr Haines he found by the simple method of going to Stonewall on a Tuesday in the holidays, when the building was empty and smelled of floor polish and the summer, and walking down the corridor to the physics room, where the door was open and a tall stooped man in a cardigan the colour of a wet pavement was writing on a board for nobody, because that was what Mr Haines did in August.

“Potter.”

“Sir.”

“Come to say goodbye, have you. Scotland.” He did not turn round. “The boy I told you about. The one who went. I never said what became of him, because I didn’t know, and I’ve wondered on and off for thirty years.” He put the chalk down. “Last month I found out. He wrote. Out of the blue, after thirty years, three pages, from an address I didn’t recognise, to say he had read about a boy of nine with a doctorate in the Telegraph, and it had put him in mind of a physics teacher in Surrey, and he thought he should say thank you while there was time, because he had meant to for thirty years and hadn’t.” Mr Haines turned round. “I don’t know what you are, Potter. I have known since question six that I don’t know. But I know that two boys have walked out of this room to somewhere I can’t follow, and the first one took thirty years to write, and I am too old to wait that long twice.”

“You won’t have to, sir. I’ve got an owl.”

Mr Haines’s eyebrows went up a quarter of an inch, and stayed there.

“An owl.”

“There’s a post from everywhere. You said so. It’s only a question of stamps.” Harry put his hand in his pocket and brought out the brass instrument Mrs Osei had given him and held it up. “And I’ve got this. It measures a thing nobody measures any more. I thought you might know what it was.”

Mr Haines looked at the instrument for a long moment. Then he took it, and turned it to the window, and his face did the thing it had done over question six.

“Good God,” he said quietly. “I haven’t seen one of these since 1958.” He handed it back. “Yes. I know what it is. Sit down, Potter. I shall tell you, and then you can go to Scotland, and you will write by the first of October or I shall come and find you, owl or no owl.”

He sat down. He was told. It took forty minutes, and it was the last physics lesson he had on that side of the curtain, and it was about a brass instrument from 1958 and a thing nobody measured any more, and he would not have traded it for the fourth floor.

Mrs Figg he saved for the last Saturday, in the garden, because he had saved everything that mattered for the garden since he was five.

The cats were in the attitudes of cats who had never had a bad day. Mr Tibbles sat on the wall with the lecture-hall face — fourteen now, and the hips had never once gone back — and watched the two of them on the bench.

“Well,” said Mrs Figg.

“Yes.”

“Five years of that café. I shall miss the scones. I shall miss—” she stopped, and looked at the geraniums, and did not finish it, and he let her not.

“Mrs Figg,” he said. “I want to tell you a thing, and I want to say it in the garden because this is where I asked you, and this is where you said the word.” He had built the sentence on the train from Egham, and had thrown it out, and had decided to say it without building. “I was five, and I had been small for three years, and I decided in a cupboard to stop, and I came out and I told Mrs Prentice and I told Mrs Bell and I told my aunt one sentence, and all of those were hard, and none of them were the hardest. The hardest was you. Because you were the one who knew, and the one who was kind, and I was afraid that if I asked, you’d have to choose, and you’d choose him, and I’d lose the one person in Little Whinging who had the good biscuits.” He stopped. “You didn’t choose him. You wrote to him. And then you spent his scone money and told him after. And you held my hand through a wall. And I’ve never — I don’t think I’ve ever said, properly, in a way that wasn’t thank you for writing — I don’t think I’d have got to any of it without you. The university, the degree, the books, the street. You held the door the whole time. You just held it from two streets away so I wouldn’t see your hand on it.”

Mrs Figg looked at the geraniums for a long time.

“I was posted here,” she said at last, “to watch for cloaks in the wrong season. Five years I watched. I saw none. And I thought, in the first year, before you could talk: this is a waste of an old woman. Nothing is going to come up this road but the milk.” She made a fist of her right hand, and opened it. “And then a boy of three walked past my gate holding a shopping basket with both arms because he’d said he would, and looked at everything, and I thought: ah. There you are. I never once minded after that. Not the café. Not the scone money. Not the letter telling him he was wrong, though I expect I shall hear about that at some length.” She reached down beside the bench, where there was a thing in a carrier bag that he had not noticed because he had been looking at her, and brought it out, and it was the Forth Bridge tin, and it was full.

“For the train,” she said. “And for whoever you sit with. There’ll be somebody. There always is. Give them the good ones first; it’s how you tell who’s worth keeping.” She put the tin in his lap. “And Harry — the summers.”

“For the roof. I promised.”

“Not for the roof. For me. I shall want to know what they’re like. The teachers. The man I write to; I’ve never once seen him in a room with a child and I should like to know how he does. The Charms one who’s got a chair ready.” She looked at him sideways, with the gladness, and the pride, and the thing that was entirely steady now and had been for a year. “I shall want it all. In the garden. With the good tin. You can leave out the parts that aren’t mine.”

“There won’t be any of those,” said Harry.

“Oh, get on,” said Arabella Figg, and turned to the geraniums so that he would not see her face, which he had already seen, and would keep.

On the wall, Mr Tibbles looked at Harry with the lecture-hall face, and then — slowly, deliberately, like a man signing a form he had been waiting five years to sign — closed his eyes, and went to sleep in the sun.

Petunia did not say goodbye. It was not a thing the house permitted. But on the last night of August she stood in the doorway of the cupboard — she had never once, in eight years, stood in that doorway — and looked at the trunk, and the swing on the wall, and the date pinned beside it with its four lines underneath, and the camp bed with the biscuit tin showing under it, and the wand on the blanket, and said, in the fence voice:

“You’ll want the box room when you’re back. Dudley can have it in term.”

“I’d rather keep this. If that’s all right.”

Petunia looked at the cupboard for a long moment, with the face with the wall out of it.

“It’s all right,” she said. And then, after a while, not in the fence voice: “I never came in here. Eight years. I thought it would be — I thought I’d see something I couldn’t stand.” She looked at the swing. “It’s a study.”

“Yes. It always was. From about three.” He stood up. “I’d like to leave the swing on the wall. And the date. If you wouldn’t mind not — if you wouldn’t mind leaving them.”

“I shan’t touch anything.” She turned to go, and stopped, and said, to the hall: “There are sandwiches in the fridge for the morning. And a flask. It’s a long way to Scotland.”

“Aunt Petunia.”

“What.”

“Thank you. For the roof.” He hesitated. “And for coming in. Both times. And for the box with the swing in it. And for—”

“Harry,” said Petunia Dursley, and her voice did a thing it had done once before, across a kitchen table, with a shoebox between them, “get some sleep. Your uncle wants to be on the road by eight so he can tell the Rotary about the traffic.”

She went. He sat on the camp bed in the smallest office in England with a trunk at his feet and a wand on the blanket and a dead man’s pencil in his pocket and a brass instrument from 1958 and a stick of chalk from the back of a door and a Forth Bridge tin full of the good ones, and he did not sleep, and he had known he would not, and for the last time under those stairs he took stock, in English, in his head.

Eleven. The better part of two thousand, and I have stopped counting, and I am counting tonight because it is the last night I shall count here.

I have a telephone and have not used it, and have found out what it is by not using it, and I think it is going to be more than anyone knows. I have four hundred and three questions in order. I have a theory about the cold that a Charms master has read four times. I have the asking, and the seal, and a design for a press that will not be allowed and may be, and a woman in tartan who said as it stands.

I have a swing on a wall, and I am leaving it. I have a woman who held a door and a woman who held my hand and a woman who held a roof, and a man with a knife for a face who got better, and a man with chalk on his elbows who waited thirty years for a letter and will not wait that long again. I have a cousin who said good luck.

Tomorrow there is a wall at King’s Cross with a sentence in it, and I have been told where, so it will not trouble to turn me, and I am going to read it on the way through anyway, and sit on a train with a tin of biscuits, and give the good ones first.

Then the step. Then the blankets. Then the cold.

Then everything else.

He looked at the inside of the cupboard door one more time — the date, and Faster, and They shout; I can hold, and He listened once; find out who else can, and The cold has a grammar — and he did not add anything, because there was nothing left to add that could be written on a door.

Then he turned the light off, and lay in the dark, and listened to the house tick, for the last time as the only one who knew how long each tick was, and did not mind that at all.

Contents

Chapter 21

Platform

Vernon drove, because Vernon drove, and talked about the traffic for the whole of the A3, and Petunia sat in the front with the flask on her knee and said nothing, and Dudley sat in the back with Harry and the owl and did not elbow him once, which was a thing Harry noticed at the Guildford turn and filed, and was still thinking about at Wandsworth.

King’s Cross at half past ten on the first of September smelled of diesel and pigeons, and he stood on the concourse with the trunk on a trolley and Hedwig on top of the trunk and his family round him in the attitudes of people who did not know how to do this, and looked at the departure boards, and then at the platforms, nine and ten, and the brick pillar between them with a newspaper kiosk against it, and did not feel the hand come.

It was there. He could see it working — the same age as the Charing Cross Road, and written, he could tell now, with five years of grammar behind him, by the same school of sentence that had built the wall in the yard: for those who know, a way. A woman with a pushchair went past the pillar and her eyes slid off a stretch of brick and she did not know they had. But it did not touch him. He had been told, on a pavement, by a woman in tartan, where the door was, and the sentence could tell that he knew, and left him alone.

So he read it instead. The hand was only the first half; the second half was the brick itself, which was a door that did not look like one and did not open like one, and he could see, now that nothing was turning his eyes, exactly where the sentence said go.

“Through there,” said Harry. “The pillar. Between nine and ten. You walk at it.”

“You walk at a wall,” said Vernon.

“Yes, Uncle Vernon. It isn’t a wall for me. It’s a wall for you.” He looked at Petunia. “Aunt Petunia. I don’t think you can come through. I think it’s — I think it’s for people who can see it. I’m sorry.”

Petunia looked at the pillar for a long moment with the flask on her knee — she had carried it from the car and had not yet handed it over — and her face did a thing he had seen once, at a lake, on a bench, with a magazine she had not read.

“No,” she said. “No. I know. It was the same for her. Mum and Dad stood where I’m standing.” She held out the flask. “Take this. It’s tea. The sandwiches are in the trunk, on top, in the greaseproof.”

He took the flask.

“Aunt Petunia—”

“Go on,” said Petunia Dursley, in the fence voice, and then, not in it: “Write to the house. Not to him.” A jerk of the head at Vernon, who was examining the departure boards. “To the house. I shall know it’s for me.”

“Yes.”

“And come back in the summer.”

“For the roof.”

“For the roof,” she agreed, and did not say the other thing, and did not need to, and he saw her hands, which were in her good gloves, close once on nothing, and open.

Dudley had been standing with his hands in the pockets of the Smeltings blazer that he hated, looking at the owl. Now he took one hand out, and there was a thing in it, and the thing was a football cap, old, from a club Dudley had supported for a fortnight in 1987, that came down over the forehead to the eyebrows, and that had been in a drawer in Dudley’s room for four years and had not, Harry had assumed, been missed.

“You’ll want this,” said Dudley. He did not look at Harry. He looked at the owl. “For — you know. If you don’t want them looking at you.”

Harry took the cap.

He had worn it four times through a wall with an old woman’s hand in his, and read his own history in it on a bench, and he had put it back in Dudley’s drawer each time without a word, and he had not known until this moment, on the concourse at King’s Cross, with the diesel and the pigeons, that Dudley had known. Had known, every time. Had said nothing, every time. Had kept the cap in the drawer and not asked and not told Vernon, for four years, which was — Harry did the sum, standing there, and it came out larger than he could easily hold — which was the longest Dudley Dursley had ever kept anything in his life, including a secret, including a kindness.

“Thank you,” said Harry. “Dudley. Thank you. I didn’t know you knew.”

“Course I knew,” said Dudley, to the owl. “It’s my cap.” And then, red, fast, to the pillar: “Good luck. Again. At the — thing.”

“Good luck at Smeltings.”

“Yeah,” said Dudley, and put his hand back in his pocket, and that was all, and it was the first conversation the two of them had ever had that was not about a want, and Harry put it on the shelf with the straight fingers and the swing, and went to push a trolley at a wall.

He did not look back. He had decided he would not, in the car, somewhere around Wandsworth, because he had learned at the bottom of some steps in Egham what a person looks like who is watching someone go somewhere they cannot follow, and he did not want his aunt to have to arrange her face for him twice in one morning.

He walked at the pillar, and declined, and went through.

The train was scarlet and the platform was full and the noise of it came at him like the first day at Waterloo, except that every face in it was theirs — cloaks and hats and cardigans, a boy with a toad, a family of redheads in a knot round a mother, an owl shouting at another owl — and he stopped, on the threshold, as he had stopped on every threshold of his life that mattered, and looked.

Ten seconds. A long while.

Then he found an empty compartment at the back, because the back was where the people who did not know anyone sat, and he wanted to see who did not know anyone. He got the trunk up with help from two redheaded twins who were plainly used to it and who looked at his forehead and at each other and did not say anything, which he filed as decent, and put Hedwig on the seat by the window and the Forth Bridge tin on the seat beside him, and sat down with his back to the engine, which was the way to see the platform, and watched.

He had decided on the train from Egham in August what he was for, on this train. Not to be looked at.
