He was going to be looked at; he had read the index; there was nothing to be done about it except wear the cap, and he had decided he would not wear the cap, because a boy who hid his face on the first morning would have to go on hiding it. He was for looking. Figg had said there would be somebody, and that you gave them the good ones first, and he had a tin, and seven hours, and every face on that platform was a page he had not read.

He read them. The redheaded knot — six of them, and a mother who hugged like she meant it, and a small girl who was not going and was crying about it, and a tall one with a badge who was being important at the others. The pale boy from Madam Malkin’s, with a father who looked like a man who had never once been looked at the way Harry looked at him now, and who looked back, briefly, down the platform, and whose face did a thing that Harry filed under knows exactly who I am and has thought about what to do with that, and set on the shelf for later. A girl with a great deal of brown hair and her nose in a book, being seen off by two people in Muggle coats who had the faces of dentists or accountants and were trying not to cry, and who were, he saw at once, the first people on that platform who looked the way Petunia had looked at the pillar. A round boy with a toad and a grandmother in a hat with a bird on it, and the toad already looking for a way out.

Somebody. There was always somebody.

The whistle went, and the platform began to move, and he did not wave at anyone, because there was no one on that platform to wave at, and he found that he minded that less than he had expected and more than he would have liked, and he put it on the shelf too.

The round boy with the toad was the first, and he came in crying, which Harry had expected, because he had watched the toad make its decision through the window at a quarter past eleven.

“Have you — sorry — have you seen a toad? Trevor. He’s — I’ve lost him, again, I always—”

“Sit down,” said Harry. “Please. Have a biscuit; they’re the good ones. I saw him go. He went left out of your compartment at eleven sixteen, and toads go down and damp, and the nearest down-and-damp on a train is the lavatory at the end of this carriage, and the door was open at the time. He’ll be behind the pipe under the basin. They always get behind the pipe.”

The round boy stood in the doorway with his mouth open.

“How do you — you saw him?”

“I see most things. It’s a habit. I’m Harry.” He held out the tin. “What’s your name?”

“Neville. Longbottom.” He took a biscuit, mechanically, and did not eat it. “Behind the pipe?”

“Behind the pipe. I’ll come, if you like. He’ll be cold; it’s a cold pipe. We should put him somewhere warm after.”

He was behind the pipe. Neville got him out, with Harry holding the door, and stood in the corridor with a cold toad in both hands and his face doing the thing Harry had seen Mrs Osei’s do over an acceptance letter, and said, “Nobody ever — people don’t usually help. With Trevor. They just sort of—”

“I know,” said Harry. “I’ve been the toad.” He said it before he had decided to, and it was true, and Neville looked at him, and something in the round frightened face settled a quarter-inch, and Harry thought: there. Good ones first.

They put Trevor in the Forth Bridge tin, with the lid off and the biscuits moved to a seat, because the tin had been warm in Harry’s lap for an hour and a toad is not particular about what it sits in. Neville sat down opposite and did not leave, and Harry did not ask him to, and that was the first.

The second was the redhead, and he came in because every other compartment was full, and said so, and sat down.

“Ron. Weasley. Everywhere else is — is that a toad? In a biscuit tin?”

“He’s cold,” said Neville, defensively.

“Right,” said Ron. He looked at Harry. He looked at Harry’s forehead. He looked away, red, and then — this was the thing Harry noticed, and liked at once, and filed under decent with the twins — he looked back, deliberately, at Harry’s face and not his forehead, and said, in the voice of someone making himself do a thing, “Sorry. Fred and George said you were on. I didn’t — you don’t have to—”

“I know,” said Harry. “It’s all right. You can look. Everyone’s going to; I decided this morning I wasn’t going to mind, and I nearly don’t.” He pushed the biscuits across. “Have one of the good ones. My — a woman who looked after me packed them. She said to give the good ones first.”

Ron took one. He ate it. He looked at the tin, and the toad, and Neville, and the owl, and Harry, and said, with his mouth full, “Is it true you’ve got a degree? Fred said — but Fred says things—”

“Yes.”

“A Muggle degree.”

“Yes. In mathematics. It’s a kind of — it’s a piece of paper that says you can do a thing.” He hesitated, because this was the first time he had said any of it to someone on this side who was his own age, and he found he wanted to say it right. “It doesn’t mean anything here. I know that. I didn’t get it to mean anything here. I got it to find out whether I could, and I could, and now I’m here, and I don’t know anything about this—” he gestured at the train, the owl, the toad in the tin “—except what’s in books, and I’ve been told the books are wrong about a lot of it, and I was hoping somebody would tell me where.”

Ron looked at him for a long moment, with the biscuit halfway to his mouth, and Harry watched him do the sum that everyone in that world did — Boy Who Lived; a degree; here, on a train, asking — and arrive at a total that was not the one he had come in with.

“The books are wrong about Quidditch,” said Ron, with great firmness. “Quidditch Through the Ages says the Cannons are a mid-table side. They’re not. They’re rubbish. They’re my team.” He took another biscuit. “What else d’you want to know?”

“Everything,” said Harry. “In order. Start with the Cannons.”

Ron started with the Cannons. It took forty minutes, and it was the first forty minutes of Harry’s life in which someone his own age had told him something he did not know and could not have read, and he listened to all of it, at a speed he had never once in ten years been able to listen to anyone, which was to say with a quarter of his mind, and the other three-quarters watching Ron Weasley’s face do the thing faces did when they were telling you about a thing they loved, and he thought: this is the thing of which the café was the small dose.

The third was the girl with the hair, and she did not come in because the other compartments were full. She came in because she had heard, three compartments down, that Harry Potter was in the back carriage with a degree, and she wanted to see whether it was true, and she said so, in the doorway, in a sentence with no pause in it anywhere.

“Is it true you’ve got a doctorate from the University of London because I read about it in the Telegraph last year and my parents said it must be a different Harry Potter because the Harry Potter in Modern Magical History is a wizard and wizards don’t go to Muggle universities but then I saw you on the platform and you’ve got the — well — and I thought it can’t be but I wanted to ask because nobody ever just asks they just say things, I’m Hermione Granger, is it true?”

Neville had gone small. Ron had the face of a boy watching a thing happen that he had been warned about and had not believed. Harry, who had spent ten years reading faces at two hundred to one and had never once been talked at faster than he could think, sat with the Forth Bridge tin on his knee and the toad in it and found that he was — he examined it carefully, because it was new — he was delighted.

“Yes,” he said. “It’s true. Sit down. Have a biscuit; they’re the good ones. What’s your question?”

“That was my question.”

“No it wasn’t. That was the thing you asked. Your question is why. You want to know why a wizard would bother.” He moved the biscuits to make room. “It’s a good question. Nobody else has asked it. The Deputy Headmistress hasn’t asked it. I’d like to answer it, if you’ll tell me yours first. Why did you read Modern Magical History before you got here? Nobody reads that. It’s dull. I know; I’ve read it four times.”

Hermione Granger sat down. She did it the way Carrow had sat down over forty pages at a long table: as if her legs had made the decision before she had.

“Because I wanted to know what I was walking into,” she said. “Because they sent a letter and a woman in a hat and said I was a witch and gave me a list, and nobody told me what the rules were, and I don’t go anywhere without knowing the rules, so I read everything on the list and then I read everything the shop had that wasn’t on the list, and Modern Magical History was the only one that was about now, and it was mostly about you, and you were on the platform.” She took a biscuit. “That’s not an answer to why you’d bother with a Muggle degree.”

“No. Here’s mine. I bothered because it was the only room I’d ever been in where they judged the thing on the board instead of the face of the person who wrote it. A child of eight can stand up in front of a man from Cambridge who came to fail him, and if the thing holds, it holds, and the man has to say so. I wanted to find out what that felt like. It felt like being seen.” He looked at her. “You read everything on the list and everything not on the list because nobody told you the rules. I did the same thing, for the same reason, starting at five. I think we’re going to get on, Hermione Granger, and I think the first thing you should know about me is that I am going to ask you questions back, and I am going to ask them fast, and nobody has ever once kept up, and I would very much like it if you did.”

She looked at him for a long moment, with a biscuit in her hand, and he watched her — he had all the time in the world to watch her — go through surprise, and suspicion, and arithmetic, and arrive somewhere he recognised, because he had seen Carrow arrive there over page nine.

“Go on, then,” said Hermione.

“What is a spell?”

“A — it’s a — ” She stopped. He watched her stop. It was, he thought, the finest thing he had seen on the train. “The book says it’s an incantation and a wand movement that—”

“That’s what it does. What is it?”

“I don’t—” She put the biscuit down. “Nobody’s asked me that. I’ve read seven books and none of them say. They say what to say and where to point and what happens. They don’t say what it is.”

“No,” said Harry. “They don’t. I’ve read all seven and forty others and I can tell you exactly where each of them stops saying, and it’s always the same page. Page one. They stop before they start.” He leaned forward. “I think I know what it is. I’ve thought I knew since I was six. I’ve written it down and nobody can read what I wrote because I had to invent the marks. I’m going to find out this year whether I’m right, in a classroom, with a man who’s got a chair ready, and I would like — I think I would like someone who reads everything on the list and everything not on the list to be in the room when I do, because I have never once had anyone in the room, and I’m told it’s better.”

Hermione Granger did not say anything for three seconds, which for him was most of a morning, and he spent the morning watching her face and thinking that he had been right on the platform, that there was always somebody, and that Figg had been right about the biscuits, and that this was what chasing looked like from the outside, because he had never once in his life been able to see it from the inside.

“What marks?” said Hermione.

“I’ll show you,” said Harry. “Not today. When I know you better. They took me four years and they’re in a biscuit tin and there’s a toad in the other one.” He pushed the tin across. “Have another. Ron was explaining the Cannons. He says the books are wrong about them.”

“The books are wrong about everything,” said Hermione, with feeling, and took another, and did not leave, and that was the third.

The fourth came at half past one, and did not sit down, and was the pale boy from Madam Malkin’s, with two larger boys behind him in the attitudes of furniture.

“So it’s true. They’re saying all down the train. Harry Potter’s in the back with—” he looked at Ron, at Neville, at Hermione, at the toad in the tin, and his face arranged itself into a thing it had plainly been practising since the stool “—with company. I’m Malfoy. Draco Malfoy.”

Ron made a sound. Draco Malfoy heard it, and turned, and the practised face got a line ready, and Harry — who had watched this boy’s father on the platform for eleven seconds and read the whole of what he intended off the side of his face — said, before the line could land:

“Your father knows exactly who I am. I saw him look. He’s thought about what to do about it; I saw that too. You haven’t. You’ve come to see what he’d want you to see.” He said it without any edge at all, because he had none; he had seen this boy’s face in the shop go quiet in a way that had nothing to do with the father, and he had filed it under later, and later had arrived. “Sit down, if you like. Have a biscuit. They’re good ones. I’d rather find out what you think than what he does, and I don’t think anybody has ever asked you that, because I know what a boy looks like who’s never been asked. I was one.”

The compartment was very quiet.

Draco Malfoy stood in the doorway with his hand half-out for a line he had rehearsed and found that the line had nowhere to go. Harry watched him look for it — at Ron, who was red; at Hermione, who had her chin up; at Neville, who had gone small — and not find it anywhere, because nobody in the compartment had done anything he had been told to expect, and the boy had not, Harry saw, been told what to do when that happened. Nobody had told him there was a when.

“I don’t—” said Draco, and stopped.

“No,” said Harry. “I didn’t think you would. It’s all right. You don’t have to decide anything on a train.” He put a biscuit on the seat by the door, where a boy could take it or not without anyone watching him do either. “That one’s yours if you want it. The offer’s open. So’s the question. I ask everyone the same one; you can have it now or later. What is a spell?”

Draco looked at the biscuit.

“That’s a first-year question,” he said, with contempt that did not quite reach his face.

“It is,” said Harry. “Nobody in seven books can answer it. Try.”

The pale boy stood a moment longer. Then he said, to the two large boys behind him, without turning, “Come on,” and went, and the biscuit stayed on the seat, and nobody touched it, and at Peterborough it was gone, and Harry had not seen who took it, which was the first thing in a year he had not seen, and he filed that too, under later, and under something else that he did not yet have a word for, and thought might be hope.

“That,” said Ron, when the door had shut, “was Malfoy. His dad was — my dad says — you gave him a biscuit.”

“I gave him a question,” said Harry. “The biscuit was so he wouldn’t have to answer it in front of you. His father knows who I am. I’d like to know who he is before his father tells him.” He looked at the empty seat by the door. “And I’ve been the boy nobody asked. It’s a cold place. I don’t leave people in it if I can help it, even ones I don’t like yet.”

Hermione was looking at him with the face from over the biscuit.

“Yet,” she said.

“Yet. I don’t know him. I know his face in a shop. That’s a page, not a book.” He turned to Neville. “Trevor’s warm. Feel the tin.”

Neville felt the tin.

The sandwiches were in greaseproof, on top of the trunk, and there were enough for four, which he had not expected and which told him that his aunt had seen the platform in her head before she had seen it with her eyes, and had known there would be somebody, and had cut accordingly.

He gave them out. Ron had three. Hermione had one and talked through it. Neville had one and fed the crust to the toad, who was not interested. Harry had the flask, which was tea, and was still warm, and he put his hand on the side of it and let it be warm for a quarter-second longer than it had been and nobody saw, and he thought: two hundred miles. She made it for the whole way.

“Your mum made these?” said Ron, with his mouth full.

“My aunt. My — I live with my aunt and uncle. My parents are—” He stopped, because it was the first time he had had to say it to anyone who did not already know, and found he did not know the shape of the sentence. “You know what my parents are. It’s in the book Hermione read.”

“Sorry,” said Ron, red. “I didn’t—”

“It’s all right. You asked about the sandwiches. The sandwiches are my aunt. She didn’t want me to come here and she’s kept a promise about it for ten years and she cut enough for four.” He looked at the greaseproof. “She can’t see the platform. She stood where her parents stood when my mother went, and handed me a flask, and said write to the house. I said I would.”

The compartment was quiet for a moment, in the way compartments are when something has been said that is larger than the sandwiches.

“Mine cried,” said Hermione, abruptly. “On the platform. Both of them. They tried not to.”

“I saw. They looked like my aunt.” He hesitated, and then said it, because she had chased and had earned it: “I think everyone whose parents stood on that side of the barrier this morning should probably sit together. I think we’re going to need each other for things the others won’t understand. That’s not a rule. It’s a — it’s a thing I noticed.”

“You notice a lot,” said Hermione.

“Yes. It’s the only thing I’m actually good at. Everything else came from that.” He put the flask down. “What house do you want?”

She told him. At length, with reasons, from Hogwarts: A History, and he listened to all of it, and then asked her why the book said Gryffindor was the best and whether a book written by a Gryffindor might have a view, and she opened her mouth and shut it and said, “Oh,” in the voice of someone who had just found a comma in the wrong place, and he thought: there. She’s going to be the first one I show the marks to. Not today. But soon.

The country went north and went dark, and the train went on, and at some point Neville fell asleep with his hand on the Forth Bridge tin, and Ron fell asleep with his head against the window and his mouth open, and Hermione did not fall asleep but stopped talking, which was rarer, and sat with Hogwarts: A History open on her knee at the chapter about the Sorting and did not read it, and looked out at the dark.

Harry did not sleep. He had not expected to. He sat with his back to the engine and the swing in his inside pocket and the holly in his other pocket, on the hook still, and Mr Prentice’s pencil and Carrow’s chalk and Mrs Osei’s brass instrument from 1958 in the bag at his feet, and the box file in the trunk with nine hundred and forty pages in it that nobody alive could read and that he had decided on this train, somewhere past Peterborough, that a girl with a great deal of hair was going to learn to, and he looked out at Scotland coming, and took stock, because Carrow had taught him to, and he did it without a pen, in the long quiet, for nobody.

A toad found by looking. A boy who told me about the Cannons for forty minutes and I listened with a quarter of my mind and it was the best forty minutes. A girl who chases. A boy who didn’t take the biscuit in front of anyone and took it at Peterborough. Four sandwiches. A flask still warm, which was me, a little, and her, mostly.

Figg was right. There was somebody. There were three.

Tonight: a hat, and a hall, and a man with a very strong cup of tea and a letter in his pocket, and a question first, and then four hundred and two others. Tomorrow a chair.

Then the step. Then the blankets. Then the cold.

Then everything else.

The train began to slow. Hermione looked up from the chapter about the Sorting.

“Are you nervous?” she said. Quietly, so as not to wake the others. It was, he realised, the first personal question anyone his own age had ever asked him.

He thought about it honestly, which was the only way he knew how.

“No,” he said. “I’ve been getting ready for this since I was five. I’ve been getting ready for it longer than that, in a way I can’t explain yet. I’m not nervous.” He looked out at the dark, where there were lights now, a long way off, high up, a great many of them. “I’m something else. I haven’t got a word for it. I’ve got seven languages and none of them have it.”

“What does it feel like?”

He thought about a doorstep, and a cupboard, and a kitchen floor, and a long table, and a bench, and a board with chalk on the back of a door, and a woman in tartan saying as it stands, and a flask that was still warm.

“Like the lights coming on,” said Harry Potter. “In a library. When you’re already inside.”

Contents

Chapter 22

The Hat

The castle came up out of the dark across the lake like a thing that had been waiting a long time to be looked at properly, and he looked at it properly, from a boat, with Neville gripping the gunwale and Hermione saying the dates of the towers under her breath and Ron saying nothing at all for the first time in seven hours, and he thought: a sentence in a place. The biggest one he had ever stood in front of. Older than the wall, older than the roof, so old and so many-handed that he could not read it from the boat at all, only feel the hum of it come across the water like the street in the Alley turned up to the sky.

He stopped reading. He had learned at the roses that there were things you read and things you let pass through you, and he let the castle pass through him, in a boat, at eleven, and did not try to understand it, and that was the first time in his life he had decided not to.

McGonagall was in the entrance hall in the tartan, which he had known she would be, and she looked down the line of first-years with the face that had declined to be impressed in a lounge in Surrey five weeks ago, and when her eyes came to him they stopped for exactly as long as they stopped on everyone else, and then went on, and he thought: good. She’s not going to make it easy. He had not wanted her to.

She told them about the houses. He had read it. He listened anyway, in case she went a different way, and she did, slightly, at the end, where the book said your house will be like your family and she said your house will be where you are judged first, by people who have not met you, and I should like you to bear in mind that they will be wrong, and he filed that, and looked at Hermione, who had heard it too, and whose chin had gone up.

Then the doors.

The Great Hall he had read about in two books and seen a drawing of in one, and none of them had said the thing that mattered, which was the sound. Four hundred people and a ceiling that was the sky, and the hum of the place under all of it — the castle’s sentence, up close now, in the stone under his feet and the air over his head, so thick that the candles floating in it seemed to be floating on it — and he walked up the middle of it in a line of eleven-year-olds with every face in the room turning to find the scar, and he did not wear the cap, and he did not look at the floor, and he found that he could do it, and that he had known since the pillar that he could.

The staff table. He read it in the four seconds it took to walk the last twenty feet, which was most of a quarter-hour, and he did it the way he had read the platform, for who did not know anyone.

A very small man at the end with a great deal of white hair and an expression of such naked, bouncing delight at the sight of the first-years that Harry knew him at once — a chair ready — and liked him before he had said a word. A woman with earth under her nails. A woman with a face like a hawk and a tray of something. A pale thin man in a turban who was looking at the first-years and whose face Harry read, and read again, and stopped on, because there was something in it that did not sit right — not the nervousness; nervousness was ordinary; something under the nervousness, a wrongness in the way the face was held, as though two people were holding it and had not agreed how. He had never seen a face like it. He filed it where he filed things that had edges, under later, and under look again, and went on.

A man with black hair and a face like a shut door, who was looking at Harry, and whose look went into him like a splinter, and who looked away, and whose face, in the quarter-second before it shut entirely, did a thing Harry had seen once, on his aunt, across a kitchen table, at the word eyes. He filed that too, carefully, under later, and under her, and did not understand it, and intended to.

And in the middle, in the big chair, with half-moon spectacles catching the candles and a beard that went to his belt, the man who had written a sentence on a doorstep in the time it took to put down a basket, and had paid for books without a word, and had told him which chapter to read, and had asked him four questions through a squib and had the answers in a drawer, and had a letter in his pocket that Harry could not see and knew was there the way he knew the hum of the castle was there.

Dumbledore was looking at him.

Not the way the room was looking — at the scar, at the story, at the morning they had woken up to. The way Carrow had looked at forty pages. The way Aldous had looked at page ninety. The way a man looks at a thing he has read about for five years in other people’s handwriting and is now, for the first time, allowed to see.

Harry looked back. He had decided on the train that he would, and he did, for the whole of the four seconds, and at the end of them Albus Dumbledore inclined his head, one inch, the way a man from Cambridge had in a hall with long windows, and Harry understood that the man had read his receipts and his two pages about the cold and had decided what he was before he arrived, and had just, in one inch, let him know it.

He inclined his own, one inch, back. Tomorrow at nine, he thought, as clearly as if it were on a shelf. The step first.

Then there was a stool, and a hat.

The hat sang. He had read that it did, and had not believed the book, and it did, and the song was doggerel, and he listened to all of it with a quarter of his mind, because the other three-quarters were on the thing under the song, which was the hat itself, which was — he stood in the line of first-years with his heart beginning to go — which was a sentence.

Not a sentence in a place, like the castle. Not a sentence in a thing, like the wall. A sentence that thought. He could feel it, from twenty feet, the way he had felt the cold off six pages by a clerk: a thing that held itself, that cast itself over and over, that had been doing so for a thousand years in a hat — and that was kind. That was the difference, and he felt it so clearly that he nearly said it aloud. The clerk’s shapes ate. This one asked.

A sentence that eats sentences, except that it doesn’t eat. It reads.

He had written, at nine, under the stairs, nobody has tried to read one; everybody has tried to keep them off; different question. He had been writing about the cold. He had not known there was one in a hat.

“Abbott, Hannah.”

He watched. He had five years of watching faces behind him and he watched the hat go on and come off, and he timed it — four seconds, nine, two, eleven — and he watched what happened to the children’s faces in the gap, and it was the thing he had seen in the sports hall at six: the weather of a mind being asked something. Some of them argued. He could see it. Some of them simply sat.

“Granger, Hermione.”

She went up the way she had come into the compartment, with no pause in her anywhere, and sat, and the hat went on, and he watched her face, and her face did a thing he had not seen it do on the train, which was stop. Hold. The way Carrow’s had on the back of a door. And then — he could see it; nobody else in the hall could have — she argued. Her lips did not move but her chin went up and her eyes went to the ceiling that was the sky and she was, he would have bet the Forth Bridge tin, laying out the case for Gryffindor from Hogwarts: A History, and the hat was asking her who had written Hogwarts: A History, and she was saying oh.

Forty-one seconds. A long time, for them.

“RAVENCLAW!”

The blue table cheered. Hermione took the hat off with the face of someone who had just lost an argument and was not sorry, and went to the blue table, and looked back once at the line — at him — with a look that said you did that, and he thought: I asked a question. You did the rest.

“Longbottom, Neville.”

Neville sat on the stool as if it might be taken away from him. The hat went on and stayed on, and Harry watched the round face go through fear and more fear and then, very slowly, something else, something he had seen on it in a corridor with a cold toad in both hands — nobody ever helps — and the hat said “GRYFFINDOR!” and Neville got up and walked off with the hat still on and had to come back for it, and the red table laughed, and Harry did not, because he had read what the hat had seen, and it had seen what Harry had seen behind a pipe, and he thought: good. Somebody else looked.

“Malfoy, Draco.”

The hat barely touched him. “SLYTHERIN!” And the pale boy went to the green table with his father’s walk and did not look at the line, and then, sitting down, did — once, quickly, at Harry — and looked away, and Harry thought about a biscuit on a seat at Peterborough and did not know what the look meant and did not pretend to.

And then the M’s went by, and the N’s, and the O’s, and a girl called Patil went to the blue table and her twin to the red, and the hall had got quiet in a way it had not been quiet before, because everyone in it could count, and then:

“Potter, Harry.”

The silence as he walked to the stool was the loudest thing he had ever heard. He had read the index. He had sat on a bench. He knew what he was to them. He walked through it the way he had walked at the pillar, declining, and sat down on the stool, and McGonagall’s hands put the hat on his head, and it came down over his eyes, and the hall went away, and there was a voice.

Oh, said the voice. Oh. You’re fast.

He had never in his life heard anyone say that to him. He had never in his life been in a conversation where the other party could tell. He sat on the stool in the dark of the hat with the hall gone and found that his heart, which had been going since the song, had stopped going and started doing something else, something he had not felt since a kitchen floor at six when a thing in him had come up to meet a thing that was wrong.

You can tell, he said. He did not say it with his mouth. He had never needed to; the magic had never needed a mouth; and the hat, it seemed, did not either.

Of course I can tell. I’m sitting in it. A pause, which was not a pause, which was the hat doing something very like what Harry did when he went back along a shelf. Good heavens. Good heavens. How long have you been—

Since a doorstep. Fifteen months old. Two hundred to one, or near enough; I measured it at four with washers and string.

Two hundred. Another not-pause. I have been on heads for a thousand years, boy, and I have never once been on one where I was the slow party. Do you know how strange this is? I am having to wait for you to finish thinking. Nobody has ever— The voice stopped, and when it came back it had a thing in it that Harry recognised from Carrow, from Aldous, from a very small man at the end of the staff table with bouncing hair: Oh, this is going to be the best Sorting I have had since 1710. Show me. All of it. I can keep up. Go.

He showed it.

He had never shown anyone. There had never been anyone who could be shown; Mrs Figg had the shape of it, Carrow had the mathematics, McGonagall had the receipts, and none of them had ever once been able to stand inside a quarter-second with him and see. The hat could. He opened the shelves — not all of them; he had decided on the stool what the hat would get and what it would not, because he was eleven and had a rule about rooms — and let the hat walk them, at his speed, which the hat could nearly match, and the hat walked.

A cupboard. A playpen, and a star crossing a gap between rooftops. A woman at a cooker saying just like. The marks. A cat getting up off a kitchen floor. It has parts. A pot warmed from forty yards. A pub found by declining. An index on a bench with an old woman’s hand in his. A long table and page nine. A roof read from a step. Six pages by a clerk. Nine hundred and forty pages in a box file. The asking. The seal. The feather that held. A light in a shop for four seconds. A woman in tartan saying as it stands. A flask still warm at York.

It took the hat most of a minute to walk it, which for Harry was most of a morning, and he sat in the dark and let it, and when the hat came back it did not say anything for a long moment, which was the first time Harry had ever felt a thing older than himself be quiet because it did not know what to say.

Well, said the hat at last. Well. I am sworn to secrecy, you know. Always have been. It is in the sentence they made me with; I could not tell him if I wanted to and I find — this is new; I shall have to think about it — I find I don’t want to. This is yours. You should be the one to say it, when you say it. A pause. You know what I am.

A sentence that thinks. Self-holding. Cast once, a thousand years ago, and casting itself ever since. Harry hesitated, and then said it, because the hat had earned it. I read about the things in the prison in the sea when I was nine. A clerk said they fed. I wrote down that if they ate sentences they were made of them, and that nobody had ever tried to read one. You’re what I think they are. Except you don’t eat. You read.

The hat was quiet again.

Yes, it said, eventually, in a voice that had lost its bounce. Yes. Nobody has ever put it that way to me. I do not like it. But yes. We are — cousins, perhaps. The same grammar, bent two ways. And then, sharper: And you mean to read them.

Yes.

It will be the most dangerous thing anyone has ever done in this school.

I know. It’s eighth on a list. I am going to do the first seven first.

Hm, said the hat, which was, Harry thought, the exact sound Mrs Prentice made, and he nearly laughed on the stool.

And then the hat did a thing he had not been ready for, and it did it gently, the way you would hold out your hands to a child to show him a trick.

There is something in here that isn’t yours.

He went very still, in the dark.

You know that, said the hat. I can see that you know. You have it behind a door.

Yes. He said it carefully, because this was the one shelf he had decided the hat would not be shown, and the hat had found it anyway, the way the asking would have, and he supposed that was fair. I found it when I was seven. The night I read the index, on the train home, with the book in a bag against my leg. I went back along the shelf to the green light and I found — there’s a grain in it. In the memory, and in me. Small, and cold, and it isn’t mine, and it doesn’t like me, and I didn’t know what it was and I knew what it wasn’t, which was me. So I put a door on it. The way I put a door on the cupboard when I was three. It can’t get out and I don’t go in. It’s on the list. Item nine. I’m going to do the first eight first.

And you have told no one.

There was no one who could stand still long enough to be told. You’re the first. A pause. Will you?

I cannot. It is in my sentence. The hat’s voice had changed again, and what was in it now Harry had heard once, from a squib on a garden bench, and once from a woman with a knife for a face in a room with the rain on the windows. But I will tell you this, boy, since I am the first and may be the only for a while: you have done exactly right. A door, and a list, and the first eight first. There are men in this castle who could not have done that at seventy. Do not open it alone. When the time comes, you will know who to open it with, and it will not be who you think.

All right, said Harry. Thank you. And then, because it was the only being in the world that could keep up with him and he was eleven and had never once been able to ask: Can I ask you something that isn’t about the Sorting?

You have been asking me things that are not about the Sorting for a minute and a half. The hall is beginning to wonder. Go on.

Does it hurt? Being a sentence that thinks. For a thousand years. In a hat.

The silence this time was different from the others.

Nobody, said the hat, very quietly, has ever asked me that.

I know. I ask everyone the same question and nobody has ever asked it back. I wanted to be the one who did. Does it?

Sometimes, said the hat. Once a year, for about an hour, it is the best thing in the world. The rest of the time I sit on a shelf in his office and think. A pause. You would know about that.

Yes, said Harry. I would.

Then we had better get you sorted, cousin, before Minerva comes and takes me off, which she has done once, in 1952, and has never let me forget. The bounce came back into the voice, deliberately, the way Carrow put her glasses back on. You know what the choices are. You have read the book. You have, I perceive, already had an opinion about who wrote the book. Make your case. I like a case.

He made it. He had been making it since the compartment.

Gryffindor, for my parents, and because the asking takes a kind of nerve, and because a round boy with a toad is going there and I said I’d been the toad. Slytherin, because I have a plan with eight items on it and a lever and a press and I intend to move a world, and that is what the book says they are for, and I think the book is being rude about it. Hufflepuff, because every single person who ever held a door for me would have gone there, and I have been thinking since a garden bench that holding doors is the only thing worth being good at. And Ravenclaw, because of the grammar, and because the biscuit tin, and because a girl who reads everything on the list and everything not on it has just gone to the blue table with the face of somebody who lost an argument and wasn’t sorry.

That is four cases, said the hat. You are supposed to make one.

I know. Here it is. He took a breath, in the dark, on the stool, in the hall that had gone silent. I don’t think it’s a house question. I think the four houses are four doors into the same building and I am going to walk through all of them before I am done, because I have to; the table I mean to be has to have a chair at it from every one. So the question isn’t which door is mine. The question is which door has the people in it who will chase. Who will keep up. Who will ask the question back. I have had exactly one person in my life do that, on a train, this afternoon, and she is at the blue table, and I would like to be where she is, because I have been the only one in the room for a very long time and I am told it is better with company.

The hat was quiet for a long moment. Then it made a sound that was not a word and was, he thought, the nearest a thousand-year-old sentence came to laughing.

You, said the hat, are going to be the most interesting seven years of my existence, and I have had Dumbledore.

Will you put me there?

I am going to put you where you asked to go, said the hat, which nobody has ever asked me to do in those words, and which I find is exactly where I would have put you anyway, and I shall let you decide which of those two facts to believe. Go and sit with the girl who chases. Give her the good biscuits. And Harry—

Yes?

Come and see me. He keeps me on a shelf. Nobody comes. You know the way to a shelf.

I’ll come, said Harry. I’ve got four hundred questions and I’m saving them for him, but I’ll think of some for you. I’m good at questions.

I had noticed, said the hat, and then, aloud, to the hall, in the voice it used for the hall, which was not the voice it had been using for him:

“RAVENCLAW!”

Four minutes and nine seconds. He had timed it. The hall had been silent for the last two, and when the hat came off and the candles came back and the ceiling that was the sky came back with them, four hundred faces were looking at him with an expression that he recognised, because he had seen it on Mr Dyer of the county in a corridor, breathing: the face of people who have just watched a thing go past the end of the sheet.

The blue table found its voice first. It was not a cheer, exactly; it was something more considered, the sound of a table of people who had just been handed a thing and were working out what it was, and then it became a cheer, and he walked to it through the sound, and sat down beside a girl with a great deal of hair who was looking at him with the face from the compartment.

“Four minutes,” said Hermione.

“Four minutes and nine seconds.”

“What did you talk about?”

“Everything,” said Harry. “It could keep up. Nobody’s ever been able to keep up. I had a lot saved.” He looked at her. “You argued with it. About the book. I saw your chin.”

“It asked me who wrote Hogwarts: A History,” said Hermione, with the air of someone still slightly affronted, “and I said a Gryffindor, obviously, and it said well then, and I said oh.” She paused. “You did that.”

“I asked a question. You did the rest. That’s how it works.”

“How what works?”

“I don’t know yet,” said Harry. “I’ve never had anyone to do the rest before. I think it’s going to be the whole of the next seven years.” He turned, because the last of the first-years were going up — Weasley, Ronald — and watched Ron sit on the stool, and the hat went on, and Ron’s face did nothing at all for three seconds and then did everything at once, and “GRYFFINDOR!” and the red table went up like a flock off a field, and Ron walked to it looking back at the blue table, once.

Harry raised one hand, an inch. Ron raised his back. That was all. It was, Harry thought, the second conversation of his life that was not about a want.

He did not hear the speech. He had read it — Hermione had told him on the train what Hogwarts: A History said the Headmaster usually said, and the Headmaster said something else entirely, four words, which Hermione would be indignant about for a week — but he did not hear it, because he was looking at the man who said it, and the man was looking back, over the half-moons, over four hundred heads, and the look was the one from the staff table, the allowed to see look, and under it, now, something else, which Harry read, and read again, and placed.

The man was afraid. Not of him. The way Mrs Figg had been afraid, in a garden, five years ago — not of him; for the rest of you. Dumbledore was sitting at the high table of his own school looking at a boy of eleven whom he had left on a step and read about in receipts and asked four questions of through a squib, and he was afraid, Harry saw, of what he was going to be asked at nine o’clock tomorrow morning, and he was going to answer it anyway, and he had the answer in his pocket, and he was — this was the part that went in warm — he was glad. Under the fear. The way the street had been glad. The way Dedalus Diggle had been glad. Glad to see him, and afraid of him, and sure, and kind, and all of those at once, which Figg had said was what he was like, and Figg had never once been wrong about a face.

Harry inclined his head, one inch, for the second time.

Dumbledore smiled. It was a small smile, and it did not reach the fear, and it did not try to, and Harry found that he respected it more for that than he would have for a larger one.

Then there was food, and he ate it, and listened to Hermione and a girl called Padma argue about the ceiling, and watched the staff table with a quarter of his mind, and the pale man in the turban with another quarter, and the man with the shut face with a third, and kept the fourth for the very small man at the end with the bouncing hair who had, Harry saw, not taken his eyes off the blue table since the hat had said the word, and who caught Harry looking, and beamed, and raised his goblet an inch, and Harry raised his, and thought: tomorrow. A chair.

Ravenclaw Tower had a door with a bronze eagle on it that asked a riddle, and he answered it in the time it took the eagle to finish asking, and then said nothing, and waited, and Hermione, beside him, said, “Wait — don’t — let me—” and got it in eleven seconds, and the door opened, and she looked at him with the face from over the biscuit and said, “You knew.”

“I had a head start. I’ve had a head start since I was fifteen months old. It isn’t fair and I’ve stopped pretending it is.” He held the door. “You got it in eleven seconds. That’s not a head start. That’s you. I’d rather watch you do it than do it myself; I’ve done it myself for ten years and I know how it comes out.”

“That’s—” Hermione stopped in the doorway. “That’s a very odd thing to say.”

“Yes. I’m a very odd person. I did warn you, on the train. I said I’d ask questions fast and nobody had ever kept up.” He looked at her. “You kept up. In the hat. Forty-one seconds and you argued. I saw.”

“I lost.”

“You didn’t lose. You said oh. That’s the only thing worth saying to a hat.” He went through the door. “Come on. There’s a window, I’m told, that looks at the mountains. I’ve never seen a mountain. I’ve read about them.”

The dormitory was round, and blue, and had five beds, and his was by the window, which he had not arranged and suspected a very small man with bouncing hair of having arranged for him, and he sat on it at midnight with the others asleep and the mountains outside being mountains in the dark, and did the thing he had done at every threshold of his life, which was to take stock.

But not of what he had. He had done that in a cupboard the night before, and it had not changed, except that there were three more people in it now and a hat.

He went through the List instead. The whole of it, again, from the top, because Carrow had taught him that when a thing had changed you did not amend it, you rewrote it, so that you could see what you had thought after and not only before.

Things Worth a Life — Hogwarts, 1 September 1991, midnight

0. Tomorrow at nine: the step. The whole thing. He has it in his pocket; I saw him know it was there. Ask properly. Listen. Then the blankets.

1. Learn what the thing is. I know what it is. Find out whether they do. Tomorrow, in a chair.

2. The table. Two halves of one country. I have a girl who chases and a boy who told me about the Cannons and a boy who said I’ve been the toad back to me with his eyes, and a pale boy who took a biscuit at Peterborough when nobody was looking. That is four chairs. The table needs more. Find them.

3. Death. For Amos. For the girl on page seven. There is a man at this school who the books say has thought about it longer than anyone alive. He has a letter in his pocket. Start there.

4. Pain. There is a hospital eleven miles from a photograph, and a Healing text with a transposed table that killed two people whose names are in the back of the Edition. Find the Healer who uses that book. Give her the asking.

5. Waste. There is a child at a kitchen table somewhere that nobody can see. There are probably several in this hall. I know what they look like; I have been one. Look.

6. The curtain. I asked a woman in tartan and she said as it stands. The man in the big chair sits on the body that decides what it stands as. Second question, after the step.

7. 141. Not a duty. I have a tower with a window that looks at mountains and a girl who argues with hats. Some of it is why you’d want the life.

8. The cold. The hat is its cousin. The hat reads. I told it so and it did not like it and said yes. Nobody has ever read one. I am going to. First seven first.

9. (He came to the number and sat with it for a long time, the way he had sat with Death at eight, and put nothing beside it, because the hat had said do not open it alone, and he had decided, on the stool, in the dark, that for once in his life he would take advice.) Not yet. Not alone. The hat says I will know who. Trust the hat; it is the only thing that has ever kept up.

He went over it twice. Then at the bottom, smaller, because it was not a thing worth a life but a thing he wanted to have understood on his first night, the last line.

I was the only one in the room for ten years, and I thought that was the price. It was not the price. It was the cupboard. The room was always out here; I only had to walk through the wall. There were three people on the train and a hat on a stool and a very small man with a chair, and there will be more, and I am going to find every one of them, and I am going to give them the good biscuits first.

Then he took out a sheet, and wrote the first letter, because he had promised, and because there was a post from everywhere and an owl on the windowsill who could not be told anything.

Dear Aunt Petunia, I am here. The flask was still warm at York. There were enough sandwiches for four and there were four of us, and I do not know how you knew, and I have decided not to ask. I am in a tower with a window that looks at mountains. The woman in tartan is the Deputy Head and she does not make it easy, which I wanted. The Headmaster looked at me across the hall and I think he knew I was going to ask him tomorrow, and I think he is going to tell me, and I think I shall tell you what he says, in the summer, in the garden, if you want it. You can leave out the parts that aren't yours. I don't think there will be any. I kept the cupboard. Please leave the swing on the wall. This is for the house. Harry

He gave it to Hedwig, who took it the way Mr Tibbles had always taken instruction, and went out of the window into the dark over the lake, north and then south, and he watched her go until he could not see her, which was a long time, and then he lay down in a bed by a window in a tower in Scotland, eleven years old, the better part of two thousand, with a wand on the hook on the table beside him and a chair waiting in the morning and a step to ask about at nine, and he did not sleep, and he had known he would not, and he lay in the dark and listened to a castle hum, and found that he could not tell how long each tick was, because the castle did not tick; it sang, one long note, a thousand years old, under everything.

He let it pass through him. He did not try to read it.

That could wait till morning. He had, as usual, plenty of time.

Contents

Chapter 23

The Step

The door to the Headmaster’s office was a gargoyle that wanted a password, and he did not have one, and he had known since the train that he would not, and he had decided what to do about it on the stairs.

“I have an appointment,” he said to the gargoyle, at one minute to nine. “It was made in July of 1989 and confirmed on the thirty-first of July this year by the Deputy Headmistress. I don’t know the word. I’d be grateful if you’d tell him I’m here.”

The gargoyle looked at him. He looked at the gargoyle. It was, he decided, a sentence in a thing with a listening part, like the roof — a thing that checked what approached and asked it a question — and he stood still inside himself and let it ask, and did not try to answer in any way but the one he had, which was to be exactly who he was and wait.

The gargoyle moved.

The stair behind it went up by itself, which he had read about and had not believed, and he stood on it with his hands at his sides and let it carry him, and read the sentence in it as he went — a small one, old, cheerful, somebody’s idea of a joke in about 1400 — and arrived at a door, and the door was already open, and the room beyond it was full of morning.

It was a round room, and it was full of things that moved. He had read about the instruments, in Hogwarts: A History, and had not understood what they were for, and standing in the doorway with the sun coming in long and gold across them he understood at once that nobody else did either, because they were not instruments. They were sentences. A hundred of them, small and silver, each holding itself, each casting itself over and over on a side table, puffing and turning and ticking, and he could read perhaps a third of them from the door, and the third he could read were all watching something, and he thought: he built a hundred eyes and set them on a shelf, and he sits in the middle of them, and he understood something about the man before the man had said a word.

The man was behind the desk, in the big chair, with the beard to his belt and the half-moon spectacles, and he was not reading or writing or doing anything with tweezers. He was sitting with his hands folded on an empty blotter, waiting, the way a man waits who has been waiting for ten years and has decided not to pretend otherwise for the last minute of it.

And on a shelf behind him, between a battered sword in a case and a silver dish of something that smoked, sat a hat.

Good morning, cousin, said the hat, in the voice it had used in the dark. He has been sitting like that since half past seven. I have been trying to make conversation and he keeps telling me to hush.

Harry did not smile. He found it hard.

“Good morning, Professor Dumbledore.”

“Good morning, Dr Potter.” The voice was lighter than he had expected, and older, and had under it the thing he had read off the man’s face across the Great Hall: fear, and gladness, and the two of them not quite agreeing which was to speak first. “Come in. Sit down. There is a chair that has been the right height for a very small Charms master since 1971 and I have borrowed it for the morning. There is tea. It is very strong. I was told that was the requirement.”

Harry came in. He sat in Flitwick’s chair, which was the right height, and looked at the tea, which was very strong, and did not drink it yet.

Fawkes, on his perch, looked at him with a bird’s side-on regard, and then did a thing Harry had not read about in any book, which was to put his head down, briefly, the way Mr Tibbles had closed his eyes on a wall, and Harry thought: the examiner has turned up and found it already passed, and then put that away, because it was not the morning for jokes.

“You have a question,” said Dumbledore.

“I have four hundred and three. They’re in order. I’d like to ask the first one and then stop, and then ask the second, and then I think we’ll see.” He put his hands in his lap. “I was told, by two women who know you, that you would have an answer and that it would be good and true and not the whole thing. I asked for the whole thing through Mrs Figg. I’d like to ask for it again now, to your face, so that it’s on record that I did.” He looked at the man across the empty blotter. “Why did you leave me on the step?”

Dumbledore did not answer at once. He put his hand inside his robes, and brought out an envelope, and the envelope was not new — the corners were soft; it had been carried — and he put it on the blotter between them and did not push it across.

“I wrote this in July of 1989,” he said. “At midnight, at this desk, with your degree in a tray beside me and a book by a dead friend of mine that you had read. I wrote it because Arabella had told me you would ask, and I knew that when you did, I would be tempted to give you the version of the answer that I had at that moment, which would be better than the one I had in 1986 and worse than the one I have now, and that you would know, because Arabella says you have spent your life reading the sides of faces for the part that is not being said. So I wrote down what I believed before I met you, so that I should not be able to improve it.” He looked at the envelope. “It is the whole thing. It is the whole thing as it stood two years ago, and I have read it eleven times since, and I have not changed a word, and I find that I agree with all of it and that I am ashamed of about a third.”

He pushed it across.

“You may read it now, here, with me in the room, which I should prefer, because I should like to see your face. Or you may take it away and read it alone, which you have every right to. Or I may tell you, in my own voice, which will be worse, because I shall be tempted.”

Harry looked at the envelope. He had been reading sentences since he was six and had never once been handed one that had been carried.

“Here,” he said. “With you in the room. I’d like to see your face too.”

He opened it. He read it in the time it took the man across the desk to breathe in, and then he read it again at the speed of his hand, because he had decided somewhere between Dear Harry and Albus Dumbledore that this was a letter to be read slowly, in front of the man, so that the man could watch, and so that the watching would cost the man what it ought.

I left you there because your mother died to keep you alive, and her death made a thing that I could use, and the only place it would hold was under her sister’s roof.

I left you there because I was afraid of what our world would make of you if it had you at fifteen months.

And I left you there because I was tired, Harry, and grieving, and I had made a great many decisions that autumn very quickly in the dark, and this was one of them, and I have never been certain it was right, and I did not go back to check, for eight years, because I was afraid that if I went back I would find I had been wrong and would not be able to bear it.

That is the third reason. It is the true one. It is the one I shall find hardest to say to your face.

He read to the end. If, when you have asked, you want it — ask for that too. He had asked for it. Here it was.

He put the letter down on the blotter, face up, and looked at Albus Dumbledore across it, and the man was not looking at the letter. He was looking at Harry, and his face was doing a thing that Harry had seen on exactly one other face in his life, across a kitchen table in Surrey, over a shoebox, in the dark: the face of a person who has been standing in front of a nailed-shut drawer for ten years and has just watched someone else open it.

The office was very quiet. The instruments ticked. The hat, on its shelf, for once, said nothing at all.

“Thank you,” said Harry.

Dumbledore’s face did not move. “For—”

“For the third reason. The other two I’d worked out. I read the roof from the step when I was nine and I knew what the first one was; I read the index on a bench at seven and I knew what the second one was. I didn’t have the third. I couldn’t have. It isn’t the sort of thing you can read off a receipt.” He looked at the letter. “You were tired, and you were afraid you’d been wrong, and you didn’t go back and check. That’s — Professor, that’s the most ordinary thing anyone has ever told me. Everyone I’ve ever respected has done it once. Mrs Figg waited two years to let me ask her what she was because she was afraid of the answer. My aunt kept a shoebox for ten years and never once looked in it twice a year because she couldn’t bear what was in it. I made a rule at two and didn’t review it till I was five because I was afraid of what the review would say. The third reason isn’t a wizard’s reason. It’s a person’s.” He stopped. “I came in here with a question. I think I came in expecting to be angry. I had the anger ready; I’ve had it since I was four, in a drawer, the way my aunt had hers. And I find I can’t use it, because you’ve just handed me the one thing that makes it not apply, which is that you were a man in the dark who made a mistake and knew it and couldn’t look. I know what that is. I’ve been it.”

He picked up the tea, which was very strong, and drank some of it, because his mouth had gone dry and because he wanted the man to see him do an ordinary thing.

“I’m not forgiving you,” he said. “I want to be clear about that, because I think you’d like me to and I think it would be a lie, and I don’t lie to my aunt and I’m not going to start with you. It isn’t mine to forgive. It was done to a baby, and the baby isn’t here; I am. What I can do is what a colleague does when another colleague says I made this mistake and here is exactly how. I can say: yes. You did. Here is what it cost, in my words, so that it’s on the record in both our hands.” He set the cup down. “It cost a cupboard, which I chose and would choose again, and which the Quill wrote on an envelope and frightened Professor McGonagall, and which was the best room I’ve ever had. It cost ten years of a woman at a sink being afraid of me, which I ended with one sentence at five and could have ended at three if I’d known there was a sentence. It cost a warming charm I couldn’t read till I was eight and a roof I couldn’t read till I was nine. And it cost you — I can see what it cost you; it’s on your face; it was on it last night across the hall — it cost you ten years of not knowing, and reading my receipts, and asking me about Dementors through a squib because you’d forbidden yourself the front door.” He looked at the letter again. “That last one I mind about more than the cupboard. You could have asked me. At seven. At eight. I had four hundred questions and you had four and we could have had the whole conversation by owl and nobody would have had to be afraid. That’s the cost I’d like on the record. Not the step. The silence after it.”

Dumbledore did not say anything for a long time. Then he took his spectacles off, and did not polish them, and held them, and looked at the boy in Flitwick’s chair with eyes that were, without the half-moons, very old and very blue and entirely open.

“Yes,” he said. “Yes. That is the cost. You have put it more exactly than I did in two years of trying.” He put the spectacles back on. “Dr Potter, I have been a headmaster for thirty-five years and have had a great many conversations in this room in which a person told me what I had done wrong, and I have agreed with perhaps four of them, and I have never before been told by someone who had the right to be angry that they could not use the anger because I had handed them the thing that made it not apply. I do not know what to do with that. I shall have to think about it. I suspect I shall be thinking about it for some time.”

“Good,” said Harry. “That’s what the whole thing is for.” And then, because the man had given him the third reason and it had cost what it ought: “Professor. Thank you for the warming charm. I read it from memory when I was eight, in an examination hall, with my pen down. It was the best thing I had ever seen anyone write. I’ve seen the roof since and it’s better, but the charm was first. You wrote it in the time it took to walk down a path with a basket, and it checked. It didn’t warm. It asked how cold I was and gave exactly the difference. I’ve built my whole — I’ve built a great deal on that bracket. I wanted you to know that the first sentence I ever heard was one of yours, and that it listened.”

The man across the desk closed his eyes.

“Minerva said,” he said, after a moment, with them still closed, “that you would arrive as a person to whom one had better not come with the answer already in one’s pocket. I had it in my pocket for two years. I find I should have had something else in there as well, and I do not know what, and I think you have just told me.” He opened his eyes. “A handkerchief, possibly.”

“I’ve got one,” said Harry. “My aunt packed four. Would you like it?”

Albus Dumbledore looked at the Boy Who Lived for a long moment across his own letter, and then — Harry saw it start at the corner of the beard and go up — laughed. Not the small smile from the hall. An actual laugh, short and startled and real, the laugh of a man who had braced for ten years against a blow and had been handed a handkerchief instead.

“No,” he said. “No, thank you. I have my own. I have not needed it in this room in some time.” He folded his hands on the blotter again, over the letter, and the fear had gone out of his face, and what was left was the gladness, and the sureness, and the kindness, all three, which Figg had said was what he was like, and Figg had never once been wrong about a face. “The second question. You said there would be a second, and then we would see.”

“The second is the roof,” said Harry. “The whole of it. I read it; I have most of it; there’s a bracket at the heart of it about blood that I couldn’t get into. I’d like to know what it is, and what it’s against, and whether what it’s against is still out there, because the books say vanished and I know what careful books are for.”

“Yes,” said Dumbledore. “Yes, I thought that would be second. Drink your tea, Dr Potter. This one takes longer.”

It took an hour, and it was the first hour of Harry’s life in which an adult told him everything he asked and did not stop at the drawer.

The roof was Lily. He had known that — he had seen the shape on the swing at the heart of it — but he had not known how, and Dumbledore told him how, plainly, with his hands folded, the way a man tells a colleague a result: that a mother had stood between a wand and a child and had been given the choice to step aside and had not, and that the not-stepping had made something, a sentence of a kind that no wizard could write on purpose, because it was written in the only ink that could not be bought, and that the sentence said, as near as it could be put, this one is paid for. And that it had held — had turned a curse that had never once been turned, and had broken the man who cast it — and had gone on holding, in the child’s skin, and would go on holding as long as the child was a child and had a home with the blood of the one who had paid.

“The bracket you could not read,” said Dumbledore, “is the one I could not write. I did not write the roof, Dr Potter. I anchored it. Your mother wrote it. I found it on you, on the step, still warm, and I built a house round it the way one builds a wall round a spring, and the wall is mine and the spring is hers, and the part you could not get into is the spring.”

Harry sat very still in the small chair.

“That’s why it listened,” he said. “The roof. I thought it was your hand. It’s got your hand all over the outside. But the listening part — the part that checks for intent and asks — that’s not yours. That’s hers. You built round a thing that was already asking.”

“Yes.”

“She could do that.” It was not a question. “The thing I do. The asking. Not the way I do it — not on purpose, not with marks — but once, when it mattered, with everything she had, she asked, and the world said yes.” He looked at his hands. “Mrs Figg said she was the same. You find the door they already use. Your mother was the same; everyone said. I thought she meant the people. I don’t think she only meant the people.”

Dumbledore was quiet for a moment.

“No,” he said. “No, I do not think she did. Lily Evans was the finest natural Charms student I ever taught, and Filius will tell you the same, and neither of us ever once saw her shout.”

He put that on the shelf. It went in warm, and then it went in deep, and he left it there to do what it would do, because there was more, and the more was colder.

What the roof was against was a man who was not dead. Dumbledore said so plainly, without the careful words, and Harry had known it and found that knowing it and hearing it in that voice in that room were two different things. A wraith. Something that had been a man and had made itself into a thing that could not die in the ordinary way, by means Dumbledore said he was not yet certain of and would tell Harry when he was, and that had been broken on a step in Godric’s Hollow and had gone somewhere — a forest, a mountain, a mind — and was waiting, and would, Dumbledore was as sure as he had ever been of anything, come back. The roof was against that. The sentry in the mackintosh was against that. The ten years of not coming up a path were, in part, against that, because a path walked up by Albus Dumbledore was a path that could be watched.

“I should have told you at seven,” said Dumbledore. “By owl. You are right about the silence. I had reasons for it and they were the second kind — the fear kind — and I shall not dress them up for you now.”

“No,” said Harry. “Don’t.” He had his hands in his lap and was looking at the hundred silver sentences on the side tables, the third of them he could read, all watching something. “Those are for him. Aren’t they. The instruments. You built a hundred eyes and set them watching for a man who isn’t dead.”

“Some of them. Some of them watch the roof. One of them—” Dumbledore paused, and Harry saw him decide to go through a door he had been standing beside “—one of them has watched you, Dr Potter, since the autumn of 1981. Not what you did. Whether you were alive, and whether you were in the house, and whether the roof held. It is the small one, on the end, that turns clockwise. It has never once stopped.”

Harry looked at the small one on the end. It turned clockwise. He read its sentence, which was short, and simple, and had been cast once in a terrible autumn by a tired man and had held itself for ten years, and it said, as near as he could put it, is he still there.

He had thought he had been the only one in the room for ten years. He had not been. There had been a small silver thing on the end of a shelf in Scotland, asking, every second, in a sentence he could read, is he still there, and the answer had always been yes.

“All right,” he said, after a while. It came out less steady than he had meant it to, and he let it. “All right. That one I’ll forgive you for. Not the rest. That one.”

“I shall take that,” said Dumbledore gravely, “as the first instalment.”

He did not tell the man about the door.

He had decided on the stool, in the dark, with a hat that was the cold’s cousin saying not alone, and not who you think, and he had reviewed the decision on the stair at one minute to nine and found it held. The man across the desk had just told him everything he asked. He had told him the third reason and the spring and the small silver thing on the end. He had been, for an hour, the most honest adult Harry had ever sat across from, including a woman with a knife for a face, and the grain behind the door was cold and small and did not like him and was — Harry was nearly sure now, with wraith said aloud in the room — was of the thing the roof was against, and the man across the desk had a hundred eyes set watching for that thing, and if Harry said I think there is a piece of it in me, every one of the hundred eyes would turn, and the man’s face would do a thing Harry did not want to see on it yet, and the door would be opened by the wrong hands at the wrong time with the whole of a castle watching.

Not alone. Not who you think.

So he put it back behind the door, and put the door on the shelf, and asked the third question instead, which was the one that had been second on the list since a lounge in Surrey.

“Professor. I asked Professor McGonagall whether the Statute would let me warm a Muggle hospital. She said as it stands. She said you sit on the body that decides what it stands as. I have a design for a thing that presses a charm into an object, over and over, exactly, and I think it works, and nobody has built one because nobody could hold the first copy steady, and I can. The first charm I’d press is yours. The one from the step. Into blankets. For a ward in London where a girl of six had her photograph in a newspaper two years ago because the heating was a line in a budget.” He had said it to McGonagall in a lounge and it had carried; he said it now in a round room full of eyes and found it carried further. “I want to know what it would take.”

Dumbledore looked at him for a long time.

“Minerva told me you had asked her that,” he said. “She told me in the voice she uses for things that have frightened her, which I have heard perhaps six times in sixty years. I have thought about it for five weeks.” He unfolded his hands. “It would take a case. Not an argument — I have heard your argument, through Minerva, and it is a good one, and the Wizengamot does not sit to hear good arguments; it sits to hear cases. A thing that exists. A charm that has been pressed. A blanket that has been warmed. A Muggle who has been warmed by it and did not know and came to no harm. A reason why the Statute’s own preamble — which you have read, and I have read, and perhaps nine other people now living have read — does not forbid it. And a man who sits on the body to bring the case, in a form it cannot send back for want of a comma.” He put his hands flat on the blotter. “Bring me the case, Dr Potter. Build the thing. Press the charm. Show me a blanket. Give me a Muggle who was warm. Then I shall take it to the Wizengamot, and I shall lose, the first time, because they always lose the first time, and then we shall see.”

“How long?”

“How long do you need?”

Harry thought about a biscuit tin under a camp bed, and a very small man with a chair ready, and a girl who argued with hats.

“Give me a term,” he said.

Dumbledore’s eyebrows went up a quarter of an inch, and Harry thought of Mr Haines, and nearly smiled.

“A term.”

“I’ve had the design since March. I’ve had the charm since I was fifteen months old. I’ve never had a wand or a room or anyone who could see the marks, and as of last night I have all three. A term.” He paused. “And I’ll want to use Professor Flitwick. And the hospital wing. And I’ll want Hermione Granger in every room I’m in, because she chases, and because I have been the only one in the room for ten years and I’m told it’s better.”

“You may have Filius; he has been asking me for you since February of 1987. You may have Poppy, if you can persuade her, which you will, because you will ask her properly. You may have Miss Granger in any room she consents to be in, which I suspect will be all of them.” Dumbledore sat back. “And Dr Potter. We shall meet. Here. Once a week, for an hour, at a time of your choosing, and you will ask me whatever is next on your list and I will answer it, and when I cannot I shall say so and say why, and you will tell me what you have built, and when I think it is dangerous I shall say so, and you will listen, and we shall each of us do the other the courtesy of assuming the other has reasons.” He paused. “That is what I do with colleagues. I have not had one who was eleven. I find I should like to try.”

“Thursdays,” said Harry. “At four. I had Thursdays at four for five years with a professor of mathematics and I’d like to keep them.”

“Thursdays at four,” said Dumbledore, and wrote it down, on the blotter, under the letter, in a hand that slanted and was kind.

He stood up to go, at ten past ten, with four hundred questions left and a term, and at the door he stopped, because there was a thing he had been not saying for an hour and a half, since the Great Hall, since four seconds at a staff table, and he had decided on the stair that he would say it last, when the man had earned it, and the man had.

“Professor. One more. It isn’t on the list; it’s from last night.”

“Yes?”

“There’s something wrong with Professor Quirrell’s face.”

Dumbledore did not move. But the hundred silver sentences on the side tables did a thing, all at once, that Harry had not seen them do in an hour and a half, which was to go, for a quarter of a second, perfectly still.

“Go on,” said Dumbledore, very quietly.

“I read faces. It’s the only thing I’m actually good at; everything else came from it. I read his for four seconds across the hall and I read it again at breakfast for eleven and I’ve been reading it since, and it isn’t one face. It’s one face being held by two people who haven’t agreed how. The nervousness is real; it’s his. There’s something under it that isn’t nervous at all, and isn’t his, and it looks at things — it looked at me — the way the small silver one on the end looks at the roof.” He kept his voice level, because the sentence had been built on the stair and he wanted it to carry. “I don’t know what that means. I don’t have a word for it; I have seven languages and none of them have it. I know what a face looks like when two people are in it, because I have been reading one face for ten years that had a woman in it and a fear in it and they never once agreed, and this is like that, except that the second one isn’t a fear. It’s a person. And it’s cold.”

The office was silent. Fawkes had put his head up.

Albus Dumbledore looked at the boy in the doorway for a long moment, and his face did not do any of the things Harry had seen it do that morning. It did a new thing. It went old. Not tired; old, the way the castle was old, the way a sentence is old that has been holding itself for a very long time and has just felt, for the first time in years, something lean on it.

“Dr Potter,” he said. “Sit down again. Please. I think we had better move Thursday to now.”

Harry sat.

On the shelf behind the desk, between the sword and the dish, the hat said, very quietly, in the voice it had used in the dark, Oh, cousin. Oh, well done. He has been pretending not to see that for a fortnight and nobody in this castle could tell him so but you, and then said nothing more, because the man was speaking, and what the man said took the rest of the morning, and it was the beginning of the first thing they ever did together, and it was not on the List, and it was, Harry would think later, exactly the sort of thing the List had been for.

Contents

Chapter 24

The Chair

What the man had said, for the rest of that morning, came to three things, and Harry put them in order that night, by the window, in the words he would use if anyone ever asked him what he had agreed to.

One. He had seen it too. A fortnight ago, at a staff meeting, in the set of the man’s shoulders when nobody was speaking to him; and had set two of the hundred eyes watching, and had told himself it was nerves. He said so plainly, which I respected. He said: I have been pretending not to see it because the alternative was a thing I was not ready to see in my own school, and you have made me ready, and I am not grateful yet but I shall be.

Two. I am to watch. Not approach. Not test. Watch, the way I watch everything, and tell him on Thursdays what the face is doing, because I can read it and he cannot, and that is — he said this — the first time in sixty years that anyone has been able to do a thing in this castle that he could not, and he would like to get used to it slowly.

Three. He would not tell me what he thinks is in the face. He said he had a guess and that the guess was the second kind of reason — the fear kind — and that he was not going to give me a fear and ask me to carry it to Charms. He said: when I know, you will know, within the hour. I said that was the same silence he had just apologised for. He said yes, it was, and that he was going to commit it anyway for a fortnight, and that I could hold him to it, and I shall.

So: a fortnight. Then I ask again. In the meantime: a chair.

The first Charms lesson was on the Wednesday, in a room on the third floor with high windows and a lectern that had been built for a man of ordinary height and had a stack of books on it that had not, and the man standing on the books was the very small one from the end of the staff table, with the white hair and the bouncing delight, and he had plainly not slept.

“Good morning, good morning. Sit anywhere. Not you, Mr Boot, that chair has a leg. Good morning.
