I am Professor Flitwick, and this is Charms, and I have been teaching it for forty-one years, and I should like to begin by telling you that I have never once been bored, and I intend to find out this morning whether that record holds.” He beamed at them. He had a face built for beaming and had plainly used it hard. “Wands out. Not pointed at anyone. Mr Goldstein, that is someone.”

Harry took the holly out of his pocket. It was the first time he had taken it out in a room with other people in it since a shop in July, and he felt the room notice, the way the Great Hall had noticed the scar — a turning of heads, a quiet — and he did not look up, and he did not look down, and he laid the wand on the desk in front of him with the tip towards the window and his hands flat beside it, and waited.

Beside him, Hermione had hers out and was holding it the way the book said and looking at him, not the book.

“Now,” said Flitwick. “I know what the other houses do in the first lesson. They do the register and they tell you what a charm is and they send you away with a chapter. I am not going to do that, because I have never in forty-one years seen the point of telling a child what a thing is before they have done one, and because I have twenty-six eleven-year-olds in front of me with wands they have never used, and the single most important thing I can do with them this morning is let them find out what it feels like.” He picked up his own wand, which was short and dark and had been in his hand so long it looked like a finger. “The simplest charm there is. Light. The word is Lumos. The movement is a short flick, up, like this. You are going to say the word and do the flick and most of you are going to get nothing, and some of you are going to get a spark, and one or two of you are going to get a flicker, and I am going to be delighted with every single one of those outcomes, because every one of them is the first time, and the first time is the only time it is ever new.”

He looked, for a quarter-second, at the back of the room. Harry saw it. Nobody else could have.

“Go on, then. All together. Nobody will hear you over everyone else.”

Twenty-six eleven-year-olds said Lumos and flicked.

Most of them got nothing. Padma Patil got a spark, bright and gone. Terry Boot got a flicker that went out when he breathed. Hermione, beside him, got a light — a real one, small, yellow, wavering at the tip of her wand like a candle in a draught — and made a sound Harry had heard from Mrs Osei over an acceptance letter, and the light went out, and she said “Oh,” and did it again, and got it again.

Harry had not said anything. He had not flicked. He was sitting with his hands flat on the desk beside the holly and watching Hermione’s light, because he had never once in his life been in a room where someone else was doing it for the first time, and it was, he thought, the finest thing he had seen since a board in the rain.

“Dr Potter.”

The room went quiet in a different way.

Flitwick was looking at him from the stack of books with the beam turned down to something more careful, something Harry recognised from a woman in tartan on a step: a professional, who had read the receipts, and had a chair ready, and had decided to find out.

“You have not tried it.”

“No, sir. I was watching Hermione. I’ve never seen anyone do it for the first time. I wanted to.” He put his hand on the holly. “I’ll try it now, if you’d like. I should tell you first that I have done it once before, in a shop, and that it didn’t come out the way the book says, and that I promised Professor McGonagall on a pavement I would warn people before I did a thing that might be impossible so that nobody found out in a shop again. So: this might not be sparks. I’m sorry. I don’t know how to make it be sparks.”

Flitwick looked at him for a long moment.

“Dr Potter,” he said, “I have been waiting since February of 1987 to see what you do with a wand, and I have read two pages about Dementors four times and a professor of mathematics’ letter to a county council and a children’s book about prime numbers, and I have a chair in my office that I borrowed back from the Headmaster yesterday afternoon because I was told I might need it, and I should like you to do whatever it is, in front of these twenty-five children, because they are going to spend seven years in a school with you and they had better find out on the first morning what that means.” He put his own wand down. “Lumos. Please.”

Harry picked up the holly.

He had held it for sixteen years without sending anything down it, and had learned what it was by the holding — not a telephone; a thing that edited, that sanded a ragged sentence on its way through according to its grain — and he had wondered, for sixteen years, what it would do with a sentence that had nothing to sand. He had found out once, for four seconds, in a shop. He found out now.

He did not say the word. He had never needed the word; the word was a handle for people who could not see the thing; he had been seeing the thing since he was six. He built light — the candle’s cousin, the bracket sanded smooth by a thousand hands, held in the back of his mind the way you hold a note — and he did the flick, because the room expected a flick and he had decided on the stair at nine o’clock the day before that he would give rooms what they expected where it cost nothing, and he sent it, and held.

The light came up at the tip of the holly like a thought arriving.

White. Steady. A single point, not a flicker, not a spark, lighting the dust in the air of the room so that every mote of it hung still and separate, and he held it — one second, two, three — and the room did not breathe, and Flitwick on his stack of books had gone perfectly still with his hand on the lectern.

Then Harry did the thing he had done in a cupboard the night of the Standard Books, in his head, and had never done with a wand, because he had never had one: he found the pushing part of the sentence, the part that said how much, and he turned it down. Slowly. The way you would turn a lamp. The white point dimmed — not went out; dimmed — to half, to a quarter, to a glow at the tip no brighter than the end of a cigarette, and held there, and he turned it back up, to full, and down again, and let it go.

The dust fell.

Nobody said anything for a long time, and the long time was long for them.

“That,” said Terry Boot, from the chair with a leg, in a voice that had gone very small, “isn’t in the book.”

“No,” said Harry. “The book has Lumos. It doesn’t have a dimmer. I wrote the dimmer when I was seven, on paper, in a cupboard, because it seemed to me that a light you couldn’t turn down was only half a light, and nobody in seven books had thought to ask. That’s the first time I’ve done it with a wand. It works.” He put the holly down on the desk with the tip towards the window. “I’m sorry. I did say it might not be sparks.”

Flitwick had not moved. He was still standing on his stack of books with his hand on the lectern, and his face was doing a thing that Harry had seen on exactly one other face in his life, and the face had been Mrs Figg’s, in a kitchen, over a cat that had just gone up the stairs.

“Dr Potter,” said Filius Flitwick, very quietly. “I think I should like to sit down.”

“There’s a chair, sir. You said.”

“It is in my office.”

“Then I think,” said Harry, “that we had better go to your office. After class. If that’s all right. I’ve got something in a biscuit tin I’d like to show you, and I’d like Hermione to come, because she got a light on the first go and I saw her hold it steadier the second time by watching, and I think she can learn to see it, and I’ve never had anyone who could.”

Flitwick looked at Hermione, who had gone red to the hair and had her wand still in her hand with nothing at the end of it and was looking at Harry with the face from over the biscuit on the train.

“Miss Granger,” said Flitwick. “Did you.”

“I — yes, sir. I think so. I watched how still he kept his hand. It wasn’t his hand. It was — I don’t know what it was. But I watched it and mine stopped wobbling.” She swallowed. “Is that cheating?”

“Miss Granger,” said Flitwick, and the beam came back into his face like a tide, “that is Charms. That is the whole of it. Forty-one years I have been trying to teach children to watch, and you have just done it on the first morning because the thing you were watching was worth it.” He got down off his stack of books. “Class. You have all cast your first charm, and I am delighted with every one of you, and that is not a thing I say; it is a thing I mean. Chapter one for Friday. Dr Potter and Miss Granger will stay. Mr Boot, mind the leg.”

Flitwick’s office was on the same floor and was small and warm and full of books that had been read, and the chair was in it, and Flitwick sat down in it the moment he was through the door.

“Show me,” he said.

Harry put the box file on the desk. It was the one from Carrow’s cupboard, with the spine gone soft, and he opened it and took out the Second Edition — nine hundred and forty pages, in the private marks, with the nearest English beside each in the round hand — and put the first page in front of a very small man who had thought about the shape of a charm for forty-one years and had, Harry suspected, never once been shown one.

Flitwick looked at it for a long time.

He could not read it. Harry had known he would not be able to; nobody could; the marks were a language with one speaker. But he watched Flitwick’s eyes go down the page the way Carrow’s had gone down forty pages at a long table — checking — and he watched them stop, a third of the way down, at the place where the spine of the thing was drawn, the pointing and the pushing, and Flitwick put one small finger on it and said, in a voice that had lost all its bounce:

“That’s Blishwick.”

“Yes, sir. The spine is. He got the spine. He got the pointing part and the pushing part and he guessed at the middle and the guess was wrong and he said so in a footnote and apologised. I wrote not quite in his margin when I was seven. This is the rest.”

“The rest.” Flitwick turned the page. The second page was brackets — the first twenty of the hundred and sixty, each with its name in their language and his, each with what it did and what it cost and how it had been sanded. He turned another. He turned twelve, and did not speak, and Hermione stood by the door with her hands clasped and her chin up and did not speak either, and Harry sat on the edge of a stool that had been made for first-years and waited, which he was good at.

“I can’t read it,” said Flitwick at last. He said it the way Carrow had said page nine is wrong: as a fact, to be stated plainly and faced. “Not a word of it. I know what it is — I have been looking for this page for forty-one years and I know it when I see it, the way a man who has never seen the sea knows it from the smell — but I cannot read a single mark.” He looked up. “Can anyone?”

“No, sir. Not yet. I made the marks when I was six because English had no words, and I’ve been the only one who could see what they stand for, because I’m the only one who could—” he stopped, at the edge, and stepped back from it, because it was not yet time and because this man would ask how and deserve an answer “—who could stand still long enough to see the thing go by. Blishwick said it couldn’t be seen. He was right, for him. It can be seen. I’ve been seeing it since I was six and writing it down since I was seven and I have never once had anyone to show, and I would like — Professor, I would very much like to teach you to read it. And Hermione. I think it can be taught. I think the seeing can’t, or not yet, but the reading can, the way you can read Greek without having been to Athens. It would take a year. I have a term for the first thing and seven years for the rest, and I’d rather not spend them as the only speaker.”

Flitwick looked at the page under his finger for a long moment.

“Dr Potter,” he said, “when I was twenty-two I wrote to the Department of Mysteries and asked whether anyone there had ever observed the casting moment, and they wrote back and said that the question was not well-formed. I have had the letter in a drawer for sixty years. I should like you to know that I am going to burn it this evening.” He closed the Second Edition, carefully, with both hands, the way Harry held things on stages. “Saturday mornings. Here. Nine o’clock. You will teach me to read it and I will teach you what I know about this—” he touched his own wand, the short dark one that looked like a finger “—which is not nothing, and which I suspect you will find has holes in it, and I should like you to tell me where. Miss Granger will attend. Miss Granger will attend every session, because she held a light steady on the second go by watching and I have never once seen that and I want to see it again.”

“Yes, sir,” said Hermione, in a voice that had very nearly gone.

“And now,” said Flitwick, “I should like to ask the question I have been wanting to ask since a squib wrote to the Headmaster in 1987 that you sit on the floor and are sorry for a cat until the cat gets up. I have read that sentence perhaps forty times. I told the Headmaster it had the shape of a prayer.” He leaned forward in the chair. “Is it?”

Harry thought about it honestly, which was the only way he knew how to think about anything.

“No, sir,” he said. “A prayer doesn’t expect an answer you can check. This does. You ask, and either the cat gets up or it doesn’t, and if it doesn’t you’ve asked wrong and you go back and find out where. It’s more like—” he searched, and found it in a room with chalk on the back of a door “—it’s more like a proof. You build it honestly, every step, and you put it in front of the world, and the world either says yes or it says fourth line from the bottom, you’ve let the thing have two names. It doesn’t care who you are. It only cares whether the thing holds.” He paused. “I didn’t know that about proofs until I met a professor of mathematics. I knew it about cats first.”

Flitwick sat back in the chair.

“A proof,” he said. “Hm. Yes. That is — yes.” He looked at the biscuit tin. “Dr Potter. The thing you did in my classroom. The dimmer. I have been teaching Lumos for forty-one years and I have never once thought to ask whether it could be turned down, and I am a Charms master, and that is my job. Why has nobody asked?”

“Because nobody could see the part that says how much. It happens in the quarter-second. You say the word and flick and the light comes and you think the word did it, and you never see that there was a sentence under the word with a part in it you could have turned.” He looked at the very small man in the chair. “You’re all shouting down a telephone with your eyes shut, sir. It works. It’s worked for a thousand years. But you’ve never once seen what you were saying, and so you’ve never once edited it, and there are a hundred and sixty charms in the Standard Books and I think about forty of them have a dimmer nobody’s found.”

Flitwick did not say anything for a while. Then he laughed — short, startled, the way Dumbledore had over a handkerchief — and the laugh had forty-one years in it.

“Forty,” he said. “Forty. Miss Granger, make a note. Saturday. Nine. Bring the tin.”

“The dimmer,” said Hermione, on the stairs down, in a sentence with no pause in it anywhere. “You turned it down. You said there’s a part that says how much and you turned it and nobody has ever — how did you — what does it look like, the part, is it in the marks, is that what the marks are, can I—”

“Saturday,” said Harry.

“But—”

“Saturday. I promised him first. You’ll be in the room; you’ll see it the same minute he does.” He stopped on the landing, where there was a window that looked at the mountains, and looked at them, because he had never seen a mountain before Sunday and had decided to look at them whenever there was one. “Hermione. You held yours steady on the second go. By watching. I want you to understand what that was, because Flitwick does and I don’t think you do yet.”

“I watched your hand.”

“You watched past my hand. You said so; you said it wasn’t the hand. You watched the thing the hand was doing, which you can’t see yet, and your own went steadier anyway, because something in you saw it even though you didn’t.” He turned from the window. “I spent four years learning to see it. You did it in a second by looking at someone who could. I don’t know what that means. I think it means that it can be caught. Like an accent. I think it means that if I stand in rooms with people long enough and hold things steady, some of them are going to start holding things steady without knowing why, and then I can show them the marks and they’ll already half know them.” He paused. “I think that’s how it spreads. Not the tin. The watching. The tin is for after.”

Hermione looked at him on the landing with the face from over the biscuit, and he watched her — he had all the time in the world to watch her — go through surprise, and suspicion, and arithmetic, and arrive at the place she had arrived at on the train, which was oh.

“That’s why you want me in every room,” she said slowly. “Not because I chase. Because I catch.”

“Both. The chasing is why I like you. The catching is why it matters.” He started down the stairs. “And because I’ve been the only one in the room for ten years and I’m told it’s better. Come on. We’ve got Transfiguration, and I promised a woman in tartan I wouldn’t bore her first-years by being perfect at it, and I’ve got to work out how to do that before two o’clock.”

“How do you do that?”

“I don’t know yet,” said Harry. “I’ve never had to be bad at anything on purpose that I couldn’t make look like an accident. It’s going to be the hardest thing I do this week.”

It was.

He put Flitwick’s face on the shelf that night, by the window that looked at mountains, with the others — Carrow’s over page nine, Figg’s over the cat, Aldous’s over page ninety — because he had understood, in a small warm office with a chair in it, a thing about the man that he wanted to keep.

He has been standing on a stack of books for forty-one years, teaching children to shout down a telephone, and knowing — knowing the whole time, the way Blishwick knew — that there was a thing under the shout that he could not see. He wrote to the Department of Mysteries at twenty-two and they told him the question was not well-formed. He kept the letter sixty years. He is going to burn it tonight.

I do not know what it is like to want to see a thing for sixty years and not be able to. I have never once wanted to see a thing and not seen it; that is the whole of what I am. But I watched his face when he put his finger on the spine and said "that's Blishwick", and I think I know now what the far side of the wall looks like from the other side, and I think it looks like a very small man in a chair who has just been shown the sea.

Saturday. Nine. The tin. I am going to teach a Charms master to read. I taught myself off cereal packets and it took five months. He has sixty years of knowing there was something to read. I think it will take him a term.

Hermione catches. I did not know that could happen. If it can happen to her it can happen to others. Count them. Make a list of who holds steadier after watching. That is the first page of the table.

Hedwig was on the sill, back from Surrey with a letter in a hand he knew from a shoebox, and he took it, and it said, on one side of one sheet, in the fence voice: The swing is on the wall. The gutters are fine. Your uncle told the Rotary about the traffic. Tea was at six. — P.D. And under that, in the other voice, smaller, as if added after the ink had dried: I am glad the flask was still warm.

He put it in the box file with the Second Edition and McGonagall’s two sentences about the Trace, because it was for the house and the house was in there now too, and lay down by the window with the castle singing one long note under everything, and did not sleep, and had known he would not, and found he did not mind, because it was Wednesday, and Saturday was three days off, and three days was most of two years, and he had a Charms master to teach to read.

Contents

Chapter 25

Two Brackets

He failed at the match four times before Professor McGonagall caught him at it, and the four failures were the most difficult magic he had ever done, and she saw through every one of them from the front of the room without moving from her desk.

The lesson had begun the way Hogwarts: A History said her lessons began, which Hermione had told him on the train and he had not believed, and which turned out to be true: a desk, and a pig, and the pig a desk again, in about four seconds, with no word spoken and no visible movement of the wand beyond a thing that might have been a nod. The room had made the sound rooms made. Harry had not made any sound at all, because he had been reading it — the whole sentence, as it went past, the old bracket and the new and the long patient becoming between them, held in a mind that had been holding brackets for thirty-four years and did not know it was being watched — and when the pig was a desk again he sat very still and thought: she holds both. She holds them for a quarter of a second and does not know she is doing it and does it anyway, and she has done it ten thousand times and never once seen it go by.

“Transfiguration,” said McGonagall, “is some of the most complex and dangerous magic you will learn at this school. Anyone messing about in my class will leave and not come back. You have been warned.” She looked down the rows. Her eyes went over Harry at the same speed they went over everyone else, and he thought, again, good. “Matches. One each. By the end of the hour I should like a needle. I should be surprised to get one.”

Hermione got one in forty minutes, which McGonagall remarked upon in a voice that gave nothing away and that Hermione would carry for a week. Padma got silver on the end. Terry Boot got a match that had developed opinions about being a match and expressed them by rolling off the desk.

Harry had decided, on the stairs down from Flitwick’s office with a promise in his pocket, that he would not do it on the first try. He had reasoned it out between the third floor and the second, which was most of an afternoon. A woman in tartan had said I will not have you bore my first-years by being perfect at them, and she had meant it, and she had been right: he had watched Hermione get a light on the first morning and had understood that a room full of first times was a room full of a thing he had never once had and could not have, and that a boy at the back turning matches to needles at a glance would take the first time out of twenty-five other children’s hands. So he would be ordinary. He had never once been ordinary on purpose at anything, and he had a wand now, and the wand changed what ordinary could mean.

Because the thing on kitchen floors would not come for a lie. He had proved that forty times; it sat with its hands folded at I want the pencil to move and would not get up for a sentence he did not mean. But the wand was not the thing on kitchen floors. The wand carried. It took what he sent down it and sanded it and delivered it, and it did not, as far as sixteen years of holding had told him, ask whether he meant it. It only asked whether the sentence was there.

So he could send a sentence that was less. On purpose. A bracket with a part left out. Not a lie — he was not telling the match it was a needle and hoping; he was telling it, precisely, be about a third of the way to a needle and stop, which was a true thing to say and a thing he had never once in his life been able to do with a cat or a pot or a pair of hands, because the asking would not have stood for it. The wand stood for it. He found that out on the first try, with the match going a dull grey halfway up and stopping exactly where he had said, and sat for a quarter-second looking at it with a feeling he did not have a word for and wrote down later as the first time I have ever been able to be bad at something honestly.

He did it three more times. Silver at one end. A point and no eye. A needle that was still, obstinately, made of wood. Each one a sentence with a part turned down, sent true, held, delivered; each one the exact failure a first-year would have produced if a first-year had been able to produce exactly what he intended, which no first-year could. He was, he thought, at the fourth, rather proud of them. They were good failures. They had the texture of the real thing.

“Dr Potter.”

She had not moved from the desk. She was looking at him down the length of the room with the square spectacles catching the window, and her face was doing nothing at all, which he had learned on a step in Surrey was the face she used when she had decided something.

“Come here. Bring the match.”

He went. The room watched him go, the way the Great Hall had watched him walk to a stool, and he carried the match the way he carried things on stages.

“Put it down.”

He put it down on her desk. It was the fourth one: a needle, to the eye, in every respect, except that it was wood.

McGonagall looked at it. Then she looked at him, over the spectacles, for a long moment, and said, quietly enough that only the front row would hear and plainly enough that the front row would carry it:

“That is a very good imitation of a first-year. It is, I think, the best imitation of a first-year I have ever seen, and I have been imitated by a great many of them. The silver on the second one was particularly well judged. Do it properly.”

He looked at her. She looked back. He had the strong sense that she had been waiting since a lounge in Surrey to say do it properly to him in her own classroom, and that she had enjoyed the four matches as much as he had, and that neither of them was going to say so.

“Yes, Professor.”

He picked up the holly. He built the sentence — the old bracket, match, and the new, needle, and the becoming between them, the one he had built at seven under the stairs and kept for the day he had a wand — and he held both, the way the book said no student could and the woman at the desk did ten thousand times without seeing, and he sent it, and the match was a needle.

Steel. An eye. A point that caught the window. No silver halfway; no wood left; a needle, the way the desk had been a pig.

Then, because she had said properly and he had decided on the stairs that properly meant the whole thing, he held the two brackets the other way round, and sent it again, and the needle was a match.

Then a needle. Then a match. Four times, each one clean, each one in the time it took to breathe, and he put the holly down on her desk with the tip towards the window and his hands flat beside it, and the match lay there being a match, and the room did not make any sound at all.

McGonagall looked at the match for a long time.

“Back,” she said at last. “And forth.”

“Yes, Professor. It’s the same sentence. You just hold the brackets the other way. I don’t know why the book says you can’t go back; you can. I think the book means most people can’t hold both long enough to go either way on purpose, and so they go the one way they’ve practised and stop. You go both. I watched you do it with the pig.”

The room had stopped breathing. Hermione, in the second row, had her hand over her mouth.

“You watched me,” said McGonagall.

“Yes. You held the desk and the pig at the same time for about a quarter of a second and you didn’t know you were doing it. You’ve done it ten thousand times. It’s — Professor, it’s very good. It’s the cleanest I’ve seen anyone hold two since the roof. I wanted to tell you that in a room where it would be rude and I’ve decided I don’t mind being rude about this one thing.”

Minerva McGonagall looked at the Boy Who Lived across a match on her desk for a long moment, with her face doing nothing at all, and then she did a thing he had seen her do once, on a pavement in the Charing Cross Road, with a carrier bag from a shop that had closed in 1979: the severe mouth moved, at one corner, by an amount that only he could have measured.

“Dr Potter,” she said. “Sit down. Help Mr Boot with his match, which has just rolled under Miss Patil’s chair. Do not turn it into anything. Do not turn anything of Mr Boot’s into anything. Help him.” She picked up the match from her desk and looked at it. “My office. Tuesdays. Six o’clock. We shall discuss what you did and did not watch me do, and you will not do it in this classroom again, because I will not have twenty-five children spend seven years watching you turn things back and forth instead of learning to turn them the one way, and because—” the mouth did the thing again “—because I have been teaching this subject for thirty-four years and I should like, for once, to be told what I am doing by someone who can see it, and I should like to be told in private, where I can be as rude back as I please.”

“Yes, Professor.”

“And Dr Potter.” He had turned. “The four matches. I shall want to know how you did those as well. I have never once seen a student fail exactly. It is not a thing that can be done.”

“No, Professor,” said Harry. “It wasn’t. I found out at the first one that it was. I’ll tell you on Tuesday. I’ve written it down.”

He went and helped Terry Boot with his match, which had rolled under Padma Patil’s chair and was lying there. He did not turn it into anything. He sat beside Terry and watched Terry try, and on the fourth try he said, “Hold the wand a bit stiller — not your hand, the — never mind your hand. Look at the match. Don’t look at the wand. The wand knows where it is,” and Terry looked at the match, and the match went silver at one end, and Terry Boot said “Oh,” in the exact voice Hermione had used over a light, and Harry sat back and thought: there. That’s two.

Tuesday at six was a small office with a fire in it and a cat basket with no cat and a desk that had been tidy for thirty-four years, and she did not offer him tea, which he respected, and she sat behind the desk with her hands folded and said, “The four matches. Begin.”

He began. He told her about the thing on kitchen floors and the thing in the wand and the difference between them — that the first would not come for a sentence he did not mean, and the second would carry any sentence that was there, and that a sentence that said a third of the way and stop was a thing that was there — and she listened with her face doing nothing, and when he had finished she said:

“You are telling me that a wand does not care whether the wizard means it.”

“I’m telling you it doesn’t check. The thing I do without one checks; it goes looking for whether the need is real and won’t move if it isn’t. The wand doesn’t go looking. It takes what you give it and sands it and sends it. I think that’s why wizards can do things they don’t mean — hurt people they didn’t mean to hurt, in a temper, with a sentence they didn’t build. The wand didn’t ask. I think that’s the most dangerous thing about them and I don’t think anyone’s said so in a book, because nobody’s ever had both to compare.”

McGonagall was quiet for a long moment.

“Nobody has ever had both,” she said.

“No. I had the one for five years before I had the other. I thought the other would be the same thing with a — with a better line. It isn’t. It’s a different thing. It’s faster and it carries and it doesn’t ask. I’ve had it eight days and I’ve done four honest failures and a dimmer and a match both ways and I’m already a little afraid of it, Professor, in a way I was never once afraid of a cat.” He looked at the fire. “I think that’s right. I think I should be. I think the first wizards were, and then somebody made a stick that sanded the fear off along with everything else, and nobody’s been properly afraid since.”

She did not say anything for a while. Then she got up, and went to a cabinet, and came back with a thing he had not expected, which was a photograph, in a frame, old, and she put it on the desk between them and did not sit down.

It was a classroom. This classroom; he knew the windows. Thirty children in robes, in rows, in the slightly stiff attitudes of children who have been told to hold still for a thing that will take a while, and in the second row, with red hair down her back and her chin up and a face that was looking at the camera as if working out how to help it and not having decided whether it deserved it, a girl of eleven. And behind her, one row back, with black hair standing straight up and an expression of elaborate innocence, a boy who was quite plainly in the middle of doing something to the chair in front of him.

“Nineteen seventy-one,” said McGonagall. “The first week. Your mother turned her match into a needle in twenty minutes, which was the fastest I had seen, and then — I had not asked for this — turned it back, because, she said, she wanted to see whether she could, and I told her that it could not be done, and she said oh, in a voice I have never forgotten, and did not do it again in my classroom for seven years, and I have wondered on and off since whether she went on doing it somewhere else.” She looked at the photograph. “Your father turned the leg of the chair in front of him into a snake in the second week and was given detention, which he treated as a compliment. He was the best natural Transfigurer I taught in thirty years and he never once, in seven years, did a thing carefully. Your mother did everything carefully. They were, between them, the two halves of what I have spent my life trying to teach, and neither of them had the other half, and I have never said that to anyone.”

Harry looked at the girl in the second row for a long time.

Turned it back. Because she wanted to see whether she could. And was told it could not be done, and said oh.

“She could see it,” he said. “Not the way I can. But she could feel there was a back. She did it once, and you told her no, and she stopped in your classroom and went on somewhere else, because she wasn’t going to argue with a professor at eleven and she wasn’t going to stop either.” He looked up. “That’s — Professor, that’s what my aunt said. She kept trying till the end. And Mrs Figg. Your mother was the same. Everyone said. And the Headmaster, on Monday: neither of us ever once saw her shout. She was asking. All along. Not the way I do; she didn’t have the marks; but she could feel the shape of it and she went where it went and she didn’t let anyone tell her it couldn’t be done, she just said oh and did it somewhere they weren’t looking.”

McGonagall did not say anything.

“And he never did a thing carefully,” said Harry, more slowly, looking at the boy with his hand on the chair leg. “Which is the wand. The wand doesn’t ask. He had the wand and the nerve and he sent, and she had the — the other thing, the asking, and she held. The two halves.” He stopped. “And I’ve got both. That’s what you’re telling me. That’s why you got the photograph out.”

“I got the photograph out,” said McGonagall, “because you turned a match back and forth four times in my classroom and said I don’t know why the book says you can’t, and I had heard that sentence once before, in that room, in 1971, in a different voice, and I have carried it for twenty years, and I wanted you to know whose it was.” She sat down. “And yes. Both. You asked me on a pavement whether I knew it was impossible. I did not say then what I thought, which was that I had taught two children who between them could have done it, and that they had not lived to find out, and that I had just watched their son do it in a shop. I am saying it now. In private. Where I may be as rude as I please.”

He sat in the small office with the fire going and the cat basket with no cat and the photograph on the desk, and found that he could not, for a moment, say anything at all, which was becoming, he thought, a habit of his in rooms with women in them who had held doors.

“May I—” he said, and stopped, and tried again. “Professor. May I look at it a while longer. I’ve got one photograph of her, on a swing, with my aunt. I haven’t got one of him at all. I didn’t know he — I didn’t know about the chair.”

“You may look at it as long as you like,” said Minerva McGonagall. “And then I am going to tell you what you watched me do with the pig, in your own words, and you are going to tell me whether I have understood them, and I warn you now that if I have not I shall want to know why, at length.” She paused. “That is what Tuesdays are. I had them explained to me by a professor of mathematics in a covering letter two years ago. I believe the phrase was she found the Hungarian trap in February and he would have been in there a month. I have never in my life been anyone’s Hungarian trap, Dr Potter, and I find I should like to try.”

He looked at the photograph a while longer. Then he told her, in his own words, what she did with the pig, and she had understood it, nearly, and where she had not she wanted to know why, at length, and it was half past eight before either of them noticed, and she said that will do in the voice she used for things that had not done at all, and he went down the stairs in the dark with a boy and a chair leg on the shelf beside a girl on a swing, and thought that he had come to Scotland with one photograph and now had two, and that the second had been in a cabinet for twenty years waiting for someone to say I don’t know why the book says you can’t.

Hermione was in the common room with A Beginner’s Guide to Transfiguration open at the chapter on reversal, which was two paragraphs long and said that it was not possible, and she looked up when he came in and said, “You were two and a half hours.”

“She had a photograph.”

“Of what?”

He thought about it. He had decided on the train that there were things that were his and things that were everyone’s and that he would learn, this year, where the line was, and he found that the line had moved while he was in a small office with a fire.

“My parents,” he said. “In her classroom. Nineteen seventy-one. My mother turned her match back and was told it couldn’t be done and said oh.” He sat down across from her. “Hermione. I need you to understand something about Tuesdays, and I’m going to say it badly because I’ve never said it before. I’m not going there to learn Transfiguration. I know Transfiguration; I’ve had the book since I was seven. I’m going there because a woman who has done a thing ten thousand times without seeing it has agreed to let me tell her what it looks like, and to be rude back, and I have never had anyone to tell. I had a professor of mathematics for five years and I could tell her the maths, but not — not this. This is the first time.”

“I know,” said Hermione. “I was in Flitwick’s office. I saw his face.” She closed the book. “Can I come? On Tuesdays?”

“No.”

She looked at him.

“Not because I don’t want you there. Because she doesn’t, yet, and she’s right not to. Saturdays are Flitwick’s and you’re in them because he said so and because you catch. Tuesdays are hers, and she’s a woman who kept a photograph in a cabinet for twenty years and got it out tonight for one person, and I’m not going to walk in next week with someone she didn’t ask for.” He looked at the fire. “Give her a term. She’ll ask. She asked me on a pavement whether I knew a thing was impossible; she’ll ask you something by Christmas, and when she does it’ll be because she’s decided, and not because I pushed her through a door. That’s how she works. I’ve read her.”

Hermione sat with that.

“You read everyone,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Have you read me?”

He had, on a train, in four seconds, and every day since, and he thought about whether to say so and decided that the line had moved far enough for one evening.

“Some,” he said. “Enough to know you’d ask that. Not enough to know what you’d want me to say.” He stood up. “Saturday. Nine. The tin. I’ll show you the pointing part first; it’s the easiest. You’ll see it in about a week and then you’ll be annoyed you didn’t see it before, and that’s how everyone’s going to feel about everything, and I think it’s the best feeling there is, and I’ve never once got to watch it happen to someone else.”

“You watched Terry.”

“Terry got a match to go silver. That’s a spark. I mean the other thing. The oh.” He went towards the stairs. “My mother said it in 1971. I’d like to hear it a great many more times before I’m done.”

He wrote it that night by the window, with the mountains outside being mountains, in the back of the Edition, in English, for the reader in fifty years, because this was the sort of thing the reader would need.

The wand does not ask. I have had it eight days and I know this now the way I know the hall clock. It carries and it sands and it sends and it does not once stop to find out whether I mean it. That is why I could fail four times exactly, which the thing on kitchen floors would never have let me do, and it is why I am a little afraid of it, and I think I am right to be, and I think the fear is the first honest thing I have learned here that is not in a book.

She held the desk and the pig at once. She did not know. I told her in a room where it was rude. She got out a photograph.

My mother turned a match back in 1971 and was told it could not be done and said oh. My father turned a chair leg into a snake and took the detention as a compliment. She asked; he sent. I have both. McGonagall has taught two thousand first-years and never said that sentence to anyone and said it to me in a room with a fire, as rude as she pleased.

I came here with one photograph. I have two.

Terry Boot said oh. That is two of twenty-five in a week. Count them.

He did not sleep. He had known he would not. He lay by the window with a castle singing one long note under him and a boy with his hand on a chair leg on the shelf beside a girl on a swing, and he found that for the first time in his life he was not thinking about the next thing, and he let that be, for a while, because he had read somewhere that a person was allowed to.

Contents

Chapter 26

The Shut Door

The dungeon was cold, and the man at the front of it had a face like a door that had been shut on purpose, and he began the register, and when he reached the P’s he stopped.

“Ah, yes,” said Professor Snape, softly, to the room. “Harry Potter. Our new — celebrity.”

The Slytherins at the back made the sound Slytherins made. The pale boy among them did not; Harry noted that, and noted that the pale boy was watching not Snape but him, with the face from a stool in a shop, and filed it. The Ravenclaws did not make any sound at all, because they had been in a Charms room on Wednesday and a Transfiguration room after it and had learned, in a week, that making a sound when Harry Potter was addressed was a way of missing something.

Harry said nothing. He had decided on the stairs down — most of an afternoon — what he would do in this room, and the first thing he would do was not answer anything that was not a question.

Snape finished the register. He put the parchment down. He looked at the room with the shut-door face and said the thing about there being no foolish wand-waving here, and about bottled fame and brewing glory and stoppering death, and Harry listened to all of it with a quarter of his mind, because it was good — it was very good; it was a man who loved a subject speaking about it in the only register he had left — and with the other three-quarters he watched the face.

He had read it for four seconds across the Great Hall and eleven at breakfast and some minutes since, in corridors, at the staff table, and he had the shape of it now and did not like the shape, because it was not a shape he could do anything about. It was a face that had been shut for a long time, by its owner, from the inside, the way his own cupboard door had been shut, except that this man had not chosen the cupboard. He had been put in it, by something, a long time ago, and had decided to stay, and had nailed the door, and the nails had rusted. Harry knew the shape. He had seen it on his aunt for ten years. He had never once seen it on a man.

And under the shut door — he had seen this on the first night, in a quarter-second, and had filed it under her and not understood it — there was a thing that happened to the face when it looked at Harry’s eyes. Not the scar. The room looked at the scar; Snape did not; Snape looked at the eyes, and the door flinched, every time, the way Petunia’s had flinched across a kitchen table at the word eyes, and then shut harder.

He did not know what it meant. He was going to.

“Potter.”

It had been eleven seconds since the register. He had expected longer.

“What would I get if I added powdered root of asphodel to an infusion of wormwood?”

The room went still. Hermione’s hand went up beside him and he put his own hand, under the desk, on her wrist, lightly, and she lowered it, and looked at him, and he did not look back, because he was looking at the man.

“The Draught of Living Death, sir. It’s in Magical Drafts and Potions, chapter twelve, but the proportions in chapter twelve are for a standard cauldron and the book doesn’t say so, and the standard cauldron it means is a Size Two pewter, which isn’t the one on the first-year list. If you brew it to the chapter in the cauldron on the list you get something that will put a person to sleep for about a week and give them a headache, which isn’t death and isn’t living either.” He kept his voice level and his hands flat. “I don’t know whether you wanted the book answer or the real one. I’ve given you both. I’m sorry if that was more than you asked.”

Snape did not move.

“Where,” he said, after a moment, “would you look if I told you to find me a bezoar.”

“In the stomach of a goat, sir, which is where the book says, and which is true, and which is also useless, because nobody keeps a goat in a cupboard. In the student stores, second shelf, left, in a jar labelled in a hand that isn’t yours; I read the labels on Tuesday when I came down to find the room. There are four. Two are old. I’d use the newer ones; a bezoar loses about a tenth of its — I don’t know your word; its asking — every ten years, which the book doesn’t say but the Healing text does, in the fourth chapter, two pages before a dosage table that has its columns the wrong way round and has killed two people.”

Something happened at the corner of the shut door. It was not a flinch. It was the thing Harry had seen on Carrow over page nine and on Aldous over page ninety and had not expected to see here: a professional, given cause, deciding not to show it.

“The difference,” said Snape, very quietly, “between monkshood and wolfsbane.”

“There isn’t one, sir. They’re the same plant. It also goes by aconite. The three names are because three different sets of people needed it for three different things and named it for the thing they needed, and the book lists it under all three without saying they’re the same, which I think is the single worst piece of indexing in the Standard series and I’ve read all seven and the two that come after.” He paused. “That one was the book answer. I don’t think there’s a real one that’s different. I checked.”

The dungeon was very quiet. Somewhere at the back, a Slytherin who had been about to laugh had decided not to.

Snape looked at him down the length of the room, with the door shut, and the thing at the corner of it that was not a flinch.

“You have read,” he said, “all seven.”

“Yes, sir. And Waffling, and the Healing text, and the two Advanced ones, and a thing on the theory of enchantment that the Headmaster told me to read chapter nine of twice. I was told the first-year list when I was seven and I didn’t see why I should stop there.” He had not meant to say that; he had decided on the stairs to answer only questions; but the man had asked three and had meant them, and Harry had learned at a long table that when someone asked three questions and meant them, you gave them the fourth for nothing. “I’ve read the theory, sir. I haven’t brewed. I’ve never had a cauldron or a fire I was allowed to use or anyone to say not like that. I know what everything on your shelves does and I don’t know what any of it feels like in a ladle. If you want to find out where I’m bad at this, that’s where. I’d rather you found it than didn’t.”

Snape did not say anything for a long time, for him; it was four seconds, which was most of a morning, and Harry spent the morning watching the door, and the door did not open, but something behind it moved, once, like a man in a shut room shifting his weight.

“Then we shall find out,” said Snape. “Pairs. The boil cure. Page four. Mr Potter will work alone.”

He was bad at it.

Not very. He had Mrs Osei’s hands, which were careful, and Mr Haines’s put your hand up and I’ll come over, and sixteen years of holding a wand without sending anything down it, and all of that went into a ladle and a fire and a cauldron that was the one on the list and not a Size Two pewter. But he had never once done it, and the doing was a different thing from the knowing, the way the half of physics that lived in a room had been a different thing, and he found out where he was bad at it in the first ten minutes, which was heat.

He could read the book. He could read, for that matter, the potion — there was a sentence in it, faint, forming, as the dried nettles went in and the snake fangs after, a thing coming together in the cauldron that had the same spine as everything else and a bracket in the middle that was about skin, and he could watch it build, which he suspected nobody else in that room could. But the book said simmer, and he did not know what a simmer looked like, because he had never stood over one, and he had the flame a quarter-turn too high for a full minute before he saw the sentence in the cauldron begin to go ragged at one edge and understood why.

He turned it down. The sentence steadied. He made a note, in his head, where he kept the notes that had not like that in them: simmer is when the bubbles come up singly and break without sound. Nobody wrote that down because everybody knew.

Snape came past at the half-hour. He did not stop. He looked into the cauldron for a second — Harry saw him read the colour, which was right, and the surface, which was right now, and the faint ring on the inside of the pewter a quarter-inch above the liquid, which was where it had been for a minute at the wrong heat and which no one who had not stood over ten thousand cauldrons would have seen — and said, without pausing, in the soft voice:

“Too hot. For how long?”

“About a minute, sir. At the start. I didn’t know what a simmer looked like.”

“And now?”

“Single bubbles. No sound. I’ll know next time.”

Snape went on down the row. He did not say anything else. But Harry, who was watching the back of a shut door go away from him between two rows of cauldrons, saw the shoulders do a thing — very small, a quarter-inch, the thing Mrs Figg’s had done at a sink the night of a cup of tea — that was a man putting down, for one step, something he had been carrying a long way, and picking it up again before anyone could see.

The potion, at the end, was correct. Snape looked at it, and at the ring a quarter-inch up the pewter, and said, “Adequate,” and moved on, and Harry understood that the word had cost him something and had been paid anyway, and put it on the shelf beside Carrow’s hm.

At the back, the pale boy’s potion had gone orange, and the pale boy was looking at Harry’s cauldron with an expression that had nothing of his father in it at all.

Neville found him in the courtyard at break. He had had the same dungeon on the other side of the timetable, and it showed.

“He asked me what a bezoar was,” said Neville. He sat down on the bench beside Harry without being asked, which was new, and good, and Harry noted it. “In front of everyone. And I knew, Harry, I knew it was the goat, Gran’s got one in a jar, and I opened my mouth and it just — went. And he said clearly fame isn’t everything, Longbottom, and neither is a famous grandmother, and Ron said something and lost us five points and I melted the cauldron.” He looked at his hands. “I melted a cauldron. On the first day. He said he’d never seen it done in the first ten minutes.”

Harry looked at him. Neville Longbottom, round and frightened and eleven, with a toad in his pocket that Harry had found behind a pipe, sitting on a bench in a courtyard in Scotland having had the whole of himself taken out by a man with a shut door, and Harry had two hundred times as long as anyone else to sit and feel how real it was. Knowing a thing is small does not make it light to the one carrying it. He had written that at seven, about Dudley and a times table, and it was still true, and it was still the only thing in the world worth knowing about teaching.

He thought about the front door, and rejected it. Neville had been through the front door that morning and it had a man behind it with nails in it.

“Neville,” he said. “What’s a bezoar made of?”

“It’s — it’s a stone. From a goat.”

“What’s it made of, though. What’s in it. Why does it work.”

Neville looked at him. “I don’t — the book doesn’t—”

“The book doesn’t. Your gran’s jar does. You’ve looked at it, haven’t you. On the shelf. For years.”

“…Yes.”

“What colour is it?”

“Grey. Sort of — layered. Like a — like an onion, if you cut it. Gran said it builds up. In the goat. Round a bit of grass it couldn’t digest.” Neville’s voice had changed, a little, without his noticing, the way Dudley’s had changed over the sixes in a football rhyme. “It’s plant in the middle. It’s a plant the goat couldn’t — and then the goat puts stuff round it, year after year, and that’s the stone.”

“And what does a bezoar do?”

“It’s an antidote. For most poisons.”

“And what are most poisons?”

Neville opened his mouth, and shut it, and Harry watched the thing happen on his face that had happened on Hermione’s over a light and Terry Boot’s over a match, the oh coming up from somewhere under the fright like a plant coming up through a paving slab.

“Plants,” said Neville. “Most poisons are plants. Monkshood. Belladonna. Hemlock. They’re — they’re all Herbology, Harry, I know all of those, I know what they look like, Gran’s got half of them in the—” He stopped. “It’s a plant stone that stops plant poisons. It’s — the whole thing’s plants. The whole subject. It’s Herbology with a fire under it.”

“Yes,” said Harry. “It is. You know more about half his shelves than anyone in that room, including me, because I’ve read them and you’ve grown them. He asked you what a bezoar was and you froze because he asked it like a man who wanted you to fail, and you’d never had anyone ask you anything about a plant who wanted you to fail, and it wasn’t the question. It was the door he asked it through.” He stood up. “Next time he asks you a thing, don’t look at him. Look at the jar. In your head. On your gran’s shelf. You’ll know. You always knew. You just didn’t know it was the same shelf.”

Neville sat on the bench looking at his hands, and then at Harry, and the fright was still in his face and would be for years, but there was a thing beside it now that had not been there at breakfast.

“You did that on purpose,” he said slowly. “The questions. You walked me round to it.”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

Harry thought about a kitchen table, and a tennis ball on the stairs, and a cousin who had got to twelve twelves with one wobble and had come home two feet taller and had said you just have to not be thick about it, and had, four years later, handed him a cap on a station concourse.

“Because you almost never move a person by the front door,” he said. “You find the door they already use. Yours is plants. It’s a good door. It opens onto more of the castle than you think.” He picked up his bag. “And because I’ve been the boy nobody asked properly, and it’s a cold place, and I don’t leave people in it. I told Malfoy that on the train. I meant it about him and I mean it more about you.”

He went to Herbology. Neville sat on the bench a while longer, and then — Harry saw it from the door — got out a quill and began, in the back of his Potions book, in a round careful hand, to write down the names of the plants on his grandmother’s shelf, and what each one was for.

Thursday at four was a round room full of eyes and a very strong cup of tea, and he reported the face.

He had kept a tally. Eleven sightings since Monday: breakfast, three corridors, dinner, a staff-table glance across the Hall, the back of a turban going up a staircase. He gave them in order, with the times, and what each one had shown, in the plain words he would have used for Carrow about a proof: the second one is more present at meals. The second one looks at the Slytherin table and the first one looks at his plate. On Tuesday at eleven past eight the second one looked at me for a full second and the first one did not know it had happened; I watched the face not know.

Dumbledore listened with his hands folded and did not interrupt, and when Harry had finished he said, “Thank you,” in a voice that meant it, and then, after a moment: “You have not asked me the fourth question.”

“No, sir. I said a fortnight. It’s Thursday. I’ll ask on Thursday week.” He drank the tea. “I’ve got another, though. It’s on the list; it’s number eleven; I’m moving it up because of this morning.”

“Go on.”

“Professor Snape flinches when he looks at my eyes. Not the scar; everyone looks at the scar. The eyes. He did it on the first night and he did it in the dungeon this morning, twice, and then he shut the door harder. My aunt did the same thing across a kitchen table when she told me I had my mother’s eyes. Mrs Figg said everyone said I had my mother’s eyes. Professor McGonagall got a photograph out on Tuesday of a girl in 1971 with red hair who turned a match back and said oh.” He put the cup down. “And my aunt told me there was a boy. Before Hogwarts. From the bad end of their town, with hair that wanted washing, who knew what my mother was before she did, and told her, and my aunt hated him for it for thirty years.” He looked at the man across the desk. “Professor Snape’s hair wants washing. He teaches a subject that is Herbology with a fire under it. He flinches at my eyes and shuts the door. I don’t know what the question is yet. I know there is one. I’d like you to tell me whether I’m reading the right face.”

Dumbledore did not say anything for a long moment. The hundred eyes ticked.

“You are reading the right face,” he said at last. “And I am not going to tell you what is behind it, Dr Potter, because it is not mine. It is his, and it is the one thing in this castle that I have never once pushed through a door, in eleven years of having the right to, and I am not going to start by handing it to his student in the first week.” He paused. “But I will tell you this, since you asked whether you were reading it right. You are. And the door is not nailed from the outside. He nailed it. And he has had the nails in his pocket the whole time, and has never once, in eleven years, taken them out, and I have stopped asking him to.”

Harry sat with that.

“He put the right ones in,” he said slowly. “Nails. From the inside. The way I did a cupboard door at three.” He looked at the tea. “Then it’s a front-door question. He’ll only open it himself, and only if someone stands outside and asks properly and doesn’t push. I know how that works. I did it to my aunt. It took a cup of tea and five years.”

“It may take longer than five years.”

“I’ve got seven here. And I’m — Professor, I’m good at waiting. It’s the one thing I was good at before I was good at anything.” He finished the tea. “I’m not going to ask him yet. I’m going to be adequate in his dungeon for a term and let him find out where I’m bad at it and say so, because he found the ring on the pewter this morning and nobody else alive could have, and a man who can see a quarter-inch of heat on a cauldron can see when he’s being handled, and I don’t want to handle him. I want to ask him. When he’s ready. Which I’ll know, because I read faces, and his is the only one in this castle that I can’t read all the way to the back, and I think that’s because there’s somebody standing behind it holding the door, and I think I know who, and I think she had red hair.”

Dumbledore closed his eyes.

“Thursday week,” he said, after a while, with them still closed. “Ask me the fourth. I shall have the answer. And Dr Potter — the ring on the pewter. He mentioned it. At lunch. To Minerva. He said the boy saw me see it, in a voice I have not heard him use about a student in eleven years, and Minerva asked him what voice it was, and he did not answer, and I have been thinking about it since.” He opened his eyes. “It was not an unkind voice. That is the whole of what I can tell you about it. I thought you should have that.”

“Thank you,” said Harry, and put it on the shelf under her, beside a flinch and a photograph and a sentence about an awful boy, and went to Herbology, and did not think about anything else for the whole of the way down, which was a long way, by his own clock, and not nearly long enough.

He sat with it that night, by the window, with the mountains outside and the castle singing, and he went carefully, because it was the first thing in a week that he did not understand.

A man with a shut door flinches at my eyes and found a quarter-inch of heat on a pewter cauldron that nobody else could have seen, and said "adequate" as if it cost him, and said to McGonagall at lunch "the boy saw me see it" in a voice she had never heard him use. My aunt knew a boy from the bad end of town with hair that wanted washing who told my mother what she was before she knew. The Headmaster says I am reading the right face and will not tell me what is behind it because it is not his, and that the nails are on the inside, and that he has stopped asking.

I know what a door nailed from the inside is. I had one. Somebody with a cup of tea stood outside mine and did not push and I came out. It took five years. I have seven.

I am not going to handle him. I am going to be bad at his subject honestly, in front of him, every Friday, and let him find where, and say thank you when he does, because he is right every time and because I think nobody has said thank you to that man in a very long while and I would like to find out what his face does when someone does.

Neville said oh. Over a bezoar. That is three.

The pale boy's potion went orange and he looked at mine with nothing of his father in his face. Four, nearly. Not yet.

Hedwig was not on the sill; she was somewhere over Surrey with a letter for the house and one for a librarian and one for a man with chalk on his elbows, and the room was quiet, and the castle sang, and he lay down by the window and did not sleep, and found that the thing he was thinking about was not the List, or the term, or the press, or the cold.

It was a man in a dungeon, with the nails in his pocket, who had seen a quarter-inch of heat and said adequate, and had flinched, twice, at a dead woman’s eyes in a living boy’s face, and had not once looked at the scar.

Her, he thought. He looks at her. Everyone else looks at the morning they woke up to. He looks at her.

He did not know what to do with that yet. He put it on the shelf, next to the photograph, and found that the shelf had got a little warmer, and did not know why, and let it be.

Contents

Chapter 27

Two People Holding It

Defence Against the Dark Arts smelled of garlic, and the man teaching it stuttered, and the stutter was real, and Harry had known it was real since the first syllable, and that was the problem.

He had been watching the face for nine days by then — fourteen sightings, each with its time and what it had shown, in a column at the back of the forty-seventh pad, which was for Thursdays now and for the reader in fifty years, under a heading he had not been able to bring himself to make any plainer than Q. — and he had the shape of it the way he had the shape of Snape’s door and McGonagall’s pig. Two people. One of them nervous, and thin, and clever in a frightened way, and there, in the ordinary sense, the way everyone was there: a man who had gone somewhere and come back wrong and knew it and had built a stutter and a turban and a smell of garlic round the knowing like a wall round a spring. And the other one.

He did not have a word for the other one. He had seven languages and none of them had it. It was not a face. It was a thing that used the face, sometimes, for a second, the way you might use a window — a cold, still, perfectly attentive looking-out that borrowed the first man’s eyes and did not borrow the stutter, because it had no need of one, because it was not afraid of anything in the room.

Except — he had seen this on the Tuesday, at eleven past eight, and had written it down and had not told the Headmaster the whole of it — except, perhaps, him.

“The — the — the Dark Arts,” said Professor Quirrell, from the front of a room with the curtains half drawn, “are n-not a thing, you understand, not a — a list. They are a — an approach. One m-must — one must learn to recognise the approach. Before one can—” He stopped, and swallowed, and looked at his notes, and the first man’s hands shook, slightly, on the lectern.

Harry watched the hands. He had learned at two that hands told you what faces had decided not to. The first man’s hands were afraid. They had been afraid since the man walked in. And then, for a quarter of a second — most of a minute, in the only hours Harry lived in — the hands went still, utterly, the way a cat’s go still on a wall, and the face above them turned, by about four degrees, towards the back of the room where the Ravenclaws sat, and the eyes in it were not the first man’s eyes at all, and they found Harry, and rested.

He did not look away. He had decided on the stair at nine o’clock a fortnight ago that he would not. He sat with his hands flat on the desk and let the thing look, and looked back, and read it, and what he read was this: that it knew him. Not the way the room knew him — the scar, the story, the morning. The way the small silver instrument on the end of the Headmaster’s shelf knew him. Is he still there. Except that this one was not glad.

And behind the door in him — the door he had put on a cold small grain at seven on a train home with a book against his leg, the door the hat had found in the dark and said do not open alone — something moved.

Not much. A shift, the way a sleeper shifts. The grain behind the door had been still for four years, small and cold and disliking him, and it had never once moved, and now, with a thing in a man’s eyes resting on him from the front of a classroom, it turned over, once, like a thing that has heard its name said in another room.

The door held. He had built it at seven and reinforced it at nine and it held, and he sat very still and felt it hold, and the quarter-second ended, and the face at the front was the first man’s again, and the hands shook, and the stutter came back, and Professor Quirrell said, “—b-before one can — can hope to — yes. Page six,” and twenty-six Ravenclaws opened their books, and Harry opened his, and did not read a word of page six, because he was writing, in his head, at the back of the pad, under Q., the fifteenth entry, and the entry said:

Friday, 9.14. It looked. Thirty seconds, mine. It knows me the way the instrument knows me and it is not glad. And the thing behind my door moved. First time in four years. I do not know what that means. I know that it means the two are not strangers.

Thursday cannot come fast enough. That is the first time I have ever written that sentence.

He did not tell Hermione.

He wanted to. He had never once in his life wanted to tell anyone anything — there had never been anyone who could be told; he had told a hat and a dead woman in a dream — and he found, walking out of a room that smelled of garlic with a girl beside him saying something about page six, that the wanting was a physical thing, like the hunger had been, and that it had a shape, and that the shape was her.

But the Headmaster had said watch and tell me, and had said it as a colleague, and Harry had agreed, and the agreeing had been the first thing they had ever done together, and he was not going to break it in the second week because a thing had moved behind a door. So he did the next thing, which he had worked out on the train in August would be the hardest thing about having people in the room, and which he had not expected to need so soon.

“Hermione. There’s a thing I can’t tell you.”

She stopped on the stairs.

“I’m telling you that there is one. So that you know I’m not hiding it from you. I’m — it isn’t mine. Someone told me a thing as a colleague and asked me not to, and I said I wouldn’t, and I’m not going to, and I wanted you to know there was a shape where a thing should be, so that when I’m — when I go quiet, in a room, or look at someone too long, you don’t think it’s you.” He looked at her. “I’ve never had anyone in the room to not-tell before. I don’t know how it’s done. I’m doing it badly. I’m sorry.”

Hermione looked at him for a long moment on the stairs with Defence under her arm.

“Is it about Quirrell?”

He did not say anything, which was a thing she had learned, in two weeks, to read.

“Right,” said Hermione. “Right.” She started down the stairs again. “Then I shan’t ask. And Harry — that was done fine. I’d rather be told there’s a door than not told there’s a door. I grew up in a house where nobody told me there were doors and I kept walking into them.” A pause. “Thursday’s your day with him, isn’t it. The Headmaster.”

“Four o’clock.”

“Then tell him that if he’s going to have you not-tell me things, he’d better have good ones.” She said it to the stairs. “Because I catch. He should know that. If it matters, I’ll catch it anyway, eventually, and it would be better all round if it was on purpose.”

He put that on the shelf. It went in warm, and then it went in somewhere else, somewhere he did not have a name for yet, and he left it there.

Thursday at four was a very strong cup of tea and a hundred eyes and a man who had, Harry saw from the door, already decided to tell him.

“The fourth question,” said Dumbledore, before Harry had sat down. “Ask it. I said a fortnight and it has been a fortnight and I find I have been wanting you to ask since Tuesday, which is new, and uncomfortable, and I should like to get it over with.”

Harry sat in Flitwick’s chair. “What’s in Professor Quirrell’s face?”

“Voldemort.”

He said it plainly, the way he had said wraith, and the hundred eyes did the thing they had done a fortnight ago — went still, for a quarter-second, all at once — and the hat on its shelf, which had been about to say good morning, said nothing.

Harry sat with it. He had known. He had known since Tuesday at eleven past eight and had not let himself write the word, and now the word was in the room, in the voice of the only man alive the books said had ever frightened it, and he found that knowing it and hearing it were, as always, two different things, and that the second was colder.

“How,” he said.

“I do not know. That is the honest answer and you shall have it first. I know that a man who was not dead went somewhere in the autumn of 1981 and was less — less than a man, less than a ghost, a thing that could not hold itself up — and that such a thing, if it is to go on, must find something to hold it. A place. A creature. A mind.” Dumbledore’s hands were folded on the blotter and did not move. “Quirinus Quirrell went to Albania a year ago, on a sabbatical, to study the Dark Arts at their source, which was foolish, and I told him so, and he went. He came back in July with a stutter he had not had and a turban he had not worn and a smell of garlic he had not needed, and I looked at him across a staff table and saw a frightened man, and told myself that was all I saw.” A pause. “You looked at him for four seconds and saw two.”

“What does it want?”

“A thing that is in this castle, that was not in this castle until the first of September, and that I put here.” He unfolded his hands. “Dr Potter. On the thirty-first of July, while you were in Gringotts depositing one galleon and four sickles, a vault was broken into. It was empty. It had been emptied that morning, by a very large man on my instructions, and the thing that had been in it is now on the third floor of this school, behind a door, in a corridor that I have told the students is out of bounds on pain of a very painful death, and I have done that—” he met Harry’s eyes “—on purpose. So that the thing in Quirrell’s face would know where it was.”

The office was very quiet.

Harry had read Hogwarts: A History. He had read the law, and the preamble, and the dissenting pamphlets of 1692. He had read a thin book about a prison in the sea. He had not read anything that prepared him for a kind man with a hundred eyes telling him, with his hands unfolded, that he had put a thing in a school full of children so that a monster would come for it.

“You’ve made a trap,” he said. “Out of the school.”

“Yes.”

“With first-years in it.”

“Yes.”

“That’s—” He stopped. He had not been angry on Monday a fortnight ago with a letter on the blotter; he had had the anger ready for ten years and found he could not use it. He found now that he had a different anger, one he had not known he owned, and that it was fresh, and that it was not for himself at all. “Professor. There are two hundred and eighty children in this building. Eleven of them are in my dormitory. One of them melted a cauldron on Friday because a man asked him a question through a shut door, and you have put that on the third floor and told them not to go near it in a voice that every eleven-year-old alive hears as an invitation, and you have done it on purpose, and you are telling me this as a colleague, and I am eleven, and I have read the Statute and the preamble and I have never once read anything in any book on either side of the curtain that says a headmaster may do that.”

“No,” said Dumbledore. “You have not. Because no book says it, because no headmaster has, because it is not a thing that is done. I am doing it anyway.” He did not raise his voice. He did not need to; the hundred eyes had gone still and stayed still. “Shall I tell you why, or would you like to be angry first? You have earned the anger. I should not like to take it from you by explaining.”

Harry sat in Flitwick’s chair with his hands in his lap and the fresh anger in him like a thing he had just been handed and did not yet know the weight of, and he thought about Carrow, who had said you put your weight where you’ve got weight, and about a woman in tartan who had said they will be wrong, and about a sentence he had said to a cupboard door at five: Let them deal with it. That is their job. Yours is to stop making it hard.

“Explain,” he said. “I’ll be angry after. I’d like to be angry about the right thing.”

Dumbledore inclined his head, one inch.

“A wraith,” he said, “cannot be killed, because it is not alive. It cannot be caught, because it is not solid. It can be driven out — of a host, of a place — and when it is driven out it goes somewhere else, and finds something else to hold it, and the something else is, as often as not, worse than the last. I drove it out of a forest in 1983 and it went to a mountain. I drove it out of the mountain in 1986 and it went to Albania. I did not drive it out of Albania; it came back on its own, in a man’s head, because it had heard that a thing it wants was in a vault, and it is now in my school, where I can see it, for the first time in ten years, every morning at breakfast.” He paused. “If I confront it tonight — and I could; Quirinus is not a difficult man to confront — it leaves him. In an hour it is in a bird, or a snake, or a Hufflepuff, or a man in Hogsmeade, and I have lost it again, and I shall not find it for another five years, and when I do it will have had five years to think. So I have done the other thing. I have given it a reason to stay. To stay in one man, in one castle, under a hundred eyes and a staff of eleven who are not fools, and to want a thing badly enough that it will go to the one place in this school where I have arranged for it to be pinned — not driven out; pinned — and there, Dr Potter, and only there, I believe I can do something to it that has not been done to it before. I may be wrong. I have been wrong about a great deal. But I have thought about this since 1981, and it is the one plan I have ever had that puts the thing where I can reach it, and I have decided to be sure.”

“And the children.”

“Are in the one building in Britain where a wraith will not harm them, because it needs this building, and a wraith that wants a thing does not spend itself on first-years. It will go to the third floor. It will not go to the dormitories. I know the thing, Dr Potter; I have known it since it was eleven and sat in a chair not unlike yours; it has never once in its life been wasteful.” Something moved in his face. “That is the one virtue it has, and I am relying on it, and I am aware of what it says about me that I am.”

Harry sat with that for a long time, by any clock.

He found, when he came to the end of the long time, that the fresh anger was still there and had not gone anywhere, and that beside it, now, was a second thing, which was that the man was right. Not right to do it. Right that it was the only plan that put the thing where he could reach it. Harry had read the law and the history and the clerk’s six pages and he knew what the thing had done, in the careful prose of books that went quiet around it, and he had been trying since he was nine to read the cold, and here was a man who had spent ten years getting the cold to come and sit in one chair, and had done it, and it was in a room that smelled of garlic on the first floor.

“I don’t agree,” he said. “I want that on the record. I think a school is the wrong place and I think sure is the second kind of reason, the fear kind, wearing a better coat, and I think you know that, because you said I have decided to be sure and not I am sure, and I read the difference.” He let that land. “And I think you’re right that it’s the only plan that pins it. I’ve been trying to read one of these things since I was nine. You’ve got one to sit still. I understand what that’s worth. I don’t think it’s worth two hundred and eighty children and I don’t think I can stop you and I don’t think I should try, because you’ve been doing this since before my aunt was born and I’ve been doing it a fortnight.” He looked at the man across the desk. “So here is what I’m going to do instead, and I’d like you to agree to it, because I think it’s the thing a colleague would ask for.”

“Go on.”

“Three things. One: I’m not going near the third floor. Not to look, not to test, not for Hermione, not for anything. You have my word and I don’t give it often; I gave it to a woman on a pavement and to my aunt at a sink and that’s the list.
