Two: I watch, every day, the way I have been, and I tell you on Thursdays, and I tell you more — I tell you what the thing does when it looks at me, because it does look at me, and today in a classroom it looked for thirty of my seconds, and it knows me the way the small one on the end knows me, and it isn’t glad.” He stopped. He had decided on the stairs that he would say the next part and had nearly decided, on the stairs, not to, and the deciding had taken most of a day. “And three: Professor Flitwick knows. Not everything. Not the Stone — I don’t need to know what the thing is; you said a thing and I’ll leave it at a thing until you say otherwise. But Flitwick knows that I can read a face in that classroom that nobody else can, and he knows what the face is doing, week by week, because I tell him on Saturdays, so that there is one person in this castle with a chair ready who knows what I know and is not you. Because you’re sure. And I would like someone in the building who isn’t.”

Dumbledore looked at him for a long moment across the blotter with the hundred eyes gone still and the hat on its shelf not saying anything.

“Filius,” he said at last, “has read your two pages on Dementors four times and burned a letter from the Department of Mysteries on your account, and would, I think, keep a confidence from me if you asked him to, which is a thing I have never once in forty years been able to say about Filius, and I find I am not certain whether to be alarmed or relieved.” He unfolded his hands. “Yes. To all three. And Dr Potter — the thing you said. I have decided to be sure. You are right. It is the fear kind. I have had it on for ten years and I had not noticed it was a coat.” He paused. “Thursday. Tell me what it does when it looks at you. All of it. I shall want the thirty seconds.”

“You’ll have them,” said Harry, and did not say the other thing — and something behind a door moved — because that was item nine, and the hat had said not alone and not who you think, and the man across the desk with a hundred eyes and a trap on the third floor was, Harry had just decided, in that chair, with fresh anger in him and a kind man being right about the wrong thing, precisely who he thought.

Flitwick heard it on the Saturday, in the small warm office, with Hermione sent to fetch a book from the common room that did not need fetching, and he heard it in the chair, and when Harry had finished he did not say anything for a long time, and then he said:

“Albus has put that on the third floor.”

“Yes, sir. A thing. He didn’t say what and I didn’t ask.”

“I know what.” Flitwick’s small face had gone a colour Harry had not seen on it. “I helped him move it. He said it was for safekeeping. He asked me to — never mind what he asked me to. Dr Potter, that man—” He stopped. He took his short dark wand out and looked at it and put it away again, which Harry understood was what Flitwick did instead of swearing. “Two people. In Quirinus’s face. And it looks at you.”

“For thirty seconds, yesterday. Mine. It knows me.”

“Of course it knows you. It—” Flitwick stopped again, and Harry watched him arrive at the edge of a thing and decide, as the Headmaster had, not to hand a fear to a boy for Charms. “Saturday. You will tell me every Saturday what it did. I shall write it down. If it ever — if the face ever does a thing you have not seen it do before, you will not wait for Saturday. You will come to this office, at any hour, and if I am not in it you will go to Minerva, and if she is not in hers you will go to the Headmaster, and if he is not in his you will go to Severus, and I know what you are going to say about Severus and you will go anyway, because he was in that war and I was not and he knows what that face is from the inside in a way none of the rest of us do.” He looked at Harry. “Promise me.”

“I promise. Sir — Severus?”

“Severus,” said Flitwick. “Any hour. Now. Hermione will be back in two minutes and she has read your face for a fortnight and she will know something has been said, and you had better decide before she opens that door how much of a shape you are going to let her see, because that girl could find a door in a wall with her eyes shut, and I should know, I have been teaching her to for a fortnight and she is catching.” The beam came back, with an effort Harry could see. “Page thirty-one. The pointing part. Where were we.”

Hermione came back with the book that did not need fetching and looked at the two of them and said nothing, and sat down, and opened the Second Edition at the pointing part, and read the first mark aloud in a careful voice, and got it right, and Flitwick said “Yes,” with real pleasure, and Harry sat on the first-year stool and watched a girl who caught learn to read a language with one speaker, and thought about a face with two people in it, and a thing behind a door that had moved, and a man who had decided to be sure.

Severus, he thought. Any hour.

Not who you think.

He did not know yet. He put it on the shelf, next to a flinch and a photograph, and went back to the pointing part.

That night he wrote the fifteen sightings out fair, in the forty-seventh pad, and under them, in English, for the reader in fifty years, the thing he had decided in a chair with the fresh anger in him.

He has made a trap out of a school. He is right that it is the only plan that pins the thing, and wrong that it is worth the building, and he knows both, and has decided to be sure, which he admits is a coat. I have told him I do not agree. I have told him I will not go near the third floor. I have told Flitwick. Those are the three things a colleague can do when he cannot stop the senior man and does not think he should try, and I have done them, and I am still angry, and I think the anger is right and I am going to keep it where I can see it.

The thing in the face looks at me. It knows me the way the instrument knows me. And the thing behind my door moved, once, when it looked, like a sleeper hearing its name. I have not told him that. I have not told Flitwick. The hat said not alone and not who I think, and I have just found out who I think, and it is the man with a hundred eyes who has decided to be sure, and I have decided — this is the decision; I am writing it so I cannot pretend — that he is exactly who the hat meant. Not him. Not yet.

Flitwick said Severus, any hour. I do not know what that means. I have put it on the shelf next to the others. The shelf under her is getting crowded.

Hermione read the first mark aloud today and got it right. Flitwick said yes. I have been the only speaker of a language for five years and as of this morning there are two and a half.

Fourteen days of a fortnight were spent on this. I would spend them again. But I came here for the blankets, and the cold, and the table, and I have a term, and a fortnight of it is gone on a face, and I am going to spend next week on a bronze disc with Flitwick and a girl who catches, because the man upstairs is sure and I am not and the only answer I have ever had to a thing I could not stop was to build something else beside it.

He closed the pad. He did not sleep. He had not expected to. He lay by the window with the castle singing one long note under him, and somewhere on the first floor a man with a stutter was asleep in a room that smelled of garlic with a thing in his head that was not asleep at all, and somewhere behind a door in Harry Potter a small cold grain that had been still for four years was still again, and he felt it be still, and did not trust it, and kept the door shut, and waited for morning, which was a long time coming, and came.

Contents

Chapter 28

The Workshop

Hermione read her first whole sentence on the third Saturday, at twenty past ten, in Flitwick’s office, off page thirty-four of a biscuit tin, and the sentence was warm, and she read it the way a child reads a first sentence off a cereal packet — a mark at a time, aloud, with her finger on each — and when she got to the end of it she stopped, and did not say oh.

She said nothing at all. For four seconds. Which was long for her and most of a quarter-hour for him, and he sat on the first-year stool and watched her face and did not breathe, because he had never once in his life seen this from the outside and had wanted to since he was six.

“It’s—” she said. And stopped. And then, slowly, with her finger still on the last mark: “It’s not a description. I thought it was a description. I thought you’d written down what the charm does, in a — in a code, and I was learning the code. It isn’t that. This is — Harry, this is what happens. In the — in the moment. This is the thing I can’t see. You’ve written down the thing I can’t see, and I’m reading it, and I can’t see it and I can read it and those are—” She looked up. “Those are different. Aren’t they. Seeing and reading. You can do both. I can only do one.”

“Yes,” said Harry. “That’s exactly it. That’s the whole thing. You’ve got it in three weeks and I didn’t have the words for it till I was eight.”

“But if I can read it—” She was looking at the page again. “If I can read it, then I know what the pushing part is, and I know where it is in the sentence, and I know that in Lumos it’s the third mark and it’s the one you turned down. So if I say Lumos and I think about the third mark while I—”

She had her wand out before she had finished the sentence. Flitwick, in the chair, made a small sound that was not quite a word. Harry did not move.

“Lumos.”

The light came. Yellow, small, wavering, the light she had made on the first morning and every morning since. And then — he saw it; he was the only one in the room who could have — her face did a thing, a going-inward, a reaching for the third mark, and the light did not dim.

But it steadied.

It stopped wavering. Not all the way; it was not his white point; it was a candle in a room where someone had shut a door. But it had been a candle in a draught for three weeks and it was not now, and she held it, and looked at it, and the thing came up through her face from wherever it had been since a train, and she said it, at last, very quietly, to the light:

“Oh.”

Flitwick had both hands over his mouth.

“I didn’t turn it down,” said Hermione. “I couldn’t find the — I know where the mark is but I can’t find the thing it’s for, not in me, not yet. But I knew it was there. And knowing it was there was enough to—” She let the light go out. “Harry. Is that what it is? Is that what you meant by catching? That you don’t have to see it. You just have to know it’s there and the knowing does something.”

“I think so,” said Harry. “I didn’t know that till just now. I’ve only ever done it from the inside. I’ve never watched anyone do it from the outside who knew the marks.” He looked at Flitwick. “Sir. She steadied it by reading. Not by watching me. By reading the page and knowing the third mark was in the sentence. That means it isn’t only catching. It means the marks work — not as a record. As a handle. Like the incantation is a handle, except that the incantation is a handle for the whole sentence and the marks are handles for the parts.”

Filius Flitwick took his hands away from his mouth.

“Forty-one years,” he said, in a voice that had no bounce in it at all. “Forty-one years I have told children that the word is the handle. The word. One handle, for the whole thing, and they shout it, and it works, and that is Charms.” He looked at the page under Hermione’s finger. “And you are telling me that there are — how many marks in Lumos?”

“Seven and a bracket.”

“Seven handles. For a thing I have taught as one.” He got out of the chair, which Harry had not seen him do voluntarily since the first Saturday, and stood very small in the middle of his own office with his short dark wand in his hand, and looked at it. “Miss Granger. Do it again. I want to watch your face.”

She did it again. He watched her face. Harry watched him watch, and saw the moment — a quarter-second; most of a minute — when a Charms master of forty-one years saw something go past that he had never seen, not the thing itself, not the sentence, but the shadow of it on a girl’s face as she reached for a third mark, and understood that there was a shape where he had always been told there was a word.

“Oh,” said Filius Flitwick, very softly, to nobody.

Harry put that on the shelf, next to a woman’s face over a cat, and did not say anything, because some things were better carried than said, and because there were now, by his count, three and a half speakers of a language that had had one for five years, and he wanted to sit with the number before it changed again.

The room was Flitwick’s idea, and the people were nobody’s, and that was, Harry decided afterwards, exactly how it should have been.

“You cannot do this in my office,” Flitwick said, on the fourth Saturday, when there were three of them and a biscuit tin and a bronze disc the size of a saucer that he had got from somewhere and would not say where. “Not because I mind. Because my office is twelve feet across and you are going to need a board, and a bench, and somewhere to put the things that go wrong, and you are going to need — Dr Potter, you know what you are going to need, I have watched you not say it for a month.”

“People,” said Harry.

“People. You cannot build a thing that presses a charm into a blanket with one eleven-year-old who can see and one who can read and one old man who can do neither. You need hands. You need people who will stand in a room while you hold something and come out the far end holding steadier, because that is what you told Miss Granger on a landing and I have been thinking about it since and I believe you are right.” He pushed the bronze disc across the desk. “There is a classroom on the fourth floor that has not been used since 1964 because the floor slopes and the windows face the wrong way for anything a teacher wants. It has a board. I have the key. I am going to give it to you, and I am not going to ask who you let in, and I am going to come on Saturdays and sit in the corner and be told things, and when the Headmaster asks me what is going on in the sloping room I am going to say Charms club, which will be true.”

Harry looked at the key. It was large and old and had a sentence in it so small he nearly missed it, which said, as near as he could put it, this one opens.

“Sir,” he said. “Why?”

Flitwick considered the question with the seriousness it had not quite deserved.

“Because I wrote to the Department of Mysteries at twenty-two,” he said, “and they told me the question was not well-formed, and I kept the letter sixty years and burned it a month ago because a boy showed me a page. And because I watched Miss Granger steady a light by reading a mark, and I have taught two thousand children and never once seen a thing I did not know was there. And because—” the beam came up, and under it something older “—because you are going to do this with or without a room, Dr Potter, in a cupboard if you have to, you have the face of a boy who has done things in cupboards, and I should rather it was a room with a board, and me in the corner, and Minerva knowing where to find you.” He stood up. “Charms club. Thursdays and Saturdays. Bring who you like. Give them the good ones first; I am told that is the method.”

He did not pick them. He had decided that on the train in August — you find the door they already use — and he held to it, and the room filled the way a room fills when there is a thing in it worth coming for, which is sideways, one at a time, each for a reason that was not the reason.

Terry Boot came first, because Terry had got a match to go silver by looking at the match instead of the wand and had said oh, and had come up to Harry in the common room on the Monday and said, “The match. You said the wand knows where it is. How does it know?” — and Harry had said, “Come to the sloping room on Thursday and I’ll show you,” and Terry had come, and had sat on a bench while Harry held a feather two inches off the desk for four seconds, perfectly still, and had said, afterwards, slowly, “It wasn’t your hand. Hermione said it wasn’t your hand. It wasn’t,” and had come back on Saturday without being asked.

Padma Patil came second, because she had watched Terry come back on Saturday, and because she was the kind of person who did not go anywhere twice without a reason and did not like watching other people have reasons she did not. She came in with her chin up and sat down at the far end of the bench and said, “I want to know what you did with the match in McGonagall’s class. Not the needle. The four before it. Nobody fails exactly. I watched you do it and I have been annoyed for a month.” Harry told her. She listened with her arms folded, and at the end she said, “That is the most useful thing anyone has told me since I got here,” and stayed.

Neville came third, and came from the wrong house, and nearly did not come at all.

“I can’t,” he said, in the courtyard, when Harry asked. “It’s a Ravenclaw thing. I’d — Harry, I melted a cauldron. I can’t sit in a room with Padma Patil and—”

“Neville. What did you write in the back of your Potions book?”

Neville went red. “Plants. Gran’s shelf. What they’re for.”

“How many?”

“…Forty-one.”

“Forty-one plants and what each one does. Padma Patil has read the book. You have grown the book.” Harry looked at him. “There is going to be a thing in that room that presses a charm into a blanket, and a charm that checks whether a person is cold, and I have no idea — none — what happens when you press a sentence into wool, because wool is a plant, nearly, or it came off a thing that ate plants, and I don’t know what plants do when you ask them things, and you’re the only person in this school under thirty who does.” He paused. “That’s a real reason. I don’t make up reasons. Come on Thursday. Sit by the door if you want. Nobody is going to ask you what a bezoar is.”

Neville came on the Thursday. He sat by the door. He said nothing for an hour, and then Harry, holding the bronze disc, said, half to himself, “It’s not taking the sentence; it slides off; bronze is smooth and I don’t know why that matters—” and Neville said, from the door, in a small voice, “It’s like a seed. You can’t plant on glass. It needs something to hold on to,” and the room went quiet, and Harry turned round and looked at him for a long moment and said, “Yes. Yes, that’s — Neville, that’s it, that’s the thing I’ve been missing since March,” and Neville Longbottom sat by the door of a sloping room in a Ravenclaw tower and went a colour that was not red, and came back on Saturday, and sat one bench further in.

Penelope Clearwater came fourth, and came because she was a fifth-year prefect and had noticed that four first-years and a Gryffindor were spending Thursday evenings in a room that had not been used since 1964, and had come to send them to bed, and had stood in the doorway for forty minutes watching a match become a needle and a needle a match, and had said, at the end, in a changed voice, “Flitwick knows about this.”

“He gave us the key.”

“Right.” She had looked at the board, where Harry had drawn the seven marks of Lumos in chalk from the back of a door in Egham. “Right. Then I’m going to come on Saturdays, and if anyone asks, you’re a Charms club and I’m supervising, and Potter — I did my OWLs in June and I got an O in Charms and I have never once in five years seen anything like what you did with that needle, and I would like to know why, and I’m prepared to be a prefect about it.” She had sat down. She had not got up again.

And Ron came fifth, and came last, and came for the biscuits, and that was the reason that was not the reason.

Mrs Figg’s tin had arrived by owl on the Wednesday — a new one, enormous, with the Humber Bridge on it, and a note in the right hand that said for the room. Give them the good ones first. I am told there is a room. — A.F. — and Harry had put it on the bench in the sloping room and said nothing, and on the Thursday Ron Weasley had put his head round the door at half past six, having followed a rumour and a smell.

“Neville said there were biscuits.”

“There are. The good ones are on the left. Sit down.”

Ron sat down. He ate four. He watched Harry hold a feather and Hermione steady a light and Padma fail at a match exactly, which she had got the hang of and was unbearable about, and at the end of the hour he said, through the fifth biscuit, the thing nobody else in the room had dared to say in a month:

“This is mad. You know that. You’re eleven and you’ve got a prefect taking notes and Neville talking about seeds and you’re trying to make a — what did you call it — a press, that puts charms in blankets, for Muggles, which is illegal, and Flitwick gave you a key.” He swallowed. “Why?”

The room went quiet.

Harry put the feather down. He had been waiting a month for someone to ask that, and had known it would not be Hermione, who chased, or Terry, who caught, or Padma, who was annoyed, or Neville, who sat by the door, or Penelope, who had an O in Charms and was prepared to be a prefect about it. It would be the one who came for the biscuits and asked the dumb question, because the dumb question was the one everyone else had decided was beneath them, and it was the only one that mattered.

“Because there’s a girl in a hospital in London,” he said, “who was six two years ago and had her photograph in a newspaper because the ward was cold at night and the heating was a line in a budget. And eleven miles from her there’s a hospital on this side where they mend a spine in three places over a weekend and mention it in passing. And nobody — Ron, nobody, in three hundred years — has ever once put the two of them in the same room.” He looked at the Humber Bridge tin. “I can hold a sentence steady. That’s the only thing I can do that nobody else can. If I can hold it steady enough to press it into a bronze disc, and the disc can press it into wool, then a thousand blankets can carry a charm that asks a child how cold she is and gives exactly the difference, and the child never knows, and comes to no harm, and is warm. That’s the whole of it. That’s what the room is for. The Headmaster said bring me a case and I said give me a term and this is the term.” He paused. “It is illegal. As it stands. I asked. The woman in tartan said as it stands in a voice that meant she’d thought about it. I’m going to build the thing and press the charm and show them a blanket and then I’m going to find out what it stands as.”

Ron sat with the fifth biscuit halfway to his mouth for a long moment.

“Right,” he said. “Right. That’s — okay. That’s a reason.” He ate the biscuit. “My mum knits. Jumpers. Every Christmas. She’d — if you need someone to tell you about wool, I mean, I don’t know anything about wool, but I know someone who’s made about four hundred jumpers out of it and complains about every one.” He looked at Neville. “Is that the sort of thing you meant? Seeds?”

“Yes,” said Neville, from one bench further in. “That’s exactly the sort of thing.”

“Right,” said Ron Weasley, and reached for a sixth, and did not leave, and that was five, and a prefect, and a very small man in the corner who had said nothing for an hour and whose face Harry had been watching, and whose face was doing the thing it had done over a girl reading a mark.

Hermione waited until the room had emptied.

He had known she would. He had read her for six weeks and she had a thing she did when she was carrying a sentence she had not yet decided to say, which was to tidy, and she had tidied the bench and the board and the Humber Bridge tin, and when there was nothing left to tidy she turned round and said it.

“You’re using us.”

He did not say anything.

“As instruments. You said so, on the landing — I think that’s how it spreads; the watching. You want people in the room so they’ll catch. Terry caught. I caught. You’re counting. You told me you were counting. You said that is the first page of the table.” Her chin was up. “I don’t mind being in the room, Harry. I want to be in the room; I’d rather be in this room than anywhere I’ve ever been. But I want to know whether I’m in it because you like me or because I steady a light faster than Padma, and I’d like you to tell me the truth, because you told me on a train that you don’t lie to your aunt and I’ve been assuming that extends.”

He sat on the bench in a room that sloped, with a bronze disc in front of him that would not take a sentence because it was smooth, and thought about how to answer honestly, which was the only way he knew how to think about anything.

“Both,” he said. “I told you that on the landing and I meant it and I still do. I like you because you chase. I need you because you catch. I don’t know how to have one without the other, Hermione; I’ve never had either before, I had a professor of mathematics who was both and I didn’t know there was a difference till you asked me just now.” He looked at the disc. “And yes. I’m counting. I count everything. I counted my uncle’s shouting and I count the seconds the thing in Quirrell’s face looks at me and I count how many people in a room say oh. It’s how I know what’s real. If I didn’t count I wouldn’t know that Terry caught in two sessions and you caught in one and Padma hasn’t yet and Neville said seeds and that was the most important thing anyone has said in this room including me. Counting is how I see you. It isn’t instead of liking. It’s how the liking knows it isn’t making things up.”

Hermione looked at him for a long time with her chin up.

“That,” she said finally, “is the strangest thing anyone has ever said to me, and I’ve had a hat tell me who wrote Hogwarts: A History.” She sat down on the bench across from him. “You count because otherwise you wouldn’t trust that you liked me.”

“I count because otherwise I wouldn’t trust anything. I’ve been the only one in the room for ten years. You learn to check.” He pushed the disc an inch towards her, which was Carrow for take it. “I’m sorry. I should have said it before you had to ask. I said on the stairs I didn’t know how to have people in the room and I don’t and I’m doing it badly and I’d rather you told me every time than not.”

“I will,” said Hermione. She picked up the disc. “Every time.” She turned it in her hands. “Neville’s right. It’s smooth. It needs something to hold on to. What if you scored it? Like — like a seed tray. Grooves.”

Harry looked at the disc in her hands for a quarter-second, which was most of a minute, and in the minute he saw a bronze disc with the marks of the doorstep sentence cut into it — not pressed; cut; a sentence in a thing the way the runes were sentences in things, except in his marks instead of theirs, so that the disc did not have to hold the sentence by being asked; it held it by being it — and the whole of the design in the biscuit tin turned over, once, and came down facing a different way.

“Hermione,” he said. “Grooves.”

“Is that—”

“That’s March. That’s the thing I’ve been missing since March. I was trying to press it in. You carve it. You carve it in my marks, and then the disc is the sentence, and then the disc can press because it isn’t holding anything, it just is—” He stood up. “Flitwick. Is he still—”

“He went to dinner.”

“Then tomorrow. Nine. Bring the disc. And Hermione—” He stopped at the door. “Thank you. For asking. And for the grooves. The grooves are better than the asking and I didn’t think anything would be.”

“The asking was better,” said Hermione, with her chin up. “The grooves were just a seed tray. Go to dinner. You haven’t eaten since breakfast; I counted.”

He wrote it in the forty-seventh pad that night, by the window, with the mountains, and he wrote it for the reader in fifty years, because the reader would want to know where the table had started.

Five, and a prefect, and a very small man in a corner. Terry, who caught. Padma, who was annoyed and fails exactly now and is unbearable about it. Neville, who said seeds. Penelope, who got an O and is prepared to be a prefect about it. Ron, who came for the biscuits and asked why, which nobody else would, and then said his mother knits.

And Hermione, who read a whole sentence off page thirty-four and said oh, and steadied a light by knowing the third mark was there, which means the marks are handles and not a record, which Flitwick said oh about, which makes three and a half, and then asked me whether I was using her, and I said both, and she said grooves.

Grooves. Carve it. The disc is the sentence. I have been trying to press since March and the answer was a seed tray and a girl who tidies when she is carrying a thing she has not decided to say.

I am going to count the people who say oh in that room and I am going to tell them I am counting and I am going to let them be angry about it and tell me every time. That is the table. Not the blankets. The blankets are what the table is for. The table is six people and a tin from a woman in Surrey who has never once been in a room with any of them and sent the good ones anyway.

Thursday the thing in the face looked at me for twelve seconds and the door did not move. I am counting that too.

He closed the pad. The Humber Bridge tin was on the sill where he had put it to keep it from Ron, and he looked at it, and at the mountains, and at a bronze disc on the table beside the bed that was smooth and would not take a sentence and would, tomorrow, at nine, be cut, and he lay down and did not sleep and had known he would not, and found that for the first time since a doorstep he was not the only speaker of anything, and that it was better, as he had been told, and that nobody had told him it would be this much better, and that the reader would need to be told that too, later, when he had the words, which he did not, in any of seven languages, yet.

Contents

Chapter 29

The Asking

The graver was Flitwick’s, and was older than Flitwick, and had a handle worn to the shape of a hand that was not Harry’s, and he cut the doorstep sentence into bronze with it over nine evenings in a room that sloped, and it was the slowest thing he had ever done, and he would not have had it faster.

He had known his hands were the slow part since he was two. He had drilled a tongue under a blanket and a pen in an examination hall and a ladle over a flame, and each time the knowing had been a different kind of humbling, and this was the deepest. A sentence he could hold in his head for a quarter-hour of his own time without a mark slipping took him an hour, with a graver, to cut one mark of — the pointing part, a shape like a hook, which was the simplest of the seven and which came out, the first evening, with a wobble in it that he could see from across the room and that Neville, from his bench by the door, could not.

“It looks fine,” said Neville.

“It isn’t. The hook’s meant to close by a hair and it doesn’t. If I press from that the copy will point nearly at the thing and nearly is a cold child in the next bed.” He put the graver down. “Hermione. Would you read it back to me. From the disc. Not the page.”

She read it back from the disc. She got to the hook and stopped, and looked at it, and said, “It — doesn’t close,” and he said, “No,” and she said, “I can see that,” in a voice that had surprise in it, and he said, “Yes. That’s reading. You’re reading bronze now. That’s the first time anyone’s read a sentence off a thing that wasn’t me or a page.”

He recut the hook. It took the rest of the evening. On the second evening he cut the pushing part. On the fourth the bracket began, and the bracket was the whole of the doorstep — the part that checked, that went looking for how cold a thing was and compared it to a thing and gave exactly the difference — and it took five evenings, and on the ninth, at a quarter to nine, with Flitwick in the corner and Penelope taking notes she had stopped pretending were for anyone and Ron eating the last of the Humber Bridge, he put the graver down and the sentence was in the bronze, all of it, every mark, and he sat back and did not touch it.

“Is it done?” said Terry.

“It’s in. I don’t know if it’s done. I don’t know what a sentence does when it’s carved in my marks instead of theirs; nobody does; the runes have forty marks and I’ve got two hundred and twenty and nobody has ever cut one of mine into anything.” He looked at the disc. It lay on the bench, a saucer of bronze with grooves in it, and it was — he reached, the way he reached for a sentence going past at the roses — it was humming. Faint. Under hearing. The way the wall in the yard hummed; the way the castle sang. “It’s holding. Not because I’m holding it. Because it is it. Neville was right. It had something to hold on to.”

Flitwick got out of his chair and came and looked at the disc and did not touch it either.

“Dr Potter,” he said. “That is a rune.”

“Yes, sir. In a language with four speakers.”

“There are forty runes. There have been forty for a thousand years. Nobody has ever—” He stopped. “You have made a new rune. On a Thursday. With my graver.”

“I’ve made a sentence in a thing. The runes are sentences in things in their marks; this is one in mine. It’s the same idea. It’s only new because nobody could see the sentence to cut it.” Harry looked at the hum. “I don’t know if it’ll press. That’s next. That’s November. Tonight I just wanted to know whether bronze would hold a thing I’d written, and it will, and I’d like to sit with that for a minute before I find out what it’s for.”

He sat with it for a minute, which was most of three hours, and the room let him, and at the end of it he put the disc in the tin with the other carved things, and the two sentences in green ink and a letter for the house in the box file with the Second Edition, and went to dinner, and did not eat much, and Hermione counted, and said so.

The asking he cast on the Saturday, in the sloping room, because he had decided in September that the first composed sentence he ever sent down holly would be that one, and because Flitwick had a chair, and because he had promised a woman on a pavement that he would tell someone before he did a thing that might be impossible, and he had told Flitwick on the Thursday and Flitwick had said bring a cup.

He brought a cup. It was from the Ravenclaw common room and had a crack in it that ran from the lip to the handle, and he put it on the bench and sat down in front of it and took out the holly, and the room went quiet, because the room had learned in six weeks what it meant when he took out the holly and did not flick.

“This is a sentence I wrote at nine,” he said. “On paper, in a cupboard. It isn’t in any book; it isn’t any of theirs; I built it from the grammar because there was a part of the thing that mends that I could see and nobody else could, and the part was the looking. Everything they’ve got mends a this. Reparo, for a thing that’s broken. A hundred Healing charms, each for a thing that’s wrong, each with its name. The thing that came up through me on a kitchen floor when I was six didn’t know what was wrong with a cat. It went and found out. I wrote that part down on its own. No mending. Just the question.” He put his hand flat on the bench beside the cup. “If it works, it won’t fix the cup. It’ll tell me what’s wrong with it. I know what’s wrong with it; I can see the crack. That’s why it’s a cup and not a person. I want to know whether it answers, and I want to be able to check.”

“And if it doesn’t work?” said Padma.

“Then I find out where I wrote it wrong, and go back. That’s what Saturdays are.” He looked at Flitwick. “Sir. You’ve got the chair?”

“I am in the chair, Dr Potter.”

Harry held the asking.

Forty lines of his marks, built at nine, held in the back of his mind for two years without once being sent, the way a note is held in singing: a bracket that was only the looking part, that went into a thing and asked of every part of it are you the shape you are for? and came back not with a mending but with a map. He had never once sent it. He had held it so long that it had worn a groove in him the way the doorstep sentence had worn one in the bronze, and he found it there, waiting, and sent it down the holly, and held.

Nothing happened to the cup.

That was right. He had not told it to do anything. He had asked. And the asking went down the wand and out through the grain and into the cup, and he felt it go — thinner than a mending, the way the pencil had been thinner than the cat, sent and not risen — and he felt it go looking, the way Mrs Prentice went along a shelf, hand out, sure of the general area, through the cup, every part of it, the lip and the handle and the glaze and the clay under the glaze, asking each part the same question, and then it came back.

It came back as a shape.

Not a picture. Not words. A shape, in the place in him where sentences arrived, the way the roses had arrived, the way the hat had — a map of the cup with the wrongness in it marked, and the wrongness was the crack, lip to handle, which he had known, and was also more than the crack: was a place under the glaze a quarter-inch from the handle where the clay had been fired too fast sixty years ago and had a weakness in it that had not yet become a crack and would, in about a year, if somebody put hot tea in it on a cold morning.

He sat very still.

“Well?” said Hermione.

“It works.” He said it quietly. “The crack, lip to handle. Which we knew. And a — there’s a place by the handle, under the glaze, where it was fired wrong. It isn’t cracked yet. It will be. The asking found it.” He looked at the cup. “I didn’t know that was there. Nobody could have known that was there. The asking looked, and it was there, and it told me.”

“Can I—” said Terry, and stopped, and said it anyway: “Can I check? Is there a way to check?”

“Yes,” said Harry. “That’s the whole point. There’s always a way to check or it isn’t worth doing.” He pushed the cup across the bench. “Hot water. From the urn. Pour it in and watch the handle.”

Terry poured. The room watched the handle. And at eleven seconds — Harry counted — a second crack opened, hairline, a quarter-inch from the handle, under the glaze, exactly where the map had said, and ran an inch, and stopped.

Nobody said anything.

Then Padma Patil, who had been annoyed for six weeks and had got the hang of failing exactly and was unbearable about it, said, in a voice with none of the annoyance in it at all: “It knew. It knew before the water. Harry, that’s not a charm. That’s—” She stopped, because she did not have the word, and Harry found that he loved her a little for stopping, because he did not have it either.

“It’s a question,” he said. “That gets an answer you can check. Carrow would call it an instrument. Mrs Osei would call it a probe. I called it the asking because I was nine and it was the first word I had.” He looked at the very small man in the chair. “Sir. I’d like to try it on something that isn’t a cup. Not a person — not yet, not here. But I’d like to ask you first, because if it works on a person it has to be asked, every time, and I want to practise the asking of the asking before I need it.”

Flitwick had not moved. He was looking at the second crack in the cup with his small hands folded in his lap and his face doing the thing it had done over a girl reading a mark, and he did not answer for a long moment, and when he did his voice had no bounce in it.

“My wrist,” he said. “The left. I broke it in 1947 in a duel I should not have fought and it was set by a man in a hurry and it has ached in the cold for forty-four years and three Healers have told me it is as good as it will get.” He held it out, across the arm of the chair, palm down, the way an old woman had once held out two hands across a table. “Ask it.”

Harry did not take the wrist. He had learned on a kitchen floor that you did not need to touch; you needed to mind. He held the asking, and sent it, and it went down the holly and across the three feet of air between a bench and a chair and into an old man’s wrist, looking, and came back.

A break, old, set wrong, a quarter-inch out of true. Which three Healers had known. And — the map was very clear about this, the way it had been clear about the glaze — a second thing, not in the bone, in the thin tough cord that ran beside it, that had been pulled in the same duel and had never been looked at because the bone had been the thing that showed, and had healed short, and was the thing that actually ached in the cold, and had ached for forty-four years while three Healers looked at a bone.

He told him. Plainly, the way Carrow told a result.

Flitwick sat in the chair with his wrist held out and did not say anything for a long time, and then he drew the wrist back, slowly, and looked at it, and turned it, and pressed with his other thumb at the place a quarter-inch from where the Healers had pressed, and his face went white and then did something else.

“There,” he said. “That’s — Dr Potter, that is exactly — forty-four years and nobody—” He stopped. “You could mend it.”

“I think so. Not today. Not without asking Madam Pomfrey, because it’s a thing in a person and I’ve only ever mended a cat and a squib and a pair of hands, and there is a hospital wing in this building with a Healer in it and I would like her to be in the room when I do, because she knows what a wrist is and I know what a sentence is and I think those are the two halves.” He put the holly down. “And because that’s the whole of what the asking is for, sir. Not me doing it. Her doing it. A Healer with this doesn’t need page ninety-one. She asks the body what’s wrong and the body tells her, and she checks, and if the two of us can get it into her hands I don’t care whose name is on it.”

Flitwick looked at him for a long moment, holding his own wrist.

“Poppy,” he said, “will not believe a word of it.”

“No, sir. Neither did Carrow. Neither did Aldous. Neither did Snape about the ring on the pewter. That’s what checking is for.” Harry stood up. “Will you introduce me? Properly. Not as — not as the thing in the index. As someone who has a question for her and would like her to ask it back.”

“Wednesday,” said Filius Flitwick, and flexed his wrist, and winced, and did not stop looking at it. “I shall take you myself. And Dr Potter — I should like it on record, in whatever you keep your records in, that I have been a Charms master for forty-one years and I have just watched a first-year find a thing in my own arm that three Healers missed, with a sentence he wrote in a cupboard at nine, and that I did not once think to ask whether the question was well-formed.”

“It’s on record, sir,” said Harry. “I count everything.”

Madam Pomfrey did not believe a word of it.

She said so, in the hospital wing, on the Wednesday evening, with the long room empty but for one Hufflepuff second-year in the end bed with a wrist in a sling, and Flitwick standing very small beside Harry with his face set, and Hermione a step behind with the forty-seventh pad open and a quill, because Harry had asked her to come and write down whatever he said in the marks as he said it, so that there would be a record that was not in his head.

“A charm that diagnoses,” said Madam Pomfrey. She was a square woman with a square face and hands that had set ten thousand bones and did not believe in anything that had not set one. “Filius. I have been a Healer for thirty-one years. There is no such charm. There are two hundred and six charms in the Healer’s Compendium and every one of them does something, and the doing is how you know what was wrong, and if a first-year had written a two hundred and seventh at nine in a cupboard I should expect to have heard about it from St Mungo’s and not from the Charms master in my own ward at eight o’clock on a Wednesday.” She looked at Harry. Not at the scar; he noted that, and liked her for it. At him. “Dr Potter. I have read about you in the Prophet. I have been told about you by Minerva, who does not exaggerate, and by Filius, who does. I should like you to understand that in this room I do not care what you are. I care what is wrong with the child in that bed, and I know what is wrong with him, because I set it, and I will not have him experimented on.”

“No, Madam Pomfrey,” said Harry. “Nor would I. May I tell you what I’m asking for, and then you can say no?”

She folded her arms. He had been folded-arms at by Carrow and Aldous and a goblin and he knew what it meant, which was go on, then.

“I’m not asking to do anything to him. I’m asking to ask him a question, from here, without touching him, that he won’t feel and that won’t change anything. The question comes back to me as a — as a map. Of where the wrongness is. If the map says what you already know, then I’ve wasted four seconds of your evening and you can send me to bed. If the map says something you don’t know, then you can check it, with your hands, which have set ten thousand bones, and if I’m wrong you’ll know in a minute and if I’m right you’ll know in a minute, and either way you’ve lost a minute.” He paused. “That’s the whole of it. A question and a check. It’s what Professor Flitwick let me do to his wrist on Saturday, and I found a thing three Healers missed, and he has been pressing the place with his thumb for four days and not believing it.”

Pomfrey looked at Flitwick. Flitwick, with great dignity, pressed the place with his thumb and winced.

“Forty-four years,” he said. “Poppy. Forty-four years and it was the cord.”

Pomfrey looked at his wrist for a long moment with a professional’s face. Then she looked at the Hufflepuff in the end bed, who had been following all of this with his mouth open.

“Mr Cadwallader,” she said. “Do you consent to being asked a question you will not feel by a first-year with a doctorate?”

“Er,” said Cadwallader. “Yes? Yes, Madam Pomfrey.”

“Four seconds,” said Pomfrey, to Harry. “And then you tell me what you have, in plain English, and I check it, and if you have made that boy’s wrist one iota worse I shall have you out of this ward and out of this school and I do not care whose name is in whose index.”

“Yes, Madam Pomfrey. Hermione — write it as I say it.”

He held the asking. He sent it, across twenty feet of ward, into a second-year’s wrist in a sling, and it went looking, and came back.

“A clean break,” he said, “across the small bone on the outside, two inches above the hand. Set well. Knitting. About a third of the way there.” Pomfrey’s face did not move; that was what she knew. “And a hairline. In the other bone, the big one, on the inside, an inch higher. Very fine. Not displaced. It didn’t show when the other broke because the other was the thing that showed. It’s also knitting, on its own, because it’s small, but it’s knitting a hair out of true and in about three weeks when the sling comes off and he leans on it it’ll go, and you’ll set it then and wonder why a boy who was mending broke again.” He let the asking go. “That’s what the map says. I don’t know whether it’s right. Check it.”

Pomfrey did not move for a moment.

Then she crossed the ward, and took the sling off Cadwallader’s arm with hands that had done it ten thousand times, and did not look at the small bone at all. She put two fingers on the big one, an inch above the break, on the inside, where the map had said, and pressed, gently, and Cadwallader said “Ow,” in a surprised voice, and Pomfrey’s face did a thing that Harry had seen on exactly one Healer’s face in his life, and the Healer had been in a letter from a squib about a man called Amos.

She took out her wand and said a word and the wrist glowed faintly along its length for a second and she looked at the glow where her fingers had been, and was still.

“Filius,” she said, without turning round. “Come here.”

Flitwick went. He looked at the glow. He said nothing.

“Hairline,” said Madam Pomfrey, very quietly, to the wrist. “Radius. An inch proximal. Non-displaced. I did not see it. I set the ulna and I did not look at the radius because the ulna was the thing that showed.” She put the sling back on, carefully, the way Harry held things on stages. “Mr Cadwallader, you will keep that on for five weeks and not three, and you will not lean on it, and you will tell no one why, and if you do I shall know.” She turned round.

Harry had never once in his life been looked at by a Healer who had just found a thing she had missed, and he found that it was a face he would have crossed a very long way to see, and that he had, in a sense, since a bus stop.

“What is it?” said Madam Pomfrey. “Dr Potter. In plain English. What is that.”

“A question,” said Harry. “That the body answers. That’s all. There’s a part of mending that goes looking before it mends, and nobody can see the part because it happens in the quarter-second before the charm, and I can see it, and I wrote it down on its own with the mending left off. It doesn’t do anything. It asks. The body says. You check.” He hesitated, and then said the rest, because she had crossed a ward on his word and had found a thing and had said I did not see it in front of a second-year. “Madam Pomfrey. The Healer’s Compendium. The fourth chapter. The dosage table on page ninety-one.”

She went very still.

“The columns are transposed. A printer did it sixty years ago and nobody checked because the table looks right and tables that look right are where—” He stopped. “I found it when I was eight, in a cupboard, and put a margin on it that said surely not, and didn’t know it had killed anyone. The Headmaster told me it had killed two. I asked for their names. He sent them. I have them in the back of a pad because I thought somebody should.” He took the pad from Hermione, and turned to the back, and held it out. “I don’t know whether you knew either of them. I thought you might. I thought — if you were going to use the asking, you should know what the asking is for, and it isn’t cups.”

Poppy Pomfrey took the pad. She read the two names. She read them for a long time, for two names, and when she looked up her square face had done a thing that square faces are not built for.

“The second one,” she said. “She was a student here. 1962. I was a trainee. I was not the one who—” She stopped. “I knew her. I did not know it was the table. We were told it was the dose. We were told the Healer had—” She closed the pad. “Sixty years. The columns. And a boy of eight in a cupboard wrote surely not.”

“Yes.”

“And nobody at St Mungo’s—”

“I don’t know, Madam Pomfrey. I’ve never been to St Mungo’s. I’d like to. It’s on a list.” He held out his hand for the pad, and she gave it back, and he put it under his arm. “I’d like to teach you the asking. Not me casting it; you. I don’t know whether it can be taught; I’ve been teaching a Charms master and a girl who catches to read it for a month and they can, and nobody has yet cast a thing of mine but me. But if anyone alive could, it would be a person who has set ten thousand bones and knows what a wrist is for, because the asking is only a question and the person asking has to know what to ask. I don’t. I know sentences. You know wrists. I think those are the two halves. I’ve been saying that about everything since I was eight and I’ve never once been wrong.”

Pomfrey looked at him for a long time across her own ward with the second-year in the end bed watching with his mouth open and a very small man beside her pressing his wrist with his thumb.

“Wednesdays,” she said at last. “Eight o’clock. This ward. You will bring the girl, because somebody has to write it down who is not you, and you will bring Filius, because somebody has to sit in a chair and tell me when I am being a fool, and you will cast nothing on any patient in this room without my hand on the bed and my word first, and when I say stop you will stop in the middle of a mark.” She unfolded her arms. “And Dr Potter. The names. I should like a copy. I have a drawer.”

“You’ll have them tonight,” said Harry. “I’ll write them out myself. In the round hand. For the drawer.”

He wrote them out that night, by the window, in the blue pen E.P. had given him, two names on a sheet of good paper, for a drawer in a hospital wing, and under them, because the reader in fifty years would want to know, he wrote the date and Madam Pomfrey knew the second one; she was a trainee; they were told it was the dose, and folded it, and put it in an envelope, and gave it to Hedwig, who took it across a castle at midnight to a ward with a square woman in it who did not sleep either.

Then he wrote the day in the forty-seventh pad, for the reader, and for Hermione, who would want to see what he had made of it.

The asking works. On a cup, which I could check, and found a thing under the glaze that nobody could have known and that cracked at eleven seconds where the map said. On an old man’s wrist, and found the cord three Healers missed in forty-four years. On a second-year’s arm across twenty feet of ward, and found a hairline a Healer had not looked for because the other bone was the one that showed, and she checked it with two fingers and a word and said I did not see it in front of the boy, which is the bravest thing I have seen anyone do in this castle, and I have seen a man admit a trap.

She knew the second name. 1962. She was a trainee. They were told it was the dose.

Wednesdays. Eight. The ward. She said bring the girl because somebody has to write it down who is not me, and I have been the only one writing it down for five years, and I did not know how much I wanted someone else to be the one until a Healer said so in a voice that was not kind and was better than kind.

The disc hums. The asking answers. The table has a Healer at it now, which is the chair I wanted most and thought would take longest. Four weeks. I gave him a term.

Item four on the List was Pain. Mrs F's hands, forty years. Flitwick's wrist, forty-four. A boy in the end bed who would have broken again in three weeks and wondered why. I did not mend any of them tonight. I asked, and somebody who knows what a wrist is for is going to do the mending, with a question in her hand that she can check, and that is better than me doing it, and I have known since a kitchen floor that it would be and I have never once until tonight seen what it looks like.

It looks like a square woman with two fingers on a boy's arm saying ow in a surprised voice. I would not trade it for the fourth floor.

He closed the pad. The mountains were out there being mountains. The castle sang. The bronze disc in the biscuit tin under the bed hummed its small hum, holding a sentence a man had written on a doorstep and a boy had cut into metal with a dead duellist’s graver, and in a ward across the castle a Healer of thirty-one years sat with a drawer open and two names in a round hand and did not sleep, and in a dormitory in a tower a girl who had written forty lines of a language with one speaker into a pad in a clinical voice lay awake deciding whether she was annoyed about being brought to a ward to be a scribe or whether it was the most important thing she had ever been asked to do, and decided, somewhere towards one, that it was both, and that she would tell him so, every time.

He did not sleep. He had not expected to. He had, for once, something better than the next thing to think about, which was the last one, and he let himself.

Contents

Chapter 30

Brooms

The broom was a sentence in a stick, and it was listening, and he stood over it on the grass with twenty-five Ravenclaws and Hufflepuffs on a grey Thursday afternoon and did not say up, because he had just understood what the stick was for and wanted to sit with it for a second before he asked it anything.

Madam Hooch had hawk’s eyes and a hawk’s way of standing and had told them to put out their right hands and say up, and twenty-four children had said up in the voices of children shouting at a dog, and some of the brooms had come and some had rolled over and one — Terry Boot’s — had hit him in the shin. Harry had put out his hand and had not said anything, because the moment his hand went over the broom he had felt it, the way he had felt the hat from twenty feet: a sentence, old, carried in the wood and the twigs, holding itself, cast once by somebody long ago and casting itself ever since.

And the sentence had a listening part.

He had thought, since he was seven, that the listening part was the thing he had that nobody else did — that they told and he asked — and he had found a listening part in the roof, and in the doorstep, and had put both down to the man who wrote them and the woman who had asked once with everything she had. He had not expected to find one in a school broom with half its twigs gone. But there it was. The broom was not waiting for up. The broom was asking, over and over, in a small patient sentence that had been running since before his grandparents were born, where do you want to go? — and up was just the word most people shouted at it because nobody had ever told them it was a question.

“Potter,” said Hooch. “You’re not saying it.”

“No, Madam Hooch. Sorry. I was — I was reading it.” He looked down at the broom. “It’s asking. Isn’t it. It wants to know where. The word’s just — the word’s how people answer without knowing they’ve been asked.”

Hooch looked at him with the hawk’s eyes for a long moment, and he had the sense — he had been having it a great deal, in a month — of a professional of long standing being told something about her own instrument that she had known in her hands for thirty years and had never once heard said.

“Answer it, then,” she said.

He did not say up. He thought here, the way he would have thought it at a cat, and the broom came into his hand like a thing that had been waiting to be asked properly, and sat there, and hummed.

He was good at it. He had not expected to be, and was, and the reason was the reason for everything else.

A broom went where you wanted. That was the whole of a broom; Ron had explained it on a train for forty minutes and Quidditch Through the Ages had explained it for two hundred pages and neither had said the thing that mattered, which was that wanting was a sentence, and most people sent it ragged. They wanted up and left and faster in the shouting way, the eyes-shut way, and the broom — which was listening, which had been built to listen — got a ragged answer and did its best, and the best was a wobble, and the wobble was what people called learning to fly.

He did not wobble. He sat on a school broom twenty feet over a lawn in Scotland and held where the way he held a feather, steady, every mark of it, and the broom did exactly and only what he had asked, and he found — the first time he banked, with the castle going round him and the lake coming up gold on the left — that it was the first thing he had ever done with his body that his body was not the slow part of.

Because the broom was fast. The broom was his speed, nearly; it answered in the quarter-second, and in the quarter-second he had three minutes to decide, and so for the first time in his life the thing he was riding and the thing he was thinking with were going at something like the same rate, and the lawn went under him and the towers went past and he was not waiting for anything, and he laughed out loud over the Hufflepuffs with the wind in his face, which he had not done since a cupboard and a pencil, and Hooch, on the ground, did not tell him to come down.

“Potter,” she said, when he did. “Who taught you?”

“Nobody, Madam Hooch. It’s the first time. It’s just — it asks, and I can answer steady. That’s all. Everyone else is shouting.”

“Everyone else,” said Hooch, “has been shouting at brooms for a thousand years, and I have taught flying for twenty-six of them, and I have never once seen a first-year bank without a wobble.” She looked at him with the hawk’s eyes. “You’ll want the team. Flitwick’s lot are short a Seeker.”

He had known that was coming since the lake came up gold. He had thought about it on the way down — most of a quarter-hour — and had decided, and did not like the decision, and was going to keep it.

“No, Madam Hooch,” he said. “Thank you. I’d like to fly. I’d like to fly a great deal. I don’t want to play.”

“Why not.”

He looked at the broom in his hand, humming, asking.

“Because I can see the Snitch,” he said.

He proved it on the Sunday, because Ron would not believe him and because Hooch had told Flitwick and Flitwick had told the captain and the captain was a fourth-year called Davies who had come to find him in the sloping room.

“Just try,” said Davies. “One go. Hooch says you bank like a seventh-year. We lost to Hufflepuff last year by a hundred and ten because our Seeker couldn’t find the thing in a fog. One go, Potter. Humour me.”

So he went to the pitch on the Sunday morning, with Davies and Ron and Hermione — who had come, she said, to count — and Davies let the Snitch out of its box, and it went up into the grey like a thought, and Harry sat on a school broom at the centre line and did not move.

He read it.

The Snitch was a sentence too. Of course it was; everything in this world that moved by itself was a sentence somebody had put in a thing, and this one was small and gold and fast, and its sentence said, as near as he could put it, do not be caught. But do not be caught was not a plan. It was a rule. And a rule, he had learned at two from a woman at a cooker, ran the same way every time, because rules did not know when they were wrong. The Snitch ran a pattern. A good one, a thousand years sanded, with nine or ten turns in it that looked random to anyone watching at one-to-one and were not random at all to anyone who could stand still inside a quarter-second and watch the sentence choose.

He watched it choose eleven times. It took four seconds. Then he knew where it would be in two more, and went there, and it was, and he closed his hand on it, and sat on the broom at the far end of the pitch with a gold thing beating in his fist like a heart, and looked back down the length of the grass at three people who had not seen him move.

“Again,” said Davies, in a changed voice.

He let it go. It went up. He read it — nine turns this time; the pattern had a second pattern under it that came out when it had been caught once, which he thought was rather clever of whoever had built it — and went where it would be, and it was, and he had it, and the whole thing had taken six seconds.

“Again.”

“Davies,” said Harry. “It won’t be different. I can do it as many times as you like and it will take about five seconds each time and I will not once miss, because I am not catching it. I’m reading it. It has a pattern and I can see the pattern choose and I go where it’s going to be. That isn’t Seeking. That’s—” he looked for the word and found it in a geography room with a man who smelled of pipe “—that’s playing chess against someone who can’t see the board. I did that for four years every Wednesday to a man I liked and I threw the last game so he’d have one to tell the club about. I’m not going to do it to Hufflepuff.”

Davies sat on his broom with his mouth open.

“You’d win,” he said. “Every match. For seven years.”

“Yes. And nobody in the stands would have a game to watch, and the Hufflepuff Seeker would spend seven years finding out what it feels like to be hunted by someone who isn’t looking, and I know what that feels like, and it’s cold, and I don’t do it to people.” He let the Snitch go. It went up into the grey and hung there, and he watched it hang, and did not read it. “Ron. Tell him.”

Ron, who had been sitting on his broom beside Davies, said slowly, “He’s right. It’d be — it wouldn’t be Quidditch. It’d be Harry, with a broom, and everyone else sort of — there.” He looked at Harry. “Mate. You could be the best Seeker in the history—”

“I know. That’s why I shouldn’t.” He turned the broom. “I’ll fly with you. Sundays. Here, if Hooch lets us. I’ll fly as hard as you like and I won’t read anything and you can try to knock me off, and when Ravenclaw play Hufflepuff I’ll be in the stands shouting for a Seeker who can’t see the board, because that’s what the game is, and I’d like to find out what it feels like to watch one.” He looked at Davies. “Find someone who wobbles. Then teach them not to. That’s a Seeker. I’m a — I don’t know what I am. I’m a boy who can read a Snitch and has decided not to.”

Davies looked at him a long moment. Then he looked at the Snitch, hanging in the grey, unread.

“Potter,” he said, “that is the most Ravenclaw thing anyone has ever said to me, and I am a Ravenclaw, and I hate it, and you’re right.” He went to get the box. “Sundays. I’ll square it with Hooch. And if you ever change your mind—”

“I won’t. But I’ll tell you first if I do. I tell people first. It’s a rule.”

Hermione, on the grass, had been counting. She came over when Davies had gone and looked up at him on the broom and said, “Five seconds and six. You could have done it in three. You waited.”

“I wanted him to see it twice. Once could have been luck. I don’t do luck; I did it once at four about a star crossing a gap and I’ve never trusted it since.” He got off the broom. “Was that right? Saying no?”

She thought about it honestly, which she had learned from somebody.

“Yes,” she said. “And you hated it. I counted that too.”

He flew with Ron on the Sunday afternoon, and did not read anything, and it was the best two hours he had spent in the open air since a bench in the Alley with an old woman holding his hand.

Ron flew the way Ron did everything, which was all at once and with his whole heart and no plan, and he was good, in the way that Dudley had been good at football — not clever, not careful, but there, in the thing, every second of it, shouting at a broom that had been built to be asked and getting, somehow, by sheer wanting, most of what he shouted for. Harry flew beside him and did not hold anything steady and let the broom wobble under him for the first time in his life and found out what learning to fly was, which was a thing he had never once had the chance to find out about anything, because he had always been able to see the answer before he had the question.

“You’re wobbling,” Ron shouted, over the wind, delighted.

“I know! I’m doing it on purpose! I wanted to know what it was like!”

“What what’s like?”

“Being bad at a thing because I don’t know how to do it! I’ve never — Ron, I’ve never once in my life not known how to do a thing I was doing! This is—” he wobbled, hard, and corrected it the shouting way, the way everyone did, and the broom did its best, and the best was a lurch that put the lake briefly where the sky should have been “—this is brilliant!”

Ron laughed so hard he nearly came off. They flew until Hooch came out and shouted at them, and then a bit after, and when they came down Ron said, “You could read it, though. The whole time. You could have stopped wobbling any second.”

“Yes.”

“But you didn’t.”

“No. I wanted to be in the game. Not watching it. I’ve watched everything my whole life, Ron. I wanted one afternoon of not knowing where the Snitch was going to be.” He looked at the broom. “It was the best afternoon I’ve had here. Don’t tell Hermione; she counts.”

“She’ll know anyway,” said Ron. “She catches. You said.”

“I did say,” said Harry, and found he was grinning, and did not stop for some time.

Draco Malfoy found him on the Monday, in the entrance hall, between Charms and lunch, with two large boys in the attitudes of furniture and the face from a stool in a shop that had been practising for a month.

“Potter. I hear you turned down the Ravenclaw team.” He said it to the furniture, not to Harry. “Davies was telling anyone who’d listen. Reads the Snitch, apparently. Catches it in five seconds and won’t play.” He looked at Harry at last. “Afraid of losing to a Slytherin, are we? Or is it that a doctor doesn’t stoop to—”

“I ate a biscuit on a train,” said Harry, “and I didn’t see who took the one on the seat by the door. It went at Peterborough. It’s the first thing in a year I didn’t see, and I’ve been thinking about it since, and I’d like to ask you what you thought when you ate it, because I think you did, and I think nobody has ever asked you what you thought about anything.”

The entrance hall was not quiet; it was lunchtime. But the six feet of it round the two of them went quiet, and the furniture shifted, and Draco Malfoy stood with a line ready and the line did not go anywhere, because nobody in the six feet had done anything he had been told to expect.

“I didn’t—” he said.

“It’s all right. You don’t have to say. I didn’t see, so I don’t know, so you can tell me it wasn’t you and I’ll have to take your word, which is the first time in a year anyone’s had that over me and I think it should be you, because I think you took it, and I think you took it when nobody was looking because you didn’t want to be seen taking a thing from someone your father has thought about what to do with.” He kept his voice level, because the sentence had been built on a Sunday afternoon with a broom wobbling under him and he wanted it to carry. “I turned down the team because I can see the Snitch and it wouldn’t be a game. Not because of you. I don’t do things because of people, Malfoy. I do them because of what they are. I’d have turned it down if Ravenclaw were playing nobody.” He paused. “And I’d still like to know what you thought about the biscuit. The offer’s open. It was open on the train. It’s open now.”

Draco looked at him for a long moment with his father’s face on and something under it that was not his father’s at all, and Harry watched the two of them fight it out behind the pale eyes the way two Vernons had fought behind a purple one, and this time it was not no contest, and this time he could not tell who had won.

“It was dry,” said Draco Malfoy, at last, to the floor, very fast, as if the words had got out before he could stop them. “The biscuit. It was a bit dry.”

“They were from a tin from Edinburgh. They’d been in it since July. My — a woman who looked after me packed them.” Harry did not smile. He wanted to and did not, because a boy who had just said a true thing to the floor did not want to be smiled at. “Next time I’ll give you one from the new tin. It’s got the Humber Bridge on it. They’re better.”

“I don’t want your—”

“I know. The offer’s open anyway. Everyone’s is.” He shouldered his bag. “Lunch. If you want to know what I thought about the Snitch, I’m in the sloping room on the fourth floor on Thursdays. Nobody’s going to ask you what a bezoar is. Neville already knows.”

He went to lunch. He did not look back; he had learned at the bottom of some steps in Egham what it did to a person to be looked back at while they were deciding something. But Hermione, who had been standing in the doorway of the Great Hall for the whole of it with her chin up, said, when he reached her, very quietly:

“It was dry.”

“Yes.”

“That’s the first true thing he’s said in this building.”

“I know. It’s a start. I counted.”

The broom came apart on the Saturday, in the sloping room, on a bench, with Flitwick in the chair and the Workshop round it, and it was the first thing Harry had ever taken apart that had been built by somebody who could listen.

It was a school broom, the oldest Hooch had, with half its twigs gone and a crack in the handle that she had been meaning to have mended since 1979, and she had given it to Flitwick with the hawk’s eyes narrowed and said if the boy can tell me why it pulls left I shall want to know. Harry had not taken it apart with his hands. He had sat in front of it with the holly and sent the asking into it, the way he had into a cup, and the map had come back, and the map had been — he sat very still with it for a long time — the map had been beautiful.

“It’s got three,” he said. “Three sentences. Not one. I thought a broom was one thing that listened and it isn’t. There’s one that holds it up — that’s the old one, that’s the one that’s been running since before anybody in this room was born, it’s enormous, it’s like the wall in the yard — and there’s one that steers, that’s the listening one, the one that asks where you want to go, and that one’s newer and somebody good wrote it, and there’s a third one—” He stopped. “There’s a third one that cushions. That makes it not hurt to sit on. And that one’s the newest and it’s bad. It’s written by somebody who was shouting. It’s ragged. And it’s rubbing against the steering one, the way two people hold a face, and that’s why it pulls left. The cushioning charm’s pulling the steering charm’s sleeve.”

Flitwick had got out of his chair.

“Three,” he said. “Three separate—”

“Yes, sir. Layered. Somebody wrote the lift a thousand years ago and somebody wrote the steering four hundred years ago and somebody wrote the cushioning about sixty and they never once met and the three sentences are sharing a stick and nobody has ever read all three at once to see whether they get on.” He looked at the broom. “They nearly do. The old two are fine; they were written by people who could hold. The third one wasn’t. I could — sir, I could rewrite the third one. Clean. In my marks. Cut it into the handle under the crack where it wouldn’t show. It would stop pulling left and it would be the first broom in the world with a cushioning charm that didn’t argue with the steering.”

“And then,” said Hermione, from the bench, with the pad open, “you could do it to all of them.”

The room went quiet.

“A press,” said Harry slowly. “A press for brooms. Not blankets. A seal with a clean cushioning sentence in it that you press into the handle and the broom stops pulling left, every broom, every time, and nobody has to shout at it.” He looked at Hermione. “That’s — that’s not the blankets. That’s not a hospital. That’s a shop. That’s a thing you could sell to Davies’s father.”

“Yes,” said Hermione. “And the money would go to the ward. And the Wizengamot wouldn’t have to amend anything, because it’s a broom, and brooms are allowed.” Her chin was up. “You said the table needed more chairs. I’m saying some of the chairs have to be paid for. I grew up with two dentists. I know what a ward costs.”

Harry sat on the bench in a room that sloped and looked at a girl who had grown up with two dentists and had just, in one sentence, turned a press for blankets into a press for everything, and understood that he had been right on the platform and right on the landing and right in the entrance hall, and that the thing of which all of them had been the small dose was this: somebody else getting there first, in the room, out loud, where he could count it.

“Hermione Granger,” he said, “that is the most useful thing anyone has said in this room since Neville said seeds, and I am going to write it down with your name on it in a pad that a person is going to read in fifty years, and the person is going to know who thought of selling the press.”

“I didn’t think of selling it. I thought of paying for the ward.”

“I know. That’s why it goes in with your name.” He turned to Flitwick, who was standing very small in the middle of the room with his hand on a broom that had three sentences in it and looking at it the way he had looked at page one of a biscuit tin. “Sir. Can I have the broom?”

“Dr Potter,” said Filius Flitwick, “you may have every broom in this castle, and I shall tell Rolanda why, and she will not believe me, and then she will fly one, and then she will.” He took his hand off it. “Three sentences. In a stick. Four hundred years and nobody read all three.” He went back to the chair and sat down in it and looked at nothing for a while. “I burned the wrong letter,” he said, eventually, to the room. “I should have burned the Compendium.”

He wrote it in the forty-seventh pad that night, by the window, with the mountains, and he gave it the title it had earned.

Brooms.

A broom is three sentences sharing a stick, written by three people who never met, four hundred years apart, and the newest one was written by somebody shouting and it argues with the steering and that is why Hooch's oldest broom pulls left. I can read all three at once. Nobody has. I am going to rewrite the third one clean and cut it under the crack and give it back to Hooch and not tell her which one I fixed and see if she can tell.

I can see the Snitch. I caught it twice in eleven seconds and said no to the team, because it would not have been a game, and Ron said it'd be Harry with a broom and everyone else sort of there, and Davies said it was the most Ravenclaw thing anyone had said to him and he hated it and I was right. I flew with Ron on Sunday and wobbled on purpose and did not read anything for two hours and it was the best afternoon I have had in the open air since a bench, and I did not know until I was doing it that I had never once in my life been bad at a thing I did not know how to do.

Draco Malfoy said the biscuit was dry. To the floor. Very fast. It is the first true thing he has said in this building and Hermione counted it before I did.

Hermione said: do it to all of them. And then: the money goes to the ward. And then: brooms are allowed. Three sentences, in a room, out loud, in the time it took me to see one, and the one I saw was the smallest. I have been the only one in the room for ten years and I did not know the room would be faster than me. Not at thinking. At arriving.

Item two on the List is the table. The table has a Healer and a Charms master and a prefect and a boy who said seeds and a boy who came for the biscuits and a girl who said do it to all of them. And a pale boy who said dry to the floor, nearly. Not yet.

Hooch has twenty-six brooms. Davies's father has a shop. A ward in London has a line in a budget. I had a term. I have nine weeks of it left and a press that does not yet press and a disc that hums and a girl who got there first.

Faster. But not alone. Those are not the same thing any more. I did not know that until Sunday, wobbling.

He closed the pad. The broom was in the corner of the dormitory where he had put it, humming its three hums, two old and clean and one ragged and sixty years old, and he looked at it a while, and at the mountains, and thought about a Snitch hanging in the grey, unread, because he had decided to let it, and found that of all the things he had done in six weeks in a castle, that was the one he was proudest of, and that he could not have explained why to anyone, in any of seven languages, except perhaps to a man who smelled of pipe and had once watched a knight thrown with love.

He did not sleep. He had not expected to. He wrote a letter to Mr Pole instead, about a Snitch, and gave it to Hedwig, and she went out over the lake into the dark, south, with a post from everywhere, and he watched her go until he could not, which was a long time, and did not mind.

Contents

Chapter 31

Interlude: The Staff Room

The staff room, Hogwarts, the evening before Hallowe’en, 1991

“It doesn’t pull left,” said Rolanda Hooch, to the fire, for the third time.

Nobody answered her, because everybody in the room had heard it twice already, and because it was the kind of sentence that did not want an answer so much as a witness.
